# Decorative Endpaper

<column-break/>

->**[Decorative Pattern]**<-

<note>This page features a repeating botanical motif common in high-quality bookbinding from the early modern period. The design utilizes a "palmette" or "anthemion" style—a decorative element derived from the fanned leaves of a palm tree or honeysuckle, which has its roots in ancient Greek and Egyptian art.</note>

The pattern consists of vertical columns of teardrop-shaped frames. Each frame encloses a stylized fan of radiating leaves, printed in a deep green pigment against a light, off-white background. The motifs are arranged in an interlocking grid that covers the entire surface of the page, serving both an aesthetic and protective function for the manuscript's interior.

<column-break/>

# Decorative Endpaper

<image-desc>
This page consists entirely of a repeating decorative pattern, commonly used as endpaper in historical bookbinding. The design is a block-printed or lithographed grid of stylized motifs in dark green and off-white/cream. Each individual unit is a spade-shaped enclosure containing a symmetrical palmette <note>A decorative motif resembling the fan-shaped leaves of a palm tree, common in classical and neoclassical design.</note> or fan-shaped floral design with five main radiating petals. These units are arranged in straight vertical columns and horizontal rows, creating a dense, rhythmic floral pattern across the whole surface. There is a visible horizontal seam or line near the top edge where the pattern registration shifts slightly or the paper was joined.
</image-desc>

# ۞ Biography of the Author ۞

<margin>
8749
.A35
F26

SJ
4-78
</margin>

He is the Judge <note>Arabic: al-Qadi</note> Abu al-Walid Muhammad ibn Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Rushd <note>Known in the West as Averroes (1126–1198), a preeminent philosopher and jurist of Islamic Spain</note>. He was born and raised in Cordoba, and was renowned for his virtue and his dedication to the acquisition of knowledge. He was peerless in the science of jurisprudence <note>Arabic: fiqh</note> and legal theory, having studied under the jurist and memorizer of traditions Abu Muhammad ibn Rizq. He was also distinguished in the science of medicine, being an excellent writer with a refined clarity of expression. In medicine, he authored the book *The General Principles* <note>Arabic: Kitab al-Kulliyat; famously known in Latin as the 'Colliget'</note>, which he composed with great mastery. There existed a deep friendship between him and Abu Marwan ibn Zuhr <note>Known in the West as Avenzoar, a famous physician of the same era</note>. When Ibn Rushd composed this book on general medical matters, he intended for Ibn Zuhr to compose a book on specific, localized ailments so that their two works combined would serve as a complete manual for the craft of medicine. For this reason, Ibn Rushd says at the end of his book the following:

> This concludes the discussion on the treatment of all categories of diseases in the briefest and clearest manner possible. There remains for us in this section the discussion on the healing of each individual symptom of the diseases that affect each specific organ. Although this is not strictly necessary—as it is already implicitly contained within the general principles previously discussed—it serves as a completion and a practical exercise. In it, we descend to the treatments of diseases according to each organ, which is the method followed by the authors of the *Medical Compendiums* <note>Arabic: Kunanish; a term for medical handbooks or pharmacopoeias</note>. Thus, we combine in our discussions the general principles with the specific applications, for this craft is the most deserving of crafts in which one should descend to specific details as much as possible. However, we shall delay this until a time when we are less occupied, due to our current concern with other pressing matters.

Therefore, whoever comes into possession of this book without that specific section, and wishes thereafter to consult the *Compendiums*, the most suitable of them is the book titled *The Facilitation* <note>Arabic: al-Taysir</note>, which was composed in our time by Abu Marwan ibn Zuhr. I myself requested this book from him and transcribed a copy of it.

<summary>This preface introduces the famous philosopher-physician Averroes (Ibn Rushd) and explains how his theoretical medical text, the 'Kulliyat', was designed to be complemented by Ibn Zuhr’s practical manual, the 'Taysir'.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, Ibn Rushd, Ibn Zuhr, Avenzoar, Kitab al-Kulliyat, Kitab al-Taysir, medicine, Cordoba, jurisprudence, medical compendiums</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Averroes (Ibn Rushd), specifically discussing his medical writings and his relationship with the Almohad court.</meta>

# Biography of the Author

This was the reason for its publication. As we have stated, it is a book of "Particular Discourses" <note>Arabic: *al-aqawil al-juz'iyya*; referring to specific medical treatments for individual diseases</note> which I have noted are in close agreement with the "Universal Discourses" <note>Arabic: *al-aqawil al-kulliyya*; referring to general medical principles or his work *Kulliyat fi al-Tibb*</note>. However, in that work, he mixed the treatments with symptoms and the underlying causes, following the custom of the authors of medical compendiums <term>Kunnash: a type of medical handbook or notebook common in the medieval Islamic world</term>. There is no need for the reader of this book of ours to engage with that; rather, the treatment alone is sufficient for him. In short, whoever masters what we have written regarding the universal principles will be able to distinguish right from wrong in the treatments and medicinal compositions described by the authors of those compendiums.

**I was told** by the Judge Abu Marwan al-Baji, who said: "The Judge Abu Walid ibn Rushd <note>Averroes (1126–1198), the famous Andalusian philosopher and physician</note> was of sound judgment, brilliant, yet modest in his dress and strong in spirit. He had occupied himself with the mathematical sciences and with medicine under Abu Ja'far ibn Harun, remaining with him for a long period and acquiring from him much of the philosophical sciences <term>al-ulum al-hikmiyya: a term encompassing philosophy, logic, and the natural sciences</term>."

Ibn Rushd had served as a judge in Seville before Cordoba. He was highly esteemed by al-Mansur <note>Abu Yusuf Yaqub al-Mansur, the third Almohad Caliph</note> and held a prominent position in his state. Likewise, his son al-Nasir respected him greatly. 

He said: "When al-Mansur was in Cordoba, preparing to set out on a military expedition against Alfonso <note>Alfonso VIII of Castile; this refers to the period leading up to the Battle of Alarcos</note> in the year 591 AH <note>1195 AD</note>, he summoned Abu Walid ibn Rushd. When he arrived, the Caliph showed him great honor and drew him so close that he surpassed the seating position of Abu Muhammad Abd al-Wahid, the son of Sheikh Abu Hafs al-Hintati, the companion of Abd al-Mu'min. This Abu Muhammad was the third or fourth of 'The Ten' <note>The inner council of the Almohad movement</note>. Al-Mansur had formed a marriage alliance with this Abu Muhammad, marrying him to his daughter due to his high standing; from this union, Abd al-Wahid was granted a son named Ali, who is now the ruler of Africa <note>Ifriqiya, modern-day Tunisia and eastern Algeria</note>."

When al-Mansur brought Ibn Rushd close and seated him by his side, he conversed with him. Then, Ibn Rushd departed while a group of students and many of his companions waited for him. They congratulated him on his high standing with al-Mansur and the favor shown to him. He replied: "By God, this is not something that warrants congratulations. The Commander of the Faithful has brought me closer in a single instance than I ever hoped for or expected." Meanwhile, a group of his enemies had begun to spread rumors that the Commander of the Faithful had...

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the philosopher Averroes (Ibn Rushd) and his relationship with the Almohad Caliph al-Mansur.</meta>

# Biography of the Author

He ordered his execution, but when he emerged unharmed, he commanded some of his servants to go to his house and tell his family to prepare for him a <term>qatt</term> <note>a type of dish, possibly a leek stew or a specific meat preparation</note> and boiled squabs until he arrived. His intention in doing so was merely to comfort their hearts regarding his safety. Later, al-Mansur <note>Abu Yusuf Yaqub al-Mansur, the third Almohad Caliph</note> became resentful toward Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd <note>Averroes</note> and ordered him to reside in Lucena, which is a town near Cordoba that was formerly inhabited by Jews, and forbade him from leaving it. He also took offense at a group of other notable and virtuous men, ordering them to stay in other locations. He made it appear that he did this to them because of the claim that they were occupied with <term>Wisdom</term> <note>Philosophy</note> and the <term>Sciences of the Ancients</term> <note>The Greek sciences, including physics and metaphysics</note>. This group included Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd, Abu Jafar al-Dhahabi, the jurist Abu Abd Allah Muhammad ibn Ibrahim (the judge of Bejaia), Abu al-Rabi’ the Blind, and Abu al-Abbas the Memorizer (the poet al-Qarabi). They remained in this state for a time. Then, a group of notables in Seville testified on behalf of Ibn Rushd that he was not as he had been accused, so al-Mansur became pleased with him and the rest of the group. This occurred in the year 595 <note>1198 CE</note>. He appointed Abu Jafar al-Dhahabi as the <term>Mizwar</term> <note>a head or provost</note> of the students and the <term>Mizwar</term> of the physicians. Al-Mansur used to describe and praise him, saying: "Abu Jafar al-Dhahabi is like pure gold; the smelting process only increases its quality."

The Judge Abu Marwan said that among the reasons for al-Mansur’s resentment toward Ibn Rushd was that whenever he attended al-Mansur’s council and spoke with him or debated a scientific matter, he would address al-Mansur by saying, "Listen, my brother." Furthermore, Ibn Rushd had authored a book on animals <note>likely his commentary on Aristotle's *History of Animals*</note> in which he mentioned various species and described each one. When he mentioned the giraffe, he described it and then said: "I saw the giraffe at the court of the King of the Berbers," meaning al-Mansur. When this reached al-Mansur, it distressed him, and it was one of the reasons that led to his resentment toward Ibn Rushd and his banishment. It is said that among the excuses Ibn Rushd offered was his claim: "I only said 'King of the Two Lands' <note>original: *Malik al-Barrayn*</note>, but it was misread by the reader as 'King of the Berbers' <note>original: *Malik al-Barbar*</note>." The death of the Judge Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd—may God have mercy on him—occurred in Marrakesh at the beginning of...

<summary>This page details the exile of Averroes to Lucena under Caliph al-Mansur due to his philosophical pursuits and perceived personal slights, followed by his eventual pardon and the linguistic misunderstanding regarding the "King of the Berbers."</summary>

# Biography of the Author

The year five hundred and ninety-five <note>1198 CE</note>, at the beginning of the reign of al-Nasir <note>The Almohad Caliph Muhammad al-Nasir</note>. Ibn Rushd <note>Known in the West as Averroes</note> had lived a long life. He left behind a son who was a physician, learned in the craft, called Abu Muhammad Abd Allah. He also left behind sons who occupied themselves with jurisprudence and served as judges in the provinces. Among the sayings of Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd is: "Whoever occupies himself with the science of anatomy increases his faith in God."

Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd authored many books, including:
*   **The Book of Attainment** (*Kitab al-Tahsil*), in which he collected the differing opinions of the scholars among the Companions <note>of the Prophet Muhammad</note>, the Successors, and those who followed them; he supported their schools of thought and clarified the points of ambiguity that are the source of disagreement.
*   **The Book of Introductions** on jurisprudence.
*   **The Distinguished Jurist’s Primer** (*Bidayat al-Mujtahid*) <note>original: "Nihayat al-Mujtahid"</note> on jurisprudence.
*   **The Book of Generalities** (*Kitab al-Kulliyat*) <note>A famous medical encyclopedia known in Latin as the *Colliget*</note>.
*   **Commentary on the Poem** attributed to the Chief Sheikh, Ibn Sina <note>Avicenna</note>, on medicine.
*   **The Book of Animals**.
*   **Compendia of the Books of Aristotle** on physics and metaphysics.
*   **The Book of Essentials** on logic, which is appended to them.
*   **Summaries of the Books of Aristotle**, which he summarized completely and exhaustively.
*   **Summary of the Metaphysics** by Nicolaus <note>Nicolaus of Damascus</note>.
*   **Summary of the Metaphysics** by Aristotle.
*   **Summary of the Ethics** by Aristotle.
*   **Summary of the Posterior Analytics** <note>original: "Kitab al-Burhan"</note> by Aristotle.
*   **Summary of the Physics** <note>original: "al-Sama' al-Tabi'i"</note> by Aristotle.
*   **Commentary on the Book of the Soul** (*De Anima*) by Aristotle.
*   **Summary of the Book of Elements** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Book of Temperaments** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Book of Natural Faculties** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Book of Causes and Symptoms** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Book of Recognition** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Book of Fevers** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the First Book of Simple Drugs** by Galen.
*   **Summary of the Second Half of the Method of Healing** by Galen.
*   **The Incoherence of the Incoherence** (*Tahafut al-Tahafut*), in which he responds to the book *The Incoherence [of the Philosophers]* by al-Ghazali.
*   **The Methodology of Proofs** on the science of theology <note>original: "ilm al-usul"</note>.
*   **A small book titled *The Decisive Treatise*** (*Fasl al-Maqal*) on the connection between wisdom <note>philosophy</note> and the divine law.
*   **Important Questions** on the *Posterior Analytics* of Aristotle.
*   **Commentary on the Book of...**

<summary>This page details the death of Averroes in 1198 CE, his family's professional legacy in medicine and law, and provides an extensive list of his writings covering Islamic law, medicine, and Aristotelian philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes (Ibn Rushd), Aristotle, Galen, Avicenna (Ibn Sina), al-Ghazali, anatomy and faith, bibliography, Islamic jurisprudence, Aristotelian commentaries, Medieval medicine</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliographic listing of the works of Ibn Rushd (Averroes), moving from his general legal and medical texts to his more specialized philosophical treatises, specifically his critical engagements with Aristotle, al-Farabi, and Ibn Sina (Avicenna).</meta>

# Biography of the Author

*   **The Syllogism** by Aristotle <note>The *Prior Analytics*, a foundational text on logic</note>.
*   **A Treatise on the Intellect**.
*   **A Treatise on the Syllogism**.
*   **A Book Examining Whether the Intellect Within Us**—which is called the material intellect <note>The human capacity for potential thought</note>—is capable of ultimately perceiving the separate forms <note>Abstract, non-material entities or divine truths</note> or if that is not possible; this is the investigation that Aristotle promised us he would undertake in the book *On the Soul* <note>original: "Kitab al-Nafs" (De Anima)</note>.
*   **A Treatise Proving that the Beliefs of the Peripatetics** <note>The followers of Aristotle</note> and the beliefs of the theologians <note>original: "al-Mutakallimun"</note> of our religious community regarding the manner of the world’s existence are close in meaning.
*   **A Treatise Introducing the Perspective of Abu Nasr** <note>al-Farabi, a major earlier philosopher</note> in his books written on the craft of logic which are in the hands of the people, and the perspective of Aristotle on the same; detailing how much of the logical craft found in Aristotle’s books is present in each of his [al-Farabi's] books, and the extent of what he added due to the difference in their views.
*   **A Treatise on the Conjunction of the Separate Intellect with Man**.
*   **A Treatise also on the Conjunction of the Intellect with Man**: revisions and inquiries between Abu Bakr ibn Tufayl <note>The philosopher who introduced Averroes to the royal court</note> and Ibn Rushd regarding his prescriptions for medicine in his book titled *The Generalities* <note>original: "al-Kulliyat" (The Colliget)</note>.
*   **A Book Examining Questions that Arose in Metaphysics** <note>original: "al-Ilm al-Ilahi", literally "The Divine Science"</note> within the *Book of Healing* <note>original: "Kitab al-Shifa"</note> by Ibn Sina <note>Avicenna</note>.
*   **A Question Concerning Time**.
*   **A Treatise Refuting the Doubt** of those who objected to "The Sage" <note>Aristotle</note> and his proof for the existence of prime matter <note>The fundamental, formless substance of the universe</note>, and demonstrating that Aristotle’s proof is the manifest truth.
*   **A Treatise in Refutation of Abu Ali ibn Sina** regarding his division of existing things into that which is "absolutely possible," "possible in itself but necessary through another," and "necessary in itself."
*   **A Treatise on Temperament** <note>The balance of qualities—hot, cold, dry, moist—in the body</note>.
*   **A Question on the Bouts of Fever**.
*   **A Treatise on Putrid Fevers**.
*   **Questions on Wisdom** <note>Philosophy</note>.
*   **A Treatise on the Motion of the Celestial Sphere**.
*   **A Book on the Matters in which Abu Nasr** <note>al-Farabi</note> disagreed with Aristotle in the *Book of Demonstration* <note>The *Posterior Analytics*</note>, concerning its arrangement and the laws of demonstrations and definitions.
*   **A Treatise on Theriac** <note>An ancient herbal compound used as a universal antidote and panacea</note>.

<summary>This page catalogs Ibn Rushd’s technical treatises, highlighting his defense of Aristotelian logic and physics against both theological critics and the interpretations of earlier Islamic philosophers like al-Farabi and Avicenna.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Aristotle, Avicenna, al-Farabi, Ibn Tufayl, logic, metaphysics, material intellect, prime matter, medical fevers, theriac</keywords>

| 60 | A point regarding the paths taken by the Lawgiver <note>The "Lawgiver" refers to the Prophet Muhammad or the divine authority behind the Religious Law.</note> in establishing Transcendence |
| 62 | An inquiry into the verification of the discussion on the attribute of **Corporality** <note>original: "al-jismiyya"; the question of whether God can be described as having a physical body.</note> |
| 66 | (**Discussion on Direction**) <note>original: "al-jiha"; the theological debate over whether God is literally "above" the world or exists without a spatial location.</note> |
| 68 | A point stating that the attribution of "Direction" to God the Exalted is established by both Religious Law and Reason |

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and begins the actual text of the "Decisive Treatise." It introduces the author's formal titles and states the central legal-philosophical question: whether the study of philosophy is permitted, prohibited, or required by Islamic law.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law</header>

# In the Name of God, the Compassionate, the Merciful

The most distinguished jurist, the unique, the great scholar, the eminent leader, the most just judge, Abu al-Walid Muhammad bin Ahmad bin Muhammad bin Ahmad bin Rushd <note>Commonly known in the West as Averroes (1126–1198 AD).</note>, may God Almighty be pleased with him and have mercy upon him, said:

۞ Now then ۞ Praising God with all His many praises ۞ and prayers be upon Muhammad, His purified and chosen servant and messenger ۞ The purpose of this discourse is to examine, from the standpoint of religious law <term>Religious law (original: "al-nazar al-shar‘i"): Examination based on the principles of the Shari'a rather than purely secular logic.</term>, whether the study of philosophy and the sciences of logic is permitted by the Law, or prohibited, or commanded—either as a recommendation or as an obligation. 

We say: If the activity of philosophy is nothing more than reflection upon existing things and considering them insofar as they indicate the Creator—that is, insofar as they are manufactured objects, for indeed existing things only indicate the Creator through the knowledge of their craftsmanship, and the more complete the knowledge of the craftsmanship, the more complete the knowledge of the Creator—and since the Divine Law has recommended reflection upon existing things and urged it, it is clear that what this name <note>The "name" here refers to "philosophy."</note> signifies is either obligatory by the Law or recommended.

That the Law calls for reflection upon existing things by the use of the intellect and seeking knowledge of them through it is evident in more than one verse from the Book of God, Blessed and Exalted is He, such as His saying: ۞ **"Reflect, then, O you who have eyes to see"** <note>Quran 59:2.</note> ۞ This is a clear text for the obligation of using intellectual reasoning <term>Intellectual reasoning (original: "al-qiyas al-aqli"): The use of logical syllogisms or deduction to reach a conclusion.</term>, or both intellectual and legal reasoning together. And such as His saying, Exalted is He: ۞ **"Have they not considered the kingdom of the heavens and the earth and whatever things God has created?"** <note>Quran 7:185.</note> ۞ This is a text urging the investigation of all existing things.

->And know that among those whom God the Exalted singled out for this knowledge and honored was Abraham, peace be upon him.<-

<summary>Ibn Rushd begins his treatise by defining philosophy as the study of the world to better know its Creator. He argues that Islamic scripture does not merely permit but actually mandates the use of logic and intellectual reasoning to understand the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, Ibn Rushd, Logic, Philosophy and Religion, Rationalism, Shari'a, Quranic Interpretation, Intellectual Reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 2, moving from the general definition of philosophy to the specific Quranic evidence that mandates intellectual reflection. It establishes a logical link between the religious command to "reflect" and the necessity of studying formal logic (syllogism).</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law</header>

As the Almighty said: ﴿ **And thus we showed Abraham the kingdom of the heavens and the earth** ﴾ <note>original: "وكذلك نري ابراهيم ملكوت السموات والارض" — Quran 6:75.</note> and so on in the verse.
The Almighty also said: ﴿ **Do they not look at the camels, how they are created? And at the sky, how it is raised?** ﴾ <note>Quran 88:17-18.</note> and He said: ﴿ **And they reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth** ﴾ <note>Quran 3:191.</note> among many other verses that are too numerous to count.

Since it has been established that the Divine Law has made it obligatory to use intellectual reflection <term>Reflection (original: "al-nazar"): In this context, it refers to the systematic use of the intellect to investigate the nature of reality.</term> upon existing things and to give them consideration; 
and since "consideration" <term>Consideration (original: "al-i'tibar"): A technical term here for the act of drawing a conclusion about something unknown based on something already known.</term> is nothing more than inferring the unknown from the known and extracting it from it—and this is what constitutes a syllogism <term>Syllogism (original: "al-qiyas"): A form of logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two premises. Ibn Rushd uses this to bridge the gap between religious "reflection" and formal Greek logic.</term> or is done by syllogism—it is therefore mandatory that we apply our reflection to existing things by means of intellectual syllogism. 

It is clear that this manner of reflection to which the Law has called and urged is the most perfect type of reflection, using the most perfect type of syllogism, which is called "Demonstration" <term>Demonstration (original: "burhan"): In the Aristotelian tradition followed by Ibn Rushd, this is the highest form of proof, yielding certain and necessary knowledge rather than mere opinion.</term>. Since the Law has urged the knowledge of God Almighty and His creations through Demonstration, it is either preferable or indeed necessary for anyone who wishes to know God, Blessed and Exalted, and all existing things through Demonstration, to first learn the types of demonstrations and their conditions. They must learn how the demonstrative syllogism differs from the dialectical <note>original: "al-jadali" — reasoning based on generally accepted opinions rather than certain truths.</note>, the rhetorical <note>original: "al-khatabi" — persuasive speech used to move an audience.</note>, and the sophistical <note>original: "al-mughaliti" — deceptive or fallacious reasoning.</note> syllogisms. 

This is impossible without first knowing what a "syllogism in the absolute sense" is, how many types there are, and which of them are valid syllogisms and which are not. This, in turn, is impossible unless one first knows the parts of which a syllogism is composed—namely, the premises and their various types. 

Thus, it is duty-bound upon the believer in the Law, who obeys its command to reflect upon existing things, to first learn these things <note>Meaning the rules of logic.</note> which serve as the "tools" for reflection, just as tools serve for manual work. For just as a jurist <term>Jurist (original: "al-faqih"): A specialist in Islamic law (Fiqh).</term> deduces from the command to "gain understanding of the law" the necessity of knowing the various types of legal analogies—knowing which are valid and which are not—likewise, the Knower <note>original: "al-'arif" — here referring to the philosopher or the one seeking deep intellectual knowledge of God.</note> must deduce from the command to reflect upon existing things the necessity of knowing intellectual syllogism and its types. Indeed, it is...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that because the Quran commands humans to reflect on creation, it implicitly commands the study of formal logic (syllogism), as logic is the tool required for true intellectual reflection.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, Quranic evidence, syllogism, demonstration, logic, legal obligation, reflection, jurisprudence</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s legal and philosophical justification for the study of logic. Having established on the previous page that "reflection" is a religious obligation, he now argues that this reflection must take the form of logical demonstration, and that Muslims must utilize the works of earlier civilizations (specifically the Greeks) to master these tools.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law</header>

It is all the more appropriate because if the jurist <term>Jurist (original: "al-faqih"): An expert in Islamic law (Sharia) who derives specific rulings from religious texts.</term> can deduce from the saying of the Almighty: 
> ﴿ **Reflect, O you who have vision** ﴾ <note>original: "فاعتبروا يا أولي الأبصار" — Quran 59:2. This is the cornerstone of Ibn Rushd's argument; he equates the religious command to "reflect" with the logical process of deduction.</note>
the obligation of knowing legal syllogism <term>Legal Syllogism (original: "al-qiyas al-fiqhi"): Often called legal analogy; the process in Islamic law of applying a ruling from a known case to a new case based on a shared cause.</term>, then it is even more fitting that the Knower of God <term>Knower of God (original: "al-'arif billah"): Here referring to the philosopher or the one who seeks knowledge of the divine through rational demonstration.</term> should deduce from it the obligation of knowing intellectual syllogism <term>Intellectual Syllogism (original: "al-qiyas al-'aqli"): This refers to formal logic and demonstration as defined by the Greek tradition, particularly Aristotle.</term>. 

One cannot argue that this type of inquiry into intellectual syllogism is a "heretical innovation" <term>Innovation (original: "bid'ah"): In Islamic law, an innovation in religious matters that has no basis in the Quran or the teachings of the Prophet, often viewed negatively.</term> simply because it did not exist in the earliest days of Islam. For indeed, the study of legal syllogism and its various types is also something that was developed after the first generation, yet it is not considered an innovation. 

The same must be believed regarding the study of intellectual syllogism. There is a specific reason for this that is not appropriate to mention here, but most of the followers of this religion affirm intellectual syllogism, except for a small group of literalists <term>Literalists (original: "al-Hashawiyya"): A derogatory term used for those who reject rational interpretation of scripture in favor of extreme, sometimes anthropomorphic, literalism.</term>, and they are refuted by the sacred texts themselves.

Since it has been established that the Divine Law makes it obligatory to study intellectual syllogism and its varieties—just as it requires the study of legal syllogism—it is clear that if no one before us has investigated intellectual syllogism and its types, we are duty-bound to begin that investigation ourselves. We must help one another, with the predecessor aiding the successor until the knowledge of it is complete. This is because it is difficult, if not impossible, for a single person to grasp everything they need regarding this subject on their own from scratch; just as it is difficult for one person to deduce everything needed regarding the types of legal syllogism. Indeed, the study of intellectual syllogism is even more in need of this collective effort, even if it has not been examined before.

Thus, it is clear that we must seek help for our purpose from what has been said by those who preceded us in this matter, regardless of whether those individuals share our religion or not. For when an instrument is used to validate an act—such as a tool for ritual slaughter—the validity of that instrument does not depend on whether it belonged to someone of our faith or not, provided it meets the conditions of correctness. By "those who do not share our religion," I mean those ancients <note>The "ancients" (original: "al-qudama") refers specifically to the Ancient Greek philosophers, primarily Aristotle, whose works on logic had been translated into Arabic.</note> who studied these things before the era of Islam. Since this is the case, and since everything required for the study of intellectual syllogisms has already been most thoroughly investigated by the ancients, we ought to take up their books and examine them.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that if legal reasoning is accepted in Islam, then logical reasoning must also be accepted. He insists that Muslims should study the logic developed by the Ancient Greeks, as truth is universal regardless of the religion of the person who discovered it.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Decisive Treatise, legal syllogism, intellectual syllogism, Aristotle, innovation, literalists, cumulative knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that Muslims must build upon the knowledge of previous civilizations, specifically the Greeks. Having established that "reflection" is a religious duty, he now argues that knowledge is a cumulative process that requires "tools" (logic) and centuries of collaborative effort across generations.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law</header>

So we should examine what they <note>Referring to the "ancients" or the Greek philosophers like Aristotle.</note> have said regarding this. If it is all correct, we accept it from them, and if there is something in it that is not correct, we draw attention to it. Once we have completed this type of investigation and have acquired the instruments with which we can engage in **reflection** on existing things and the evidence of **craftsmanship** <term>Craftsmanship (original: "al-san'a"): Ibn Rushd uses this term to describe the universe as a designed product, implying that studying the "mechanics" of nature reveals the "Artisan" or God.</term> within them—for he who does not know the craftsmanship does not know the product, and he who does not know the product does not know the **Maker**—it then becomes necessary that we begin investigating existing things in the order and manner we have learned from the art of knowledge through **demonstrative syllogisms** <term>Demonstrative Syllogism (original: "al-maqayis al-burhaniyya"): In the Aristotelian tradition, this is the highest form of logic that leads to certain, undeniable truth, as opposed to mere opinion or probability.</term>.

It is also clear that this goal can only be achieved regarding existing things by conducting investigations one after another, and that the later scholar should seek the help of the earlier, much like what occurs in the mathematical sciences. For if we were to imagine that the art of **geometry** did not exist in our time, nor the art of **astronomy** <term>Astronomy (original: "ilm al-hay'a"): Literally "the science of the configuration [of the heavens]," referring to the study of the positions and movements of celestial bodies.</term>, and a single person attempted by himself to discover the sizes of the celestial bodies, their shapes, and their distances from one another, he would not be able to do so—for example, knowing the size of the sun relative to the earth or the dimensions of other stars—even if he were the most naturally gifted of men, unless by revelation or something resembling revelation. Indeed, if he were told that the sun is about one hundred and fifty or sixty times larger than the earth, he would consider the speaker mad; yet this is something that has been proven in the science of astronomy in a way that those who master that science cannot doubt.

The reason for using the mathematical sciences as an analogy is that the science of the **principles of jurisprudence** <term>Principles of Jurisprudence (original: "usul al-fiqh"): The methodology used to derive legal rulings from the Quran and Prophetic traditions.</term> and jurisprudence itself were only completed over a long period of time. If a man today attempted, on his own, to grasp all the arguments devised by the thinkers of the **legal schools** <term>Legal Schools (original: "al-madhahib"): The various systems of interpretation in Islamic law (e.g., Maliki, Shafi'i, etc.).</term> regarding the disputed issues debated in most Islamic lands—excepting the West <note>original: "al-Maghrib" — referring to North Africa and Al-Andalus (Spain), where Ibn Rushd lived and worked.</note>—he would deserve to be laughed at, because such a task is impossible, even though the work has already been completed. This is self-evident not only in the theoretical sciences but also in the practical ones, for there is no craft that a single person can create entirely from scratch.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that knowledge is a collective, historical project where later generations must build upon the "tools" and discoveries of earlier ones, citing astronomy and Islamic law as examples of sciences that no single person could master alone.</summary>
<keywords>demonstrative syllogism, craftsmanship, the Maker, astronomy, geometry, principles of jurisprudence, collective knowledge, the West (Maghrib)</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that knowledge is a cumulative effort. He moves from the general principle that we must use the "tools" of previous thinkers to the specific conclusion that studying ancient Greek philosophy is a religious obligation for those who are intellectually capable.</meta>

<page-num>٦</page-num>
-> # The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

...itself, so how much more so for the craft of crafts, which is **Wisdom** <term>Wisdom (original: "al-hikma"): In this context, Ibn Rushd uses "Wisdom" as a synonym for Philosophy, specifically the systematic study of the universe through reason.</term>.

Since this is the case, if we find that those from the nations of the past who preceded us have conducted an investigation into existing things and reflected upon them as the conditions of **demonstration** <term>Demonstration (original: "al-burhan"): The highest form of logical proof, which yields certain knowledge rather than mere opinion.</term> require, it is mandatory for us to look at what they said and what they established in their books. Whatever among their findings is in agreement with the truth, we shall accept from them, rejoice in it, and thank them for it. Whatever is not in agreement with the truth, we shall point it out, warn against it, and excuse them <note>Ibn Rushd encourages a gracious but critical approach to Greek philosophy: take the truth, discard the errors, and recognize that the ancients were doing their best without the benefit of Islamic revelation.</note>.

Thus, it has been made clear from this that investigating the books of the ancients is **obligatory by Divine Law**, since their aim and purpose in their books is the very purpose to which the Law has urged us. Whoever forbids the study of these books to those who are qualified—namely, those who possess the two requirements of **natural intelligence** and **legal integrity** combined with intellectual and moral virtue—has barred people from the door through which the Law calls them to the **knowledge of God**. This is the door of reflection that leads to knowing Him with true knowledge. This is the height of ignorance and distance from God the Exalted.

If someone goes astray while looking into these books and slips—whether due to a deficiency in their natural capacity, or due to a poor order of study, or because they were overcome by their passions, or because they did not find a teacher to guide them to an understanding of what is in them, or because of a combination of these causes—it does not follow that we should prevent those who are qualified from studying them. For this type of harm is something **accidental** <term>Accidental (original: "bi-al-'arad"): A philosophical term referring to a quality that is not part of the essence of a thing. Ibn Rushd argues that philosophy is essentially good, and any harm it causes is a side effect of the user's personal failings.</term>, not essential. It is not right that something which is beneficial by its nature and essence should be abandoned because of a harm that is present in it only by accident.

Therefore, the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) said to the man who complained after being told to give his brother honey for a digestive ailment—for the diarrhea increased after he drank the honey: *"God spoke the truth, and your brother’s belly lied."* <note>original: "صدق الله وكذب بطن أخيك" — A famous Hadith used here to illustrate that the medicine (philosophy) is correct, while the "patient's" (the reader's) reaction is the problem.</note> Indeed, we say that the one who forbids the study of the books of Wisdom to those who are fit for it, simply because some wretched people might be thought to have gone astray from...

<summary>Ibn Rushd concludes that studying ancient philosophy is a religious duty for the intellectually and morally fit, arguing that any potential harm is an "accidental" byproduct of the reader's flaws rather than an "essential" flaw in philosophy itself.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, ancient philosophy, demonstration, accidental vs essential, knowledge of God, religious obligation, logic</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument from page 6. Having established that the study of ancient philosophy is a religious obligation, he now addresses the objection that philosophy might lead some believers astray or into heresy.</meta>

<page-num>٧</page-num>
-> # The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

...before they can look into it is like someone who prevents a thirsty person from drinking cool, sweet water until he dies, simply because some people choked on it and died. For death by choking on water is an **accidental** occurrence, whereas death by thirst is **essential** and necessary. <note>Ibn Rushd uses Aristotelian logic here: a "disaster" caused by philosophy is a rare side effect (accidental), but the loss of knowledge caused by forbidding it is a fundamental tragedy (essential).</note> This risk that occurs in this craft <note>The "craft" refers to philosophy.</note> is something that occurs in all crafts. For how many a jurist <term>Jurist (original: "faqih"): A specialist in Islamic law (Fiqh).</term> has found that his legal study became the cause of his lack of piety and his immersion in worldly affairs? In fact, we find this to be the case with most jurists, even though their craft, by its very essence, requires practical virtue. If it is not unusual for an obstacle to arise in a craft that requires **practical virtue**, then it is certainly not unusual for it to happen in a craft that requires **intellectual virtue**.

Once all this is established, and since we, the community of Muslims, believe that this Divine Law of ours is true, and that it is the very Law that alerts us to this happiness and calls us toward it—namely, the **knowledge of God**, the Mighty and Majestic, and of His creations—this fact is established for every Muslim by the method of belief that his innate character and nature requires. This is because the natures of men vary in the way they reach conviction. 

There are those who reach conviction through **demonstration** <term>Demonstration (original: "al-burhan"): Logical proof that leads to certain, undeniable truth.</term>. There are those who reach conviction through **dialectical arguments** <term>Dialectic (original: "al-jadal"): Reasoning based on generally accepted opinions rather than certain premises; often associated with the scholars of theology (the Mutakallimun).</term>, reaching the same level of certainty as the man of demonstration because his nature allows for nothing more. And there are those who reach conviction through **rhetorical arguments** <term>Rhetoric (original: "al-khitaba"): Persuasive speech that appeals to emotions and imagination, intended for the general public.</term>, just as the man of demonstration believes through demonstrative arguments.

Since this Divine Law of ours has called people through these three methods, the conviction it provides is universal to every human being—except for those who deny it out of stubbornness with their tongues, or those for whom the methods of the call to God the Exalted have not been established due to their own self-neglect. For this reason, the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—was specifically sent to "the red and the black" <note>original: "al-ahmar wa al-aswad." This is a classical Arabic idiom meaning all of humanity, regardless of ethnicity or background.</note>, because his Law contains all the methods of calling people to God the Exalted. This is stated explicitly in the words of the Exalted:

> "Invite to the way of your Lord with **wisdom** and **good instruction**, and argue with them in a way that is **best**." <note>Quran 16:125. Ibn Rushd interprets this verse as referencing the three methods: Wisdom (Demonstration), Good Instruction (Rhetoric), and Arguing Best (Dialectic).</note>

If these Divine Laws are true and call for the investigation that leads to the knowledge of the truth, then we, the community of Muslims, know with certainty that demonstrative investigation will not lead to anything contrary to what is contained in the Law. For **truth does not contradict truth**; rather, it agrees with it and bears witness to it. <note>This is the central thesis of the book: there can be no fundamental conflict between Reason (Philosophy) and Revelation (Religion).</note>

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that philosophy's potential to mislead is a rare side-effect, not a reason to ban it. He concludes that since Islam uses demonstration, dialectic, and rhetoric to reach all people, the "truth" found in philosophy can never contradict the "truth" of Revelation.</summary>

<meta>This page follows page 7, where Ibn Rushd (Averroes) argued that since humans have different intellectual capacities, the Divine Law uses various methods to lead them to truth. He now addresses what happens when the conclusions of philosophy (demonstration) appear to conflict with the literal word of scripture.</meta>

<page-num>٨</page-num>

-> ### The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

Now, if this is the case, and **demonstrative reasoning** <note>original: "al-nazar al-burhani." This refers to Aristotelian logic which seeks absolute certainty through proofs.</note> leads to a certain type of knowledge about a particular existing thing, then that thing must either be unmentioned in the Divine Law, or it must be addressed by it. 

If it is unmentioned, then there is no conflict; it is similar to those legal rulings that were unmentioned but which the jurist <term>Jurist (original: "faqih"): An expert in the practical application of Islamic law (Fiqh).</term> infers through **legal analogy**. If the Divine Law does speak of it, then the literal meaning of the text will either agree with what demonstration has proven, or it will disagree. If it agrees, there is nothing further to say. If it disagrees, we must seek its **allegorical interpretation**. 

The meaning of **allegorical interpretation** <term>Allegorical interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): The process of uncovering a deeper, metaphorical meaning in a text when the literal meaning seems impossible or contradictory.</term> is to shift the meaning of a word from its literal sense to a metaphorical one, without violating the linguistic customs of the Arabic language. This includes the practice of naming a thing by its likeness, its cause, its consequence, its companion, or other such methods used in the various categories of **metaphorical speech**. 

If the jurist does this in many legal rulings, then it is even more appropriate for the person of demonstrative science to do so. For while the jurist possesses only **probabilistic reasoning**, the person of knowledge <note>Referring to the philosopher or the scientist.</note> possesses **certain reasoning**. We state with absolute certainty that whenever a demonstration leads to a conclusion that contradicts the literal sense of the Divine Law, that literal sense is open to interpretation according to the rules of Arabic interpretation. This is a principle that no Muslim doubts and no believer questions. 

One’s certainty in this increases greatly when they engage with this concept and attempt to harmonize **reason** and **revelation** <note>original: "al-ma'qul wal-manqul," literally "the reasoned and the transmitted."</note>. Indeed, we say that whenever the literal meaning of a scriptural statement contradicts a conclusion of demonstration, if one examines the rest of the Law’s parts, one will find other phrases whose literal sense supports that interpretation, or comes close to supporting it. 

For this reason, Muslims have reached a consensus <note>original: "ijma'."</note> that it is not necessary to take all the words of the Divine Law literally, nor is it necessary to take them all metaphorically. However, they disagree on which specific texts should be interpreted and which should not. For example, the **Ash’arites** <note>A school of theology that emphasizes God's absolute power and interprets some divine attributes metaphorically.</note> interpret the verse about "God sitting on the Throne" and the tradition regarding "God’s descent," while the **Hanbalites** <note>A school of law and theology that generally adheres to the literal meaning of the text regarding God's attributes.</note> take those literally. The reason for the inclusion of—

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that truth (philosophy) and truth (revelation) cannot contradict; when they seem to, scripture must be interpreted metaphorically according to the rules of the Arabic language. He notes that even traditional theologians already use this method of interpretation, though they disagree on which specific texts require it.</summary>

Having established that "Truth does not contradict truth," he now addresses why the Divine Law contains apparent contradictions and the role of scholarly consensus (Ijma') in interpreting these texts.</meta>

<page-num>٩</page-num>

-> ### The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

<center>( 2 )</center>

The reason the Divine Law contains both an **apparent** <term>Apparent (original: "zahir"): The literal or surface-level meaning of a scriptural text.</term> and an **inner** <term>Inner (original: "batin"): The deeper, allegorical, or symbolic meaning of a text.</term> meaning is the diversity of human natures and the variation in their innate capacities for **assent** <note>In logic, "assent" (tasdiq) is the mental act of accepting a proposition as true.</note>. 

The reason for the inclusion of conflicting "apparent" meanings in the Law is to alert those **firmly rooted in knowledge** <note>A reference to Quran 3:7, describing scholars capable of deep interpretation.</note> to the **allegorical interpretation** <term>Allegorical interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): The process of uncovering a deeper meaning when the literal sense conflicts with reason.</term> that reconciles them. This meaning is alluded to in the words of the Exalted: 

> “It is He who has sent down the Book to you; in it are verses that are precise... [and others unspecific]... and those firmly rooted in knowledge.” <note>Quran 3:7. Ibn Rushd interprets this verse to mean that scholars are capable of knowing the hidden interpretations of difficult verses.</note>

Now, one might ask: "In the Divine Law, there are things that Muslims have reached a **consensus** <term>Consensus (original: "ijma'"): The unanimous agreement of the Muslim community, or its scholars, on a point of law or doctrine.</term> to take literally, things they have agreed to take allegorically, and things they differ upon. Is it permissible for a logical demonstration to lead to an allegorical interpretation of something they agreed to take literally, or a literal interpretation of something they agreed to take allegorically?" 

We respond: If consensus were established through a **certain** <note>original: "yaqini." A path that provides absolute, infallible proof.</note> path, then such a shift would not be valid. However, if the consensus is only **probable** <note>original: "zanni." Based on likelihood rather than absolute proof.</note>, then it may indeed be valid. 

For this reason, **Abu Hamid** <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), a major theologian who famously criticized philosophers.</note>, **Abu al-Ma'ali** <note>Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni (1028–1085), a prominent theologian and teacher of al-Ghazali.</note>, and other leaders of rational inquiry have stated that one cannot definitively charge someone with **unbelief** <note>original: "kufr." Declaring a person to be outside the faith.</note> for breaking consensus regarding interpretation in such matters. 

It may further be shown to you that consensus cannot be established in **theoretical matters** <term>Theoretical matters (original: "nazariyat"): Issues of belief, philosophy, and theology, as opposed to practical laws.</term> with the same certainty as it can in **practical matters** <term>Practical matters (original: "amaliyat"): Issues of religious law and ritual practice (like how to pray).</term>. This is because it is impossible to establish consensus on any given issue in a specific era unless that era is strictly defined and limited in our view, and all the scholars living in that era are known to us—meaning we know their identities and their total number—and that the position of every single one of them on the issue is transmitted to us through **multiple reliable chains** <note>original: "naql tawatur." A report so widely supported by different sources that it cannot be a lie.</note>. 

Furthermore, it must be proven to us that the scholars of that time agreed that there is no "apparent" and "inner" meaning in the Law, that knowledge of every issue must not be hidden from anyone, and that the path to knowing the Law is the same for all people. 

However, it has been reported of many of the **first generation** <note>original: "al-sadr al-awwal." The earliest Muslims and companions of the Prophet.</note> that they believed the Law has both an apparent and an inner meaning, and that the inner meaning should not be known by those who are not experts or are unable to understand it. For example, **al-Bukhari** <note>Author of the most famous collection of Prophetic traditions (Hadith).</note> narrated that **Ali** (may God be pleased with him) said: 

> “Speak to the people only of what they can recognize. Do you want God and His messenger to be called liars?” 

Similar things have been narrated from a group of the **pious ancestors** <note>original: "al-salaf." The early generations of Muslim scholars.</note>. How then can...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that scripture contains "inner" meanings to challenge scholars to think deeply. He argues that scholarly consensus (Ijma') is almost impossible to prove in abstract or theoretical matters, meaning philosophers shouldn't be labeled heretics for unique interpretations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument regarding the impossibility of establishing a definitive scholarly consensus on theoretical or philosophical matters, contrasting it with the consensus found in practical legal matters.</meta>

<page-num>١٠</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law

It is possible to conceive of a **consensus** <term>Consensus (original: "ijma'"): The unanimous agreement of the community's scholars.</term> being transmitted to us regarding a theoretical question, yet we know for certain that in every age there are scholars who believe that there are things in the **Divine Law** <note>The "Shari'a" here refers to the entirety of revealed scripture.</note> whose true nature should not be disclosed to all people. This is different from what occurs in **practical matters** <note>Practical matters (amaliyyat) refer to religious duties like prayer or transactions, which must be performed by everyone and thus require clear, public consensus.</note>, for all people believe in disclosing those to everyone equally. To establish consensus in practical matters, it is sufficient that the issue becomes widely known and no disagreement is reported to us; this is enough for consensus to occur. However, the case is different for **theoretical matters** <note>Theoretical or "scientific" matters (ilmiyyat) refer to complex theological or philosophical questions about the nature of God or the universe.</note>.

If you were to ask: "If it is not mandatory to charge someone with **unbelief** <term>Unbelief (original: "takfir"): To declare a person a non-Muslim (an infidel) due to their beliefs or actions.</term> for breaking consensus regarding an **interpretation** <term>Allegorical interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): Moving from the literal meaning of a text to a symbolic one.</term>—since consensus cannot truly be conceived in such things—then what do you say about the Muslim philosophers such as **Abu Nasr** <note>Al-Farabi (c. 872–950), a major early Islamic philosopher.</note> and **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), one of the most influential philosopher-physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note>?"

For **Abu Hamid** <note>Al-Ghazali (1058–1111), the famous theologian who wrote "The Incoherence of the Philosophers" to refute the Aristotelian ideas of Al-Farabi and Avicenna.</note> has decisively charged them with unbelief in his famous book known as **The Incoherence** <note>The Incoherence of the Philosophers (Tahafut al-Falāsifa).</note> regarding three issues: 
1. The claim of the **eternity of the world** <note>The idea that the universe has no beginning in time.</note>;
2. The claim that the Exalted does not know **particulars** <note>Individual, changing things, as opposed to universal, eternal truths.</note>—exalted be He far above that;
3. And the interpretation of what has been transmitted regarding the **resurrection of bodies** and the states of the afterlife.

We say: It is apparent from what he said on the matter that his charging them with unbelief was not definitive. This is because he explicitly stated in his book **The Criterion of Distinction** <note>The Criterion of Distinction between Islam and Godless-heresy (Faysal al-Tafriqa).</note> that charging someone with unbelief for breaking consensus is subject to uncertainty. Furthermore, it has become clear from our discussion that it is not possible to establish a consensus in such questions, because of what has been related from many of the **early ancestors** <term>Early ancestors (original: "Salaf"): The first three generations of Muslims, regarded as authorities on faith.</term>, let alone others: that there are interpretations which must not be disclosed except to those who are people of interpretation. 

These are **those firmly rooted in knowledge** <note>Quran 3:7. Ibn Rushd argues that the verse should be read as "No one knows its interpretation except God and those firmly rooted in knowledge," whereas others stop the sentence at "God."</note>. For our preferred view is to stop at the words of the Exalted: "and those firmly rooted in knowledge." This is because if the scholars did not know the interpretation, they would possess no special merit of **assent** <note>Belief or acceptance of truth.</note> that would grant them a degree of faith not found among those who are not scholars. Yet God has described them as being those who believe in it, and this must refer to the faith that comes from **demonstration** <term>Demonstration (original: "burhan"): A logical proof that leads to certain knowledge.</term>. This can only exist alongside knowledge of the interpretation. For the believers who are not scholars are those whose faith in it is not based on demonstration. Thus, if this faith which God used to describe the scholars is specific to them, it must be by means of demonstration; and if it is by demonstration...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that Al-Ghazali's charge of "unbelief" against philosophers is not legally binding because scholarly consensus is impossible to prove in theoretical matters. He asserts that "those firmly rooted in knowledge" are unique because they use logical demonstration to understand the inner meanings of scripture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s defense of the philosophers against the charges of heresy leveled by Al-Ghazali. Here, he specifically addresses the misconception regarding God’s knowledge of individual, specific things (particulars).</meta>

<page-num>۱۱</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law

This can only be achieved by knowing the **interpretation** <term>Interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): The process of deriving the inner, symbolic meaning of scripture when the literal sense conflicts with reason.</term>, for God—Exalted and Majestic—has informed us that scripture has an underlying meaning, and **demonstrative proof** <note>In philosophy, a "demonstration" is a logical proof that leads to certain truth, as opposed to rhetorical or dialectical arguments.</note> can only be established upon the truth. 

Since this is the case, it is impossible for a widespread **consensus** to be established regarding those interpretations that God has reserved specifically for the scholars. This is self-evident to anyone who is fair-minded. In addition to all this, we see that **Abu Hamid** <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), a famous theologian who criticized the philosophers in his book "The Incoherence of the Philosophers."</note> erred in what he attributed to the **Peripatetic** <term>Peripatetics (original: "Al-Mashsha'in"): The followers of Aristotle, so named because Aristotle reportedly taught while walking around.</term> philosophers. He claimed they say that God—Praised and Exalted—does not know **particulars** <term>Particulars (original: "juz'iyyat"): Individual, specific things or events (e.g., "this specific person"), as opposed to "universals" or general concepts (e.g., "humanity").</term> at all. 

On the contrary, they believe that God knows them with a type of knowledge that is not of the same nature as our own. Our knowledge is **caused** by the thing we know; therefore, our knowledge is "created" when the object comes into existence and changes when the object changes. However, the knowledge of God—Glory be to Him—concerning existence is the opposite of this: His knowledge is the **cause** of the object known. Whoever compares these two types of knowledge to one another is equating the essences and properties of two opposite things, which is the height of ignorance. 

Thus, the term "knowledge" is applied to both "created knowledge" (ours) and "eternal knowledge" (God's) only as a **pure homonym** <note>original: "ishtirak al-ism al-mahd". A homonym is a word that sounds the same but has entirely different meanings, like "bank" (a river bank vs. a financial institution).</note>. This is similar to how many names are applied to opposites in the Arabic language—for example, the word *al-Jalal* is applied to both the **great** and the **small**, and the word *al-Sarim* is applied to both **light** and **darkness**. 

For this reason, there is no single definition that can encompass both types of knowledge, contrary to what the **theologians** <term>Theologians (original: "Al-Mutakallimun"): Scholars of "Kalam" (scholastic theology) who often used dialectical arguments to defend religious dogmas.</term> of our time imagine. I have written a separate treatise on this specific question at the request of some of my companions. 

How could one imagine that the Peripatetics claim God does not know particulars through His eternal knowledge? After all, they believe that **true dreams** include warnings about specific events that will happen in the future, and that such warning knowledge comes to a person in their sleep from the Eternal Knowledge that governs and rules over the entire universe. 

Furthermore, they do not only believe that God does not know particulars in the way that we do, but they say the same regarding **universals** <term>Universals (original: "kulliyyat"): General concepts or categories, such as "mankind" or "justice."</term>. This is because the universals known to us are also caused by the nature of existing things, whereas in God’s knowledge, the matter is the reverse. Therefore, demonstrative proof leads to the conclusion that His knowledge is far too exalted to be described as "universal" or "particular." Thus, there is no point in disputing this issue.

<summary>Ibn Rushd clarifies that philosophers do not deny God's knowledge of specific events, but argue His knowledge is "causal" and fundamentally different from human knowledge. He criticizes Al-Ghazali for misrepresenting this philosophical position as heresy.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Al-Ghazali, Divine Knowledge, Particulars vs. Universals, Peripatetic Philosophy, Homonymy, Theology, Epistemology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s analysis of the charges of heresy (takfir) against philosophers. Having addressed the issue of God's knowledge of particulars on the previous page, he now turns to the second major point of contention: the origin and nature of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>۱۲</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law ۱۲

Regarding this issue—meaning whether they are to be declared **unbelievers** <note>original: "takfir"; the act of declaring a Muslim to be an infidel or apostate, which carried severe legal and social consequences.</note> or not:

As for the question of the **eternity of the world** <term>Eternity of the world (original: "qidam al-alam"): The philosophical belief that the universe has no beginning in time, as opposed to the theological belief that it was created at a specific moment.</term> or its temporal creation, the disagreement on this matter between the **Ash’arite** <term>Ash’arites (original: "Al-Ash’ariyya"): A major school of Islamic theology that sought a middle ground between rationalism and literalism, often opposing the philosophers.</term> theologians and the ancient philosophers is, in my view, almost entirely a disagreement over terminology. This is especially true regarding some of the ancients. 

This is because they agreed that there are three categories of **beings** <note>original: "mawjudat"; things that exist.</note>: two extremes and a middle ground between those two extremes. They agreed on the naming of the two extremes but differed over the middle category.

The first extreme is a being that is brought into existence from something other than itself and by something else—that is, by an **efficient cause** <note>The "agent" or force that brings something into being.</note> and out of matter. Furthermore, time precedes its existence. This is the condition of physical bodies whose "becoming" is perceived by the senses, such as the formation of water, air, earth, animals, plants, and so on. This category of beings is called **temporally created** <note>original: "muhdath"</note> by consensus of both the ancient philosophers and the Ash’arites.

The opposite extreme is a being that did not come from anything, was not caused by anything, and was not preceded by time. Regarding this, both groups also agree on calling it **eternal** <note>original: "qadim"</note>. This Being is perceived through **demonstrative proof** <note>Logical, certain reasoning rather than sensory observation.</note>; He is God—Blessed and Exalted—who is the Maker of all things, their Originator, and their Preserver, may His power be praised and exalted.

The category of being that exists between these two extremes is one that did not come from any [pre-existing] matter and was not preceded by time, yet it exists because of something—that is, because of an **Agent** <note>Referring to God as the "Factor" or "Cause" of the world's existence.</note>. This category refers to the **world as a whole**. Everyone agrees that the world possesses these three attributes. The theologians concede that time does not precede the world—or they are logically bound to this—since time, in their view, is something that accompanies motion and bodies. They also agree with the ancients that future time is infinite, as is future existence. They only differ regarding **past time** and past existence. The theologians believe that past time is finite <note>That it had a beginning.</note>, which is also the view of **Plato** and his followers. However, **Aristotle** and his school believe it is infinite, just like the future. Thus, this...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the debate over whether the world is "eternal" or "created" is largely a semantic dispute, as both theologians and philosophers agree on the world's unique nature as an entity caused by God but not preceded by time.</summary>
<keywords>eternity of the world, temporal creation, Ash’arites, Aristotle, Plato, efficient cause, ontology, Ibn Rushd, time, Islamic philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s analysis of the categories of existence. He has just defined the two extremes: purely temporal beings (like water or air) and the purely eternal Being (God). He now describes the third, intermediate category: the universe itself.</meta>

<page-num>١٣</page-num>
-> # The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

As for the other type of being, it is clear that it shares characteristics with both truly generated existence and eternal existence. Thus, whoever emphasizes its resemblance to the **eternal** <term>Eternal (original: "qadim"): That which has no beginning in time and was not brought into being from something else.</term> over its resemblance to the **created** <term>Created (original: "muhdath"): That which is brought into existence at a specific point in time from pre-existing matter.</term> calls it "eternal." Conversely, whoever emphasizes its resemblance to the created calls it "created." 

In reality, it is neither truly created nor truly eternal. This is because that which is truly created is necessarily subject to **corruption** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "corruption" refers to the process of decaying or ceasing to exist, the opposite of "generation."</note>, while that which is truly eternal has no cause. Some, namely **Plato** and his followers, have called it "eternally created," because they believe that time is finite in the past. 

Therefore, the various doctrines regarding the world are not so far apart that some should be deemed **unbelief** <note>original: "takfir"</note> while others are not. For opinions to warrant such a charge, they must be at the furthest extreme of divergence—that is, they must be direct opposites. This is what the **theologians** <term>Theologians (original: "Al-Mutakallimun"): Scholars of Kalam (speculative theology) who often used dialectical arguments to defend religious dogmas.</term> assumed regarding this issue; they believed that the terms "eternal" and "created" as applied to the world as a whole are mutually exclusive opposites. But as we have shown, that is not the case.

Furthermore, these philosophical views about the world do not even contradict the **literal meaning of the Divine Law** <note>original: "zahir al-shar'"; the plain, outward sense of the scripture before any allegorical interpretation.</note>. If one examines the literal sense of the Law through the verses concerning the creation of the world, it becomes clear that while the world's *form* is indeed created, existence itself and time have been continuous from both ends—meaning they are uninterrupted. 

This is because God’s words: *"It is He who created the heavens and the earth in six days, and His Throne was upon the water"* <note>Quran 11:7</note> imply by their literal meaning that there was an existence before this current existence—namely the **Throne** and the **Water**—and a time before this current time, meaning the time associated with the form of this existence, which is the measurement of the movement of the **celestial spheres** <note>original: "al-falak"; in medieval cosmology, the rotating shells that carry the stars and planets.</note>. 

Similarly, His words: *"On the day when the earth shall be changed into other than the earth, and the heavens as well"* <note>Quran 14:48</note> also imply by their literal meaning that there will be a second existence after this one. And His words: *"Then He directed Himself to the heaven while it was smoke"* <note>Quran 41:11</note> imply by their literal meaning that the heavens were created from something else. 

The theologians themselves do not follow the literal meaning of the Law in their claims about the world; rather, they employ **interpretation** <note>original: "ta'wil"; the process of moving from the literal to the allegorical or metaphorical meaning of a text.</note>. For it is nowhere stated in the Law that God existed with **absolute nothingness** <note>original: "al-adam al-mah d"; the concept of creation out of nothing (ex nihilo).</note>, nor can a textual proof for this ever be found. How then can the theologians' interpretation of these...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the universe occupies a middle ground between the eternal and the created, meaning the debate is largely over terminology. He asserts that even a literal reading of the Quran suggests that the world was created from pre-existing matter rather than from absolute nothingness.</summary>
