<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the world’s creation. Ibn Rushd is arguing that the apparent conflict between religious scripture and philosophical inquiry regarding whether the world is "eternal" or "created" is not a matter of heresy, but a complex intellectual challenge where scholars are permitted to differ.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
-> # The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

...the verses, that consensus has been reached upon them. The apparent meaning of the Divine Law <term>Divine Law (original: "al-Shari’a"): The religious path or revealed law, here referring to the Quranic framework.</term> regarding the existence of the world—which we have already stated—has also been expressed by a group of the Sages <term>Sages (original: "al-hukama'"): In this context, the ancient philosophers, particularly Aristotle and his followers.</term>. It appears that those who differ regarding these difficult matters are either correct and thus rewarded, or mistaken and thus excused.

For accepting something as true because of a proof established in the soul is a matter of necessity, not choice. I mean that it is not within our power to withhold or grant our belief at will, in the same way we might choose to stand up or sit down. Since "choice" is a necessary condition for religious obligation <note>In Islamic legal theory, a person can only be held accountable for actions they choose to perform.</note>, the scholar who falls into error due to a legitimate complication that occurs during his study is excused—provided he is truly among the "people of knowledge." For this reason, the Prophet (peace be upon him) said:

> If the judge exercises independent reasoning <term>Independent reasoning (original: "ijtihad"): The process of making a legal decision by personal effort or interpretation of the sources.</term> and is correct, he receives two rewards; if he errs, he receives one reward.

And what judge is greater than the one who judges existence itself—deciding whether it is this way or that? These "judges" are the scholars whom God has singled out for the task of interpretation <term>Interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): The process of moving from the literal meaning of a text to a symbolic or allegorical meaning when the literal sense conflicts with reason.</term>. This "excused error" in the Law only applies to mistakes made by scholars when they investigate the complex matters that the Divine Law has commanded them to study. 

As for an error committed by someone who does not belong to this class of people, it is pure sin. This is true whether the error concerns theoretical matters <note>Abstract concepts like the nature of the soul or the eternity of the world.</note> or practical matters <note>Daily religious duties and legal rules.</note>. Just as a judge who is ignorant of the prophetic tradition <term>Prophetic tradition (original: "Sunnah"): The body of social and legal customs derived from the life and teachings of the Prophet Muhammad.</term> is not excused if he makes a mistake in judgment, so too is the person who judges the nature of existing things without meeting the required conditions for such judgment. Such a person is not excused; rather, he is either a sinner or an unbeliever. 

Just as a judge of what is "lawful and prohibited" is required to possess the tools of independent reasoning—namely, knowledge of the principles and how to derive conclusions from them—how much more should these tools be required of the "judge of existents"? I mean that he must know the first principles of the intellect and the methods of logical deduction <term>Deduction (original: "istinbat"): The logical process of deriving a specific conclusion from general principles.</term> from them.

In short, error regarding the Divine Law is of two types: 
1. An error for which a person is excused if they are among the people of inquiry <term>People of inquiry (original: "ahl al-nazar"): Those trained in logical and philosophical demonstration.</term> regarding the subject in which the error occurred. This is similar to how a skilled physician is excused if he errs in the art of medicine, or a skilled judge if he errs in a legal ruling. However, someone who is not an expert in that field is never excused.
2. An error for which no human being is excused; rather...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that belief based on logical evidence is involuntary, and therefore scholars are excused—and even rewarded—for errors made during sincere inquiry into difficult matters. He distinguishes between the "expert" (the philosopher or trained scholar) who is excused for errors, and the "ignorant" person who sins by speaking on matters they do not understand.</summary>

<keywords>independent reasoning (ijtihad), excused error, interpretation (ta'wil), people of inquiry, logical deduction, scholarly responsibility, experts vs. non-experts</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of the "scholar's excuse" for making errors in interpretation to the definition of what constitutes actual heresy. Ibn Rushd is categorizing which beliefs are mandatory for all and which allow for metaphorical interpretation.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>

-> # The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

If [an error] occurs regarding the principles of the Divine Law <term>Principles of the Divine Law (original: "mabadi al-Shari’a"): The foundational, non-negotiable pillars of the faith.</term>, then it is unbelief <term>Unbelief (original: "kufr"): A total rejection of faith.</term>. If it occurs regarding matters beyond the principles, it is innovation <term>Innovation (original: "bid’a"): A heterodox belief or "heresy" that deviates from established tradition but may not necessarily result in total expulsion from the faith.</term>. This type of error is that which occurs in things that all various methods of evidence lead to knowing, such that knowledge of that thing is possible for everyone through these paths. 

Examples of this include the acknowledgment of God (blessed and exalted be He), of the prophecies, and of happiness and misery in the afterlife. This is because these three principles are reached by the three types of evidence which no human being is without—meaning that everyone can find conviction through the specific method they are required to know, namely: **demonstrative**, **dialectical**, and **rhetorical** proofs <note>Ibn Rushd follows the Aristotelian tradition, ranking "Demonstration" (logical proof) as the highest, followed by "Dialectic" (reasoned debate), and "Rhetoric" (persuasive speech for the general public).</note>.

Therefore, anyone who denies such things when they are a fundamental principle of the Divine Law is a defiant unbeliever—defiant with their tongue but not their heart, or through their negligence in seeking to know the evidence. For if a person belongs to the "people of demonstration," a path has been made for them to believe through demonstration; if they are from the "people of dialectic," then through dialectic; and if they are from the "people of preaching," then through preaching. For this reason, the Prophet (peace be upon him) said: 

> "I have been commanded to fight people until they say 'There is no god but God' and believe in me." 

He meant by this whatever path of the three paths of faith happens to be available to them. 

As for things which, due to their hidden nature, can only be known through demonstration, God has been subtle and kind toward those of His servants who have no path to demonstration—whether due to their natural disposition, their habits, or their lack of access to education. He did this by providing them with **metaphors** and **analogies** for these truths, calling them to believe in those metaphors, as those metaphors can be accepted through the evidence common to all—namely, the dialectical and rhetorical methods. 

This is the reason why the Divine Law is divided into an **exoteric** and an **esoteric** meaning <term>Exoteric and Esoteric (original: "al-zahir wa al-batin"): The "apparent" literal meaning for the masses versus the "inner" philosophical truth for scholars.</term>. The exoteric refers to those metaphors used for those higher meanings, while the esoteric refers to those meanings themselves, which are only revealed to the "people of demonstration." These are the categories of those four or five types of existence mentioned by **Abu Hamid** <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), the famous theologian whom Ibn Rushd is both critiquing and citing here.</note> in his book *The Criterion of Distinction* <note>Referring to al-Ghazali’s "Faysal al-Tafriqa," a work on the boundaries of faith and heresy.</note>. When it happens, as we have said, that we can know a thing as it truly is through all three methods, we have no need to strike metaphors for it; it remains in its exoteric form and is not subject to interpretation.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that fundamental religious truths are accessible to everyone through different levels of reasoning, while complex philosophical truths are presented as metaphors in scripture for those unable to grasp logical demonstrations.</summary>

<keywords>Demonstration, Dialectic, Rhetoric, Exoteric and Esoteric, Al-Ghazali, Principles of Law, Unbelief, Innovation, Metaphorical Interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's classification of Divine Law into what must be taken literally and what must be interpreted metaphorically. He is specifically addressing the consequences—unbelief or innovation—for different classes of people when they interpret (or fail to interpret) scriptural texts.</meta>

<page-num>١٦</page-num>

-> # The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

...interpretation <term>Interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): The act of moving from the literal, apparent meaning of a text to a metaphorical or allegorical meaning.</term>. This kind of apparent meaning, if it pertains to the foundational principles, then the person who interprets it metaphorically is an unbeliever <term>Unbeliever (original: "kafir"): One who has stepped outside the bounds of the faith.</term>. An example is someone who believes that there is no afterlife, no happiness and no misery, and that the Prophet only intended by these descriptions to ensure that people remain peaceful toward one another in their physical bodies and senses—treating it as a mere social trick—and that there is no ultimate goal for a human being except for their physical existence alone. 

Once this is established, it has become clear to you from our discussion that there is an apparent meaning in the Divine Law that is not permitted to be interpreted metaphorically. If such an interpretation occurs regarding the Principles, it is unbelief; if it occurs in matters following the Principles, it is innovation <term>Innovation (original: "bid’a"): A heterodox or heretical deviation from established doctrine.</term>. 

There is also another kind of apparent meaning that the People of Demonstration <term>People of Demonstration (original: "ahl al-burhan"): Philosophers and scholars who use rigorous logical proofs to reach the truth.</term> are *required* to interpret metaphorically; for them to take it literally would be unbelief. Conversely, for those who are not People of Demonstration to interpret it metaphorically and move it away from its literal meaning is unbelief or innovation in their case. 

Belonging to this category is the "Verse of the Sitting" <note>original: "ayat al-istiwa" (Quran 20:5): "The Most Merciful rose over the Throne." Literalists took this as physical sitting, while philosophers viewed it metaphorically.</note> and the "Tradition of Descending" <note>original: "hadith al-nuzul": A prophetic saying stating that God "descends" to the lowest heaven. Ibn Rushd argues this cannot be literal movement for a non-physical God.</note>. This is why the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—said regarding the Black slave woman, when she told him that God is "in the sky," that he should "free her, for she is a believer." This was because she was not among the People of Demonstration. 

The reason for this is that there is a class of people for whom conviction only occurs through the imagination. That is to say: they do not believe in a thing unless they can imagine it. It is difficult for them to believe in an existence that is not attributed to something imaginable. This also includes those who understand "relation" only as "physical place"—these are people who have advanced slightly beyond the first class in their thinking, yet they still maintain a belief in God's corporeality <term>Corporeality (original: "jismiyya"): The belief that God has a physical body or occupies physical space.</term>. 

Therefore, the answer for such people regarding these matters is that they are among the "Ambiguous Verses" <term>Ambiguous Verses (original: "al-mutashabiha"): Passages in the Quran where the meaning is not immediately clear and requires careful study.</term> and that one should pause [in the recitation] at the word of God the Exalted in the verse: ﴿ *None knows its interpretation except God* ﴾ <note>Quran 3:7. There is a famous grammatical debate on whether the sentence ends at "God" or continues to include "those rooted in knowledge."</note>. Although the People of Demonstration are in agreement that this category must be interpreted metaphorically, they may differ on the specific interpretation itself, according to each individual’s level of mastery in the art of demonstration. 

There is a third category of the Divine Law that stands between these two, where doubt can occur. One group of those who practice inquiry might attach it to the apparent meaning which cannot be interpreted, while others attach it to the inner meaning which the scholars are not permitted to take literally. This is due to the difficulty and ambiguity of this category. In this case, the one who errs is excused—meaning, the scholars. If it is then asked: "If it has become clear..."

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that while some must take scripture literally to maintain faith, philosophers (the "People of Demonstration") are required to interpret anthropomorphic descriptions of God metaphorically. He argues that teaching complex allegories to the general public can lead to unbelief.</summary>

<keywords>Interpretation, People of Demonstration, Unbelief, Innovation, Verse of the Sitting, Tradition of Descending, Ambiguous Verses, Ibn Rushd, Decisive Treatise</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's argument regarding the types of scriptural texts. He is moving from the general rules of interpretation to the specific, sensitive case of the afterlife and the resurrection, and the dangers of sharing complex philosophical interpretations with the general public.</meta>

<page-num>١٧</page-num>

-> # The Decisive Treatise — Determining the Nature of the Connection between Wisdom and the Divine Law <-

The Divine Law regarding this is of three levels. From which of these three levels, in your view, is that which has come down regarding the descriptions of the Resurrection <term>Resurrection (original: "al-ma'ad"): Literally "the place of return," referring to the afterlife and the soul's ultimate fate.</term> and its states?

We say: It is clear that the matter in this issue belongs to the class where there is disagreement. We see a group who attribute themselves to demonstration <term>Demonstration (original: "al-burhan"): Rigorous logical proof used by philosophers.</term> saying that it is obligatory to take it according to its literal meaning, since there is no demonstration here leading to the impossibility of the literal meaning; this is the method of the Ash'arites <note>The Ash'arites were a dominant school of Sunni theology that prioritized the literal word of revelation and God’s absolute power over independent human logic.</note>. 

Other people among those who practice demonstration interpret it metaphorically; these differ greatly in their interpretations. In this class, Abu Hamid <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), a famous theologian who famously criticized the philosophers but also integrated their logic into Islamic thought.</note> is counted, as are many of the Sufis <term>Sufis (original: "al-mutaṣawwifa"): Practitioners of the mystical, inward dimension of Islam.</term>. Some of them combine both types of interpretation, as Abu Hamid does in some of his books.

It is likely that the scholar who errs in this issue is excused, while the one who is correct is thanked or rewarded. This is provided that he acknowledges the *existence* [of the afterlife] and only applies a metaphorical interpretation to the *description* of the resurrection, not its actual existence—so long as the interpretation does not lead to a denial of existence altogether. 

Denying the existence [of the afterlife] in this case is unbelief <term>Unbelief (original: "kufr"): A state of being outside the faith.</term> because it is one of the foundations of the Divine Law. It is something to which assent is achieved through all three methods common to "the red and the black" <note>An Arabic idiom meaning "everyone" or "all of humanity," regardless of their intellectual level or background.</note>. 

As for those who are not among the people of science, it is obligatory for them to take it literally. For them, interpretation is unbelief because it leads to unbelief. Therefore, we believe that for those whose duty it is to believe in the literal sense, interpretation is unbelief because it leads to unbelief. Whoever reveals such interpretations to them among the people of interpretation has invited them to unbelief, and the one who invites to unbelief is himself an unbeliever. 

For this reason, interpretations should not be established except in "books of demonstration." When they are in books of demonstration, they are only reached by those who are people of demonstration. However, if they are established in books other than those of demonstration, and poetic, rhetorical, or dialectical methods are used—as Abu Hamid did—this is a mistake against both the Divine Law and Wisdom <term>Wisdom (original: "al-hikma"): Used by Ibn Rushd as a synonym for philosophy.</term>, even if the man only intended good. He intended to increase the number of people of knowledge by doing so, but he instead increased the spread of corruption without increasing the number of people...

<sig>(٣)</sig>

<summary>Ibn Rushd discusses the interpretation of the afterlife, arguing that while scholars may disagree on its exact nature, denying its existence is heresy. He warns that sharing complex philosophical interpretations with the general public is dangerous and can lead to unbelief.</summary>

<page-num>١٨</page-num>
### The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law 18

...knowledge. Through this, one group was led to disparage <term>Wisdom (original: "al-hikma")</term> <note>In this context, "Wisdom" refers to Philosophy, specifically the Aristotelian tradition.</note>, another to disparage the Divine Law, and another to attempt to combine the two. It is likely that this was one of his objectives in his books. The evidence that he intended by this to stir the natural dispositions of men is that he did not adhere to any single school of thought in his writings; rather, he is an Ash'arite with the Ash'arites, a Sufi with the Sufis, and a philosopher with the philosophers. This reached the point where, as the saying goes:

->One day a Yemeni, if I meet a man from Yemen * And if I meet a Ma'addi, then I am from Adnan<-
<note>This verse describes a person who changes their identity or affiliation depending on whom they are speaking to. "Yemeni" and "Adnan" (Ma'addi) represent the two major ancestral branches of the Arab people.</note>

It is the duty of the leaders of the Muslims to forbid those of his books which contain this type of knowledge to all except those who are truly people of science. Similarly, they must forbid the books of <term>Demonstration (original: "al-burhan")</term> <note>Demonstration refers to rigorous, logical proof, which Ibn Rushd considers the highest form of reasoning.</note> to those who are not qualified for them. However, the harm caused to people by books of demonstration is lighter, because for the most part, only people of superior natural dispositions encounter books of demonstration. This class of people is only led astray due to a lack of scientific virtue, reading without a proper order, or taking up these subjects without a teacher. 

Nevertheless, the wholesale prevention of these books is a hindrance to what the Divine Law has called for, because it would be an injustice to the best classes of people and the best classes of existing things. Justice regarding the best classes of existing things requires that they be known in their true essence by those who are prepared to know them in their true essence—and these are the best classes of people. For, in proportion to the greatness of the being, the injustice of being ignorant of it is also great. Therefore, God the Exalted said:

> Polytheism is indeed a great injustice
<note>Original: "Inna al-shirka la-zulmun 'azim." A quote from the Qur'an, Surah Luqman 31:13.</note>

This is what we saw fit to establish regarding this type of inquiry—namely, the discourse between the Divine Law and Wisdom, and the rules of <term>Interpretation (original: "al-ta'wil")</term> <note>The process of metaphorical or allegorical explanation of scripture when the literal meaning contradicts logical demonstration.</note> within the Law. Were it not for the public notoriety of these matters and the fame of the issues we have mentioned, we would not have allowed ourselves to write a single letter on the subject, nor to offer any excuse to the people of interpretation. This is because such matters should properly be discussed in books of demonstration. God is the Guide and the Giver of success to what is right.

It should be known that the purpose of the Divine Law is solely to teach true knowledge and true action. True knowledge is the knowledge of God, the Exalted, and of all existing things as they truly are, especially—

<vocab>Wisdom (Philosophy), The Divine Law (Scripture), Interpretation, Demonstration, The Ash'arites (Theologians), The Sufis (Mystics), The Philosophers, True Knowledge, True Action</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd criticizes Al-Ghazali's inconsistency and argues that while complex philosophical books should be kept from the masses to prevent confusion, they must remain available to those with the intellectual capacity to understand them. He concludes that the ultimate goal of Religion is to guide people toward true knowledge and right action.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Ibn Rushd transitions from criticizing Abu Hamid al-Ghazali’s inconsistent methods to defining the core purpose of the Divine Law. He explains how the Law addresses different levels of human intellect through different modes of teaching.</meta>

<page-num>١٩</page-num>
### The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law 19

...[knowledge derived] from the Divine Law, as well as the knowledge of happiness or misery in the afterlife. Right action consists of performing those deeds that lead to happiness and avoiding those that lead to misery. The knowledge of these actions is what is called "Practical Science." This is divided into two parts: the first consists of outward physical actions, and the knowledge of these is what is called <term>Jurisprudence (original: "al-fiqh")</term> <note>The study of Islamic legal rulings regarding external rituals and social transactions.</note>. The second part consists of spiritual actions, such as gratitude, patience, and other moral traits encouraged or forbidden by the Divine Law. The knowledge of these is what is called <term>Asceticism (original: "al-zuhd")</term> and the sciences of the Hereafter. 

This is the direction Abu Hamid [al-Ghazali] took in his book. Since people had turned away from this kind of knowledge [the spiritual] and delved deeply into the second kind [legal jurisprudence], and because this former kind is more essential to <term>Piety (original: "al-taqwa")</term>—which is the cause of happiness—he titled his book *The Revival of the Religious Sciences* <note>Original title: "Ihya' 'Ulum al-Din," al-Ghazali's most famous work which sought to integrate Sufi spirituality with orthodox law.</note>. 

But we have strayed from our primary subject, so let us return and say: Since the purpose of the Divine Law is to teach right knowledge and right action, and since teaching involves two aspects—<term>Conception (original: "tasawwur")</term> <note>The mental act of forming an idea or grasping an essence.</note> and <term>Assent (original: "tasdiq")</term> <note>The mental act of judging a proposition to be true or false; conviction.</note>—as the scholars of <term>Dialectical Theology (original: "al-kalam")</term> have explained; and since the methods of Assent available to people are three: the <term>Demonstrative</term>, the <term>Dialectical</term>, and the <term>Rhetorical</term>; and the methods of Conception are two: either the thing itself or its symbolic image;

And since it is not in the nature of all people to accept <term>Demonstrations (original: "al-barahin")</term> <note>Rigorous logical proofs based on certain premises, associated with philosophy.</note>, nor even <term>Dialectical arguments</term>, let alone demonstrative ones—considering how difficult demonstrative arguments are to teach and the long time required for those who are actually capable of learning them; 

And since the Divine Law’s primary goal is to teach everyone, it must necessarily contain all the various methods of Assent and all the various methods of Conception. Since some methods of Assent are common to the majority of people—meaning they produce conviction in them—namely the Rhetorical and the Dialectical (with the Rhetorical being even more common than the Dialectical); and some are restricted to a minority of people, namely the Demonstrative; and since the primary aim of the Divine Law is to care for the majority without neglecting to alert the elite, most of the methods explicitly stated in the Divine Law are those shared methods that allow the majority to achieve both Conception and Assent.

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that the Divine Law is designed to guide all people by using different levels of reasoning: rhetorical and dialectical for the general public, and demonstrative for the intellectual elite.</summary>
<keywords>Practical Science, Jurisprudence, al-Ghazali, Revival of Religious Sciences, Conception, Assent, Demonstration, Dialectic, Rhetoric, Education</keywords>

<meta>Ibn Rushd continues his categorization of the methods used within the Divine Law to convey truth to different types of people. This follows his explanation on the previous page regarding how the Law targets both the "Conception" of ideas and the "Assent" to their truth.</meta>

<page-num>۲۰</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law

These methods in the <term>Divine Law (original: "al-Shari'a")</term> are of four types. One is that they are common to all people yet specific to both—I mean that they are certain in <term>Conception (original: "al-tasawwur")</term> <note>The simple grasping of an idea in the mind without judging its truth or falsehood.</note> and <term>Assent (original: "al-tasdiq")</term> <note>The mental act of judging a proposition as true; the acceptance of a premise.</note>, while also being rhetorical or dialectical. These <term>Syllogisms (original: "al-maqayis")</term> <note>Logical structures where a conclusion is drawn from two or more premises.</note> are those whose premises—despite being widely accepted or generally presumed—happen to be certain, and their results, when taken as they are, happen to be the intended truths themselves rather than mere symbols of them. This category of scriptural statements does not admit any <term>Interpretation (original: "al-ta'wil")</term> <note>The process of shifting a word's meaning from its literal sense to a figurative one when the literal sense conflicts with reason.</note>, and anyone who denies it or attempts to interpret it metaphorically is an <term>Unbeliever (original: "kafir")</term>.

The second category is when the premises—despite being widely accepted or presumed—are certain, but the results are symbols of the matters intended to be produced. This category admits interpretation—specifically regarding its results. 

The third is the reverse of this: the results are the actual matters intended to be produced, while the premises are widely accepted or presumed without happening to be certain. This also does not admit interpretation regarding its results, though it may admit it for its premises. 

The fourth category is when the premises are widely accepted or presumed without being certain, and the results are symbols of what was intended to be produced. In this case, the duty of the <term>Elite (original: "al-khawass")</term> <note>Referring to the philosophers and scholars capable of logical demonstration.</note> is to apply interpretation, while the duty of the <term>Masses (original: "al-jumhur")</term> <note>The general public who rely on rhetorical persuasion rather than scientific demonstration.</note> is to accept them in their literal sense. 

In general, everything in these texts that is subject to interpretation can only be understood through <term>Demonstration (original: "al-burhan")</term> <note>A rigorous logical proof that leads to certain knowledge, associated by Ibn Rushd with Aristotelian philosophy.</note>. Thus, the duty of the elite regarding such texts is that very interpretation, while the duty of the masses is to take them literally in both respects—namely, in conception and assent—since their natures are capable of nothing more. 

Certain interpretations may occur to those who investigate the Divine Law due to the superiority of some common methods over others in producing assent—specifically when the evidence for an interpretation is more persuasive than the evidence for the literal meaning. Such interpretations are "popular" or "rhetorical," and they may be the duty of those whose theoretical faculties have reached the level of <term>Dialectic (original: "al-jadal")</term> <note>A level of reasoning higher than rhetoric but lower than scientific demonstration, often used by theologians.</note>. Within this category fall some of the interpretations of the <term>Ash'arites (original: "al-Ash'ariyya")</term> <note>Followers of a school of theology that sought a middle ground between literalism and pure reason, dominant in Sunni Islam.</note> and the <term>Mu'tazilites (original: "al-Mu'tazila")</term> <note>An earlier theological school known for prioritizing reason and using metaphorical interpretation to defend God's unity and justice.</note>, even though the Mu'tazilites are in...

<summary>Ibn Rushd categorizes religious texts into four types based on whether their premises and conclusions are literal truths or symbolic representations, determining who is permitted to interpret them metaphorically. He argues that while the masses must follow the literal sense, the philosophical elite are required to use logical demonstration to find deeper meanings.</summary>

<keywords>Interpretation (ta'wil), Demonstration (burhan), Elite vs. Masses, Syllogism, Ash'arites, Mu'tazilites, Assent and Conception</keywords>

<meta>Ibn Rushd concludes his division of human intellects into three categories and warns against the danger of exposing deep philosophical interpretations to those who lack the training to understand them, continuing his discussion on how different types of arguments affect the faith of different people.</meta>

<page-num>۲۱</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law

...the more reliable of statements. As for the masses, who are not capable of anything beyond <term>Rhetorical (original: "al-khattabiyya")</term> <note>Arguments based on persuasion and emotion rather than formal logic, designed to be accessible to everyone.</note> arguments, it is their duty to take these scriptures at their <term>Literal Meaning (original: "al-zahir")</term>; it is not permissible for them to be informed of such <term>Interpretations (original: "al-ta'wil")</term> at all.

Thus, people fall into three classes: 

One class consists of those who are not suited for interpretation at all; these are the **Rhetorical** people, who constitute the vast majority. This is because no person of sound mind is devoid of this kind of <term>Assent (original: "al-tasdiq")</term> <note>The mental acceptance of a truth.</note>. 

Another class consists of those suited for **Dialectical** interpretation; these are the <term>Dialecticians (original: "al-jadaliyun")</term> <note>Those who use "Jadal" (dialectics), a method of reasoning from generally accepted opinions rather than certain premises, typical of theologians.</note>, whether by nature alone or by both nature and habit. 

And a class consists of those suited for **Certain** interpretation; these are the <term>Demonstrative (original: "al-burhaniyun")</term> people by nature and by craft—I mean the craft of <term>Wisdom (original: "al-hikma")</term> <note>Specifically referring to the practice of philosophy and rigorous logical proof.</note>.

This interpretation should not be declared to the Dialectical class, let alone to the masses. Whenever any of these interpretations are disclosed to those unsuited for them—especially the Demonstrative interpretations, due to their distance from common knowledge—it leads both the one to whom it is disclosed and the one disclosing it to <term>Unbelief (original: "al-kufr")</term>. 

The reason for this is that the intent of interpretation is to nullify the literal meaning and establish the interpreted meaning. If the literal meaning is nullified for someone who is only capable of literal understanding, and the interpreted meaning remains unproven to them, it leads them to unbelief, provided the matter concerns the foundations of the <term>Divine Law (original: "al-Shari'a")</term>. Therefore, interpretations should not be declared to the masses, nor should they be recorded in rhetorical or dialectical books—I mean books whose arguments are of these two types—as <term>Abu Hamid (original: "Abu Hamid [al-Ghazali]")</term> <note>Imam al-Ghazali (1058–1111), a major theologian whom Ibn Rushd critiques here for mixing philosophical proofs into books intended for a general audience.</note> did.

For this reason, regarding those literal texts whose status as "apparent" or "clear" is itself a matter of confusion for everyone, and where the knowledge of their interpretation is impossible for the masses, it must be stated that such texts are <term>Ambiguous (original: "mutashabih")</term> <note>Scriptural passages that are not clear and require deep study.</note> and that none knows their interpretation except God. The <term>Pause (original: "al-waqf")</term> <note>A technical term in Quranic recitation regarding where a sentence ends.</note> must be made here in the words of the Mighty and Sublime: **"And none knows its interpretation except God."** <note>Quran 3:7. Ibn Rushd argues that the sentence should end after "God," implying that even scholars cannot know certain divine secrets, which protects the faith of the common people.</note> 

A similar approach is taken when answering questions about obscure matters which the masses have no way of understanding, such as the words of the Exalted: **"And they ask you about the Spirit; say: 'The Spirit is of the command of my Lord, and of knowledge you have been given but little.'"** <note>Quran 17:85. This is cited as an example of the Law setting a boundary for human inquiry.</note> As for the one who discloses these interpretations to those unsuited for them, he is an unbeliever because he invites...

<summary>Ibn Rushd categorizes people into three intellectual classes—Rhetorical, Dialectical, and Demonstrative—and warns that sharing complex philosophical interpretations with those unsuited for them leads to religious unbelief.</summary>
<keywords>hierarchy of intellect, rhetorical, dialectical, demonstrative, interpretation, Al-Ghazali, public vs. private knowledge, unbelief, Quran 3:7, the Spirit</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 21, Ibn Rushd is concluding his argument regarding the danger of exposing the general public—who rely on rhetorical arguments—to complex philosophical interpretations that they cannot intellectually process.</meta>

<page-num>۲۲</page-num>

-># The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

...to the people toward <term>Unbelief (original: "al-kufr")</term>, which is the opposite of the <term>Lawgiver's (original: "al-shari'")</term> <note>Referring to the Prophet or the Divine intent behind the revelation.</note> aim. This is especially true when these are corrupt **interpretations** regarding the fundamentals of the <term>Divine Law (original: "al-shari'a")</term>, as has happened with a group of people in our own time. For we have witnessed among them certain groups who thought they had become philosophers and that, through their "wondrous wisdom," they had grasped things that contradict the Law in every respect—I mean, things that do not admit <term>Interpretation (original: "ta'wil")</term> <note>The process of finding allegorical or deeper meanings in scripture when the literal sense seems to contradict reason.</note>. They believed it was mandatory to declare these things openly to the <term>Masses (original: "al-jumhur")</term>. Thus, by declaring these corrupt beliefs to the public, they became the cause of the destruction of the masses and of their own destruction in this world and the hereafter.

The comparison between the aim of these people and the aim of the Lawgiver is like the case of a person who goes to a **skilled physician** whose intent is to preserve the health of all the people and remove disease from them. He does this by establishing for them <term>Commonly Accepted Statements (original: "aqawil mushtaraka")</term> <note>Arguments or instructions that are persuasive and easy for everyone to believe and follow, even without medical training.</note> regarding the necessity of using those things that preserve their health, remove their illnesses, and avoid their opposites. He does this because he cannot turn all of them into physicians themselves; for only the physician is the one who knows the things that preserve health and remove disease through <term>Demonstrative (original: "burhaniyya")</term> <note>Scientific or logical methods that provide certain, undeniable proof.</note> methods. 

Then, another person comes along to the people and says to them: "These methods that this physician has set for you are not true," and he begins to invalidate them until they are discarded by the people. Or, he says that these methods have "interpretations," but the people do not understand those interpretations, nor does any practical conviction arise from them. 

Do you think that people in such a state will do any of the things beneficial for health or for the removal of disease? Or will this person, who has declared the invalidity of what they previously believed, be able to guide them in any way—I mean, in preserving their health? No; he will not be able to guide them, and they will not follow the rules, so **destruction** will encompass them all. 

This is what happens even if he declares *correct* interpretations to them, because they cannot understand such interpretations—to say nothing of if he declares *corrupt* interpretations to them! In the end, they will come to see that there is no health to be preserved and no disease to be removed, let alone seeing that there are specific things that preserve health and remove disease. This is the exact condition of one who openly declares interpretations to the masses.

<summary>Ibn Rushd uses the analogy of a physician to argue that sharing complex philosophical interpretations with the general public is dangerous, as it undermines their faith in the Law's practical guidance without providing an understandable alternative, leading to spiritual ruin.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Physician Analogy, The Masses, Interpretation, Divine Law, Demonstrative Method, Unbelief, Spiritual Health</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 22, Ibn Rushd (Averroes) is completing his medical analogy. He compares the Lawgiver (the Prophet) to a physician: just as a doctor uses simple instructions to keep the public healthy without explaining complex medical theory, the Lawgiver uses religious practice to maintain the "spiritual health" of the masses without exposing them to complex philosophical allegories.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

-># The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

...to the masses and to those who are not qualified to receive it alongside the Divine Law. For this reason, [publicly sharing such interpretations] corrupts them and turns them away from the Law, and he who turns others away from the Law is an <term>Unbeliever (original: "kafar")</term>. This analogy is one of <term>Certainty (original: "yaqinan")</term> <note>In Aristotelian logic, "certainty" or "demonstration" refers to a proof that is logically airtight, unlike "poetic" or "rhetorical" arguments which only persuade.</note> and is not merely poetic, as some might suggest, because the proportion is exact. Specifically, the relationship of the physician to the health of bodies is the same as the relationship of the Lawgiver to the health of souls. I mean that the physician is the one who seeks to preserve the health of bodies when it exists and restore it when it is lost, and the Lawgiver is the one who seeks this for the health of souls. This health is what is called <term>Piety (original: "taqwa")</term>. The Mighty Book [the Quran] has explicitly stated the requirement to seek this through religious actions in more than one verse, where the Exalted said:

> *"Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may attain piety."* <note>Quran 2:183. Ibn Rushd argues that the purpose of the ritual is the internal state of piety, not just the physical act.</note>

And the Exalted said:

> *"Neither their meat nor their blood reaches God, but it is your piety that reaches Him."* <note>Quran 22:37, regarding animal sacrifice.</note>

And He said:

> *"Indeed, prayer prohibits immorality and wrongdoing."* <note>Quran 29:45.</note>

This is among other verses contained in the Mighty Book regarding this meaning. Thus, the Lawgiver only seeks this [spiritual] health through religious knowledge or religious action. It is upon this health that <term>Happiness in the Afterlife (original: "al-sa'ada al-ukhrawiyya")</term> is built, just as misery in the afterlife is the result of its opposite.

It has become clear to you from this that it is not permitted to record correct <term>Interpretations (original: "ta'wilat")</term> <note>The process of explaining the allegorical meaning of scripture when the literal meaning seems to contradict reason.</note> in books intended for the masses, let alone corrupt ones. The "correct interpretation" is the **Trust** that humanity carried, while all other created beings refused to carry it and were afraid of it—meaning that which is mentioned in the words of the Exalted:

> *"Indeed, We offered the Trust to the heavens and the earth and the mountains..."* <note>Quran 33:72. In this philosophical context, Ibn Rushd identifies the "Trust" (Amana) with the human capacity for rational, allegorical interpretation of divine mysteries.</note>

And the verse continues. It was because of these interpretations—and the belief that they must be declared openly within the Law—that the various sects of Islam arose, until they began to charge one another with <term>Unbelief (original: "takfir")</term> and label each other as <term>Heretics (original: "bid'a")</term>. This is especially true of the corrupt interpretations. The <term>Mu'tazilites (original: "al-Mu'tazila")</term> <note>An early school of Islamic theology that relied heavily on reason but, according to Ibn Rushd, erred by teaching their complex doctrines to the common people.</note> interpreted many verses and traditions and declared their interpretations to the public. The <term>Ash'arites (original: "al-Ash'ariyya")</term> <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology in Ibn Rushd's time. Though they were more moderate than the Mu'tazilites, Ibn Rushd still blames them for causing social and religious division.</note> did the same, even if they used interpretation less frequently. Because of this, they cast the people into hatred, mutual loathing, and wars; they tore the Law to pieces and divided the people completely.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that "spiritual health" (piety) is the goal of the Law, and that exposing the public to complex allegorical interpretations destroys this health. He blames the theological divisions and conflicts in Islamic history on the Mu'tazilite and Ash'arite schools for publicizing such interpretations.</summary>
<keywords>Piety (Taqwa), Interpretation (Ta'wil), The Trust (Amana), Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, Spiritual Health, Sectarianism</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 23, Ibn Rushd (Averroes) has just concluded his medical analogy, arguing that the Lawgiver, like a physician, provides the masses with "spiritual health" through religious practices. He now turns to a sharp critique of the Ash'arite theologians, whom he accuses of confusing the public with flawed reasoning that satisfies neither the common person nor the philosopher.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

->### The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

Furthermore, the methods they followed to establish their **interpretations** <term>interpretations (original: "ta'wilat"): the practice of looking for a figurative meaning in scripture when the literal sense seems to conflict with reason</term> are suited neither for the **masses** nor for the **elite**. This is because, upon examination, these methods are found to fall short of the requirements of <term>demonstration (original: "burhan"): a logically certain proof that follows strict rules of reasoning</term>. Anyone familiar with the rules of demonstration can see this with the slightest reflection. Indeed, many of the principles upon which the **Ash'arite school** <note>The Ash’arites were the dominant school of speculative theology in the Islamic world during this period. Ibn Rushd, a philosopher, often clashed with their methods.</note> built its knowledge are **sophistic** <note>sophistic: reasoning that sounds clever but is actually false or misleading.</note>, for they deny many self-evident truths. For instance, they deny the persistence of <term>accidents (original: "a'rad"): properties of a thing (like color) that aren't essential to its existence</term>, the influence of things upon one another, the existence of necessary causes for effects, substantial forms, and intermediate agents.

The aggression of their theorists against Muslims reached such an extent that a faction of the Ash'arites declared anyone who does not know the existence of the Creator through the specific methods they laid out in their books to be an **unbeliever**. In truth, it is they who are the unbelievers and the misguided! From this point they disagreed: one group said the "first of all duties" is rational inquiry, while another said it is faith. This happened because they did not recognize which paths are the **common paths** through whose gates the Divine Law invited all people. They wrongly thought there was only one path, thereby missing the Lawgiver's intent; they went astray and led others astray.

If it is asked: "If neither the methods of the Ash'arites nor those of other theorists are the common paths by which the Lawgiver intended to teach the masses—and by which alone they can be taught—then which paths are these in this religion of ours?" 

We answer: They are the methods established in the **Mighty Book** [the Quran] alone. For if the Mighty Book is examined, one finds within it the three methods available to all people: the common paths for teaching the majority of people, and the specialized path [of demonstration]. Upon reflection, it becomes clear that no common methods for teaching the masses can be found that are better than those mentioned in the Quran. Therefore, whoever distorts them with an interpretation that is not self-evident, or is not clearer to everyone than the original text itself—which is something that does not exist—has nullified the Law's wisdom and nullified its intended effect in providing **human happiness**. This is very clear from the state of the **Early Generations** <note>original: "al-sadr al-awwal," referring to the first century of Islamic history, which Ibn Rushd views as a time of pure, uncorrupted understanding.</note> and the state of those who followed them.

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite theologians for using flawed logic that denies natural causality and for declaring ordinary believers "infidels." He argues that the Quran provides the only perfect methods for teaching both the public and the philosophers without causing confusion.</summary>

<keywords>Ash'arites, causality, demonstration, interpretation, the masses, the elite, human happiness, Quranic methods, sophistry</keywords>

->فصل المقال — فيما بين الحكمة والشريعة من الاتصال<-
->### The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

<sig>( 4 )</sig>

...those who came after them. For the **early generation** <note>original: "al-sadr al-awwal," referring to the first few generations of the Muslim community, often held as models of piety.</note> only attained perfect virtue and piety by following these scriptural statements without seeking figurative interpretations. Those among them who did encounter an interpretation did not think it right to express it openly. As for those who came after them, when they began to employ **interpretation** <term>interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): the practice of assigning a metaphorical meaning to scripture when the literal sense seems problematic</term>, their piety decreased, their disagreements multiplied, their mutual love vanished, and they split into various sects.

Therefore, anyone who wishes to remove this **innovation** <term>innovation (original: "bid'a"): in this context, a harmful or unauthorized addition to religious doctrine</term> from the Divine Law must turn to the Mighty Book [the Quran]. He should extract from it the arguments found therein regarding every single thing we are commanded to believe. He should strive to look at their literal meaning as much as possible, without interpreting any part of it, unless the interpretation is self-evident—meaning it is clearly understood by everyone alike. For if you contemplate the statements set down in the Law for the instruction of the people, you will find that their defense can reach a point where no one deviates from their literal meaning except for the **people of demonstration** <term>people of demonstration (original: "ahl al-burhan"): those trained in rigorous, logical philosophy, whom Ibn Rushd considers the highest class of thinkers</term>.

This unique quality is not found in any other kind of discourse. Indeed, the religious statements explicitly declared in the Mighty Book for the benefit of all have three characteristics that indicate their **miraculous nature** <note>original: "i'jaz," referring to the Islamic doctrine that the Quran is a miracle that cannot be imitated by humans.</note>:
1. No other statements provide more complete persuasion and conviction for everyone.
2. They are naturally structured so that they remain defensible until they reach a point where only the "people of demonstration" can grasp the need for interpretation (if the passage requires one at all).
3. They contain hints and signs that guide the "people of truth" <note>Referring to philosophers and scholars of logic.</note> toward the correct interpretation.

This is not found in the doctrines of the **Ash'arites** or the **Mu'tazilites** <note>The Ash'arites and Mu'tazilites were the two major schools of Islamic speculative theology. Ibn Rushd argues their methods confuse the public without satisfying the demands of pure reason.</note>. By this I mean that their interpretations are neither defensible, nor do they contain pointers toward the truth, nor are they true themselves. For this reason, innovations have multiplied. We wish we had the time and the means to devote ourselves to this goal. If God grants us a longer life, we shall establish as much of it as is made easy for us, in the hope that it might serve as a starting point for those who come after. Truly, the soul is in the deepest state of grief and pain because of the corrupt passions and distorted beliefs that have infected this Divine Law—especially the harm caused by those who attribute themselves to **wisdom** <note>In Ibn Rushd's terminology, "wisdom" (hikma) refers to philosophy.</note>, for...

<meta>This page continues the concluding thoughts of the main treatise, where Ibn Rushd argues that the friction between philosophy and religion is caused by human misunderstanding rather than inherent contradiction. He then transitions into a specific "Supplement" (Damima) addressing a complex theological question regarding God's knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

-># The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

Injury from a friend is a far more severe injury than that from an enemy. By this, I mean that **Wisdom** <term>Wisdom (original: "al-hikma"): here used as a synonym for Philosophy</term> is the companion of the **Divine Law** <term>Divine Law (original: "al-shari'a"): religious revelation and its legal/ethical framework</term> and its "milk-sister" <note>original: "al-ukht al-radi'a." This is a powerful metaphor; in Islamic law, children who share the same wet-nurse are considered siblings as close as those related by blood.</note>. Thus, injury from those who claim a connection to the Law is the most painful of all, given the enmity, hatred, and quarreling that occurs between them, even though the two are natural companions and are, by their very essence and instinct, lovers of one another. 

Indeed, Wisdom has been harmed by many "ignorant friends" who attribute themselves to her—namely, the various sects existing within the Law. May God set all things right, guide everyone to His love, unite their hearts in His piety, and through His grace and mercy, remove from them hatred and animosity. God has already removed many of these evils, ignorances, and misleading paths through this **prevailing command** <note>original: "al-amr al-ghalib," likely referring to the Almohad dynasty or the specific ruler Ibn Rushd served, whom he credits with restoring intellectual order.</note>. Through it, He has opened the way to much good, especially for those who follow the path of rational inquiry and desire to know the truth. For this command has called the masses from the mere imitation of God’s knowledge to a **middle path** <note>original: "tariq wasat"</note>—one that rises above the low level of the **blind imitators** <term>blind imitators (original: "al-muqallidin"): those who accept religious doctrines without seeking proofs or understanding</term> but stays below the disruptive quibbling of the **theologians** <term>theologians (original: "al-mutakallimin"): specifically those who used dialectical arguments to defend dogma, often criticized by Ibn Rushd for lacking true logic</term>. It has alerted the elite to the necessity of conducting a full inquiry into the very roots of the Divine Law.

## ۞ Supplement to the issue of Eternal Knowledge mentioned by Abu al-Walid in the Decisive Treatise, may God be pleased with him ۞
<note>Abu al-Walid is the formal name of Ibn Rushd (Averroes). This section is a famous appendix to his main work.</note>

May God sustain your glory, preserve your blessing, and shield you from the eyes of misfortune. Because you have surpassed many who study these sciences through your excellent intellect and noble nature, your sound inquiry has led you to encounter a doubt that arises regarding **Eternal Knowledge** <note>original: "al-’ilm al-qadim," referring to God's knowledge</note>—specifically, how it relates to **originated things** <term>originated things (original: "al-ashya al-muddatha"): things that come into existence in time, like human actions or natural events</term>. For the sake of truth, and to remove this doubt from you, it is our duty to resolve this confusion. However, we must first state the problem clearly, for he who does not understand the knot cannot untie it. 

The doubt arises as follows: If all these things were in the knowledge of God (glory be to Him) before they existed, then when they actually come into being, is His knowledge of them the same as it was before they existed? Or is His knowledge of them while they exist different from what it was before they were brought into being? For...

<summary>Ibn Rushd describes Philosophy and Religion as "milk-sisters" that are naturally compatible but harmed by sectarian theologians. He then begins a technical supplement addressing the paradox of how an unchanging God can know changing, temporal events.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Philosophy and Religion, Almohad dynasty, Eternal Knowledge, Divine Law, theologians, rational inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 26, moving from the metaphor of Wisdom and Divine Law as "milk-sisters" into a rigorous philosophical defense against a specific theological challenge. Ibn Rushd is addressing the "doubt" regarding God's knowledge of particulars—a point on which he was often accused of heresy.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

-># The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law<-

If we say that things are in God's knowledge at the moment of their existence in a state different from how they were in His knowledge before they existed, it would necessarily follow that **Eternal Knowledge** <term>Eternal Knowledge (original: "al-ʿilm al-qadim"): God's knowledge, which exists outside of time and change</term> is subject to change. It would also mean that when a thing emerges from **non-existence** into **existence**, a "new" or "additional" knowledge has occurred in God—and such a thing is impossible for Eternal Knowledge. On the other hand, if we say that the knowledge of the thing is exactly the same in both cases <note>i.e., God's knowledge is the same whether the thing is merely a possibility in the future or a reality in the present.</note>, it is then asked: is the thing in itself—meaning these **temporal existents** <term>temporal existents (original: "al-hadithat"): things that are created in time, as opposed to eternal things</term>—the same before it exists as it is when it has come into existence? It must be said that a thing is not the same before it exists as it is when it exists; otherwise, existence and non-existence would be one and the same thing.

If the opponent admits this, he is asked: Is not true knowledge the understanding of existence as it actually is? If he says yes, then it follows that if the thing itself changes, the knowledge of it must also change. Otherwise, the thing would be "known" as something other than what it actually is <note>If the thing changes from "not yet here" to "here," but the knowledge of it stays exactly the same, then the knowledge no longer matches the reality of the thing.</note>. Therefore, one of two things must follow: either Eternal Knowledge itself changes, or temporal events are not known to God at all. Both conclusions are impossible to attribute to Him, Glory be to Him.

This doubt is reinforced by what we observe in the human condition—specifically, how our own knowledge relates to non-existent things (when we imagine them) versus how it relates to them once they actually exist. It is self-evident that these two types of knowledge are distinct; if they were not, a person would be ignorant of a thing's existence at the very moment it occurred. 

The usual defense of the **Theologians** <term>Theologians (original: "al-Mutakallimun"): scholars of Kalam, or speculative theology, who often used dialectical arguments to defend Islamic creed</term> does not offer an escape from this problem. They typically answer by saying that God knows things before they exist exactly as they will be when they do exist, including their time, place, and other specific qualities. However, it is said to them: when the thing actually comes into being, has a change occurred in reality or not? This change is the transition of the thing from non-existence to existence. If they say no change has occurred, they are being stubborn <note>original: "kabaru," suggesting they are denying the obvious reality of the world</note>. But if they admit a change has occurred, they are asked: is the occurrence of this change known to Eternal Knowledge or not? If they say yes, the previous doubt returns. In short, it is difficult to conceive how the knowledge of a thing before it exists and the knowledge of it after it exists can be one and the same knowledge. 

This, then, is the formulation of this doubt in the most compelling way possible.

<summary>Ibn Rushd outlines a classic theological dilemma: if God’s knowledge is eternal and unchanging, how can He know changing, temporal events without His knowledge becoming "new" or "changed" itself? He critiques the standard explanations provided by the Mutakallimun (theologians) as insufficient.</summary>

<keywords>Eternal Knowledge, non-existence, existence, temporal events, Mutakallimun, omniscience, change, philosophical doubt</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) complex argument regarding divine omniscience. He is specifically refuting the famous theologian Al-Ghazali (Abu Hamid), who had criticized philosophers for their views on whether God knows individual, changing things (particulars). Ibn Rushd here transitions from stating the problem to deconstructing Al-Ghazali’s previous attempts to solve it.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<header>The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law 28</header>

to establish by it what we have discussed with you. Solving this doubt requires a lengthy discourse, but we intend here to point out the core insight <note>original: "nukta" — literally a "point" or "subtlety," here meaning the essential logical pivot of the argument.</note> by which it is resolved. **Abu Hamid** <note>This refers to Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111 AD), the influential Persian theologian who famously attacked philosophy in his book *The Incoherence of the Philosophers*.</note> attempted to solve this doubt in his book entitled *The Incoherence* with an argument that is not convincing. He claimed that the meaning of this is as follows: he asserted that "knowledge" and the "object of knowledge" are among those things considered **relatives** <term>relatives (original: "al-mudaf"): a logical category where one thing is defined by its relationship to another, like "father" and "son" or "right" and "left."</term>. 

Just as one of two relatives may change while the other relative does not change in itself, so it seems to be the case with things in the knowledge of God—glory be to Him. By this, he means that the things change in themselves, but His knowledge of them does not change. He gives an example of this relative relationship: a single pillar may be to the **right of Zayd**, and then it moves to become to his **left**, while Zayd himself has not changed at all in his own essence. 

However, this is not a true comparison. For the "relation" itself has indeed changed; the relationship that was "on the right" has now become "on the left." The only thing that did not change was the **subject of the relation** <note>original: "mawdu' al-idafah" — the underlying person or thing to which the relationship is attached.</note>, by which I mean the bearer of it, who is Zayd. If that is the case, and if knowledge is itself the relation <note>Al-Ghazali's logic suggests that God is like Zayd, and the world is like the pillar moving around Him. Ibn Rushd argues that if knowledge is the actual "connection" between the two, then that connection must change if the object moves.</note>, then it must necessarily change when the object of knowledge changes, just as the relation of the pillar to Zayd changes when its position changes from right to left.

The way this doubt is resolved for us is by recognizing that the status of **Eternal Knowledge** <term>Eternal Knowledge (original: "al-ʿilm al-qadim"): God’s primordial, unchanging knowledge.</term> in relation to existence is the opposite of the status of **Created Knowledge** <term>Created Knowledge (original: "al-ʿilm al-muhdath"): human or temporal knowledge, which is acquired through the senses.</term> in relation to existence. This is because the existence of a thing is the **cause and reason** for our human knowledge, whereas Eternal Knowledge is the **cause and reason** for the existing thing itself. 

If it were the case that when a thing exists after having not existed, a "new knowledge" occurred in the Eternal Knowledge—just as happens in created human knowledge—it would necessarily follow that Eternal Knowledge is an **effect** of existence, rather than its **cause**. Therefore, it is necessary that no change occurs there, as occurs in created knowledge. This error only arose from the practice of **analogizing Eternal Knowledge to created knowledge**—which is the fallacy of **judging the Unseen by the Seen** <term>judging the Unseen by the Seen (original: "qiyas al-gha'ib 'ala al-shahid"): a logical error in theology where one assumes the Divine (unseen) must operate by the same rules as the physical world (seen).</term>. The corruption of this analogy is well known. Just as no change occurs in a **Creator** <note>original: "al-faʿil" — literally "the agent" or "the doer."</note> when his work comes into existence—meaning a change that did not exist before—so too no change occurs in the **Eternal Knowledge**, glory be to Him, upon the occurrence of...

<summary>Ibn Rushd refutes Al-Ghazali’s defense of divine knowledge, arguing that God’s knowledge does not "update" like human knowledge because God’s knowledge is the cause of reality, not a result of it.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Al-Ghazali, The Incoherence of the Philosophers, Divine Knowledge, Eternal Knowledge, Created Knowledge, Logic, Relatives, Causality</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page concludes Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) specific argument regarding God’s knowledge of particulars. Having criticized Al-Ghazali’s explanation on the previous page, Ibn Rushd now provides his own philosophical resolution, distinguishing between human (passive) knowledge and divine (causal) knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

# The Decisive Treatise — On the Connection Between Wisdom and the Divine Law

is known of Him. Thus, the doubt has been resolved. It does not follow that if no change occurs in **Eternal Knowledge** <term>Eternal Knowledge (original: "al-ilm al-qadim"): the knowledge possessed by God, which exists outside of time and change.</term>, then the existing thing is not known at the moment of its occurrence as it truly is. Rather, it only follows that it is not known by a **produced knowledge** <term>produced knowledge (original: "al-ilm al-muhdath"): human-like knowledge that is "produced" or triggered only after a thing happens.</term>, but rather by an eternal knowledge. This is because the occurrence of change in knowledge when the object of knowledge changes is only a condition for knowledge that is an *effect* of the object—which is produced knowledge.

Therefore, Eternal Knowledge relates to the existing thing in a manner different from the manner in which produced knowledge relates to it. It is not that it is "unrelated" entirely, as is reported about the philosophers—that they say, due to this doubt, that God (Glory be to Him) does not know particulars. The matter is not as has been wrongly attributed to them. Rather, they hold that He does not know particulars through produced knowledge, which requires a new instance of knowing to match the new instance of the thing's occurrence. This is because His knowledge is the **cause** of the thing, not an **effect** of it, as is the case with produced knowledge.

This is the ultimate level of **transcendence** <term>transcendence (original: "al-tanzih"): the theological concept of stripping away human-like attributes from God to preserve His absolute uniqueness.</term> that must be acknowledged. For logical demonstration <term>demonstration (original: "al-burhan"): a rigorous philosophical proof.</term> has forced us to conclude that He is the Knower of all things, because their emergence from Him is due to the fact that He is a Knower, not merely because He exists, or exists with such-and-such an attribute, but precisely because He is a Knower, as the Almighty said:

> "Does He who created not know, while He is the Subtle, the All-Aware?" <note>Quran 67:14. Ibn Rushd uses this verse to show that the Creator must inherently know what He has designed.</note>

Logical demonstration has also forced us to conclude that He does not know things through a knowledge that has the attributes of produced knowledge. Therefore, it is necessary that there exists another kind of knowledge for these existing things—one that cannot be described <note>original: "la yukayyaf" — meaning its "how-ness" or modality cannot be grasped by human categories.</note>—and that is the **Eternal Knowledge**, Glory be to Him.

How can it be imagined that the **Peripatetic** <term>Peripatetics (original: "al-Mashsha'un"): the followers of Aristotle, who traditionally taught while walking. In the Islamic world, this refers to the mainstream philosophical tradition represented by Al-Farabi and Ibn Sina.</term> philosophers believe that Eternal Knowledge does not encompass particulars, when they believe that it is the source of warnings in dreams, divine revelation, and other types of inspiration?

This is what has appeared to us regarding the way to resolve this doubt, and it is a matter in which there is no uncertainty or hesitation. God is the giver of success to what is right and the guide to the truth. 

->And peace be upon you, and the mercy of God and His blessings.<-
->God knows best what is right, and to Him is the return and the final destination.<-

***

<summary>Ibn Rushd resolves the debate on divine omniscience by arguing that God’s knowledge is the cause of existence, not its effect, meaning God knows changing particulars without His own essence undergoing change. He defends the philosophers (Peripatetics) against charges of heresy, noting they even attribute revelation and dreams to this divine knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal Knowledge, Produced Knowledge, Particulars, Ibn Rushd, Peripatetics, Transcendence, Divine Omniscience, Causality, Revelation, Al-Ghazali</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new work by Ibn Rushd, "The Discovery of the Methods of Proof." While the previous page concluded his "Decisive Treatise" (Fasl al-Maqal) regarding God's knowledge, he opens this text by explicitly referencing that prior work and its conclusions regarding the relationship between philosophy (wisdom) and religious law.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

# ﴿ The Book of the Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith and an Exposition of 
## the False Doubts and Misleading Innovations that have Occurred Therein due to Interpretation
### Authored by the Learned Scholar Abu al-Walid ibn Rushd ﴾

﴿ In the name of God, the Merciful, the Compassionate ﴾ The Shaykh Abu al-Walid Muhammad ibn Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Ahmad ibn Rushd said: (And thereafter) Praise be to God, who singles out whom He wills for His wisdom and grants them success in understanding His Law <term>Law (original: "shari'a"): the divinely ordained path and legal framework of Islam.</term> and following His Way <term>Way (original: "sunna"): the traditional customs and practices of the Prophet.</term>. He has revealed to them from His hidden knowledge, the meaning of His revelation, and the purpose of His Prophet’s message to His creation, that which has made clear to them the deviation of those who stray from His faith and the distortion of the falsifiers among His community. It has become clear to them that there are forms of **interpretation** <term>interpretation (original: "ta'wil"): the process of moving from the literal meaning of a text to a symbolic or allegorical meaning.</term> for which God and His Messenger have not given permission. Perfect blessings be upon the trustee of His revelation and the seal of His messengers, and upon his family and kin.

Now then: Since we have previously clarified, in a separate treatise <note>This refers to his work "The Decisive Treatise" (Fasl al-Maqal).</note>, the harmony between **wisdom** <note>original: "al-hikma"; here Ibn Rushd uses this term to refer to philosophy.</note> and the Divine Law, and that the Law commands the pursuit of wisdom, we stated there that the Law consists of two parts: the **apparent** <term>apparent (original: "zahir"): the literal or straightforward meaning of the scripture intended for the general public.</term> and the **interpreted**. We noted that the apparent is the obligation of the masses, while the interpreted is the obligation of the scholars. As for the masses, their duty is to take the text in its apparent sense and avoid interpreting it. It is not permissible for scholars to disclose interpretations to the masses, just as Ali (may God be pleased with him) said: "Speak to people according to what they understand. Do you want God and His Messenger to be called liars?"

Therefore, I have decided to examine in this book the apparent beliefs that the Law intended the masses to hold. In all of this, I shall strive to follow the intention of the Lawgiver <note>original: "al-shari'"; referring to the Prophet Muhammad as the conveyor of the divine intent.</note> to the best of my effort and ability. Indeed, people have become utterly confused regarding this matter within this Law, to the point that stray sects and various groups have arisen. Each one of them believes that they alone follow the original Law, and that whoever disagrees with them is either an **innovator** <term>innovator (original: "mubtadi'"): someone who introduces unauthorized beliefs or practices into the religion.</term> or an **infidel** <term>infidel (original: "kafir"): a disbeliever; in this context, someone accused of having left the faith.</term> whose blood and property may be taken. All of this is a departure from the intent of the Lawgiver, and its cause is the confusion that befell them in understanding the purpose of the Law. The most famous of these groups in our time...

<summary>This page serves as the introduction to Ibn Rushd's "Al-Kashf," where he argues that the religious confusion and sectarian violence of his time stem from a misunderstanding of how to interpret scripture. He establishes a boundary between the literal faith required of the public and the philosophical interpretations reserved for scholars.</summary>
