<meta>This page continues the introduction to Ibn Rushd’s critique of theological schools. Having established that incorrect interpretations have led to confusion, he now identifies the specific groups responsible and outlines his plan to return to the "original" intentions of the religious Law regarding the knowledge of God.</meta>

<page-num>۳۱</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...four. The group called the **Ash’arites** <note>original: "al-Ash'ariyya" — A dominant school of Sunni theology that seeks a middle ground between pure rationalism and literalism.</note>, whom most people today consider to be the "People of the Sunna" [Sunnis]; the group called the **Mu'tazilites** <note>original: "al-Mu'tazila" — An early rationalist school of Islamic theology known for emphasizing human reason and divine justice.</note>; the group called the **Batinites** <note>original: "al-Batinitya" — From the word 'batin' (hidden); a group that seeks internal, esoteric meanings behind the literal words of scripture.</note>; and the group called the **Hashwites** <term>Hashwites (original: "al-Hashwiyya"): A derogatory term used for extreme literalists who were accused of filling their teachings with anthropomorphic descriptions of God and rejecting rational inquiry.</term>. 

All of these groups have held various beliefs regarding God and have diverted many of the expressions of the Law <term>Law (original: "shari'a"): The divinely revealed path found in the Qur'an and the Prophet's teachings.</term> away from their literal meanings to **interpretations** <term>interpretations (original: "ta'wilat"): The practice of moving from the apparent meaning of a text to a symbolic or allegorical one.</term> that they then applied to those beliefs. They claimed these were the original teachings intended for all people, and that anyone who deviated from them was either an unbeliever or an innovator <note>original: "mubtadi'" — Someone who introduces unauthorized changes or heresies into the faith.</note>.

If you contemplate all of these groups and consider the intent of the Law, it becomes clear that most of them are invented sayings and innovative interpretations. I shall mention those matters that function as obligatory beliefs in the Law—without which faith is incomplete—and in all of this, I shall seek the intent of the Lawgiver (peace and blessings be upon him) <note>original: "salla Allahu 'alayhi wa sallam" — A traditional blessing for the Prophet Muhammad.</note> rather than what has been established as a principle of the Law or one of its beliefs through incorrect interpretation.

I will begin by defining what the Lawgiver intended for the general public to believe regarding God (blessed and exalted be He) and the methods He used to guide them toward that, as found in the Mighty Book [the Qur'an]. We start with the knowledge of the path that leads to the existence of the **Maker** <term>Maker (original: "al-Sani'"): A philosophical term for God, emphasizing His role as the artisan or designer of the universe.</term>, as this is the first knowledge an accountable person must possess. 

Before that, however, we should mention the opinions of those famous sects on this matter. We say: 

As for the group called the **Hashwites**, they said that the path to knowing the existence of God is through **tradition** <note>original: "al-sam'" — Literally "hearing"; refers to knowledge transmitted through revelation and scripture rather than logic.</note> and not through **reason** <note>original: "al-'aql".</note>. That is to say, the faith in His existence—which people are commanded to believe—is sufficient if it is simply received from the Lawgiver and believed in, just as one receives information about the Afterlife and other matters where reason has no entry. 

This straying group is clearly falling short of the Law’s purpose in the path it established for everyone to lead them to the knowledge of God’s existence and through which He called them to acknowledge Him. This is evident from more than one verse in the Book of God, where He called people to believe...

<summary>Ibn Rushd identifies four major theological sects (Ash’arites, Mu’tazilites, Batinites, and Hashwites), accusing them of distorting the Law through sectarian interpretation. He begins his own investigation by critiquing the Hashwites for rejecting reason in the proof of God's existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the groups who have misinterpreted religious doctrine. Ibn Rushd begins to analyze how different schools—specifically the literalists and the Ash'arites—approach the proof of God's existence, contrasting their methods with the "religious proofs" found in the Qur'an.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith

<page-num>٣٢</page-num>

...regarding the existence of the Creator <note>original: "al-Bari"</note> through rational proofs explicitly stated in the [Qur'an], such as the saying of the Blessed and Exalted:
> ۞ **O people, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you** ۞
And the verse such as His saying, Exalted is He:
> ۞ **Is there any doubt about God, the Originator of the heavens** ۞
And other such verses regarding this meaning. 

It is not for someone to say that if this [rational inquiry] were obligatory for every person who believes in God—meaning that one's faith is not valid except by way of these proofs—then the Prophet, peace be upon him, would not have called anyone to Islam without first presenting these proofs to them. This is because all the Arabs already acknowledged the existence of the Creator, praise be to Him; thus, the Exalted said:
> ۞ **And if you asked them, "Who created the heavens and the earth?" they would surely say, "God"** ۞

It is not impossible for there to exist people whose dullness of mind <note>original: "fadamat al-aql"</note> and sluggishness of nature are such that they cannot understand any of the religious proofs that the Prophet, peace be upon him, established for the general public. This is the rarest of circumstances, and if such a person is found, their duty is to believe in God through hearing <note>original: "al-sama'" — referring to faith based on oral tradition or the testimony of others rather than independent reasoning.</note>. This is the condition of the **Hashwites** <note>See previous page for the definition of this literalist group.</note> in their relationship with the literal sense of the Law.

**As for the Ash’arites**, they held that the affirmation of the existence of God, Blessed and Exalted is He, can only be achieved through reason. However, they followed paths in this matter that are not the religious paths <note>original: "al-turuq al-shar'iyya"</note> to which God drew attention and through which He called people to believe in Him. Specifically, their famous method was built upon demonstrating that the world is **temporally created** <term>temporally created (original: "hadith"): The theological concept that the universe had a beginning in time and was brought into existence from non-existence.</term>. 

According to them, the temporal creation of the world was based on the claim that bodies are composed of **indivisible parts** <note>original: "ajza' la tatajazza"</note>, and that the indivisible part is created, and thus bodies are created due to its creation. The method they followed to demonstrate the creation of the indivisible part—which they call the <term>Indivisible Atom</term> <note>original: "al-jawhar al-fard"</note>—is a convoluted method that escapes many experts in the **art of dialectics** <term>art of dialectics (original: "sina'at al-jadal"): The formal method of debate and logical argument used by medieval theologians to defend doctrine.</term>, let alone the general public. Furthermore, it is not a **demonstrative** method <note>original: "ghayr burhaniyya" — Ibn Rushd uses this to mean a proof that does not meet the strict logical standards of Aristotelian philosophy.</note>, nor does it lead with certainty to the existence of the Creator. This is because, even if we assume the world is temporally created, it follows (as they say) that it must have a creator who brought it into being; however, there arises [a difficulty]...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Qur'an provides natural, rational proofs for God's existence that the Prophet used, but notes that the Ash'arite theologians have replaced these with overly complex and non-demonstrative theories like the "indivisible atom." He contrasts the simple faith of the masses and the literalists (Hashwites) with the convoluted intellectualism of the Ash'arite school.</summary>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the theological methods used to prove the existence of God. Having previously mentioned that the Qur'anic proofs are more suited for the general public, he now delves into a specific logical dilemma regarding the nature of the "Producer" (God) that the Mutakallimun (theologians) struggle to resolve.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٣</page-num>

...regarding the existence of this **Producer** <note>original: "al-muhdith" — the one who brings things into being after they did not exist.</note>, there is a doubt that the **art of theology** <term>Kalam: speculative Islamic theology that uses rational arguments to defend religious doctrines.</term> does not have the strength to resolve. Specifically, we are unable to categorize this Producer as either **eternal** <note>original: "azali" — existing without a beginning.</note> or **produced** <note>original: "muhdath" — brought into existence at a specific point in time.</note>. 

As for Him being produced, this is impossible because He would then require another producer, and that producer would require another, leading to an **infinite regress**, which is logically impossible. 

As for Him being eternal, then His **act** <note>original: "fi'l"</note> which is connected to the **produced effects** <note>original: "al-maf'ulât" — the created things in the world.</note> would also have to be eternal. If the act is eternal, then the effects themselves would have to be eternal. However, a produced thing <note>the world</note> must have its existence linked to a **produced act**. 

The only exception would be if they [the theologians] admitted that a produced act can come from an eternal agent—for the produced effect must be linked to the act of the agent. But they do not admit this, for one of their foundational principles is that "whatever is inseparable from produced things is itself produced." 

Furthermore, if the agent acts at one time and does not act at another, there must be a **cause** <note>original: "illa"</note> that made one of the two states <note>acting vs. not acting</note> more likely than the other. One would then ask the same question about that cause, and the cause of that cause, leading again to an infinite regress. 

What the **theologians** <note>original: "al-mutakallimûn"</note> say in response to this—that the produced act occurred through an **eternal will** <note>original: "irâda qadîma"</note>—is neither a rescue nor a solution to this doubt. This is because the **will** is distinct from the **act** that is connected to the produced effect. If the effect is produced, it is necessary that the act connected to its creation also be produced. 

Whether we assume the will is eternal or produced, or whether it precedes the act or accompanies it, they are forced to allow one of three things regarding the Eternal [God]: 
1. A produced will and a produced act.
2. A produced act and an eternal will.
3. An eternal act and an eternal will.

A produced thing cannot come from an eternal act without an intermediary, even if we were to grant them that it could come from an eternal will. To claim that the **will** itself is the **act** connected to the produced effect is something **unintelligible**; it is like assuming an effect without an agent. The act is distinct from the agent, distinct from the effect, and distinct from the will. The will is a **condition** for the act, not the act itself. 

Furthermore, this eternal will would have had to be connected to the non-existence of the produced thing for an infinite duration <note>original: "dahran la nihaya lahu"</note>, since the produced thing was non-existent...

<sig>( ٥ )</sig>

<vocab>The Art of Theology (Kalam), The Producer (al-Muhdith), Eternal (Azali), Produced Effects (al-Maf'ulât), Theologians (al-Mutakallimûn), Eternal Will (al-Irâda al-Qadîma), Cause (al-'Illa), Ancient/Eternal (Qadîm), Agent (Fâ'il)</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite theologians' attempt to explain how an eternal God creates a temporal world. He argues that their concept of "Eternal Will" fails to logically bridge the gap between an unchanging Creator and the start of a new action in time.</summary>
<keywords>Kalam, causality, Eternal Will, infinite regress, Ibn Rushd, Ash'arites, creation, Aristotelian logic</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the Ash'arite theologians (the Mutakallimun). He is finishing a logical puzzle: if God's Will is eternal but its effect (the world) is temporal, why did the world start *at that specific moment* and not sooner? He argues that the theologians' logic leads to a paradox where an infinite amount of time must pass before anything can happen, which is impossible.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٤</page-num>

...for an infinite duration, then the Will would not connect to the intended object <note>the world</note> at the time it required its creation except after the passing of an infinite duration. Since that which is infinite cannot, by definition, pass away, it would follow that this intended object could never emerge into actual existence, or else an infinite duration would have to come to an end—which is impossible. 

This is exactly the same **demonstration** <note>original: "burhan"</note> that the **theologians** <term>Mutakallimun: scholars who use rational speech and logic to defend religious tenets</term> relied upon to prove the **origination of the celestial spheres** <note>the idea that the rotation of the heavens must have a beginning in time</note>. 

Furthermore, a **Will** that precedes the intended object and relates to it only at a specific time must undergo a new "determination to create" at the moment of creation. This determination could not have existed before that moment. If the **Willer** <note>God</note> did not possess a state at the time of the action that was additional to His state at the time when the Will required the *absence* of the action, then there would be no reason why the existence of that action at that specific time would be more likely than its non-existence. 

All of this involves such branching complexities and difficult doubts that even the most skilled scholars of **theology** <term>Kalam</term> and **philosophy** <term>Hikmah</term> cannot fully escape them, let alone the general public. If the masses were commanded to acquire knowledge through these paths, it would be a case of "tasking them with the impossible" <note>a reference to the theological concept of *taklif ma la yutaq*</note>. 

Moreover, the methods these people have followed regarding the **origination of the world** <note>the world having a beginning in time</note> combine two flaws: the public does not have the natural disposition to accept them, and they are not truly **demonstrative** <note>logically certain/proven</note> in nature. Therefore, they are suitable for neither the scholars nor the public. 

We shall point this out briefly by saying that the paths they have taken in this matter are twofold. 

The first—which is the most famous and the one relied upon by the majority of them—is built upon three premises. These serve as the foundations for what they seek to prove regarding the **origination of the world**:

1.  That **substances** <term>jawahir: the basic "stuff" of the world</term> are never inseparable from **accidents** <term>a'rad: temporary qualities like color, motion, or temperature</term>; that is, they are never free of them.
2.  That accidents are **produced** <note>original: "haditha" — they have a beginning in time</note>.
3.  That whatever is inseparable from produced things is itself produced. 

As for the first premise—which states that **substances** are never devoid of **accidents**—if they mean by "substances" the perceptible bodies we see around us that exist by themselves, then this premise is correct. However, if by "substance" they mean the **indivisible part** <term>al-jawhar al-fard: the "atom" or the smallest possible particle that cannot be split</term>, which is what they intend by the term "substance"...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the theologians for using overly complex and logically flawed arguments to prove the world's creation, arguing that their methods are neither accessible to the public nor scientifically sound. He begins dismantling their primary argument, which relies on the relationship between "substances" and "accidents."</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Mutakallimun, Kalam, creation of the world, substances and accidents, logic, atomism, celestial spheres, Will of God</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the theologians (the Mutakallimun), shifting from the nature of God's Will to the physical composition of the universe. He is specifically addressing the theory of "atomism"—the idea that matter is made of indivisible parts—which the Ash'arite school used to explain how God creates the world.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٥</page-num>

...individual part, for regarding this there is a doubt that is not small. That is because the existence of an **indivisible substance** <term>Jawhar fard: literally "the single essence," often referred to in philosophy as an "atom"—the smallest possible unit of matter that cannot be split further</term> is not self-evident. There are intense, contradictory arguments regarding its existence, and the **art of theology** <term>Kalam: the practice of using rational speech and logic to defend religious tenets</term> lacks the power to extract the truth from them. That task belongs only to the **art of demonstration** <note>original: "sina'at al-burhan"; refers to rigorous, scientific logic used by philosophers</note>, and the practitioners of this art are very few.

The arguments used by the **Ash’arites** <term>Ash’arite: a dominant school of Islamic theology that sought a middle ground between pure reason and literalism</term> to prove its existence are, for the most part, **rhetorical** <note>appealing to common sense or persuasive speech rather than strict logical proof</note>. Their most famous argument is their claim that it is a matter of primary knowledge that we only say an elephant is "larger" than an ant because the elephant has more parts than the ant. If this is the case, they argue, then the body must be composed of these parts and is not a simple, single whole. If the body perishes, it dissolves into these parts, and if it is composed, it is composed from them.

This error entered their thinking because they confused **discrete quantity** <term>Discrete quantity: things that can be counted as separate units, like a pile of stones or the number ten</term> with **continuous quantity** <term>Continuous quantity: things that are measured as a whole, like a line, a surface, or a physical body</term>. They assumed that what applies to the discrete must apply to the continuous. This [logic] is true for numbers; we say one number is "more" than another because of the quantity of parts (units) within it. However, this is not true for **continuous quantity**. For the continuous, we use the terms "greater" or "larger," not "more" or "less." We use "more" and "fewer" for numbers, but we do not say a number is "larger" or "smaller" <note>in the geometric sense of magnitude</note>. 

If we followed their view, all things would be mere numbers, and there would be no such thing as continuous magnitude at all. Consequently, the **art of geometry** would be exactly the same as the **art of arithmetic**. 

Yet, it is self-evident that every magnitude can be divided into two halves—meaning the three magnitudes: the line, the surface, and the body. Furthermore, a **continuous quantity** is that which allows a boundary to be placed in its middle where the ends of the two parts meet; this is impossible in numbers. 

However, this is also countered by the fact that the body and all other parts of continuous quantity accept division. Every divisible thing must either be divided into something that is itself divisible, or into something that is indivisible. If it is divided into the indivisible, then we have found the **part that cannot [be further divided]**...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite theologians' "common sense" proof for atoms, arguing they mistakenly treat physical bodies like sets of numbers rather than continuous geometric shapes. He explains that if bodies were just piles of indivisible parts, geometry would be reduced to simple counting.</summary>

<keywords>Atomism, Indivisible Substance, Ash’arite Theology, Discrete vs Continuous Quantity, Ibn Rushd, Geometry vs Arithmetic, Kalam</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite argument for "origination" (haduth). He is exploring the logical problems that arise when one tries to prove God's existence by claiming the world is made of "indivisible parts" (atoms) and "accidents" (temporary properties).</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٦</page-num>

...is divided, and if it is divided into something further divisible, the question returns regarding this new division: does it divide into a divisible part, or into something indivisible? If the division were to go on to **infinity**, then a finite object would contain **infinite parts**. Yet, it is a matter of **primary knowledge** <note>self-evident truths that the mind accepts without needing a proof</note> that the parts of a finite thing must themselves be finite.

Among the common doubts that follow their position is the question: when an **indivisible part** <term>al-juz' al-ladhi la yatajazza': the atom</term> comes into being, what exactly is the recipient of that "coming-to-be"? This "coming-to-be" is an **accident** <term>‘arad: a non-essential property, like a color or a movement, which cannot exist without a "substance" to hold it</term>. Once the thing has fully emerged into existence, the process of "coming-to-be" has ceased. According to their own principles, **accidents** cannot exist apart from **substances** <term>jawahir: the underlying "stuff" or essence of a thing</term>. This forces them to claim that the "coming-to-be" belongs to a specific existing thing and for a specific existing thing. 

Furthermore, they might be asked: if a thing comes into existence from something other than non-existence, to what does the "action of the agent" <note>the Creator's act of making something</note> actually attach? In their view, there is no middle ground between **non-existence** and **existence**. If the act of the agent does not attach to non-existence (since it is nothing), and it does not attach to that which already exists (since it is already finished), then it must attach to some **intermediate essence** between non-existence and existence. This is precisely what forced the **Mu’tazilites** <term>Mu’tazila: an early school of Islamic theology known for its emphasis on reason and human free will</term> to claim that there is a "thingness" or "essence" within **non-existence** <note>the theory of "al-ma'dum shay'" (the non-existent is a "thing")</note>. Both groups are also forced to argue for the existence of **the void** <term>al-khala’: empty space or a vacuum</term>. 

As you can see, the **art of dialectic** <term>sina'at al-jadal: a method of argument based on commonly accepted premises rather than certain, scientific proofs</term> lacks the power to resolve these doubts. Therefore, this approach should not be made the basis for **the knowledge of God** (blessed and exalted is He), especially for the **general public**. The path to knowing God is much clearer than this, as we shall explain in our later remarks.

As for the **second premise**—which claims that all **accidents** are "originated" <note>muhdatha: having a beginning in time, rather than being eternal</note>—it is a doubtful premise. The obscurity of its meaning is similar to the obscurity found in the nature of the **body** itself. This is because we only observe *some* bodies being originated, and similarly, we only observe *some* accidents. There is no difference in the logical jump required to move from the **observed** to the **unseen** in both cases. If it were mandatory to apply the judgment of observed accidents to unseen ones—meaning we judge that what we have *not* seen is originated based on what we *have* seen—then it would be equally mandatory to do the same for...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the complex logical puzzles of atomism and "accidents" are too confusing to serve as a foundation for faith, critiquing both the Ash'arite and Mu'tazilite theological frameworks. He suggests that these dialectical methods are unsuitable for the general public and hints at a simpler, more "natural" way to know God.</summary>

<keywords>divisibility, atomism, accidents, substances, Mu'tazilites, non-existence, the void, dialectic, origination, the observed and the unseen</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite cosmological argument. On the previous page, he discussed the logical difficulties of "indivisible parts" (atoms). Here, he moves to the specific problems of proving the world's beginning by looking at celestial bodies, time, and space.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٧</page-num>

...bodies, and we can dispense with inferring the **origination** <term>haduth: the state of coming into existence after not having existed</term> of bodies from the origination of **accidents** <term>'arad: non-essential properties, like color or motion, that need a substance to exist</term>. 

This is because the **celestial body**—which is the entity that people doubt should be categorized alongside observable things on Earth—is such that the doubt regarding the origination of its accidents is the same as the doubt regarding the origination of the body itself. This is because neither its origination nor the origination of its accidents has been perceived by the senses. For this reason, we ought to investigate it through the matter of its **motion**. This is the path that leads those who follow it to the knowledge of God with certainty; it is the **path of the elite** <note>the "khawass," or those capable of philosophical demonstration rather than just simple faith or dialectical debate</note>, and it is the path that God specifically granted to **Abraham**, peace be upon him, in His saying: 

> “So did We show Abraham the **dominion of the heavens and the earth** that he might be one of those who possess **certainty**.” <note>Quran 6:75. Ibn Rushd interprets "dominion" (malakut) as the underlying laws and movements of the universe.</note>

For all doubt lies within the **celestial bodies**, and many ancient observers stopped their inquiry there and believed they were gods. Furthermore, **time** is among the accidents, and it is difficult to conceive of its origination. This is because every **originated thing** must be preceded by non-existence in time; the precedence of a thing’s non-existence over the thing itself cannot be conceived except through the concept of time. 

Also, regarding the **place** <term>makan</term> in which the world exists: if every created thing in a place requires a place that precedes it, it is also difficult to conceive of its origination. If place is a **void** <term>khala': an empty space or vacuum</term>—according to the view of those who see place as a vacuum—then its origination would require another void to precede it if it were assumed to be originated. If place is the **boundary of the surrounding body** <note>the Aristotelian definition: place is the inner surface of the containing body</note> according to the second view, it would follow that this surrounding body must also be in a place, requiring one body after another, and the matter would regress to **infinity**. 

These are all complex and difficult doubts. The arguments by which they <note>the Ash'arite theologians</note> seek to demonstrate the invalidity of the **pre-eternity of accidents** <term>qidam al-a'rad: the idea that properties could have existed forever</term> are only binding against those who claim the pre-eternity of those specific accidents that the senses perceive as being originated—that is, those who posit that *no* accidents are originated. For they argue that if the accidents which appear to the senses as originated were not actually originated, they would either have to be **transferred** from one location to another or be **latent** <term>kamina: hidden or "stored" within the substance before becoming visible</term> in the location where they appeared before they were visible. They then disprove these two options and believe they have demonstrated that all accidents are originated. However, their argument only shows that those accidents which appear...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that proving the world was created by looking at its "accidents" is logically flawed, especially regarding celestial bodies, time, and space. He suggests that the true path to certain knowledge of God is through the study of celestial motion, as exemplified by the Prophet Abraham.</summary>
<keywords>origination, accidents, celestial bodies, Abraham, dominion of heavens, time, space, void, infinite regress, Ash'arites</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s systematic deconstruction of the Ash’arite proof for the world's creation. On the previous page, he noted that the "origination" of celestial bodies isn't directly observable. Here, he attacks the logical premise that if a substance always possesses changing qualities, the substance itself must have a beginning.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>٣٨</page-num>

...**originated** <term>hadith: something that began to exist in time</term>, then it is originated. This does not apply to things whose origination is not apparent, nor to things whose status is not in doubt, such as the **accidents** <term>'arad: a property like color or motion that cannot exist on its own</term> found in **celestial bodies**, including their motions, shapes, and the like. Thus, their arguments for the origination of all accidents revert to an **analogy of the witnessed to the absent** <note>original: "qiyas al-shahid 'ala al-gha'ib"—a logical method used by theologians to apply rules of the physical world (witnessed) to the divine or cosmic realm (absent)</note>. This is a **rhetorical argument** <note>in the Aristotelian sense, an argument that persuades but does not provide absolute scientific proof</note> except in cases where the transition of logic is self-evident, which only occurs when one is certain of the uniformity of nature between the witnessed and the absent.

As for the **third premise**—which states that "whatever is not devoid of originated things is itself originated"—this is an **equivocal premise** <term>mushtaraka al-ism: a term that sounds the same but has two distinct meanings</term>. This is because it can be understood in two ways:

The first meaning is: that which is not devoid of the **genus** <note>the general category or "kind"</note> of originated things, even if it is devoid of any specific individual accident. 

The second meaning is: that which is not devoid of a specific, pointed-to individual accident, as if you were to say: "that which is not devoid of *this* specific blackness."

Regarding this second concept, it is true. I mean, if a thing is not devoid of a specific, pointed-to accident, and that accident is originated, it follows of necessity that its **substrate** <term>mawdu': the underlying substance that "carries" the accident</term> is also originated. This is because if the substrate were **eternal** <term>qadim: existing from forever without a beginning</term>, it would have existed before that specific accident, but we had assumed it could never be devoid of it; this would be a contradiction.

However, as for the first concept—which is the one the **theologians** <term>Mutakallimun: scholars of Kalam (dialectical theology)</term> intend—it does not necessarily follow that the **locus** <term>mahall: the place or substance where an accident resides</term> is originated. I mean, for a thing that is not devoid of the *genus* of originated things, it is possible to conceive of a single locus—meaning a body—upon which an **infinite succession** of accidents occurs. These could be opposites or non-opposites, such as a series of motions without an end, just as many of the **ancients** <note>referring to philosophers like Aristotle</note> believed regarding the universe: that it undergoes one formation after another. 

Because of this, when the later theologians sensed the **weakness** of this premise, they sought to bolster and strengthen it. They claimed it is impossible for infinite accidents to succeed one another upon a single locus. Their reasoning was that if this were possible, no specific, "pointed-to" accident could exist in the locus today unless an infinite number of accidents had occurred before it. This, they argue, would lead to the current accident being impossible, because it would require the completion of that which has no end; and since that which is infinite can never be completed...

<summary>Ibn Rushd exposes a logical flaw in the theologians' argument for the world's beginning, distinguishing between being inseparable from a specific change and being inseparable from the general "process" of change.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Al-Kashf, accidents, origination, infinite regress, Mutakallimun, celestial bodies, logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theologians. Having discussed the definition of "origination," he now addresses the logical problem of "infinite regress"—the idea that if an infinite series of events must happen before today, today would never arrive. Ibn Rushd argues that this theological objection to an eternal world is based on a flawed analogy.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>۳۹</page-num>

<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

it would necessarily follow that this specific thing—meaning the one assumed to exist—should not exist at all. For example, regarding the **current motion of the celestial body** <note>Ibn Rushd refers to the daily rotation of the heavens</note>: if an infinite number of motions had to occur before it, then this current motion should never have come to be. 

They <note>the Ash'arite theologians</note> illustrated this with an analogy of a man saying to another: "I will not give you this **dinar** <term>dinar: a gold coin used as currency</term> until I have given you an infinite number of dinars before it." In such a case, it would be impossible for him ever to give that specific dinar. 

However, this analogy is incorrect. In this example, a **beginning** and an **end** have been established, while the interval between them is claimed to be infinite. This is because the man's statement occurs at a specific, limited moment in time, and the act of giving the dinar also occurs at a limited moment. Thus, he has stipulated that he will give the dinar at a time that is separated from the time of his speech by an infinite duration—the time required to give an infinite number of dinars. This is, of course, impossible. Thus, it is clear that this analogy does not truly resemble the philosophical problem it is meant to represent <note>Ibn Rushd argues that the universe, unlike the man's promise, has no "starting point" in the Aristotelian view, so the analogy of a "delay" does not apply</note>.

As for their claim that "whatever exists only after the existence of an infinite number of things cannot exist," this is not true in every sense. This is because things that precede one another exist in two ways: either in a **circular** <term>al-dawr: cyclical or repeating patterns</term> fashion or in a **linear** <term>al-istiqama: a straight, non-repeating sequence</term> fashion.

In those things that exist in a **circular** fashion, it is necessary for them to be infinite, unless something external occurs to end the cycle. For example:
- If there is a sunrise, there was a sunset.
- If there was a sunset, there was a sunrise.
- If there is a sunrise, then there was a sunrise before it.

Likewise:
- If there is a cloud, there was vapor rising from the earth.
- If there was vapor rising from the earth, the earth must have been wet.
- If the earth was wet, there was rain.
- If there was rain, there was a cloud.
- Thus, if there is a cloud, there was a cloud before it <note>This describes a self-sustaining cycle of cause and effect</note>.

As for things that occur in a **linear** fashion—such as a human being born from a human, and that human from another—if this sequence is **essential** <term>bi-al-dhat: a chain where each link is the primary cause of the next</term>, then it cannot proceed to infinity. This is because if the first cause in a chain of causes does not exist, the final effect cannot exist. 

However, if the sequence is **accidental** <term>bi-al-'arad: a sequence where the true power of creation comes from an external source, not the preceding link</term>—such as when a human is produced in reality by an agent other than the human parent who...

<summary>Ibn Rushd refutes the theological analogy of the "infinite dinars," arguing that infinite cycles (like weather or celestial motion) are logically possible, whereas only "essential" linear chains of causes must have a beginning.</summary>
<keywords>infinite regress, causality, circular motion, Ash'arites, Aristotelian physics, dinar analogy, essential vs accidental causes</keywords>

...he is the father who forms him, and the father's status is merely that of an instrument for the **Maker** <term>Maker: *al-Sāni’*, a term used by philosophers to describe God as the Divine Artisan or Architect of the universe</term>. Thus, it is not impossible, if that Agent is found to perform an act that has no end, that He performs it using changing instruments to create infinite individuals. All of this does not fully appear in this specific place; we have only brought it up so that it may be known that what these people imagined to be a **demonstrative proof** <term>Proof: *Burhān*, a technical term for a logical argument that yields certain knowledge</term> is not actually a proof. Nor is it among the types of discourse suitable for the general public—by which I mean the simple proofs that God has commanded all His servants to use in believing in Him. Thus, it has become clear to you from this that this method is neither scientifically demonstrative nor in accordance with the Law.

As for the second method, it is the one devised by **Abu al-Ma’ali** <note>Referring to the influential Ash’arite theologian al-Juwayni (1028–1085 CE)</note> in his treatise known as the ***Nizamiyya*** <note>The *Al-’Aqida al-Nizamiyya*, a work of theology named after his patron, the vizier Nizam al-Mulk</note>. Its foundation rests on two premises:

The first is that the world and everything in it could **possibly** be the opposite of what it is. For example, it is possible for it to be smaller than it is or larger than it is; or to have a shape different from the shape it currently has; or for the number of its bodies to be different from their current number; or for the movement of every moving thing within it to be in a direction opposite to the one in which it moves. Thus, it would be possible for a stone to move upward and for fire to move downward, or for the eastern motion to be western and the western motion to be eastern.

The second premise is that what is "possible" is **originated** <term>Originated: *Muhdath*, meaning it was brought into existence after not being</term>, and it must have an "originator"—that is, an agent who chose one of the two possibilities over the other.

As for the first premise, it is **rhetorical** <note>Ibn Rushd argues this is a persuasive but logically weak point (*khatabi*), appealing to the imagination rather than reason</note> at first glance. Regarding some parts of the world, its falsity is self-evident—such as the idea of a human existing in a form other than this human form we possess. In other cases, the matter is doubtful, such as whether the eastern motion could be western or the western motion could be eastern. This is not known by itself, as there might be a cause for it that is not self-evident, or it may be among the causes hidden from mankind. It seems that what occurs to a person at first thought when considering these things is similar to what occurs to someone looking at the parts of...

<summary>Ibn Rushd dismisses the Ash'arite "infinite regress" argument and critiques al-Juwayni’s "argument from possibility," asserting that the world's design is not arbitrary but follows a hidden logic or necessity.</summary>

...artifacts, without being a practitioner of those crafts. This is because such a person has already formed the opinion that everything, or most things, in those artifacts could possibly be the opposite of what they are, and yet the artifact would still perform the same function for which it was made—I mean its end purpose. In the eyes of such a person, there is no place for wisdom in that artifact.

However, the artisan—and whoever shares with the artisan some knowledge of that craft—sees the matter to be the opposite. He sees that in the artifact, there is nothing except that which is mandatory and necessary, or that which makes the artifact more perfect and superior if it is not strictly necessary. This is the very meaning of **craftsmanship** <term>Craftsmanship: *al-Sinā‘a*, which refers to the purposeful and intelligent design of an object</term>. It is apparent that created beings are similar in this sense to artifacts. So glory be to the Great Creator!

This premise, in terms of being rhetorical <term>Rhetorical: *Khatābiyya*, referring to arguments that are persuasive to the general public but may lack strict logical proof</term>, might be suitable for convincing the masses. However, in terms of being false and invalidating the wisdom of the **Maker** <term>Maker: *al-Sāni’*, referring to God as the Divine Architect</term>, it is not suitable for them. It invalidates wisdom because wisdom is nothing more than the knowledge of the causes of a thing. If a thing does not have necessary causes that require it to exist in the specific form that its species exists in, then there is no specialized knowledge here that belongs to the Wise Creator over anyone else. Just as if there were no necessary causes in the existence of manufactured things, there would be no craftsmanship at all, nor wisdom attributed to the artisan over the non-artisan.

What wisdom would there be in a human being if all their actions and functions could be performed by any organ whatsoever, or without an organ at all? For instance, if sight could be achieved by the ear just as it is by the eye, or smell by the eye just as it is by the nose? All of this is an invalidation of wisdom and an invalidation of the meaning by which He named Himself "The Wise"—exalted and hallowed are His names above such a thing!

We find that <term>Ibn Sina</term> <note>Known as Avicenna (980–1037 CE), whose synthesis of Aristotelianism and Neoplatonism dominated Islamic philosophy</note> yields to this premise in some way. This is because he believes that every existing thing other than the **Agent** <note>The First Cause or God</note> is, when considered in its own essence, "possible" and "contingent." He categorizes these contingent things into two types: one type is contingent in consideration of its **Maker**...

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite "principle of possibility." He moves from discussing how this principle affects our understanding of God's wisdom to a technical critique of how theologians and philosophers (like Avicenna) categorize "necessity" and "possibility." He then pivots to examine the arguments of Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni regarding whether a "permissible" world must be one created in time.</meta>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<page-num>42</page-num>

<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

And another category is that which is necessary by virtue of its agent, but possible by virtue of its own essence, and that the Being who is necessary in all respects is the **First Agent** <term>First Agent: *al-Fā‘il al-Awwal*, a philosophical term for God as the primary cause of all existence</term>. This is an argument of the utmost weakness. This is because that which is **possible** <term>Possible: *al-Mumkin*, something that can either exist or not exist, as opposed to something that must exist</term> in its essence and its substance cannot possibly become **necessary** <term>Necessary: *al-Darurī*, something that must exist by its very nature</term> because of its agent, unless the very nature of the "possible" were transformed into the nature of the "necessary." 

If it is said that what is meant by "possible by virtue of its essence" is simply that whenever its agent is imagined to be removed, the object itself is removed, we reply: this removal is impossible <note>original: *mustahīl*</note>. This is not the place for a discussion with this man <note>likely referring to the philosopher Avicenna (Ibn Sina), whose distinction between "necessary by another" and "possible in itself" Ibn Rushd famously rejected</note>. However, out of a desire to address the things this man invented, we were drawn into mentioning it. So let us return to where we were.

As for the second proposition—the one stating that the "permissible" <note>original: *al-jā’iz*, referring to things that could logically be otherwise</note> is created in time <note>original: *muḥdath*, meaning originated at a specific point in time rather than existing eternally</note>—it is a premise that is not self-evident. Scholars have differed over it: **Plato** <note>original: *Aflāṭūn*</note> allowed for a "permissible" thing to be eternal, while **Aristotle** <note>original: *Arisṭū*</note> forbade it. This is a difficult inquiry, the truth of which will only be clear to the people of the **art of demonstration** <term>Art of Demonstration: *Sinā‘at al-Burhān*, referring to those trained in strict Aristotelian logic</term>. They are the scholars whom God has singled out for His knowledge and whose testimony He has joined in the Mighty Book <note>The Quran</note> with His own testimony and the testimony of His angels.

As for **Abu al-Ma'ali** <note>Imam al-Haramayn al-Juwayni (d. 1085), a towering figure in Ash'arite theology</note>, he sought to demonstrate this premise through several other premises: 
1. First, that the "permissible" must have a "Specifier" <note>original: *mukaṣṣiṣ*</note> that makes one of its two possible descriptions more appropriate for it than the second. 
2. Second, that this Specifier can only be a "Willer" <note>original: *murīd*, one who possesses Will</note>. 
3. Third, that whatever exists through **Will** <term>Will: *al-Irāda*, the divine attribute that chooses between alternatives</term> is created in time. 

He then demonstrated that the "permissible" must come from a Will—that is, from an agent who possesses Will—on the grounds that every action comes either from **Nature** <term>Nature: *al-Ṭabī‘a*, an unconscious, automatic force</term> or from Will. Nature cannot produce one of two identical "permissibles"—meaning it does not act upon one thing to the exclusion of its identical counterpart, but rather acts upon both. 

An example of this is that **Scammony** <note>original: *al-saqmūniyā*, a plant resin used in medieval medicine as a powerful purgative to "draw out" yellow bile</note> does not, for instance, attract the yellow bile that is in the right side of the body while ignoring that which is in the left. As for the Will, it is that which singles out a thing to the exclusion of its identical counterpart. He then added to this that the world’s existence in its current location is an identical possibility to its existence elsewhere...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the philosophical distinction between "possible in essence" and "necessary by another," then examines al-Juwayni's argument that the world requires a "Willer" to choose its specific form from among identical possibilities.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Avicenna, al-Juwayni, possibility vs necessity, divine will, nature, Scammony, demonstration, Plato, Aristotle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theologians (the Mutakallimun). Having previously addressed the "principle of possibility," he now turns to their specific arguments regarding the Divine Will and the creation of the world within a "void." He is currently dissecting the logical relationship between an eternal Will and a temporally created object.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...the atmosphere in which it was created—meaning the **void** <term>Void: *al-khalā’*, an empty space or vacuum that theologians hypothesized existed before or outside the world</term>—due to it being in a different position within that void. From this, they concluded that the world was created through an act of **will**. 

The premise stating that the **will** is what specifies one of two identical options is correct. However, the premise stating that the world exists within a void surrounding it is either false or, at the very least, not self-evident. Furthermore, an absurd consequence follows from their position on this void: it would have to be **eternal** <term>Eternal: *qadīm*, having no beginning in time</term>. This is because if the void itself were created, it would require another void to exist within, leading to an infinite regress.

As for the premise stating that nothing but a **temporally created** <term>Temporally created: *muḥdath*, something that began to exist after not existing</term> object can proceed from the will, that is not self-evident. This is because the will in **actuality** <term>Actuality: *bi-l-fi‘l*, the state of being fully realized or active</term> exists simultaneously with the action of the willed object itself. This is because "will" belongs to the category of **relative terms** <term>Relative terms: *al-mudāf*, concepts that can only be defined in relation to one another</term>. It has been demonstrated that when one of two relative terms exists in actuality, the other must also exist in actuality—just as the existence of a **father** implies the existence of a **son**. 

If one exists in **potency** <term>Potency: *bi-l-quwwah*, the capacity or potential for something to exist or happen</term>, the other exists in potency. Therefore, if the will in actuality is eternal, then the willed object in actuality must necessarily be eternal. If the will in actuality were created, then the willed object must also be created in actuality.

As for the will that precedes the willed object, that is the "will in potency"—meaning a will whose object has not yet emerged into actuality because the specific action required for the object's creation has not yet been joined to that will. It is therefore evident that when its willed object does emerge, the will enters a state of existence it was not in before the object's actualization. This is because the will is the cause of the object’s creation through the mediation of the act. Thus, if the **theologians** <term>Theologians: *al-Mutakallimūn*, scholars who used dialectical reasoning to defend Islamic tenets</term> were to posit that the will is created, it would follow by necessity that the willed object is created.

The apparent sense of the **Religious Law** <term>Religious Law: *al-Shar‘*, referring to the divine revelation or scripture</term> is that it did not delve into such technical depths with the general public. For this reason, it did not explicitly state whether the Divine Will is eternal or created. Instead, it expressed the most apparent view: that the Will is what brings created beings into existence. This is seen in the words of the Almighty: ﴾*Verily, Our word unto a thing, when We intend it, is only that We say unto it: "Be," and it is*﴿ <note>Quran 16:40; original Arabic: *innamā amrunā li-shay’in idhā aradnāhu an naqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn*</note>. This is because the masses do not understand how temporally created beings can proceed from an eternal will. Rather, the truth is that the Religious Law remained silent on the eternity or temporality of the Will because this is...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the theological view that a world created in time proves an eternal Divine Will, arguing instead that a truly "active" will must coexist with its object. He notes that the Quran avoids these complex philosophical definitions of "eternal" versus "temporal" to remain accessible to common believers.</summary>

-># The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith<-

...ambiguities in the eyes of the majority. Furthermore, the **theologians** <term>Theologians: *al-Mutakallimūn*, scholars who used dialectical reasoning to defend Islamic creed</term> do not possess a definitive proof for the impossibility of a **temporally created will** <term>Temporally created will: *irādah ḥādithah*, a will that begins to exist in time, rather than being eternal</term> subsisting in an **eternal being** <term>Eternal being: *mawjud qadīm*, referring to God</term>. This is because the principle they rely upon to deny that the Will can subsist in an eternal locus is the premise we previously mentioned: "that which is not free from temporal accidents is itself temporal." We shall explain this point more fully when we discuss the Will.

It has become clear to you from all of this that the famous paths of the **Ash’arites** <term>Ash’arites: *al-Ash‘ariyyah*, the dominant school of Sunni theology which Ibn Rushd frequently critiques for being neither purely philosophical nor purely scriptural</term> in seeking knowledge of God, the Sublime, are neither certain theoretical paths nor certain scriptural paths. This is evident to anyone who reflects on the types of evidence in the **Mighty Book** <note>original: "al-Kitāb al-‘Azīz", the Quran</note> that prompt recognition of the truth—specifically regarding the knowledge of the existence of the **Artisan** <term>Artisan: *al-Ṣāni‘*, a common philosophical term for God as the Designer or Maker of the world</term>. For if you contemplate the scriptural methods, you will find that they generally combine two characteristics: first, they provide **certainty**; and second, they are **simple** rather than complex—meaning they possess few premises, so their conclusions follow closely from their initial starting points.

As for the **Sufis** <term>Sufis: *al-Ṣūfiyyah*, practitioners of the mystical dimension of Islam</term>, their path of inquiry is not a theoretical one—meaning it is not composed of premises and **syllogisms** <term>Syllogisms: *aqyisah*, logical arguments where a conclusion is drawn from two premises</term>. Rather, they claim that knowledge of God and other existing things is something "cast into the soul" once it is stripped of carnal desires and turns its thought toward the sought-after goal. To validate this, they argue from many outward meanings of the Law, such as the words of the Almighty: *“Fear God, and God will teach you”* <note>Quran 2:282</note>, and His words: *“And those who strive for Us—We will surely guide them to Our ways”* <note>Quran 29:69</note>, and His saying: *“If you fear God, He will grant you a criterion”* <note>Quran 8:29</note>, among many similar verses thought to support this meaning. 

We say: even if we grant that this method exists, it is not a general method for all people as human beings. If this were the intended method for people to use, the method of **speculative inquiry** <term>Speculative inquiry: *al-naẓar*, rational or philosophical investigation</term> would be futile, and its presence in humans would be in vain. Yet the entire Quran is a call to inquiry and **consideration** <term>Consideration: *al-i‘tibār*, the act of reflecting on things to gain deeper understanding</term>, and a reminder of the paths of reasoning. Indeed, we do not deny that the subduing of desires is a condition for the validity of inquiry—just as physical health is a condition for it—because...

<summary>Ibn Rushd concludes his critique of Ash’arite theology as being logically insufficient and then examines the Sufi claim that knowledge of God is attained through mystical experience rather than logic. He argues that while spiritual discipline is helpful, the Quran explicitly commands rational inquiry for all of humanity.</summary>

<keywords>Ash'arites, Sufism, Divine Will, speculative inquiry, Quranic evidence, Artisan, syllogism, carnal desires, rational inquiry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of theological and mystical methods of knowing God. Having dismissed the Ash’arite dialectical approach on the previous page, he now addresses the Sufi approach before introducing what he considers the true, scriptural proofs for God's existence.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>

<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

The suppression of desires is what aids in the acquisition of knowledge itself, even if it is merely a condition for it, just as health is a condition for learning even though it does not provide the learning itself. From this perspective, the Law <term>Law: *al-Shar‘*, referring to the divine revelation and religious legal framework</term> called to this method and encouraged it as a whole—meaning it encouraged action—not because action is sufficient in itself, as those people <note>Likely referring to the Sufis, who emphasized spiritual practice over theoretical reasoning</note> thought. Rather, if it is useful for theoretical inquiry, it is in the manner we have described. This is clear to whoever is fair and considers the matter for themselves.

As for the **Mu‘tazilites** <term>Mu‘tazilites: *al-Mu‘tazilah*, an early rationalist school of Islamic theology. Ibn Rushd often finds them more rigorous than the Ash’arites but still flawed</term>, none of their books reached us in this **peninsula** <note>original: "al-jazīrah", referring to Al-Andalus or the Iberian Peninsula</note> from which we might understand the methods they followed in this regard. It is likely their methods are of the same kind as those of the **Ash’arites**.

If it is asked: "Since it has become clear that none of these methods are the Scriptural path by which the Law called all people—despite their different **natures** <term>Natures: *fiṭrah*, the innate human disposition or natural constitution</term>—to acknowledge the existence of the **Creator** <note>original: "al-Bārī", the Maker or Originator</note>, then what is the Scriptural path which the **Mighty Book** <note>original: "al-Kitāb al-‘Azīz", the Quran</note> pointed toward and which the **Companions** <term>Companions: *al-Ṣaḥābah*, the first generation of Muslims who followed the Prophet Muhammad</term> (may God be pleased with them) relied upon?"

We say: The path which the Mighty Book points toward and through whose door it calls everyone, when the Mighty Book is carefully examined, is found to be limited to two classes.

The first is the path of recognizing the **Providence** <term>Providence: *al-‘ināyah*, the divine care and purposeful arrangement of the universe for the benefit of humanity</term> shown to man and the creation of all existing things for his sake; let us call this the **Proof from Providence**.

The second method is what is apparent from the **invention** <term>Invention: *ikhtirā‘*, the act of bringing something into existence or the "origination" of life and form from nothingness</term> of the essences of existing things, such as the invention of life in inanimate matter, and sensory perception, and the intellect. Let us call this the **Proof from Invention**.

As for the first method, it is built upon two principles:
1. That all existing things here are in accordance with the existence of man.
2. That this accordance is necessarily from an Agent who intended and willed it, as it is not possible for this accordance to happen by **coincidence** <note>original: "al-ittifāq", meaning by chance or accidental collision of matter</note>.

As for their being in accordance with the existence of man, certainty of this is attained by considering the suitability of night and day, the sun and the moon, for the existence of man. Likewise, the suitability of the four seasons for him, and the place in which he dwells—which is the Earth. Similarly, there appears the suitability of many...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that neither Sufi practice nor Mu'tazilite/Ash'arite theology provides the true scriptural path to knowing God. He identifies the two authentic Quranic proofs as the Proof from Providence (purposeful design) and the Proof from Invention (the miracle of life and mind).</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, Proof from Providence, Proof from Invention, Al-Andalus, Fitrah, Quranic proofs, Teleology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s explanation of the two primary proofs for the existence of God that he finds in the Qur’an: the Proof from Providence and the Proof from Invention. The previous page ended while discussing how the universe is tailored to support life.</meta>

<page-num>٤٦</page-num>

<header>### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...of animals, plants, and inanimate objects, as well as many specific phenomena such as rain, rivers, and the seas—and in general, earth, water, fire, and air. Similarly, **Providence** <term>Providence: *al-‘ināyah*, the theological concept that God specifically designed the universe to be suitable for and supportive of human life</term> is evident in the limbs and organs of the body and the limbs of animals—by which I mean their being perfectly suited to their life and existence. In short, the knowledge of this—namely, the benefits of all existing things—is included in this category. Therefore, it is obligatory for anyone who wishes to know God the Exalted with a complete knowledge to investigate the benefits of existing things.

As for the **Proof from Invention** <term>Invention: *al-ikhtirā‘*, the act of bringing something into existence from nothingness, specifically the creation of life from non-living matter</term>, it includes the existence of all animals, the existence of plants, and the existence of the heavens. This method is built upon two principles that exist potentially in the innate dispositions <note>original: *fiṭrah*, the natural human inclination toward truth</note> of all people.

The first principle is that these existing things are "invented" [created]. This is known by itself regarding animals and plants, as the Exalted says: ﴿ *Indeed, those you invoke besides God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for that purpose* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 22:73</note>. For we see inanimate bodies, then life occurs within them; thus, we know with certainty that there is a **Bringer of Life** here who bestows it, and He is God, Blessed and Exalted. As for the heavens, we know from their ceaseless movements that they are commanded to provide care for what is here [on Earth] and are made subservient to us. That which is subservient and commanded is necessarily "invented" by someone else.

The second principle is that every invented thing must have an **Inventor**. From these two principles, it follows that for all that exists, there is an Agent who invented it. This category contains as many proofs as there are invented things. For this reason, it is obligatory for anyone who desires to know God with true knowledge to understand the essences <note>original: *jawāhir*, or "substances" in a philosophical sense</note> of things, so that he may grasp the true nature of "invention" in all existing things. For whoever does not know the true nature of a thing does not know the true nature of its invention. This is what is alluded to in the words of the Exalted: ﴿ *Have they not looked into the dominion of the heavens and the earth and all the things that God has created?* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 7:185</note>.

Similarly, whoever tracks the meaning of **Wisdom** in the existence of any being—meaning the knowledge of the reason for which it was created and the purpose intended by it—his grasp of the Proof from Providence will be more complete. These two proofs are the two scriptural proofs. As for the verses that draw attention to the evidence leading...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains the two "scriptural" proofs for God's existence: the Proof from Providence (the universe's design for life) and the Proof from Invention (the creation of life and the cosmos), arguing that studying the natural world is a religious obligation.</summary>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s categorization of Qur’anic arguments for the existence of God. Having defined the Proof from Providence and the Proof from Invention, he now demonstrates how the Qur’an utilizes these methods through specific scriptural examples.</meta>

<page-num>٤٧</page-num>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith <-

...to the existence of the **Maker** <term>the Maker: *al-Sāni‘*, a term Ibn Rushd uses to describe God as a divine artisan or craftsman whose wisdom is visible in His "manufactured" creation</term> (glory be to Him) in the **Mighty Book** <note>original: *al-Kitāb al-‘Azīz*, a common honorific for the Qur'an</note>. These proofs are restricted to these two categories of evidence. This is clear to anyone who reflects on the verses contained in the Mighty Book regarding this meaning. For if you examine the verses in the Mighty Book on this subject, you will find them to be of three types: either verses that draw attention to the **Proof from Providence**, verses that draw attention to the **Proof from Invention**, or verses that combine both forms of proof together.

As for the verses that contain the Proof from Providence alone, they are exemplified by the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *Have We not made the earth a resting place, and the mountains as stakes?* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 78:6–7</note>
up to His saying:
> ﴿ *and gardens of thick foliage.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 78:16</note>
And like His saying:
> ﴿ *Blessed is He who has placed in the sky constellations and has placed therein a lamp and a luminous moon.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 25:61</note>
And like the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *Then let man look at his food.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 80:24</note>
This verse and similar examples are numerous in the Qur'an.

As for the verses that contain the Proof from Invention alone, they are exemplified by the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *So let man observe from what he was created. He was created from a fluid, ejected.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 86:5–6</note>
And like the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *Do they not look at the camels—how they were created?* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 88:17</note>
And like the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *O people, an example is presented, so listen to it. Indeed, those you invoke besides God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for that purpose.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 22:73</note>
Of this same type is the saying of the Exalted, narrating the words of **Abraham**:
> ﴿ *Indeed, I have turned my face toward He who originated the heavens and the earth.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 6:79</note>
among other countless verses.

As for the verses that combine both proofs, they are also many—in fact, they are the majority. For example, the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *O mankind, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you...* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 2:21</note>
until His saying:
> ﴿ *...so do not attribute to God equals while you know [the truth].* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 2:22</note>
His statement, "who created you and those before you," is a reminder of the **Proof from Invention**, while His statement:
> ﴿ *[He] who made for you the earth a bed [spread out]...* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 2:22</note>
is a reminder of the **Proof from Providence**. Similar to this is the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *And a sign for them is the dead earth. We have given it life and brought forth from it grain, and from it they eat.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 36:33</note>
And the saying of the Exalted:
> ﴿ *...[Those] who give thought to the creation of the heavens and the earth, [saying], "Our Lord, You did not create this aimlessly..."* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 3:191</note>

<summary>Ibn Rushd categorizes Qur'anic verses into three types: those proving God's existence through the utility of nature (Providence), those proving it through the miracle of creation (Invention), and those that combine both arguments. He provides several scriptural examples to show that these two rational methods are the primary ways the Qur'an invites humanity to recognize the "Maker."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s explanation of the Qur’anic arguments for the existence of God. On the previous page, he categorized these arguments into the Proof from Providence (purposeful design) and the Proof from Invention (creation from nothing). He now concludes his scriptural evidence and explains why this method is suitable for both intellectuals and the general public.</meta>

<page-num>٤٨</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

> ﴿ ... in vain; glory be to You, so protect us from the punishment of the Fire. ﴾ <note>Ending of Qur'an 3:191. The full context describes those who reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth.</note>

Most of the verses mentioned in this context contain both types of evidence. This path is the **Straight Path** to which God has invited people to know His existence, alerting them to this through the perception of this meaning that He has placed within their innate nature. It is to this primal nature, deeply rooted in human character, that the Exalted refers in His saying:

> ﴿ *And [mention] when your Lord took from the children of Adam—from their loins—their descendants* ﴾ <note>Qur'an 7:172</note>

up to His saying:

> ﴿ *They said, "Yes, we testify."* ﴾

For this reason, it is mandatory for anyone whose intent is to obey God through faith in Him and compliance with what His messengers brought, to follow this path. In doing so, they may be among the **scholars** <term>scholars: *al-'ulama'*, here referring to those who possess deep intellectual and spiritual insight into the workings of nature</term> who testify to the Lordship of God the Exalted, alongside His own testimony and the testimony of His angels, as He (Blessed and Exalted be He) said:

> ﴿ *Allah witnesses that there is no deity except Him, and [so do] the angels and those of knowledge, maintaining [creation] in justice. There is no deity except Him, the Exalted in Might, the Wise.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 3:18</note>

Among the evidences for His existence found in these two directions is the **Glorification** <term>Glorification: *al-tasbīh*, the act of all created things declaring God's perfection through their very existence and design</term> referred to in the saying of the Blessed and Exalted:

> ﴿ *And there is not a thing except that it exalts [Allah] by His praise, but you do not understand their exalting.* ﴾ <note>Qur’an 17:44</note>

It has become clear from these proofs for the existence of the **Maker** <term>the Maker: *al-Sāni‘*, God as the ultimate divine craftsman</term> that they are confined to these two categories: the **Proof from Providence** and the **Proof from Invention**. It has also been shown that these two methods are themselves the path of both the **Elite** <term>the Elite: *al-khawāss*, the scholars and philosophers</term> and the **Masses** <term>the Masses: *al-jumhūr*, the general public</term>.

The only difference between these two ways of knowing is in the level of detail. That is to say, the masses limit their understanding of Providence and Invention to what is perceived through initial knowledge based on sensory experience. The scholars, however, add to what is perceived by the senses a further understanding of these things through **demonstration** <term>demonstration: *al-burhān*, a logical proof that provides certain knowledge</term>—namely, of Providence and Invention. Some scholars have even stated that what the learned have discovered regarding the functions of human and animal organs amounts to several thousand benefits. Since this is the case, this method is both the **Legal** <note>original: *al-shar'iyya*, meaning scriptural or religious</note> and the **Natural** method, and it is...

<summary>Ibn Rushd concludes that the Qur'anic proofs for God's existence (Providence and Invention) are universal, appealing to both the general public's common sense and the scholar's scientific demonstrations. He emphasizes that all of creation "glorifies" God through the perfection of its design and function.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that the Qur’anic methods of proving God's existence—through observing providence and creation—are superior to dialectical theology. He now transitions from proving God's existence to proving His absolute Oneness (Tawhid), once again using scriptural evidence that appeals to human reason.</meta>

<page-num>٤٩</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

which the messengers brought and the books revealed. The scholars do not excel the general public in these two modes of inference by mere quantity alone, but rather by the depth of knowledge regarding the single thing itself. For the example of the general public when looking at existing things is like those who look at manufactured objects <term>manufactured objects: *al-masnu'at*, referring to all things in the natural world as "products" of a divine craft</term> without having any knowledge of their craft. They only know about them that they are manufactured and that they have an existing maker. 

The example of the scholars in this regard is like one who looks at manufactured objects while possessing knowledge of some of their craftsmanship and the aspect of wisdom within them. There is no doubt that the one whose state involves such knowledge of manufactured objects knows the maker, in his capacity as a maker, better than the one who knows nothing of those objects except that they are merely made. 

As for the example of the Materialists <term>Materialists: *al-Dahriyya*, a term for those who believe the universe is eternal and has no creator</term> in this regard—those who denied the Maker, Glory be to Him—it is like one who perceives manufactured objects but does not acknowledge that they are manufactured; instead, he attributes the craftsmanship he sees in them to chance and to a matter that occurs by itself.

-> ۞ Discourse on Divine Unity ۞ <-

If it is asked: if this is the scriptural way to know the existence of the Creator, Glory be to Him, then what is the scriptural way to know His Oneness as well—which is the knowledge that "there is no god but He"? For this negation <term>negation: *al-nafi*, the "no god" part of the testimony of faith</term> is a meaning added to the affirmation <term>affirmation: *al-ijab*, the "except Allah" part of the testimony</term> contained in this phrase. The affirmation has already been established in the preceding discourse, so what is sought is the proof of the negation.

We say: As for the negation of divinity from any other than Him, the path of the Law <note>original: *shari'a*, here referring to the revealed method or scriptural path</note> in that is the path stipulated by God the Exalted in His Mighty Book. This is found in three verses. The first is the saying of the Exalted: 

> ۞ *Had there been within the heavens and earth gods besides Allah, they both would have been ruined.* ۞ <note>Qur'an 21:22</note>

The second is the saying of the Exalted: 

> ۞ *Allah has not taken any son, nor has there ever been with Him any deity. [If there had been], then each deity would have taken what it created, and some of them would have sought to overcome others. Exalted is Allah above what they describe.* ۞ <note>Qur'an 23:91</note>

And the third is the saying of the Exalted: 

> ۞ *Say, "If there had been with Him [other] gods, as they say, then...* ۞ <note>The text continues on the next page with Qur'an 17:42: "...they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne."</note>
