--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) systematic exposition of the Oneness of God (Tawhid). Having established that the existence of a Creator can be known through the "evidence of providence" and "evidence of creation," he now utilizes specific Qur'anic verses to demonstrate the logical necessity of God's uniqueness, arguing that the presence of multiple deities would lead to the disintegration of the cosmos.</meta>

<page-num>٥٠</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٠</header>

> "[They would] have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne." <note>original: ﴿لا يبتغوا الى ذي العرش سبيلا﴾; Qur'an 17:42</note>

As for the first verse, its evidence is naturally embedded in our innate dispositions. This is because it is self-evident that if there were two kings, and the actions of each were identical to the actions of the other, it would be impossible for them to manage a single city. A single action cannot proceed from two agents of the same kind. Therefore, it necessarily follows that if they acted together, the single city would fall into ruin—unless one were to act while the other remained idle, and such idleness is incompatible with the attributes of divinity. For whenever two actions of the same type converge upon a single locus <term>locus: *mahall*, the physical or conceptual "place" where an effect occurs</term>, that locus is necessarily corrupted. This is the meaning of His words, the Exalted:

> "Had there been within them gods besides Allah, they both would have been ruined." <note>original: ﴿لو كان فيهما آلهة الا الله لفسدتا﴾; Qur'an 21:22</note>

As for His saying:

> "Then each god would have taken what he created" <note>original: ﴿اذاً لذهب كل اله بما خلق﴾; Qur'an 23:91</note>

This is His refutation of those who posit many gods with differing actions. For it follows that among gods with differing actions—where none is subordinate to another—there could not arise a single, unified existence. Since the world is a single unit, it follows that it cannot exist as the product of multiple gods with distinct actions. As for His words, the Exalted:

> "Say, 'If there had been with Him gods, as they say, then they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.'" <note>original: ﴿قل لو كان معه آلهة كما يقولون اذاً لا بتغوا الى ذي العرش سبيلا﴾; Qur'an 17:42</note>

This is like the first verse; I mean that it is a proof of the impossibility of two gods whose actions are one and the same. The meaning of this verse is that if there were gods in the universe capable of bringing the world into being and creating it, other than the existing God—such that their relationship to this world was that of a creator—it would be necessary for such a god to be upon the Throne with Him. Thus, two identical beings would be attributed to a single locus in a single relationship. However, two identical things cannot relate to a single locus in the same way, because if the relationship is unified, the related subject is unified. I mean, they cannot converge in their relationship to a single locus just as they cannot occupy a single place, if they are of the nature that they reside in a locus. This is true even though the nature of the relationship between God and the Throne is the opposite of this—meaning that the Throne is sustained by Him, not that He is sustained by the Throne. For this reason, God the Exalted said:

> "His Throne extends over the heavens and the earth, and their preservation tires Him not." <note>original: ﴿وسع كرسيه السموات والارض ولا يؤوده حفظهما﴾; Qur'an 2:255. Note: Ibn Rushd uses 'Kursi' (Footstool/Chair) and 'Arsh' (Throne) interchangeably in this context of divine dominion.</note>

This, then, is the evidence which—

<summary>Ibn Rushd uses logical analogies, such as the impossibility of two kings ruling one city, to explain Qur'anic verses that argue for the absolute Oneness of God. He contends that a unified universe proves a single Creator, as multiple deities would lead to cosmic corruption or division.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, Ibn Rushd, Tawhid, Divine Unity, Qur'anic Exegesis, Throne of God, Kalam, Islamic Philosophy, Oneness of God</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of theological proofs for the Oneness of God. Having previously discussed how the unity of the cosmos implies a single Creator, he now contrasts the "natural" and "scriptural" understanding of the scholars and the public against the more abstract, dialectical arguments used by the Ash'arite theologians.</meta>

<page-num>٥١</page-num>
<header>The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...by nature or by scripture in the knowledge of **Oneness**: As for the difference between the **scholars** and the **general public** <term>general public: *al-jumhur*, referring to non-specialist believers who rely on straightforward faith</term> regarding this proof, it is that the scholars understand—through the creation of the world and the way its parts exist for the sake of one another, like a single body—much more than the public understands of that. It is to this meaning that the Exalted refers at the end of the verse:

> "Exalted is He and high above what they say by great sublimity. The seven heavens and the earth and whatever is in them exalt Him, and there is not a thing except that it exalts [Allah] by His praise, but you do not understand their exaltation. Indeed, He is ever Forbearing and Forgiving." <note>original: ﴿سبحانه وتعالى عما يقولون علوا كبيرا تسبح له السموات السبع والارض ومن فيهن وان من شيء الا يسبح بحمده ولكن لا تفقهون تسبيحهم انه كان حليما غفورا﴾; Qur'an 17:43-44</note>

As for the effort the **Ash'arites** <term>Ash'arites: *al-Ash'ariyya*, a dominant school of Islamic theology that Ibn Rushd often critiques for being overly dialectical</term> put into the proof they derive from this verse—which they call the **"Proof of Mutual Exclusion"** <term>Proof of Mutual Exclusion: *dalil al-maman'ah*, a logical argument stating that two gods would inevitably hinder each other's will</term>—it is something that does not follow the course of either natural or scriptural proofs. As for it not being a **natural** proof, it is because what they say regarding it is not a formal demonstration <term>demonstration: *burhan*, a rigorous logical proof</term>. As for it not being a **scriptural** proof, it is because the general public is unable to understand what they say about it, let alone be convinced by it.

This is because they said: if there were two [gods] or more, it would be possible for them to disagree. If they disagreed, the situation would necessarily fall into one of three categories with no fourth: either both their wills are carried out together, or the will of neither is carried out, or the will of one is carried out while the other’s is not. They said: it is impossible for neither of their wills to be carried out, because if that were the case, the world would be neither existing nor non-existing. And it is impossible for both their wills to be carried out together, because then the world would be both existing and non-existing simultaneously. Thus, nothing remains but that the will of one is carried out and the will of the other is nullified. The one whose will was nullified is "incapable," and the "incapable" cannot be a god. 

The point of weakness in this proof is that, just as it is possible for the mind to imagine them disagreeing—by analogy with two willing agents in the visible world—it is also possible for them to agree; and agreement is more fitting for divinity than disagreement. If they agreed on the crafting of the world, they would be like two artisans who agreed on making a single product. If this case is like that one, then it must be said that their actions—even if they agreed—would have to cooperate, since they are directed at a single locus <note>locus: the specific "place" or object upon which an action is performed</note>, unless someone were to say: perhaps this...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite "Proof of Mutual Exclusion," arguing that it is neither logically rigorous for scholars nor simple enough for the public, and suggests that divine agreement is more plausible than disagreement.</summary>
<keywords>Oneness of God (Tawhid), Ash'arites, Proof of Mutual Exclusion, Scholars vs. Masses, Logic, Divine Will, Cooperation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite "Proof of Mutual Exclusion" regarding the oneness of God. He argues that the logical structure used by the theologians (dialectic) differs fundamentally from the natural, intuitive logic found in the Qur’an.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...one would perform some actions and the other would perform others, or perhaps they would act by alternation. However, this kind of skepticism is not suitable for the **general public**. The response to those among the **dialecticians** <term>dialecticians: *al-jadaliyyin*, those who use debate and logical argumentation to defend religious doctrines</term> who raise this doubt is to say: he who is capable of the **origination** <term>origination: *ikhtira'*, the act of bringing something into existence from nothing</term> of a part is capable of the origination of the whole. Thus, the matter returns to the fact that they both have power over everything; they would then either agree or they would disagree. In whatever way it occurred, it would be a cooperation of action.

As for alternation <note>original: *al-tadawul*, referring to the idea of two gods taking turns in their governance</note>, it is a deficiency in the right of each of them. It is more fitting that if there were two gods, there would be two worlds. Since the world is one, the Actor is one, for a single action only proceeds from a single actor. Therefore, one should not understand the words of the Exalted: "and some of them would have risen over others" <note>original: ﴿ولعلا بعضهم على بعض﴾; Qur'an 23:91</note> solely from the perspective of a difference in actions, but also from the perspective of their agreement. For actions that agree cooperate in their occurrence upon a single object, just as different actions cooperate. This is the difference between what we understand from the verse and what the **theologians** <term>theologians: *al-mutakallimun*, specifically the Ash'arite school of speculative theology</term> understand, even though there is an indication of what we have said in the words of **Abu al-Ma'ali** <note>original: *Abi al-Ma'ali*, referring to al-Juwayni (d. 1085), a famous Ash'arite theologian and teacher of al-Ghazali</note>.

It may further indicate to you that the proof the theologians understood from the verse is not the proof the verse actually contains, because the absurdity to which their proof led is different from the absurdity to which the proof mentioned in the verse leads. The absurdity resulting from the proof they claimed to be the verse's proof is more than one absurdity, as they divided the matter into three categories, yet there is no such division in the verse. Their proof, which they employed, is what the **logicians** know as the **disjunctive hypothetical syllogism** <term>disjunctive hypothetical syllogism: *al-qiyas al-sharti al-munfasil*, a logic structure of "either A or B"</term>, and which they know in their own craft as the **proof by enumeration and elimination** <term>proof by enumeration and elimination: *dalil al-sabr wa al-taqsim*, a method of listing all possibilities and disproving all but one</term>.

The proof found in the verse, however, is what is known in the craft of logic as the **connective hypothetical syllogism** <term>connective hypothetical syllogism: *al-sharti al-muttasil*, an "if-then" statement showing a necessary connection</term>, which is different from the disjunctive. Anyone who has looked into that craft even slightly will see the difference between the two proofs. Furthermore, the absurdity to which their proof led is different from the absurdity to which the proof of the **Book** <note>The Qur'an</note> leads. This is because the absurdity their proof led to is that the world would either not...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite theologians for using complex "disjunctive" logic to interpret the Qur'an, arguing instead that the scripture uses a simpler "connective" logic based on the world's inherent unity. He asserts that the idea of multiple gods taking turns or dividing tasks is logically flawed and contradicts the singularity of the cosmos.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, logic, Ash'arites, Oneness of God, Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni, syllogism, Qur'anic interpretation, theology vs philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Ibn Rushd’s discussion on the oneness of God (Tawhid) and transitions into the third chapter regarding the Divine Attributes. He distinguishes between the faith of the "true Muslim" based on rational, Qur'anic proofs and the faith of those who merely follow by name. He then begins to explain the seven essential attributes of God, starting with Knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>٥٣</page-num>
<header>### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith</header>

...[neither] existent nor non-existent, or that it be both existent and non-existent, or that God would be weak and defeated. These are perpetual impossibilities because it is impossible for there to be more than one [God]. The impossibility to which the proof of the Book <note>The "Book" refers to the Qur'an</note> leads is not an impossibility in a perpetual sense; rather, the impossibility there is linked to a specific time—namely, that the world would be found corrupted at the moment of its existence. It is as if He said: 

> "If there were, in the heavens and the earth, gods besides Allah," <note>original: ﴿لو كان فيهما آلهة إلا الله﴾; Qur'an 21:22</note>

the world would have been found corrupted in that instant. Then, He established the exception that it is *not* corrupted, so it is necessary that there is only one God. From this statement, the methods by which the Law <term>Law: *al-Shar'*, referring here to the revealed religious path or Revelation</term> calls people to affirm the existence of the Creator—the Glorified—and to deny divinity to any other have become clear. These are the two meanings contained within the declaration of oneness <term>oneness: *al-Tawhid*, the central monotheistic doctrine of Islam</term>, which is: **There is no god but Allah**. 

Whoever looks into this declaration and believes in these two meanings through the method we have described is the **true Muslim** whose creed is the Islamic creed. As for one whose creed is not built upon these proofs, even if they believe in this declaration, they are a Muslim alongside the true Muslim only by a **sharing of the name** <note>Meaning they are nominally Muslim but lack the deep, rational certainty Ibn Rushd advocates for</note>.

### ۞ The Third Chapter: On the Attributes ۞

As for the descriptions by which the **Mighty Book** <note>The Qur'an</note> has explicitly described the Maker and Originator of the world, they are the attributes of perfection found in humans, and they are seven: **Knowledge, Life, Power, Will, Hearing, Sight, and Speech**. ۞ 

Regarding **Knowledge**, the Mighty Book has drawn attention to the evidence for it in the words of the Exalted: 

> "Does He not know, He who created? And He is the Subtle, the Well-Acquainted." <note>original: ﴿ألا يعلم من خلق وهو اللطيف الخبير﴾; Qur'an 67:14</note>

The nature of this proof is that a manufactured object <term>manufactured object: *al-masnu'*, anything created or designed</term> indicates—by the order found in its parts (meaning that some parts are made for the sake of others) and by the agreement of all parts toward the intended benefit of that object—that it did not arise from a maker that acts by nature alone. Rather, it arose from a maker who arranged the steps preceding the goal before the goal was reached. Therefore, it is necessary that the maker be knowledgeable of it. 

For example, when a person looks at a house, they perceive that the foundation was only made for the sake of the wall...

<summary>Ibn Rushd concludes his proof for God's oneness, arguing that true faith requires understanding the Qur'anic evidence for design. He then introduces the seven Divine Attributes, beginning with Knowledge, which he proves through the teleological order seen in creation.</summary>
<keywords>oneness of God, Tawhid, true Muslim vs nominal Muslim, Divine Attributes, Sifat, Knowledge, teleology, Qur'anic proofs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's examination of the Divine Attributes, specifically focusing on Knowledge (*al-'Ilm*). On the previous page, he established that God must possess knowledge as the Creator of a perfectly ordered universe. Here, he navigates the complex theological debate regarding how God knows temporal events—things that change over time—without His eternal nature being affected by that change.</meta>

<page-num>٥٤</page-num>
<header>### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٤</header>

And just as a wall exists for the sake of the roof, it becomes clear that the house was only brought into existence by one who is knowledgeable in the craft of building. 

This attribute [Knowledge] is an eternal attribute, for it is not permissible for Him—Glory be to Him—to be characterized by it only at a certain time. However, one should not delve too deeply into this, as the theologians <term>theologians: *al-Mutakallimun*, scholars who used dialectical reasoning to defend Islamic creed, often criticized by philosophers for their convoluted proofs</term> do, when they claim that He knows a created event <term>created event: *al-muhdath*, something that begins to exist in time</term> at the time of its occurrence through an eternal knowledge. For this would imply that the knowledge of the event during its state of non-existence and during its state of existence is one and the same knowledge—which is unreasonable, since knowledge must necessarily follow the state of the existent object. 

Since an existent thing sometimes exists in potentiality and sometimes in actuality, the knowledge of these two states of existence must be different, because the time of its existence in potentiality is different from the time of its existence in actuality. This is something the Law <note>The "Law" (*al-Shar'*) refers to the revealed text of the Qur'an and the Prophetic tradition</note> did not state explicitly; rather, it stated the opposite: that He knows created things at the moment of their occurrence, as the Exalted said:

> "And not a leaf falls but He knows it, nor a grain in the darkness of the earth, nor anything moist or dry, but that it is in a clear record." <note>original: ﴿وما تسقط من ورقة الا يعلمها...﴾; Qur'an 6:59</note>

Therefore, it should be established in the Law that He knows a thing before it exists—knowing that it *will* be; and He knows a thing when it exists—knowing that it *is*; and He knows what has perished—knowing that it has perished at the time of its perishing. This is what the principles of the Law require. 

This is so because the common people <term>common people: *al-jumhur*, the general public who understand religion through its literal and apparent meanings</term> do not understand the meaning of "one who knows" in the observed world <term>observed world: *al-shahid*, the physical, sensory world we live in</term> except in this way. The theologians have no proof necessitating that it be otherwise, except their claim that knowledge which changes with the change of existent things is itself "created," and that the Creator—Glory be to Him—cannot be the locus of created events. This is because they claim that whatever is inseparable from created events must itself be created. We have already demonstrated the falsehood of this premise.

Thus, it is necessary to affirm this principle as it has been revealed, and it should not be said that He knows the occurrence of events and the corruption of things neither by a created knowledge nor by an eternal knowledge, for such talk is an innovation <term>innovation: *bid'ah*, a theological or religious practice not established by the Prophet or the early community</term> in Islam. "And your Lord is never forgetful." <note>Qur'an 19:64</note>

As for the attribute of **Life**, its existence is evident from the attribute of Knowledge. This is because it is apparent in the observed world that Life is a prerequisite for Knowledge. According to the theologians, a condition must have its judgment transferred from the observed world to the unseen <term>unseen: *al-gha'ib*, the divine or metaphysical realm beyond human perception</term>. Regarding what they have said about this...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that God's knowledge must correspond to the changing states of things (past, present, and future) as described in the Qur'an, rejecting the abstract theological claim that God's knowledge is static. He also begins to link the attribute of Knowledge to the attribute of Life.</summary>

<keywords>Attributes of God, Divine Knowledge, al-Mutakallimun, Actuality vs Potentiality, Attribute of Life, Observed vs Unseen, bid'ah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's systematic refutation of the Ash'arite theologians regarding the Divine Attributes. Having previously discussed Knowledge, he now addresses Will (al-Iradah) and Speech (al-Kalam). He argues against the concept of an "eternal will" for temporal objects, which he finds illogical, and provides a philosophical definition of Divine Speech as a communicative act of the Creator.</meta>

### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٥

...correct. As for the attribute of **Will** <term>Will: *al-Iradah*, the capacity of an agent to intend a specific action at a specific time</term>, His being characterized by it is evident, since it is a necessary condition for a thing to proceed from a knowledgeable agent that the agent be willing it, just as it is a condition that they be powerful. 

However, to claim that He wills temporal events <term>temporal events: *al-umur al-muhdatha*, things that come into being within time</term> through an eternal will is an innovation <note>original: *bid'ah*; a theological novelty not supported by the original religious texts</note> and something that the scholars do not find rational, nor does it satisfy the masses—specifically those who have reached the level of dialectic <note>Ibn Rushd categorizes people into three levels: the masses (rhetorical), the theologians (dialectical), and the philosophers (demonstrative)</note>. Rather, it should be said that He wills a thing's existence at the time of its actual occurrence, and does not will its existence at a time when it does not exist, as the Almighty said: **“Our only word to a thing when We intend it is to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is”** <note>Qur'an 16:40</note>. 

For among the masses, as we have said, there is nothing that compels them to say that He wills created things through an eternal will, except for the misconception of the theologians <term>theologians: *al-Mutakallimun*</term> that anything which acts as a locus for temporal events must itself be temporal.

If it is asked: from where is the attribute of **Speech** proven for Him? We say: it is proven for Him by the existence of the attribute of Knowledge in Him and the attribute of the Power of Invention <term>Invention: *al-Ikhtira'*, the divine act of bringing things into existence from nothing</term>. Speech is nothing more than a speaker performing an act by which they signify to an addressee the knowledge within themselves, or that the addressee is placed in a state where that knowledge within the speaker is revealed to them. This is an act among the many acts of an Agent. If a creature who is not a true agent—namely man—is capable of this act by virtue of being knowledgeable and powerful, then how much more so is it necessary for the **True Agent**? 

This act has another condition in the visible world <note>original: *al-shahid*; the empirical world known to humans, used here as a logical bridge to understand the divine</note>, which is that it must be through a medium, which is the spoken word. If this is the case, it is necessary that this act from God the Almighty occurs in the souls of those of His servants whom He has chosen through some medium. 

However, it does not necessarily have to be a spoken word, nor is it created [within His essence]; rather, it may be through the medium of an angel, or it may be through **inspiration** <term>inspiration: *wahyan*, direct revelation or communication to the heart without external sound</term>—meaning without the medium of a word that He creates; rather, He performs an act in the listener through which that meaning is revealed to them. It may also be through the medium of a word that God creates in the hearing of the one specifically chosen for His speech—Glory be to Him. To these three modes, reference is made in His—the Almighty’s—saying:

<vocab>Will (*al-Iradah*), temporal/created events (*al-muhdatha*), theologians (*al-mutakallimun*), the attribute of speech (*sifat al-kalam*), invention/origination (*al-ikhtira’*), the True Agent (*al-fa‘il al-haqiqi*), the spoken word (*al-lafz*), inspiration/revelation (*wahyan*)</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that Divine Will is linked to the timing of a thing's existence and defines Divine Speech as a creative act that reveals God's knowledge to His chosen servants through various modes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Divine Attribute of Speech (al-Kalam). Ibn Rushd is moving from the general definition of speech to the specific modes through which God communicates with humanity, as outlined in the Qur'an, while navigating the theological dispute between the Ash’arites and other schools regarding whether the Qur'an is "created" or "eternal."</meta>

### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٦

**“It is not for any human being that God should speak to him except by revelation, or from behind a veil, or that He sends a messenger to reveal, by His permission, what He wills.”** <note>Qur'an 42:51</note> **Revelation** <term>Revelation: *Wahy*; here meaning a direct, non-verbal transmission of meaning into the heart</term> is the occurrence of a meaning in the soul of the one receiving it without the mediation of created words; rather, it happens through the uncovering of that meaning by an act God performs within the soul of the person addressed, as the Almighty said: **“And he was at a distance of two bow-lengths or even nearer. So He revealed to His servant what He revealed.”** <note>Qur'an 53:9-10</note> 

**“From behind a veil”** refers to speech that occurs through the mediation of words that God creates in the soul of the person He has chosen for His speech. This is "true speech" <note>original: *kalam haqiqi*</note>, and it is what God specifically granted to Moses; hence the Almighty said: **“And to Moses God spoke directly.”** <note>Qur'an 4:164</note> 

As for His saying, **“or He sends a messenger,”** this is the third category, which occurs through the mediation of an Angel. It may also be considered part of God's speech when it is conveyed by the **scholars**, who are the "heirs of the Prophets," by means of **demonstrative proofs** <term>demonstrative proofs: *al-barahin*; logical or philosophical certainties that Ibn Rushd values as the highest form of knowledge</term>. From this perspective, it is correct for scholars to say that the Qur'an is the speech of God. 

It has thus become clear to you that the Qur'an, which is the speech of God, is **eternal** <term>eternal: *qadim*; existing without a beginning</term>, while the spoken words that indicate it are **created** <term>created: *makhluq*; brought into existence in time</term> by Him—Glory be to Him—and not by any human. In this way, the words of the Qur'an differ from the words we speak in other contexts; I mean that our usual words are our own actions performed by God's permission, whereas the words of the Qur'an are God's direct creation. Whoever does not understand this distinction has failed to grasp this concept and cannot understand how it is said that the Qur'an is the speech of God. 

As for the letters in the physical **Codex** <term>Codex: *al-Mushaf*; the physical book or written copy of the Qur'an</term>, they are of our own making by God's permission. They deserve veneration only because they point toward the words created by God and toward the meaning which is not created. 

Whoever looks only at the words without the meaning—that is, whoever fails to distinguish the two—will say the Qur'an is created. Whoever looks at the meaning indicated by the words will say it is uncreated. The truth lies in combining the two views. 

The **Ash’arites** <note>The Ash’arites were the dominant school of speculative theology (*Kalam*) during Ibn Rushd's time; he often critiques them for using dialectical arguments rather than strict philosophical demonstrations</note> have denied that a "speaker" is the "actor" <note>original: *fa'il*; the agent or doer</note> of the speech. They imagined that if they accepted this principle, they would have to admit that God is the "actor" of His own speech. Since they believed that a speaker is one in whom speech subsists as an internal attribute, they thought these two principles combined would require God to be an actor...

<summary>Ibn Rushd categorizes three modes of Divine revelation and explains the ontological status of the Qur'an as having an eternal meaning but being expressed through words created by God, rather than by humans.</summary>
<keywords>revelation, Wahy, Moses, Ash'arites, created vs. eternal, demonstrative proofs, Divine Speech, Codex</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into Divine Speech, specifically addressing the conflict between the Ash’arite and Mu’tazilite schools of theology. Ibn Rushd transitions from this debate to the attributes of Hearing and Sight, arguing for their logical and scriptural necessity.</meta>

### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٧
->(٨)<-

...to speech through His essence, such that His essence would be a locus for temporal events <note>original: *hawadith*; things that have a beginning in time. Traditional theologians argued God's essence cannot change or contain new events.</note>. Thus, they said that the Speaker is not a "performer" of speech, but rather that it is an eternal attribute of His essence, like Knowledge and other such attributes. This holds true for **internal speech** <term>internal speech: *kalam al-nafs*; the silent meaning or thought existing in the mind before it is spoken</term>, but it is false when applied to the speech that indicates what is in the soul, which is **articulated speech** <term>articulated speech: *al-lafz*; the physical sounds or written words</term>.

The Mu’tazilites, because they believed that speech is simply what the speaker performs, said that speech is only the articulated word. Because of this, they claimed that the **Qur’an is created** <note>The "Createdness of the Qur'an" was a major political and theological crisis in the 9th century; the Mu'tazilites argued that if the Qur'an is God's speech, it must be an act He created in time.</note>. To them, articulated speech—insofar as it is an action—does not necessarily have to reside within the one who performs it.

The Ash’arites maintain that it is a condition [of being a speaker] that the speech resides within the Speaker. This is correct in the "witnessed world" <note>original: *al-shahid*; the realm of human experience used as an analogy for the divine</note> for both types of speech—I mean internal speech and the articulated words that indicate it. However, regarding the Creator, internal speech is what resides in Him, whereas the words that indicate it <note>the physical Qur'an</note> do not reside within Him, Glory be to Him. 

Thus, because the Ash’arites required that speech *in its absolute sense* reside within the speaker, they denied that the Speaker is a "performer" of speech in an absolute sense. Conversely, because the Mu’tazilites required that the Speaker be a "performer" of speech in an absolute sense, they denied the existence of internal speech. In the position of each of these two sects, there is a portion of truth and a portion of falsehood, as should be clear to you from our discussion.

As for the attributes of **Hearing** and **Sight**, the Law <note>original: *al-shar'*; meaning the revealed religious texts and traditions</note> established them for God, Blessed and Exalted, because Hearing and Sight are specific to perceived meanings in existing things that the intellect alone cannot grasp. Since it is a condition for any Maker that He must perceive everything within the thing He has made, it is necessary that He possesses these two forms of perception. It is therefore necessary that He be knowledgeable of things perceived by sight and knowledgeable of things perceived by hearing, since these are His handiwork. 

All of these [arguments] serve as reminders in the Law of their existence in the Creator, Glory be to Him, by way of reminding us of His possession of Knowledge. In general, what is signified by the name "God" and the name "the Object of Worship" requires that He be perceptive through all forms of perception, for it would be futile for a person to worship one who does not perceive that he is worshipping Him, as the Almighty said:

> ۞ **“O my father, why do you worship that which does not hear...”** <note>Qur'an 19:42, referring to the Prophet Abraham's argument against idolatry.</note>

<summary>Ibn Rushd mediates the dispute between the Ash’arites and Mu’tazilites regarding God's speech by distinguishing between internal thought and external words. He also argues that God must possess Hearing and Sight to truly be a Creator and a worthy object of worship.</summary>
<keywords>The Speaker, Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, Qur'an, internal speech, articulated speech, Divine Attributes, Hearing, Sight</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the dialectical theologians (the Mutakallimun) regarding the nature of God's attributes. Having established that the masses should be taught what the Scripture explicitly states, he now moves to the philosophical and theological disputes regarding whether attributes like Knowledge and Life are identical to God's Essence or distinct from it.</meta>

### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٨

...neither sees nor hears nor avails you in anything?” <note>Quran 19:42, where Abraham addresses his father regarding idols.</note> And the Exalted said:
> “Do you then worship besides God that which does not benefit you at all nor harm you?” <note>Quran 21:66.</note>
This degree of description of God—Glory be to Him—and the names applied to Him is the extent which the Law <note>original: *al-shar’*; referring to the revealed religious path or Shari'a</note> explicitly stated for the general public to know, and nothing beyond that.

Among the innovations <note>original: *bid’a*; in a theological context, this refers to unauthorized or harmful additions to religious doctrine</note> that have arisen in this chapter is the questioning of these attributes: Are they the Essence itself, or are they additional to the Essence? That is: is the attribute **essential** or **qualitative**? By **essential** <term>essential attribute: *sifa nafsiyya*; a quality that describes the essence itself without implying an additional meaning, like saying God is "One"</term>, I mean that by which the Essence is described for itself, not because of a meaning residing in it that is additional to the Essence—such as our saying "One" or "Eternal." By **qualitative** <term>qualitative attribute: *sifa ma’nawiyya*; an attribute that implies a specific "meaning" or "concept" (like Knowledge) that exists within the subject</term>, I mean that which the Essence is described by due to a meaning residing within it. 

The Ash’arites <note>A dominant school of Sunni theology that emphasized God's attributes as being distinct from His essence yet eternal.</note> say that these attributes are qualitative and are additional to the Essence. Thus, they say that He is "Knowing" through a "Knowledge" that is additional to His Essence, and "Living" through a "Life" additional to His Essence, just as is the case in the witnessed world <note>original: *al-shahid*; the observable human world used as an analogy for the divine</note>. This view, however, forces them to conclude that the Creator is a **body**, because there would then be an "attribute" and a "subject," or a "carrier" and a "carried"—and this is the condition of a body. This is because they must either say that the Essence is self-subsisting and the attributes reside in it, or they must say that each one of them is self-subsisting. If the latter, then there would be many gods. This is the view of the Christians, who claimed that the **Hypostases** <term>Hypostases: *al-aqanim*; the three persons of the Christian Trinity (Father, Son, Holy Spirit), which Ibn Rushd identifies here as Existence, Life, and Knowledge</term> are three. Regarding this, the Exalted said:
> “They have certainly disbelieved who say, ‘God is the third of three.’” <note>Quran 5:73.</note>

If they say that one of them is self-subsisting while the other resides in the one that is self-subsisting, they have necessitated that God be a **substance** and an **accident**. This is because a **substance** <term>substance: *jawhar*; that which exists by itself</term> is that which is self-subsisting, and an **accident** <term>accident: *‘arad*; a quality that cannot exist on its own but must reside in a substance (like color in a chair)</term> is that which exists through another. Anything composed of a substance and an accident is necessarily a **body**.

Similarly, the reply of the Mu’tazilites <note>A school of theology known for its emphasis on reason and "Divine Unity," which led them to deny that attributes were distinct from God's Essence.</note>—that the Essence and the attributes are one and the same thing—is a notion far removed from primary knowledge; in fact, it is thought to contradict it. This is because it is thought to be part of primary knowledge that "Knowledge" must be something other than the "Knower." It is not permissible for "Knowledge" to be the "Knower" unless it were permissible for one of two relatives to be the same as its counterpart—for example, if "Father" and "Son" were the same meaning.

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques both the Ash’arite and Mu’tazilite views on Divine attributes, arguing that the Ash’arite view leads to a belief in a composite (corporeal) God or a Trinity, while the Mu’tazilite view contradicts basic human intuition.</summary>

<keywords>Divine attributes, Ash'arites, Mu'tazilites, Hypostases, Trinity, Substance and Accident, Islamic Theology, Ibn Rushd, Rationalism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God's attributes, transitioning from the Ash'arite perspective (implied by the end of the previous page) to Ibn Rushd's critique of the Mu'tazilites and the Christians. He argues that technical theological disputes about whether attributes are identical to or distinct from the Essence are unsuitable for the general public.</meta>

### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٥٩

...one and the same: This is a teaching far from the understanding of the general public <note>original: *jamhur*; referring to the masses of believers who are not trained in philosophy</note>, and to state it explicitly is an innovation <note>original: *bid’a*; a technical term for unauthorized or harmful additions to religious doctrine</note>. Indeed, it is more likely to lead the public astray than to guide them. The Mu'tazilites <term>Mu’tazilites: a rationalist school of Islamic theology that emphasized the absolute unity of God, arguing that His attributes are identical to His essence</term> have no proof for the necessity of this regarding the Primal One—Exalted is He—just as neither they nor the dialectical theologians <term>dialectical theologians: *al-mutakallimun*; scholars who used rational, often defensive, arguments to uphold religious dogmas</term> have a proof for the denial of His corporeality. This is because their denial of His corporeality is based on the necessity of the created nature of a "body" as a body, and we have already explained at the beginning of this book that they possess no proof for that; rather, those who possess the proof for it are the scholars <note>original: *al-’ulama’*; for Ibn Rushd, this specifically refers to philosophers who use rigorous logic/demonstration</note>.

It is from this starting point that the Christians slipped. This is because they believed in a multiplicity of attributes and believed them to be substances <term>substances: *jawahir*; in this context, things that exist independently rather than as qualities of something else</term> that do not subsist in another, but rather subsist in themselves like the Essence. They believed that the attributes of this nature are two: Knowledge and Life. They said: God is one in one respect and three in another respect. They mean that He is three in the sense that He is Existing, Living, and Knowing, and He is one in the sense that the sum of these is a single thing.

So, here we have three schools of thought:
1. The school of those who view them as the Essence itself, with no multiplicity there.
2. The school of those who view them as a multiplicity, and these are divided into two:
    - Those who made the multiplicity self-subsisting <note>Referring to the Christian doctrine of the Trinity</note>.
    - Those who made the multiplicity subsisting in another <note>Referring to the Ash'arite theologians who argue attributes exist *in* the Essence but are additional to it</note>.

All of this is far from the purpose of the Law <note>original: *maqsad al-shar’*; the intended goal of religious scripture and revelation</note>. If this is the case, then what the general public should be taught regarding these attributes is only what the Law has explicitly stated: acknowledging their existence without detailing the matter. Such detail is impossible to provide the general public with any certainty at all. By "general public" here, I mean everyone who is not occupied with the demonstrative arts <term>demonstrative arts: *al-sana’i’ al-burhaniyya*; the highest level of philosophical reasoning that leads to certain, indisputable truth</term>, regardless of whether they have acquired the art of dialectical theology <note>original: *sina'at al-kalam*</note> or not. For the art of dialectical theology does not have the power to reach this level of knowledge, as the highest rank of the art of theology is to be dialectical wisdom, not demonstrative. The art of dialectic <term>dialectic: *al-jadal*; reasoning based on generally accepted opinions or premises, rather than certain truths</term> does not have the power to grasp the truth in this matter. From this discussion, the extent of knowledge that has been explicitly stated to the public regarding this subject has become clear, as have the methods that...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Mu'tazilites and Christians for their complex theories on God's attributes, arguing that such technical debates confuse the public. He asserts that only philosophical demonstration, not dialectical theology, can truly resolve these issues.</summary>

## Chapter Four: On the Knowledge of Divine Transcendence

Now that these methods have been established—which the Religious Law <term>Religious Law: *al-shar’*; referring to the revealed guidance of the Quran and Sunnah</term> has followed in teaching people, first regarding the existence of the Creator, Glory be to Him; second, the methods it followed in negating any partner with Him; and third, the methods it followed in knowing His attributes and the extent explicitly stated regarding each category—we see that any portion added to this, subtracted from it, distorted, or interpreted <note>original: *ta’wil*; the practice of allegorical or metaphorical interpretation of scripture</note> does not result in the common happiness intended for everyone. It remains for us to also know the methods by which He guided people in the transcendence <term>transcendence: *tanzih*; the theological concept that God is completely distinct from and superior to His creation</term> of the Creator, Glory be to Him, from all deficiencies, the extent to which He stated this explicitly, and the reason why He restricted them to that specific amount. We shall then mention after that the methods He used to lead people toward knowing His actions and the extent to which He guided them in that. Once this is completed, we will have fulfilled our intended purpose.

We say: As for the knowledge of this category, which is transcendence and sanctification <note>original: *al-taqdis*</note>, it has been explicitly stated in several verses of the Mighty Book. The clearest and most complete of these is the saying of the Exalted: "There is nothing like unto Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing" <note>Quran 42:11</note>. His saying, "Is He then who creates like him who creates not?" <note>Quran 16:17</note> serves as the proof for the statement "There is nothing like unto Him."

This is because it is innate in the nature of all <term>innate in the nature: *maghruz fi fitrah*; the concept of an inherent, God-given human intuition</term> that the Creator must be either characterized by qualities unlike those who do not create, or by qualities that do not resemble the qualities of those who do not create; otherwise, the one who creates would not be a Creator. If we add to this principle the fact that the created being is not a Creator, it follows that the attributes of the created must either be absent in the Creator, or present in the Creator in a manner different from how they exist in the created.

We only say "in a different manner" because among the attributes found in the Creator are those whose existence we have inferred through the attributes found in the noblest of created beings here—which is man—such as the affirmation of Knowledge, Life, Power, Will, and so on. This is the meaning of the Prophet's saying (peace and blessings be upon him): "Indeed...

<meta>catchword: Indeed (Ar: *Inna*)</meta>

<summary>Ibn Rushd introduces the concept of Divine Transcendence (tanzih), arguing that while God shares certain names of attributes with humans (like Knowledge), they exist in Him in a fundamentally different way that preserves His unique status as Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Transcendence, Tanzih, Sanctification, Attributes of God, Creation, Fitrah, Quranic interpretation</keywords>

-> # The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٦١ <-

<insert>“God created Adam in His image”</insert> <note>original: *Allahu khalaqa Adama ‘ala suratihi*; a famous and controversial Prophetic tradition (hadith) that Ibn Rushd is contextualizing within the concept of transcendence.</note> Once it is established that the Religious Law has explicitly stated the negation of any similarity between the Creator and the created, and has provided the proofs necessitating this, we must understand that the "negation of similarity" implies two things:

**First:** That the Creator is devoid of many attributes found in the created.
**Second:** That attributes found in the created exist in Him in a manner that is infinitely more perfect and excellent than can be grasped by the intellect.

One should consider what the Religious Law has explicitly stated regarding these two categories, what it has remained silent about, and the wise reason for its silence. We say: As for what the Law has explicitly stated regarding the negation of created attributes from God, it includes everything that is clearly an **attribute of deficiency**. 

Among these is **death**, as the Blessed and Exalted said:
> And rely upon the Living who does not die.

Another is **sleep**, and anything less than that which implies heedlessness or a lapse in perception and the preservation of all that exists. This is explicitly stated in His words, the Exalted:
> ﴿Neither drowsiness nor sleep overtakes Him.﴾

Another is **forgetfulness and error**, as the Exalted said:
> ﴿The knowledge of it is with my Lord in a Record. My Lord neither errs nor forgets.﴾

Attaining the realization that these deficiencies are negated is nearly a matter of <term>necessary knowledge: *al-‘ilm al-daruri*; knowledge that is self-evident and requires no complex reasoning</term>. Thus, the Religious Law explicitly negates those things which are closest to being self-evident truths. As for matters that are further removed from these primary, self-evident recognitions, the Law draws attention to them by noting that they are known only to a minority of people, as the Exalted says in several verses of the Book: “But most people do not know.” For example, His words, the Exalted:
> ﴿The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most of the people do not know.﴾

And His words, the Exalted:
> ﴿[Adhere to] the fitra of Allah upon which He has created mankind. No change should there be in the creation of Allah. That is the correct religion, but most of the people do not know.﴾ <term>fitra: the innate, natural disposition or original human nature that recognizes the Creator</term>

If it is asked: "What is the evidence for negating these deficiencies from Him?"—meaning the scriptural evidence—we say: The evidence for it is what is observed in that all existing things are preserved without any disorder or corruption occurring within them. If the Creator were overtaken by heedlessness, error, forgetfulness, or slumber, the existing world would fall into disorder. God the Exalted has drawn attention to this meaning in several verses of His Book, saying:

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Religious Law negates human-like deficiencies (death, sleep, forgetfulness) from God because His perfect preservation of the universe proves He is free from such flaws. He distinguishes between self-evident divine attributes and those requiring deeper reflection, which "most people" overlook.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's examination of divine attributes. Having previously discussed attributes of deficiency (like death or sleep) which the Law explicitly negates, he now moves to the complex issue of "corporeality" (bodily nature)—an attribute that sparked intense debate among medieval theologians.</meta>

-> # The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith ٦٢ <-

> ﴿Indeed, God holds the heavens and the earth, lest they cease. And if they should cease, no one could hold them [in place] after Him.﴾ <note>Quran 35:41</note>

The verse continues, and the Exalted also said:

> ﴿And their preservation tires Him not. And He is the Most High, the Most Great.﴾ <note>Quran 2:255; the "Throne Verse" (Ayat al-Kursi).</note>

If it is asked: What do you say regarding the attribute of **corporeality**? <term>corporeality: the quality of being a physical body (jismiyya)</term> Is it one of the attributes that the Religious Law has explicitly negated regarding the Creator, or is it among those things about which the Law has remained silent?

We say: It is evident from the nature of the Religious Law that it is one of the attributes about which it has remained silent. In fact, the Law is closer to explicitly affirming it than to negating it. This is because the Law has explicitly mentioned the "Face" and the "Hands" in more than one verse of the Mighty Book. These verses might suggest that **corporeality** is one of the attributes in which the Creator excels the created, just as He excels the created in the attributes of **Power**, **Will**, and other attributes shared between the Creator and the created—except that in the Creator, they exist in a more perfect state of being. Because of this, many among the people of Islam came to believe that the Creator is a "body unlike other bodies." This is the position of the **Hanbalites** <note>Followers of the school of Ahmad ibn Hanbal, known for a literalist approach to scripture.</note> and many who followed them.

In my view, the obligatory path regarding this attribute is to follow the method of the Religious Law: one should not explicitly state either a negation or an affirmation. If any of the general public asks about this, the answer should be the words of the Exalted:

> ﴿Nothing is like Him, and He is the Hearing, the Seeing.﴾ <note>Quran 42:11; a foundational verse used to establish God's absolute uniqueness (tanzih).</note>

One should forbid such questions for three reasons. **First:** Perceiving the truth of this matter is not something easily known by itself; it is not one, two, or even three steps away from common knowledge. You can see this from the path taken by the **theologians** <term>theologians: the Mutakallimun, who used dialectic and logic to defend religious doctrines</term> on this subject. They argued: "The proof that He is not a body is that it has been demonstrated that every body is **created** <term>created: or "originated" (muhdath); having a beginning in time</term>." When they are asked how one knows that every body is created, they follow the path we mentioned previously—the argument regarding the "origination of accidents" and the idea that whatever is not free from temporal events must itself be temporal.

As we have already explained, this path is not **demonstrative** <term>demonstrative: a proof that provides absolute logical or mathematical certainty (burhan)</term>. Even if it were demonstrative, the nature of the majority of the public would not allow them to reach such a conclusion. Furthermore, that which He describes...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Religious Law remains silent on whether God has a "body," suggesting that instead of complex theological debates, the public should be directed toward the Quranic principle that "nothing is like Him." He criticizes the logical proofs used by theologians to deny God's corporeality, calling them non-demonstrative and too complex for common understanding.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's argument regarding why the Religious Law (Sharia) remains silent on the "corporeality" (jismiyya) of God. -> # The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith 63 <-

[The arguments of] these people—that He, the Exalted, is an essence with attributes added to that essence—actually require Him to be a **body** more than their denial of His corporeality (based on the argument that He is not "originated") negates it. This is the first reason why the Religious Law did not explicitly state that He is not a body.

As for the second reason, it is that the masses believe that what exists is what can be imagined and perceived by the senses, and that whatever is neither imaginable nor sensible is non-existent. Thus, if it were said to them that there is an existent being that is not a **body**, their ability to imagine Him would vanish, and He would become, in their view, something akin to non-existence. This is especially true if it is said that He is neither outside the world nor inside it, neither above nor below. For this reason, the group that affirmed **corporeality** believed that the group denying it was essentially negating God's existence <note>This refers to the "Mu'attila," those who "strip" God of attributes to the point of perceived atheism.</note>, while those who denied it believed the affirmers were "multipliers" <note>Meaning they compromised God's unity by suggesting He is composed of parts like a body.</note>.

As for the third reason, it is that if the negation of **corporeality** were explicitly stated, it would cause many doubts regarding the Religious Law—specifically concerning what is said about the **Resurrection** <term>Resurrection: Al-Ma'ad, the return of souls and bodies in the afterlife</term> and other matters. Among these are the doubts that arise regarding the **Vision** <term>Vision: Al-Ru'ya, the "Beatific Vision" or the ability for believers to see God in the afterlife</term> which has been established by the sound Prophetic tradition (Sunnah). 

Those who explicitly denied His corporeality split into two factions: the **Mu’tazilites** and the **Ash’arites**. <note>The Mu'tazilites were rationalist theologians; the Ash'arites were the dominant school of Sunni theology that sought a middle ground.</note> As for the **Mu’tazilites**, this belief [the denial of a body] led them to deny the **Vision** entirely. As for the **Ash’arites**, they wanted to combine the two matters <note>Denying God has a body while still affirming that He can be seen.</note>, but that proved difficult for them. In their attempt to reconcile the two, they resorted to sophistical statements <note>Ibn Rushd, a philosopher-jurist, often criticized the Ash'arites for using circular or illogical arguments to defend dogma.</note> whose weakness we shall point out when discussing the **Vision**.

Furthermore, [denying corporeality] necessitates—at first glance—the negation of "Direction" <term>Direction: Jiha, the spatial location of God, specifically His being "above" the world</term> regarding the Creator, the Exalted, as if He were not a body. This would turn the Religious Law into something ambiguous. This is because the sending of the Prophets is built upon the foundation that **Revelation** descends to them from the heavens. Our own Law is built upon this; namely, that the Mighty Book descended from the heavens, as the Exalted said: 

> ﴿Indeed, We sent it down on a blessed night.﴾ <note>original: "إنا أنزلناه في ليلة مباركة" — Quran 44:3</note>

The descent of **Revelation** from the heavens implies that God is in the heavens. Likewise, the fact that the **Angels** descend from the heavens and ascend to them, as the Exalted said: 

> ﴿To Him ascends the good word, and the righteous work He raises it.﴾ <note>original: "إليه يصعد الكلم الطيب والعمل الصالح" — Quran 35:10</note>

And the Exalted said: 

> ﴿The angels and the Spirit ascend to Him.﴾ <note>original: "تعرج الملائكة والروح إليه" — Quran 70:4</note>

In short...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that the Religious Law does not deny God's corporeality because doing so would confuse the masses, potentially lead to the denial of God's existence, and contradict scriptural descriptions of Revelation descending from "above." He critiques the Ash'arites and Mu'tazilites for their inconsistent handling of how a non-physical God can be "seen" or localized.</summary>

## The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith 64

...all the things that necessarily follow for those who advocate for the negation of **direction** <note>original: "nafi al-jiha" — the theological position that God is not located in a specific direction or place, such as "above."</note>, as we shall mention later when discussing **direction**. 

Among these consequences is that if one explicitly denies **corporeality**, they must also explicitly deny **movement**. If they deny movement, it becomes difficult to explain the descriptions given regarding the **Gathering** <term>The Gathering: Al-Hashr, the assembly of all souls on the Day of Judgment.</term>—specifically that the Creator appears to the people of the Gathering and that He is the one who takes charge of their reckoning, as the Almighty said:

> ﴿ And your Lord comes and the angels, rank upon rank ﴾ <note>Quran 89:22. Ibn Rushd argues that "coming" implies movement, which theologians who deny God's body find difficult to explain literally.</note>

This also makes it difficult to interpret the famous "Hadith of Descent" <note>A well-known prophetic tradition stating that God descends to the lowest heaven during the last third of the night.</note>, even though an allegorical interpretation is closer to it than it is to the matter of the Resurrection. Since the reports concerning the Resurrection are widely transmitted <term>Widely transmitted: Mutawatir, a report passed down by so many people that it is considered certain truth.</term> in the Religious Law, it is mandatory not to explicitly state to the masses anything that would lead them to invalidate these literal meanings. 

The impact of these descriptions on the souls of the masses only occurs when they are taken at their **face value**. If they are interpreted allegorically, the matter leads to one of two outcomes: 
1. Either allegorical interpretation is unleashed upon these and similar passages in the Law, causing the entire Law to be torn apart and the intended wisdom behind it to be lost. 
2. Or it is said of all these passages that they are "ambiguous" <term>Ambiguous: Al-Mutashabihat, verses whose ultimate meaning is known only to God or the deeply learned.</term>. 

Both of these options result in the invalidation of the Law and its removal from people's hearts, without the person responsible realizing the magnitude of the crime they have committed against the Law. 

Furthermore, when you consider the arguments used by those who advocate for these interpretations, you will find they are all non-demonstrative <note>original: "ghayr burhaniyya" — meaning they do not meet the strict standards of logical proof or Aristotelian demonstration.</note>. Rather, the literal meanings of the Law are more persuasive—by which I mean they produce a stronger conviction. You will see this clearly from our discussion regarding the proof upon which they built the negation of **corporeality**, as well as the proof for the negation of **direction**, as we shall explain later.

What may further indicate to you that the Law did not intend to explicitly deny this attribute to the masses is that, because this attribute—meaning **corporeality**—is also absent from the **Soul**, the Law did not explicitly define for the masses what the **Soul** is. He said in the Mighty Book:

> ﴿ And they ask you about the soul. Say: The soul is of the command of my Lord, and of knowledge you have been given but little. ﴾ <note>Quran 17:85</note>

This is because it is difficult for the masses to grasp a logical proof for the existence of an entity that is self-subsisting but is not a **body**. If the absence of this attribute were something that could be understood...

He points out that the Law even remains silent on the nature of the human soul for the same reason: common people cannot easily conceive of non-physical existence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that the Religious Law (Sharia) uses language accessible to the common person rather than the abstract, technical language of theologians. # The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Faith 65
( 9 )

The general public would not be satisfied with that [abstract reasoning]. Abraham <note>original: "al-Khalil" — The Friend of God, a title for the Prophet Abraham.</note>—peace be upon him—in his debate with the disbeliever, said:
> ﴿ My Lord is He who gives life and causes death. [The disbeliever] said: I [too] give life and cause death... ﴾ <note>Quran 2:258. This refers to Abraham's debate with King Nimrod.</note>

This was because it would not have been enough to simply say to him, "You are a physical body and God is not a body," on the basis that "every body is created," as the **Ash'arites** <term>Ash'arites: A dominant school of Islamic theology that used rationalist arguments to interpret scripture, often denying that God has physical attributes.</term> claim. Similarly, Moses—peace be upon him—would have found such an argument insufficient when debating Pharaoh regarding his claim to divinity.

Likewise, the Prophet—peace be upon him—when guiding the believers to recognize the falsehood of the **Dajjal** <term>Dajjal: The false messiah or anti-Christ figure in Islamic tradition.</term> and his claims of lordship, did not simply say that the Dajjal is a body while God is not. Instead, he—peace be upon him—said: "Your Lord is not one-eyed." He sufficed by pointing to the Dajjal's falsehood through the presence of this defective attribute [blindness in one eye]. The absence of such a defect in the Creator, Glory be to Him, is something everyone can recognize through the basic intuition of the intellect. 

All of these [theological complications], as you can see, are **innovations** <term>Innovations: "Bid'a" — here referring to technical theological methods not found in the original teachings of the Prophet or his companions.</term> that have arisen in Islam. They are the primary cause of the sectarian divisions that the Chosen One [Muhammad] foretold would split his community.

If someone asks: "If the Religious Law does not explicitly state to the masses whether He is a body or not a body, how should they be answered regarding **what He is**?" This question is natural to human beings, and they cannot be easily detached from it. The general public is not satisfied when told that a being, whose existence is acknowledged, has no "essence," for that which has no essence has no selfhood <note>original: "dhat" — refers to the core being or self of God.</note>.

We say: The duty in this regard is to answer with the response provided by the Religious Law, which is to say that **He is Light**. This is the description God used for Himself in His Mighty Book, in the same way a thing is described by the attribute that constitutes its very essence. The Almighty said:

> ﴾ God is the Light of the heavens and the earth ﴿ <note>Quran 24:35.</note>

The Prophet—peace be upon him—also described Him with this attribute in the established traditions. It is recorded that he—peace be upon him—was asked, "Have you seen your Lord?" and he replied: "He is Luminous; I see Him." <note>original: "Nurani arahu." This hadith from Sahih Muslim is often interpreted as the Prophet seeing a veil of light rather than God's essence directly.</note> In the Hadith of the Night Journey, it is said that when he—peace be upon him—drew near the **Lote-tree of the Farthest Boundary** <term>Sidrat al-Muntaha: A tree in the highest heaven that marks the limit of created knowledge.</term>, the tree was covered by such light that it shielded his vision from looking upon the radiance of His glories. In the Book of Muslim, it is stated: "God has a veil of light; if He were to uncover it, the glories of [His face] would burn..."

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the prophets used simple, intuitive proofs for God's nature rather than the complex, abstract logic of the Ash'arite theologians. He concludes that the most appropriate way to describe God's essence to the public is through the scriptural concept of "Light."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s explanation of how the Religious Law uses metaphors, specifically "Light," to describe God’s nature. He argues that this bridges the gap between the sensory world of the common person and the abstract reality of the Divine. He then begins a new section discussing the theological controversy surrounding God's "direction" or spatial location.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 66

...wherever His sight reaches. In some narrations of this tradition, it mentions "seventy veils of light." <note>original: "sab'ina hijaban min nur" — This refers to a famous Hadith (prophetic tradition) describing the majesty of God being shielded by veils of light; if they were removed, the splendor of His face would consume creation.</note> 

You should know that this metaphor is extremely appropriate for the Creator, Glory be to Him, because it combines two traits: it is something perceptible that the eyes are nevertheless incapable of fully grasping, and similarly, the intellects are incapable of fully grasping it, even though it is not a physical body. For the general public, "existence" is only that which is perceptible, while that which cannot be perceived is, in their view, "non-existent." Since light is the most noble of all perceptible things, it was necessary to represent the most noble of all existents [God] through it.

There is also another reason why He must be called "Light." This is because the state of His existence in the intellects of **scholars well-grounded in knowledge** <note>original: "al-rasikhun fi al-'ilm" — A Quranic term (3:7) for those with deep spiritual and intellectual insight.</note>—when they contemplate Him through reason—is like the state of the eyes when they look at the sun; or rather, like the eyes of bats [looking at the sun]. This description is both appropriate for both classes of people and is also the truth. Furthermore, since God, Blessed and Exalted, is the cause of all existing things and the cause of our perception of them, and since light—along with colors—shares this quality (meaning it is the cause of the actual existence of colors and the cause of our seeing them), it is rightfully so that God, Blessed and Exalted, named Himself "Light." When it is said that He is Light, no doubt arises regarding the **Vision** <note>original: "al-ru'ya" — The theological concept of seeing God in the afterlife, a major point of debate between different Islamic schools.</note> that is mentioned in the Resurrection. 

From this discourse, the primary belief regarding this attribute in the Religious Law has become clear to you, as well as the **innovations** <term>Innovation (Bid’ah): In a theological context, this refers to introducing new, often divisive, rationalistic interpretations into religious doctrine that were not present in the original community.</term> that have occurred regarding it. The Law remained silent regarding this attribute [non-corporeality] only because the common people do not acknowledge the existence of anything "unseen" that is not a body—unless they have grasped through a logical proof that there is something in the visible world with this same quality: namely, the **Soul** <term>Soul (Nafs): Here, Ibn Rushd refers to the human soul as a non-physical entity that exists within a physical body, serving as a philosophical bridge to understanding God's non-corporeal nature.</term>. Since attaining knowledge of this meaning of the soul is impossible for the general public, it was impossible for them to conceive of an existence that is not a body. When they were shielded from certain knowledge [of the soul], we knew that they would likewise be shielded from knowing this meaning regarding the Creator, Glory be to Him.

### Discussion on Direction

As for this attribute [spatial direction], the followers of the Religious Law from the very beginning never ceased to affirm it for God, Glory be to Him, until it was denied by the **Mu'tazilites** <term>Mu'tazilites: A rationalist school of Islamic theology that emphasized God’s absolute oneness and justice, often leading them to deny any attributes that might imply God has physical likeness or location.</term>. Then, they were followed in this denial by the later **Ash'arites**...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains why God is described as "Light," arguing it is the best metaphor for a non-physical being that the common people can still respect as "real." He also introduces the debate over whether God has a specific "direction" or location, noting that early believers accepted it before later theologians began to deny it.</summary>

<keywords>Light metaphor, non-corporeality, Vision of God, Soul, common vs. elite knowledge, Mu'tazilites, Direction (Jiha), Ash'arites</keywords>
