<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) argument regarding the nature of God. Having discussed the metaphor of "Light," he now transitions into a critique of the Ash’arite theologians—specifically Abu al-Ma’ali al-Juwayni—concerning the "direction" or spatial location of God, a major point of contention in medieval Islamic philosophy.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 67

...the <term>Ash‘arites</term><note>A major school of Sunni theology that sought a middle ground between rationalism and literalism.</note> such as <term>Abu al-Ma‘ali</term><note>Referring to Al-Juwayni (d. 1085), a famous Ash'arite theologian and teacher of Al-Ghazali.</note> and those who followed his opinion. All the literal meanings of the <term>Religious Law</term><note>original: "al-Shar‘" — the revealed text of the Quran and Traditions.</note> necessitate the affirmation of **direction** <note>original: "al-jiha" — the concept of God having a spatial orientation, often meaning "above" or "in the heavens."</note>, such as His saying, Exalted be He:

> **"And eight will, that Day, bear the Throne of your Lord above them."** <note>Quran 69:17</note>

And like His saying:

> **"He arranges each matter from the heaven to the earth; then it will ascend to Him in a Day the extent of which is a thousand years of those which you count."** <note>Quran 32:5</note>

And like His saying, Exalted be He:

> **"The angels and the Spirit ascend to Him..."** <note>Quran 70:4</note>

...and the rest of the verse, and like His saying, Exalted be He:

> **"Do you feel secure that He who is in heaven would not cause the earth to swallow you and suddenly it at once sways?"** <note>Quran 67:16</note>

...and other such verses which, if <term>interpretation</term><note>original: "al-ta'wil" — the practice of explaining away the literal meaning of a text in favor of an allegorical or metaphorical one.</note> were allowed to dominate them, the entire Law would be turned into an allegory. And if it were said that these are among the <term>ambiguous verses</term><note>original: "al-mutashabihat" — Quranic verses whose exact meaning is considered unclear or known only to God.</note>, the entire Law would become ambiguous. This is because all Religious Laws are built upon the principle that God is in heaven, that from it the angels descend with revelation to the Prophets, that from heaven the Books were sent down, and to it was the **Night Journey** <note>original: "al-isra'" — the miraculous journey of the Prophet Muhammad from Mecca to Jerusalem and then his ascension through the heavens.</note> of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, until he drew near to the **Lote Tree of the Utmost Boundary.** <note>original: "sidrat al-muntaha" — a holy tree in Islamic tradition marking the end of the seventh heaven, beyond which no creation can pass.</note> 

Furthermore, all the sages [philosophers] have agreed that God and the angels are in heaven, just as all Religious Laws have agreed upon that. The doubt that led the <term>deniers of direction</term><note>Those who argue that God cannot be in a specific direction because He is not physical.</note> to their denial is their belief that affirming "direction" necessitates affirming **place**, and affirming place necessitates affirming **corporeality** <note>original: "al-jismiyya" — the state of being a physical, material body.</note>. 

We say: all of this is not necessarily the case. For **direction** is not the same as **place**. This is because direction is either the surfaces of the body itself which surround it—which are six, and by this we say that an animal has a top, a bottom, a right, a left, a front, and a back—or it is the surfaces of another body surrounding the body that possesses the six directions. 

As for the directions which are the surfaces of the body itself, they are not a "place" for the body at all. As for the surfaces of the bodies surrounding it, they are its "place," such as the surfaces of the air surrounding a human being, or the surfaces of the <term>celestial spheres</term><note>original: "al-aflak" — in ancient and medieval cosmology, the transparent, concentric shells that carry the planets and stars around the Earth.</note> surrounding the surfaces of the air; these are also a "place" for the air. In this manner, the spheres surround one another and serve as a place for one another. 

As for the surface of the **outermost sphere**, it has been demonstrated that there is no body outside of it. For if that were the case, it would be necessary for there to be another body outside of *that* body...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Quran and all religions place God in "heaven" (the direction of "up") and warns that allegorizing these descriptions undermines the Law; he distinguishes "direction" from physical "place" to avoid the conclusion that God must be a physical body.</summary>
<keywords>direction, place, corporeality, Ash'arites, Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni, interpretation, celestial spheres, Quranic evidence, Ibn Rushd</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s refutation of those who deny "direction" (spatial orientation or elevation) to God. He is currently arguing that while God is not in a physical "place" like an object in a box, He occupies the most noble ontological "direction"—beyond the physical heavens—a concept he is reconciling with both Aristotelian physics and Quranic revelation.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 68

...and the matter would proceed to infinity. Thus, the surface of the outermost bodies of the universe is not a "place" at all, because it is impossible for a body to exist within it; for anything that is a **place** <note>Averroes uses the Aristotelian definition of place as the inner boundary of a containing body.</note> is characterized by the possibility of a body being found inside it.

Therefore, if proof is established for the existence of an entity in this **direction** <note>original: "jiha" — the spatial orientation or "side."</note>, it must necessarily be something other than a body. What is impossible to exist there is the exact opposite of what [the opponent] imagined: it is a physical body that cannot exist there, not an incorporeal being. Nor can they claim that there is a **void** <note>original: "khala'" — a vacuum or empty space.</note> outside the universe.

This is because the impossibility of a void has been demonstrated in the theoretical sciences; for the term "void" signifies nothing more than dimensions—namely length, width, and depth—without a body within them. If these dimensions were removed, the void would become non-existence. If the void were considered to exist, it would mean that **accidents** <note>original: "a'rad" — properties like size or color that cannot exist without a physical substance to "carry" them.</note> exist without a body, because dimensions are necessarily accidents belonging to the category of quantity.

However, it was said in the ancient views and past religions that this "place" is the dwelling of the **Spiritual Beings** <note>original: "al-ruhaniyyin"</note>, by which they mean God and the angels. That is because that "place" is not actually a physical location, nor is it contained by time. For everything contained by time and space is **subject to corruption** <note>meaning it can decay, change, or cease to exist.</note>. It follows that what is there must be incorruptible and non-contingent. This meaning becomes clear from what I am about to say:

Since there was nothing here except this perceptible existence or non-existence, and it is self-evident that an existing thing is attributed to "existence"—meaning it is said to exist *within* existence, for it cannot be said to exist within non-existence—then if there is an entity here that is the most noble of all beings, it must be attributed to the most noble part of the perceptible world, which is the **heavens**. Regarding the nobility of this part, He, the Blessed and Exalted, said:

> **"The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of mankind, but most of the people do not know."** <note>Quran 40:57</note>

All of this appears in its full clarity to the **scholars well-grounded in knowledge** <note>original: "al-rasikhun fi al-'ilm" — a Quranic term for those with deep intellectual and spiritual insight.</note>. Thus, it has become clear to you that affirming **direction** is a necessity of both **Religious Law** <note>original: "al-Shar‘"</note> and **Reason** <note>original: "al-‘Aql"</note>, and that this is indeed what the Law has brought forth.

->**Vocabulary:** The void, bodies, the Spiritual Beings, the heavens and the earth, direction, Law and Reason, well-grounded scholars.<-

<summary>Averroes argues that God exists in a non-physical "direction" beyond the universe, refuting the existence of a physical void and explaining that ancient religions correctly identified this "place" as the incorruptible realm of spiritual beings. He concludes that affirming God's "direction" (elevation) is both a logical and scriptural necessity.</summary>

<keywords>Averroes, Aristotelian physics, the void, accidents and substance, celestial nobility, al-jiha, God's location, Quran 40:57, Reason and Revelation</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument regarding "direction" (jiha). He previously established that the outermost boundary of the universe is not a physical "place" for a body, but rather a non-material orientation. He now transitions into why the religious Law (Shari’a) uses specific language to describe God and the unseen, tailored to the different intellectual capacities of human beings.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 69

I base [my argument] upon this: that the invalidation of this principle is the invalidation of the religious laws. The reason for the difficulty in explaining this concept—namely, the denial of **corporeality** <note>original: "al-jismiyya" — the state of being a physical body with dimensions.</note>—is that there is no example for it in the **visible world** <note>original: "al-shahid" — the world of sensory experience, often used in theology to create analogies for the divine.</note>. This is precisely why the Law did not explicitly state the denial of body for the Creator, Exalted is He; because the **masses** <note>original: "al-jumhur" — the general public or common believers who lack specialized philosophical training.</note> only reach conviction regarding the **unseen** <note>original: "al-gha'ib" — the divine realm or things beyond sensory perception.</note> when that thing is known to exist in the visible world.

An example of this is **knowledge** <note>original: "al-'ilm"</note>: since it was a condition for existence in the visible world, it was taken as a condition for the existence of the Unseen Creator. However, when a characteristic in the unseen is not known to exist in the visible world by most people—and is only known by the **scholars well-grounded in knowledge** <note>original: "al-'ulama' al-rasikhun" — a term from the Quran (3:7) referring to those with deep intellectual and spiritual insight.</note>—then the **Lawgiver** <note>original: "al-musharri'" — God or the Prophet as the source of religious legislation.</note> forbids seeking knowledge of it if the masses have no need to know it. An example of this is the knowledge of the **soul** <note>original: "al-nafs"</note>. Alternatively, the Lawgiver provides them with an analogy from the visible world if the masses have a need to know it for their own happiness—even if that analogy is not the exact thing intended to be understood. This is like much of what has been described regarding the states of the **afterlife** <note>original: "al-ma'ad" — literally "the return," referring to the resurrection and the Day of Judgment.</note>.

The confusion regarding the denial of **direction** <note>original: "jiha"</note> among those who deny it is not noticed by the masses, especially when it is not explicitly stated to them that [God] is not a body. In all of this, one must follow the example of the Law's method; otherwise, one would be providing an **interpretation** <note>original: "ta'wil" — allegorical or metaphorical interpretation of scripture.</note> for something the Law did not explicitly interpret.

Regarding these matters in the Law, people fall into three ranks:
1.  A class who do not perceive the doubts that arise regarding these meanings, especially when these things are left in their literal form in the Law. These are the majority, the masses.
2.  A class for whom doubts regarding these things have arisen, but they are unable to solve them. These people are above the commoners but below the scholars. It is for this class that **ambiguity** <note>original: "al-tashabu" — refers to "mutashabihat," the unclear or metaphorical verses of the Quran.</note> exists in the Law, and they are the ones whom God the Exalted has censured.
3.  As for the scholars and the masses, there is no ambiguity in the Law. 

It is according to this meaning that "ambiguity" should be understood. The example of what happens to this [middle] class regarding the Law is like the example of wheat bread: it is a beneficial food for most bodies, but it may be harmful to a very few, even though it is beneficial for the majority.

<vocab>Invalidation, Corporeality, The Visible World, The Unseen, The Masses, The Lawgiver, The Afterlife, Interpretation, Ambiguity, Scholars well-grounded in knowledge</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that the Law uses analogies from the visible world to help the masses understand the divine, and warns that forcing complex interpretations (ta'wil) on the common people causes confusion. He categorizes believers into three intellectual ranks, noting that "ambiguity" in scripture only causes spiritual harm to those who have enough knowledge to doubt but not enough to reach certainty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s exploration of how the Law (Shari’a) communicates complex divine truths to different audiences. He transitions from discussing the physical orientation of the divine to the nature of religious instruction itself, specifically how the Quran uses metaphor and why some people misinterpret these "ambiguous" passages.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 70

Likewise, **religious instruction** <note>original: "al-ta‘lim al-shar‘i"</note> is beneficial for the majority, though it may occasionally harm a minority. To this, God (Exalted is He) alludes by saying:

> "And He leads astray thereby only the defiant." <note>Quran 2:26.</note>

However, this only occurs in a small number of the verses of the Mighty Book and affects only a minority of people. Most of these instances are verses that provide information about things in the **unseen** <term>original: "al-gha'ib" — the divine realm or realities beyond human sensory perception.</term> for which there is no direct parallel in the **visible world** <term>original: "al-shahid" — the world of physical experience.</term>. Thus, these realities are expressed using things from the visible world that are most closely related and most similar to them.

Consequently, it happens that some people mistake the thing used as a metaphor for the reality itself, which leads them into confusion and doubt. This is what is called **ambiguous** <term>original: "mutashabih" — scriptural passages that are not immediately clear or could be interpreted in multiple ways.</term> in the Law. This confusion does not happen to the **scholars** <note>original: "al-‘ulama’"</note> nor to the **masses** <note>original: "al-jumhur" — the general public of believers.</note>, for these two groups are, in truth, the healthy ones. Just as suitable food only agrees with healthy bodies, [religious instruction benefits the healthy]. As for those others, they are the "sick," and the sick are always the minority. Therefore, the Exalted says:

> "As for those in whose hearts is deviation, they follow that of it which is ambiguous, seeking discord." <note>Quran 3:7.</note>

These people are the practitioners of **dialectic** <note>original: "ahl al-jadal" — those who use logical arguments to debate theological points.</note> and **speculative theology** <term>original: "al-kalam" — a school of Islamic thought that uses rational argument to defend faith, which Ibn Rushd often criticized for over-complicating simple truths.</term>. The worst harm brought upon the Law by this group is that they **interpreted** <term>original: "ta'awwalu" — the practice of ta'wil, or non-literal, allegorical interpretation.</term> much of what they wrongly assumed was not meant to be taken literally. They claimed that their specific interpretation was not the [obvious] intent, but rather that God presented it in an ambiguous form as a trial and test for His servants. We seek refuge in God from such a thought about Him!

Rather, we say that the Mighty Book of God was only rendered miraculous because of its clarity and explanation. How far from the purpose of the Law is the person who claims that something which is not ambiguous is actually ambiguous! Furthermore, such a person interprets that ambiguity according to their own claims and tells all the people that their religious duty is to believe in this specific interpretation—such as what they have said regarding the **verses of sitting on the Throne** <note>original: "ayat al-istiwa'" — Quranic verses describing God as "settling" or "establishing Himself" on a throne, which theologians debated as being either literal or metaphorical.</note> and other things they claimed were ambiguous in their literal sense.

In short, if you examine most of the interpretations that these speakers claim to be the intended meaning of the Law, you will find that they are not supported by **demonstrative proof** <note>original: "burhan" — certain, logical proof.</note>. Nor do these interpretations have the same effect as the literal meaning in terms of being accepted by the masses and motivating them to act. For the primary goal of knowledge for the masses is **action**; therefore, whatever is most beneficial for action is most worthy. However, the primary goal of knowledge for the scholars is **both things** <note>original: "al-amran" — referring to both correct knowledge/understanding and righteous action.</note>.

<summary>Ibn Rushd defends the clarity of the Quran, arguing that "ambiguous" verses are necessary metaphors for the unseen. He criticizes theologians for over-interpreting these verses and making religion unnecessarily difficult for the common believer.</summary>
<keywords>interpretation (ta'wil), ambiguous verses (mutashabih), the masses (jumhur), theologians (mutakallimun), clarity of scripture, action vs. knowledge, Throne of God (al-arsh)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of allegorical interpretation (ta’wil). On the previous page, he described how "sick" minds—those prone to doubt—misinterpret the metaphors of the Quran. Here, he uses a medical analogy to illustrate how sectarian interpretations have corrupted the original intent of the Law, leading to spiritual "illness" among the public.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 71

...both together—by which I mean knowledge and action. 

The example of one who interprets something from the **Law** <term>original: "al-shar‘" — the body of religious guidance and legislation.</term> and claims that his interpretation is what the Law intended, and then reveals that interpretation to the **masses** <term>original: "al-jumhur" — the general public of believers who are not trained in philosophy or advanced theology.</term>, is like someone who approaches a medicine compounded by a skilled physician to preserve the health of all people, or at least the majority of them. 

A man comes along whom that great compound medicine does not suit, due to a poor **constitution** <term>original: "mizaj" — in medieval medicine, the balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that determines a person's physical and mental health.</term> that affects only a tiny minority of people. This man then claims that some of those ingredients, which the first physician explicitly named in that universally beneficial compound, did not actually refer to the medicine usually signified by that name in common speech. Instead, he argues that the physician intended another medicine through a distant **metaphor** <note>original: "isti‘ara"</note>. 

He then removes that original ingredient from the great compound and replaces it with the medicine he *believes* the physician actually intended. He tells the people: "This is what the first physician meant." Consequently, people use the compound medicine according to this interpreter's version, and the **constitutions** <note>plural of "mizaj"</note> of many people are ruined as a result. 

Then others come along who notice the corruption of people’s health caused by that modified compound. They attempt to fix it by replacing some of its ingredients with yet another medicine, different from the first. This results in the people suffering a new type of disease, different from the first kind. 

A third interpreter arrives and offers a third interpretation of the ingredients in that compound, differing from both the first and second interpretations. This causes a third type of disease to afflict the people. Finally, a fourth interpreter comes and suggests yet another medicine, resulting in a fourth type of illness. 

As time passed for this "Great Compound" and people subjected its ingredients to interpretation, changing and altering them, various diseases appeared among the people. Eventually, the intended benefit of that compound medicine was lost for the majority of people. 

This is exactly the state of the **sects** <note>original: "al-firaq" — referring to the various theological schools such as the Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, and Hashwiyya.</note> that have arisen during this period regarding the **Shari’a** <term>original: "al-shari'a" — the Divine Law.</term>. This is because every sect among them has applied an interpretation to the Shari’a that is different from the others...

<summary>Ibn Rushd uses an extended medical metaphor to argue that various theological sects have corrupted the "Great Compound" of the Divine Law through misguided allegorical interpretations, causing spiritual illness in the general public.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Averroes, Interpretation, Ta'wil, Medicine Metaphor, Constitution, Mizaj, Sects, Religious Law, Shari'a</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of allegorical interpretation (ta’wil). On the previous page, he used a medical analogy to describe how the "medicine" of the Law (Shari‘a) is corrupted when scholars replace its literal meaning with metaphors that the general public cannot understand. He now names the specific groups and individuals he believes are responsible for this corruption.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 72

...the **interpretation** <term>original: "al-ta’wil" — the practice of looking for a hidden or allegorical meaning behind the literal text.</term> that the other group adopted and claimed was what the **Law** <term>original: "al-shar‘" — religious guidance.</term> intended. This caused the Law to be torn into pieces and moved very far from its original purpose. When the **Lawgiver** <note>Referring to the Prophet Muhammad.</note> (peace be upon him) realized that such a thing would inevitably happen to his Law, he said:

> My **community** <term>original: "ummati" — the global body of Muslim believers.</term> will split into seventy-two sects, all of them in the Fire except one.

By the "one," he meant the sect that follows the **literal meaning** <term>original: "zahir" — the apparent, plain-sense meaning of the scripture.</term> of the Law and does not broadcast allegorical interpretations to the public. If you reflect on the corruption that has occurred in the Law at this time due to such interpretation, it will become clear to you that this analogy of the medicine is correct.

The first to change this "great medicine" were the **Kharijites** <note>An early sect known for their radical and uncompromising interpretations.</note>, then the **Mu‘tazilites** <note>A school of theology that prioritized human reason and used rationalistic interpretations of scripture.</note> after them, then the **Ash‘arites** <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology, which Ibn Rushd often critiques for their dialectical methods.</note>, and then the **Sufis** <note>Those following the mystical path of Islam.</note>. Then came **Abu Hamid al-Ghazali** <note>A towering figure in Islamic history (d. 1111), known as "The Proof of Islam," who integrated Sufism, theology, and law, but whom Ibn Rushd finds dangerously inconsistent.</note>, and with him, the **floodwater reached the villages** <note>An Arabic idiom meaning a situation has become extreme or reached a breaking point.</note>. This is because he revealed all of **philosophy** <term>original: "al-hikma" — literally "wisdom," often used to refer to the Greek philosophical tradition.</term> to the masses, as well as the opinions of the philosophers, according to what his own understanding led him to. This was in his book titled *The Intentions of the Philosophers*, which he claimed to have written only to refute them.

Then he wrote his famous book, *The Incoherence of the Philosophers*, in which he declared them to be **disbelievers** <term>original: "kaffarahum" — to pronounce "takfir" or the status of being a non-believer.</term> regarding three specific issues because he claimed they violated the **consensus** <term>original: "al-ijma‘" — the universal agreement of the Muslim community.</term> of scholars. He also declared them to be **innovators** <term>original: "badda‘ahum" — accused them of "bid'a" or religious innovation.</term> regarding other issues. In that book, he presented confusing arguments and bewildering doubts that led many people away from both philosophy and the Law.

Later, in his book *The Jewels of the Quran*, he stated that what he set down in the book *The Incoherence* were merely **dialectical arguments** <term>original: "aqawil jadaliyya" — arguments intended to win a debate rather than to demonstrate the absolute truth.</term>, and that the truth was actually contained in his book *That Which Is to Be Withheld from Those Not Fit for It*. Then, in his book known as *The Niche of Lights*, he described the ranks of those who have "gnosis" or deep knowledge of God. He claimed that all of them are "veiled" from the truth except those who believe that God (glory be to Him) is not the direct Mover of the **first heaven** <note>In the medieval model of the universe, the outermost sphere that moves all other celestial bodies.</note>, but rather that this Mover is something that proceeded from Him. This is an explicit declaration of his belief in the doctrines of the philosophers regarding the divine sciences. Yet, in other places, he said that their divine sciences are merely "conjectures," unlike their other sciences. Finally, in his book *The Deliverer from Error*, he attacked the philosophers and suggested that true knowledge is only obtained through **seclusion** <term>original: "al-khalwa" — spiritual retreat.</term> and reflection, and that this rank is similar to the ranks of the Prophets in knowledge. And likewise...

<summary>Ibn Rushd traces the history of sectarian division in Islam to the practice of allegorical interpretation, ultimately focusing a sharp critique on Al-Ghazali for his contradictory writings that mixed philosophy and religion for the general public.</summary>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s specific critique of Abu Hamid al-Ghazali. Following the discussion on the previous page regarding the corruption of the "medicine" of the Law through improper interpretation, Ibn Rushd now identifies the specific works where he believes Al-Ghazali confused the public by mixing philosophical "wisdom" with religious "law."</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 73

->(10)<-

He explicitly stated this very point in his book which he titled **The Alchemy of Happiness** <note>Original: *Kimiya’ al-Sa‘ada*. This was a major work by the Persian scholar Al-Ghazali, intended to explain religious life through a mix of ethics and mysticism.</note>. Because of this confusion and mixing, people split into two factions: one faction set out to disparage the philosophers and **Wisdom** <term>original: "al-hikma" — here referring to philosophy and the sciences.</term>, while the other faction set out to interpret the **Law** <term>original: "al-shar‘" — the revealed religious text.</term> and sought to force it to conform to Wisdom. All of this is an error. Rather, the Law should be left in its **literal sense** <note>original: "zahir" — the plain, apparent meaning of the text as understood by a general audience.</note>. One must not declare to the general public the reconciliation between the Law and Wisdom, because declaring that is to declare the conclusions of Wisdom to them without them possessing the **demonstrative proof** <term>original: "burhan" — a logical argument that leads to certain knowledge.</term> for them. This is not permissible nor is it allowed. 

I mean that one should not declare any of the conclusions of Wisdom to those who do not possess the proof for them. Such reconciliation should only be shared with the scholars who combine both the Law and reason, not with the general public who follow the literal sense of the Law. Consequently, his actions caused a failing in both matters—I mean in Wisdom and in the Law—for some people, while preserving both matters for others.

As for his failing regarding the Law, it was due to his disclosure of **allegorical interpretation** <term>original: "al-ta’wil"</term> within it, which is something that should not be disclosed. As for his failing regarding Wisdom, it was due to his disclosure of concepts within it that should only be stated in books of demonstrative proof. 

As for his preservation of both matters, it is because many people do not perceive a contradiction between the two due to the synthesis he employed between them. He reinforced this meaning by defining the method of reconciliation in his book entitled **The Decisive Criterion between Islam and Zandaqa** <note>Original: *Al-Tafriqa bayna al-Islam wa-l-Zandaqa*. This book by Al-Ghazali attempted to define the boundaries of faith and heresy.</note>. In that work, he enumerated the categories of interpretation and concluded that the one who interprets is not an **unbeliever** <term>original: "kafir"</term>, even if they violate the consensus through that interpretation. 

Thus, what he did through these things is harmful to the Law in one way, and to Wisdom in another way. When what this man did is summarized, it becomes clear that it is inherently harmful to both—I mean Wisdom and the Law—and that it is only beneficial to them by accident. This is because disclosing Wisdom to those who are not suited for it inherently leads to either the nullification of Wisdom or the nullification of the Law; though it may, by accident, lead to a reconciliation between them. The correct path would have been to not declare...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques Al-Ghazali for confusing the public by mixing philosophy and religion, arguing that allegorical interpretations should be reserved for scholars and hidden from the masses to prevent the corruption of both faith and logic.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of how the relationship between philosophy and religion has been mishandled. Having previously discussed how Al-Ghazali’s mixing of the two caused confusion, Ibn Rushd now argues that since this "secret" harmony has already been made public, he must correctly explain that the Law and Wisdom (philosophy) are not in conflict. He then transitions to the specific theological problem of the "Vision of God" and the Mu'tazilite position on it.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 74

...through **Wisdom** <term>original: "al-hikma" — referring here to philosophy and demonstrative science.</term> for the general public. However, since this disclosure has already taken place, the correct course of action is to teach that group of the public who believe that the **Law** <term>original: "al-shari‘a" — the revealed religious text and its legal system.</term> contradicts Wisdom that it does not, in fact, contradict it. Likewise, those practitioners of Wisdom who believe the Law contradicts it must be taught that there is no such contradiction. 

This is achieved by making both parties realize that they have not truly grasped the **essence** <term>original: "kunh" — the true nature or depth of a matter.</term> of either; that is, they understand neither the essence of the Law nor the essence of Wisdom. Any opinion within the Law that is believed to contradict Wisdom is either an **innovation** <note>original: "mubtada‘" — a theological novelty or deviation not found in the original source texts.</note> in the Law and not from its true origin, or it is a mistaken view in Wisdom—meaning a faulty interpretation—just as occurred regarding the question of **God's knowledge of particulars** <note>Ibn Rushd refers to a major debate where philosophers were accused of heresy for claiming God only knows universal truths, not individual, changing events.</note> and other such issues. 

For this reason, we were compelled in this book to define the principles of the Law. For if its principles are contemplated, they are found to be in closer harmony with Wisdom than the interpretations that have been offered for them. Similarly, regarding the opinion in Wisdom thought to contradict the Law, one should know that the cause of this is a lack of comprehensive knowledge of both Wisdom and the Law. This is why we were also compelled to write the treatise titled **The Decisive Discourse on the Agreement between Wisdom and the Law** <note>Original: *Fasl al-Maqal fi muwafaqat al-hikma lil-shari'a*. This is one of Ibn Rushd's most famous works, defending the use of philosophy within Islam.</note>.

Now that this has been clarified, let us return to our subject. We say that the remaining well-known issue in this section is the **Question of the Vision** <note>The theological debate over whether believers will literally "see" God in the afterlife.</note>. It might be thought that this issue was already covered in the previous section because of the words of the Exalted: 

> "Vision perceives Him not, but He perceives [all] vision" <note>Quran 6:103. This verse was a central proof-text for those who denied the possibility of seeing God.</note> 

For this reason, the **Mu‘tazilites** <term>A rationalist school of Islamic theology that emphasized God's absolute unity and justice, often rejecting literal interpretations that implied God had physical attributes.</term> denied the Vision. They rejected the numerous and well-known **traditions** <term>original: "al-athar" — reports or sayings attributed to the Prophet or his companions.</term> found in the Law concerning it, which brought great public condemnation upon them. 

The cause of this confusion in the Law is that the Mu‘tazilites, believing in the negation of **corporeality** <term>original: "al-jismiyya" — the quality of having a physical body.</term> for God (Glorified is He), and believing it mandatory to declare this to all believers, concluded that if corporeality is negated, then **spatial direction** <term>original: "al-jiha" — location or place in space.</term> must also be negated. And if direction is negated, the Vision is negated, since every object seen must be in a specific direction relative to the one who sees it. Because of this logic, they felt forced to reject the transmitted Law. They excused their rejection of the **Hadiths** <note>Reports of the Prophet’s words or actions.</note> by claiming they are **solitary reports** <term>original: "akhbar ahad" — traditions transmitted by only one or a few chains of narrators, which some theologians argued do not provide absolute certainty.</term>, and solitary reports do not yield certain knowledge.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that perceived conflicts between philosophy and religion stem from a misunderstanding of both, referencing his work "The Decisive Discourse." He then introduces the debate over the Vision of God, explaining how the Mu‘tazilites used logic to deny it by rejecting certain religious traditions.</summary>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 75

...even though the literal meaning of the **Quran** contradicts them, namely the words of the Exalted:

-> "Eyes reach Him not" <- 
<note>Quran 6:103. The Mu'tazilites used this verse as primary evidence that God cannot be physically seen, while other schools interpreted it as God being "unfathomable" rather than "invisible."</note>

As for the <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>The Ash'arites were a dominant school of Islamic theology that sought to reconcile traditional faith with rationalist methods.</note>, they sought to reconcile these two beliefs—that is, between the denial of God's physicality and the possibility of seeing what is not a body through the senses. This proved difficult for them, so they resorted to <term>sophistical</term> <note>original: "sufusta'iyya" — referring to deceptive reasoning that is designed to look like a logical proof but is actually a fallacy.</note> arguments; that is, arguments that seem correct but are actually false. 

It seems that arguments are much like people: just as there are people of complete virtue, those of lesser virtue, and those who merely pretend to be virtuous but are not—the <term>hypocrite</term> <note>original: "al-mura'i" — literally one who "shows off." Ibn Rushd uses this to describe an argument that has the appearance of truth but lacks the substance.</note>—so it is with arguments. Some are of the highest certainty, some are less than certain, and some are "hypocritical" arguments that appear certain but are false. 

The arguments used by the <term>Ash'arites</term> on this issue include those intended to dismantle the proofs of the <term>Mu'tazilites</term> <note>The Mu'tazilites were an earlier rationalist school who argued that if God could be seen, He must be a body, which is impossible.</note>, and those intended to prove the possibility of seeing what is not a body without entailing an impossibility. 

Regarding their opposition to the <term>Mu'tazilite</term> claim that "everything seen must be in a specific direction relative to the viewer," some Ash'arites argued that this is merely a rule governing the <term>Observed</term> <note>original: "al-shahid" — in Islamic logic, this refers to the empirical, physical world that we can witness.</note> and not the <term>Unseen</term> <note>original: "al-gha'ib" — refers to the metaphysical or divine realm which cannot be directly witnessed by humans.</note>. They claimed this is not an instance where rules from the Observed must be applied to the Unseen, and that it is possible for a human to see something that is not in a direction if it is possible for a human to see by the **visual faculty** <note>original: "al-quwwa al-mubsira" — the internal power or sense of sight.</note> itself without the need for an eye. 

These thinkers have confused intellectual perception with visual perception. It is the **intellect** that perceives what is not in a direction (that is, what is not in a place). As for visual perception, its nature clearly dictates that its condition is for the object to be in a direction—and not just any direction, but a specific one. This is why vision does not occur in just any position the eye happens to be in relative to the object, but only through limited positions and limited conditions as well. These are three things: the presence of light, a **transparent medium** <note>original: "al-jism al-shaffaf" — a physical substance like air or water that allows light to pass through.</note> between the eye and the object, and the object necessarily having color. Denying these self-evident facts of vision is...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite theologians for using "sophistical" logic to argue that God can be seen without being a physical body, accusing them of confusing the way the mind thinks with the way the eyes actually see.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theologians. He is specifically addressing their attempt to use logical analogies to prove that God can be seen with the eyes without God being a physical body or occupying a specific direction.</meta>

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 76

This [approach] is a rejection of the first principles that are naturally known to everyone, and it serves to invalidate all the sciences of debate and geometry. The group—I mean the <term>Ash’arites</term> <note>A school of early Islamic theology that used rationalistic methods to defend orthodox doctrines.</note>—has claimed that one of the instances where the judgment of the <term>Witnessed</term> <note>original: "al-shāhid" — the observable, physical world.</note> must be transferred to the <term>Absent</term> <note>original: "al-ghā'ib" — the divine or unseen realm.</note> is in the case of a "condition." For example, our judgment that every "knower" must be "alive" is based on the fact that life appears in the Witnessed world as a necessary condition for the existence of knowledge. 

If this is the case, then we say to them: it also appears in the Witnessed world that these physical things [like direction and light] are conditions for **vision**. Therefore, according to your own principle, you must apply the conditions of the Witnessed world to the Absent when it comes to vision.

<term>Abu Hamid</term> <note>Referring to Al-Ghazali (1058–1111 AD), one of the most influential Muslim theologians and a frequent target of Ibn Rushd’s critiques.</note>, in his famous book titled *The Intentions* <note>original: "al-Maqasid" — referring to *Maqasid al-Falasifa* (The Intentions of the Philosophers).</note>, attempted to challenge this premise—namely, that everything seen must be in a specific direction from the person seeing it. He argued that a person sees their own essence in a mirror, yet their essence is not located in a direction other than the direction opposite the mirror. He claimed that because the person sees their essence, and their essence does not actually reside inside the mirror (which is in the opposite direction), the person is therefore seeing their essence without it being in a specific direction. 

However, this is a **fallacy**. That which is actually seen is the *image* of the person’s essence. The image itself is in a specific direction, as the image is in the mirror, and the mirror is in a direction.

As for the argument they provide to support the possibility of seeing that which is not a body, they typically rely on two arguments. The most famous one is their claim that a thing is seen either:
1.  By virtue of being colored
2.  By virtue of being a body
3.  By virtue of being a color
4.  By virtue of being **existent**

They sometimes list other categories beyond these. Then they argue: "It is false to say a thing is seen because it is a body, for if that were true, color would not be seen. It is also false to say it is seen because it is a color, for if that were true, the body would not be seen." Once all these imaginable categories are proven false, they conclude that nothing remains except that a thing is seen only by virtue of its **existence**. 

The fallacy in this statement is obvious. Of things that are seen, some are seen by their own essence—which is the case for **color**—and the **body** is seen only by means of the color. This is why anything that has no color cannot be seen. If a thing were seen only because it exists, then it would be necessary for us to see sounds and all other... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; Ibn Rushd is arguing that if "existence" were the only requirement for being seen, all non-visual senses like sound or smell would be visible to the eye.</note>

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite school and Al-Ghazali, arguing that their logical justifications for seeing God without physical conditions are fallacious. He asserts that vision requires physical conditions like direction and color, and mocks the idea that things are seen simply because they "exist."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s logical deconstruction of the Ash’arite theological position regarding the possibility of seeing God. ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 77

the five senses. [If their argument were true,] then sight, hearing, and the other five senses would become a single sense, and all of this is contrary to reason. The <term>theologians</term> <note>original: "al-mutakallimun" — scholars of speculative theology who used rational arguments to defend religious dogmas.</note> were forced, because of this problem and others like it, to concede that colors could potentially be heard and sounds could potentially be seen. All of this is a departure from nature and from what any human can comprehend.

For it is obvious that the sense of sight is different from the sense of hearing; that which is perceived by the former is different from that of the latter; the physical organ of one is different from the other; and it is impossible for sight to turn into hearing just as it is impossible for a color to become a sound. 

Those who say that sound could be seen at some point must be asked: "What is sight?" They would inevitably have to answer: "It is a faculty through which visible things—colors and others—are perceived." Then they should be asked: "What is hearing?" They must say: "It is a faculty through which sounds are perceived." Once they establish these definitions, it should be said to them: "In the presence of these sounds, is this 'sight' merely sight or merely hearing?" 

If they say "hearing only," they have conceded that it does not perceive colors. If they say "sight only," then it does not perceive sounds. If it is not sight alone (since it perceives sounds) nor hearing alone (since it perceives colors), then it must be both sight and hearing together. According to this logic, all things would become one single thing, even opposites. This is something that I believe the theologians of our faith concede—or are forced to concede—yet it is a **Sophist** <note>A reference to ancient Greek thinkers who Ibn Rushd, following Aristotle, accused of using clever but fallacious reasoning to deny objective reality.</note> view held by ancient peoples famous for their sophistry.

As for the second method adopted by the theologians regarding the possibility of **Vision** <note>original: "al-ru'ya" — referring to the theological debate over whether believers can see God in the afterlife.</note>, it is the method chosen by **Abu al-Ma'ali** <note>Al-Juwayni (1028–1085 AD), a prominent Ash'arite theologian and the teacher of Al-Ghazali.</note> in his well-known book, *The Guidance* <note>original: "al-Irshad" — a foundational text of Ash'arite theology.</note>. This method can be summarized as follows: 

The senses only perceive the essences of things. The qualities that distinguish one existing thing from another are "modes" <note>original: "ahwal" — a technical term in theology for attributes that are not quite essences but define a state of being.</note> rather than essences; thus, the senses do not perceive them. Rather, the senses perceive the essence, and the essence is the very existence shared by all existing things. Therefore, the senses only perceive the thing...

He then begins to critique Al-Juwayni's specific argument that senses perceive a universal "essence" of existence rather than distinct qualities.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theory of perception. ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 78

...as an existent. This whole line of reasoning is profoundly flawed. One of the clearest proofs of the invalidity of this claim is that if sight only perceived things <note>Specifically, if it perceived objects only as "existents" without their specific qualities.</note>, it would be impossible to distinguish between white and black. This is because things cannot be differentiated by a quality they all share in common. Furthermore, it would be generally impossible for the senses—neither for sight to perceive the distinctions between colors, nor for hearing to perceive the distinctions between sounds, nor for taste to perceive the distinctions between flavors. It would necessarily follow that all perceptions of sensible objects would belong to a single category; thus, there would be no difference between what is perceived by hearing and what is perceived by sight.

All of this is a complete departure from what any human can comprehend. In reality, the senses perceive the specific essences of objects by means of perceiving the sensible qualities unique to them. The <term>fallacy</term> <note>original: "mughalata"</note> here lies in taking that which is perceived as an essential attribute and claiming it is perceived merely by virtue of its existence. Had people not been raised on these sayings and taught to venerate those who uttered them, such arguments would never have been persuasive, nor would they have been accepted by anyone possessing a <term>sound natural disposition</term> <note>original: "fitra" — the innate, untainted human inclination toward truth and reason.</note>.

The reason for this state of confusion occurring within the Law <note>original: "al-shari'a" — here referring to the body of religious doctrines.</note>—which has forced those who claim to be its defenders into such absurd statements that would be a laughingstock to anyone with the slightest training in logic—is the public declaration of things that neither God nor His Messenger permitted. Specifically, this refers to the declaration to the <term>masses</term> <note>original: "al-jumhur" — the general public or non-philosophers who understand things through imagery rather than abstract demonstration.</note> that God is not a body <note>original: "jismiyya" — corporeality or having a physical form.</note>. This is because it is extremely difficult for a single belief to maintain that something is both a non-physical being and yet visible to the eyes, since the objects of our senses are either physical bodies or exist within bodies. 

Because of this difficulty, some have argued that this "vision" of God is actually just a "surplus of knowledge" granted at that time. However, this too is an explanation that should not be disclosed to the masses. Since the intellect of the common people cannot be separated from their imagination—indeed, anything they cannot imagine, they consider non-existent—and since it is impossible to imagine something that is not a body, and therefore impossible for them to believe in the existence of something unimaginable, the Law refrained from explicitly declaring this abstract meaning to them. Instead, it described Him...

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument regarding the nature of perception and divine attributes. ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 79

Glory be to Him; He described Himself to them using attributes that are accessible to the power of imagination, such as hearing, sight, the "Face," and so on, while simultaneously informing them that nothing among <term>imagined existents</term> <note>original: "al-mawjudat al-mutakhayyila" — things we can picture in our minds based on sensory experience.</note> is of His same kind or resembles Him. If the intent had been to inform the general public that He is not a physical body, He would not have explicitly stated any of these attributes to them. Rather, because light is the most sublime of all imagined existents, He used it as an example for them, since light is the most well-known of existents to both the senses and the imagination. By this method of conceptualization, it became possible for them to understand the meanings of the existents in the <term>Return</term> <note>original: "al-ma'ad" — the afterlife or the resurrection.</note>; that is to say, those abstract meanings were represented to them through imagined, sensible things. 

Thus, when the Divine Law’s descriptions of God (blessed and exalted be He) are taken at their literal meaning, neither this doubt nor any other arises. For if the public is told that He is "Light," and that He has a "veil of light"—as has appeared in the Quran and the established <term>Sunnah</term> <note>The body of traditional social and legal custom and practice of the Islamic community, specifically the sayings of the Prophet.</note>—and then it is said that the believers shall see Him in the hereafter as one sees the sun, no doubt or confusion arises from any of this for the general public, nor for the scholars. 

This is because it has been proven to the scholars that this "state" <note>The state of seeing God in the afterlife.</note> is actually an "increase in knowledge." However, if this were stated explicitly to the general public, the entire Law would be nullified in their eyes, or they would label the person who revealed it to them an infidel. Therefore, whoever deviates from the path of the Law in these matters has strayed from the straight path. 

If you contemplate the Divine Law, you will find that while it has provided the general public with metaphors for these meanings—without which they could not have conceptualized them—it has also alerted the scholars to the actual meanings themselves for which those metaphors were struck. It is therefore mandatory to stop at the limit set by the Law regarding the type of instruction specifically assigned to each class of people. One must not mix the two types of instruction, for that would corrupt the prophetic, legal wisdom. 

For this reason, the Prophet (peace be upon him) said: "We, the assembly of Prophets, have been commanded to treat people according to their stations and to address them according to the capacity of their intellects." Anyone who treats all people as a single group in matters of instruction is like one who treats them as a single group in a specific physical labor. All of this is contrary to both sensory evidence and reason. It has thus become clear to you from this that the "Vision" <note>original: "al-ru'ya" — the Beatific Vision of God.</note> is a literal meaning and that it is not...

# The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion 80

This section addresses the doubt that arises when the Religious Law is taken at its literal word regarding the rights of God (blessed and exalted be He). By this, I mean when the Law neither explicitly declares a denial of <term>corporeality</term> <note>original: "al-jismiyya" — the state of having a physical body.</note> nor confirms it. Since the initial religious beliefs concerning <term>transcendence</term> <note>original: "al-tanzih" — the concept that God is completely different and removed from His creation.</note> have been clarified, along with the extent to which the general public should be taught these matters, we must now move to the section concerning the knowledge of the actions of God (blessed and exalted be He). This is the Fifth Part of these arts, and with it, we shall conclude the discourse we intended.

## ﴿ The Fifth Part: On the Knowledge of Divine Actions ﴾
In this part, we mention only five issues, which are the foundations upon which this entire subject revolves: **(First Issue)** on proving the creation of the world; **(Second Issue)** on the sending of the Messenger; **(Third Issue)** on Divine Decree and Destiny; **(Fourth Issue)** on the attribution of injustice and justice to God; **(Fifth Issue)** on the <term>Return</term> <note>original: "al-ma'ad" — the Resurrection and the afterlife.</note>.

### (The First Issue: On the Temporal Origination of the World)
Know that what the Religious Law intends for us to know about the world is that it is "made" by God (blessed and exalted be He) and "invented" by Him, and that it did not come into existence by mere chance or from itself. The path the Law takes with people to establish this foundation is not the path of the <term>Ash’arites</term> <note>A dominant school of speculative theology (Kalam) that Averroes often critiqued for being too abstract for the public but not rigorous enough for philosophers.</note>. We have already demonstrated that their methods are neither certain proofs for the scholars, nor are they the general methods shared by everyone. By "general methods," I mean those that are "simple"—meaning they have few premises, and their conclusions are close to the premises that are known instinctively. As for demonstrations made through long, complex <term>syllogisms</term> <note>original: "maqayis" — logical arguments where a conclusion is drawn from two or more premises.</note> built upon various specialized principles, the Religious Law does not use these to teach the general public. Anyone who leads the public using something other than these simple methods, and interprets the Law through them, has ignored the Law's intent and strayed from the correct path. Likewise, the Law only explains such matters through analogies that have a parallel in the <term>visible world</term> <note>original: "al-shahid" — the world we can perceive through our senses.</note>. For whenever the need to teach the public a concept becomes urgent, the Law explains it using the closest possible things that resemble it, as is the case in the matters of...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 80 regarding the Fifth Part of the text, specifically the Knowledge of Divine Actions. Having established that the Religious Law (Shari'a) should speak to the public in accessible terms, Averroes now explores how the "temporal origination" of the world is taught through the "Way of Providence."</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-
-> ( 11 ) ٨١ <-

the <term>Return</term> <note>original: "al-ma'ad" — the Resurrection and the afterlife.</note> and things they have no need to know in this regard, they realize that it is not their concern, as the Almighty said regarding the Spirit: <note>Reference to Quran 17:85: "And they ask you, [O Muhammad], about the Spirit. Say, 'The Spirit is of the affair of my Lord. And mankind have not been given of knowledge except a little.'"</note>. Since this principle is established for us, it is mandatory that the method followed by the Religious Law in teaching the <term>public</term> <note>original: "al-jumhur" — the general body of believers who are not specialized in philosophy or advanced theology.</note> about the **temporal origination of the world** <note>original: "haduth al-'alam" — the doctrine that the universe had a beginning and was created in time, as opposed to being eternal.</note> must be one of the simple methods recognized by everyone. It is also necessary that, since this origination has no direct parallel in what we witness in our daily lives, the Law must use the creation of objects we can see as a metaphor for the creation of the world.

As for the path the Law took in teaching the public that the world is "made" by God (blessed and exalted be He), if you reflect on the verses that contain this meaning, you will find those methods are the **Way of Providence** <note>original: "tariq al-'inaya" — also known as the teleological argument; the idea that the universe's design and its suitability for life prove the existence of a purposeful Creator.</note>. This is one of the two methods we previously mentioned as indicating the existence of the Creator, the Almighty.

This method works as follows: When a human looks at a physical object and sees that it has been placed in a specific shape, a specific size, and a specific position—all of which perfectly suit the **benefit** <note>original: "al-manfa'a" — the utility or purpose of a thing.</note> found in that object and its desired goal—they must acknowledge that if it were found in any other shape, position, or size, that benefit would not exist. From this, they know with absolute certainty that the object has a **Maker** <note>original: "al-San'i" — the Artisan or Creator.</note> who crafted it, which is why its shape, position, and size match that benefit. It is impossible for the alignment of all these factors for the purpose of a specific benefit to happen by sheer coincidence.

For example, if a person sees a stone on the ground and finds its shape is suitable for sitting, and its position and size are also suitable for that purpose, they know that this stone was fashioned by a maker who placed it there and measured it for that spot. However, when they do not see any such suitability for sitting, they conclude that its presence in that place and its specific state occurred by chance, without an agent placing it there.

The same applies to the entire world. When a human looks at what is within it—the sun, the moon, and the rest of the stars, which are the cause of the four seasons, the cause of night and day, and the cause of...

<summary>Averroes explains that the Religious Law teaches the creation of the world through the "Way of Providence," using the analogy of a well-crafted object (like a seat) to show that the universe's design for human benefit proves it has a purposeful Maker.</summary>

Averroes is detailing how the harmony of the natural world serves as a primary, simple proof for the existence of God as a purposeful Creator, accessible to all people.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-
-> ٨٢ <-

rains, waters, winds, and the cause of the cultivation of the various parts of the earth, the existence of humans, and all other beings including animals and plants. It also mentions the fact that the earth is suitable for human habitation and all other land animals; likewise, water is suitable for aquatic animals, and air for flying creatures. If any part of this creation and structure were to be disrupted, the existence of the creatures found here would likewise be disrupted. Thus, it is known with certainty that it is impossible for this harmony, which exists in all parts of the world for the benefit of humans, animals, and plants, to occur by chance. Rather, it must proceed from an Intender who intended it and a Willer who willed it, and He is God, Mighty and Sublime. 

It is known with certainty that the world is **made** <note>original: "masnu'" — meaning something crafted, manufactured, or produced by design.</note>. This is because it is necessarily understood that this harmony could not exist if the world's existence came from something other than a **Maker** <note>original: "Sani'" — the Divine Craftsman or Architect.</note>, but rather by mere chance. As for the fact that this type of proof is definitive and yet simple, it is evident from what we have written; its foundation rests on two principles recognized by everyone. 

The first principle is that the world, in all its parts, exists in harmony with the existence of humans and all other beings found here. The second principle is that everything found to be harmonious in all its parts for a single action and directed toward a single goal is necessarily **made**. From these two principles, it naturally follows that the world is made and that it has a Maker. This is because the **Way of Providence** <note>original: "dalalat al-'inaya" — the argument from design or teleology.</note> indicates both matters together; for this reason, it is the most noble of proofs for the existence of the Maker. 

As for the fact that this type of reasoning is the very one found in the **Mighty Book** <note>original: "al-Kitab al-'Aziz" — a common honorific for the Quran.</note>, this is evident from many verses in which the beginning of creation is mentioned. Among them is the Almighty’s saying: *“Have We not made the earth a resting place and the mountains as stakes?”* until His saying, *“and gardens of thick foliage.”* <note>A citation of the Quran, Surah An-Naba (78), verses 6 through 16, which lists natural wonders as evidence of God's power and care.</note> If one reflects on this verse, one finds in it an invitation to notice the harmony of the world's parts for the existence of humanity. This is because He began by drawing attention to a matter self-evidently known to us, the human race—both white and black—which is that the earth was created with a quality that makes it possible for us to reside upon it.

<meta>This page continues the specific application of the "Proof of Providence." Having established that the universe is designed for life, Averroes (Ibn Rushd) now moves into a detailed Quranic exegesis, explaining how the physical properties of the Earth—its stability, the presence of mountains, and the cycle of day and night—are not random occurrences but intentional provisions for human and animal survival.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

Furthermore, if the Earth were in motion, or held a shape other than its current one, or occupied a position other than where it is, or possessed a size different from its current measure, it would not be possible for us to exist upon it, nor could we have been created upon it. All of this is encapsulated in the words of the Almighty: 

> Have We not made the earth a cradle? <note>Quran 78:6. The Arabic word used is "mihad," which implies a bed or a leveled expanse prepared for rest.</note>

This is because a "cradle" combines the qualities of suitability in shape, stillness, and position, and adds to this the sense of comfort and softness. How wondrous is this miracle of expression! How excellent is this blessing, and how extraordinary is this synthesis! For He has gathered in the single word "cradle" all the ways in which the Earth is suited for human existence. This is a matter that has been fully clarified by scholars through extensive discourse and over no small period of time. "But God singles out for His mercy whom He wills." <note>A reference to Quran 2:105, suggesting that deep understanding of these natural wonders is a divine gift.</note> As for the words of the Almighty:

> And the mountains as pegs? <note>Quran 78:7. The word "awtad" (pegs/stakes) refers to the stabilizing function of mountains.</note>

By this, He draws attention to the benefit found in the stillness of the Earth provided by the mountains. For if the Earth were estimated to be smaller than it is—as if it were without mountains—it would be shaken by the movements of the remaining <term>elements</term> <note>original: "al-ustuqussat" — derived from the Greek *stoicheia*, referring here to water and air.</note>, namely water and air. It would tremble and be displaced from its position, and were that to happen, animal life would necessarily perish. 

Therefore, the harmony of the Earth’s stillness with the beings upon it did not occur by chance. Rather, it occurred by the intent of a Designer and the will of a Purposer. It is, of necessity, **made** for that Purposer—Glory be to Him—and exists for Him according to the specific attributes He ordained for the existence of the creatures upon it. Then, He also drew attention to the harmony between the existence of night and day and the needs of animals, for the Almighty said:

> And We made the night a covering, and We made the day for livelihood. <note>Quran 78:10-11.</note>

He means that He made the night like a veil and a garment for the beings here, protecting them from the heat of the sun. This is because, were it not for the absence of the sun at night, those beings whose lives God has linked to the sun—namely animals and plants—would perish. Since a garment protects against heat while also acting as a covering, and since the night possesses both of these qualities, God Almighty called it a "garment" <note>original: "libas" — meaning clothing or a covering.</note>. This is among the most exquisite examples of <term>metaphor</term> <note>original: "isti'ara" — a key concept in Arabic rhetoric where a word is used outside its literal sense to create a vivid image.</note>. In the night, there is also another benefit for animals, which is that sleep occurs within it.

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's (Averroes) line-by-line commentary on Surah An-Naba, moving from the stability of the Earth to the functions of the heavens, the Sun, and the weather. He argues that the physical laws governing the cosmos are perfectly calibrated to sustain biological life.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...engulfing [the body] because the light, which stimulates the senses, retreats from the exterior of the body—which is the state of wakefulness—[to the interior]. For this reason, the Almighty said: **﴿ And We made your sleep for rest ﴾** <note>Quran 78:9. The Arabic term *subat* (rest) implies a cessation of activity or a "deep immersion" in stillness.</note>, meaning it is an engulfing rest brought about by the darkness of the night. Then the Almighty said: **﴿ And We built above you seven strong [heavens] and made a glowing lamp ﴾** <note>Quran 78:12-13. The "glowing lamp" is a metaphor for the Sun.</note>.

He expressed the meaning of their creation through the word "building," signifying the harmony, system, and order found within them. He used the term "strength" to describe the power granted to them for their movement—a movement that never tires, never falters, and never yields to weariness. Nor is there any fear that they might collapse, as high roofs and buildings do. This is indicated by the words of the Almighty: **﴿ And We made the sky a protected roof ﴾** <note>Quran 21:32. This reflects the medieval Aristotelian view of the heavens as solid, eternal spheres that do not decay.</note>.

All of this is a reminder from Him of how the heavens correspond—in their numbers, their shapes, their positions, and their movements—to the existence of what is on the Earth and what surrounds it. Indeed, if even one of the heavenly bodies were to stop for a single moment, everything on the face of the Earth would be corrupted, let alone if they were all to stop. Some have even claimed that the "Blowing of the Trumpet" <note>original: *al-nafkh fi al-sur*</note>, which is the cause of the "Thunderclap" <note>original: *al-sa'qa*; the cosmic event marking the end of the world</note>, is actually the stopping of the celestial spheres.

Then He drew attention to the specific benefit of the Sun and its suitability for the existence of what is on the Earth, saying: **﴿ And We made a glowing lamp ﴾**. He only called it a "lamp" because the original state is darkness, and light is an occurrence that visits the darkness of night. Without a lamp, a human being could not benefit from the sense of sight at night; likewise, without the Sun, animals could not benefit from the sense of sight at all. He highlighted this specific benefit of the Sun, rather than its many other benefits, because it is the most noble and the most evident of them.

Then the Almighty drew attention to the aforementioned "Providence" <term>Providence: (Arabic: *'Inaya*) The philosophical concept that God actively maintains the universe to ensure the well-being of His creatures.</term> found in the falling of rain: that it only falls for the sake of plants and animals. The fact that rain falls in a specific measure and at specific times for the growth of crops cannot be a result of mere chance; rather, the cause is the Care for what is here on Earth. Thus, the Almighty said: **﴿ And We sent down from the rain-clouds pouring water, that We may bring forth thereby grain and plants, and gardens of thick foliage ﴾** <note>Quran 78:14-16.</note>. The verses in the Quran that draw attention to this concept are numerous, such as the words of the Almighty: **﴿ Have you not seen how God created seven heavens... ﴾**

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that the physical structure of the universe is not random but purposefully designed to support human life. He transitions from describing the celestial bodies to a direct theological critique of the Ash'arite school of thought.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...in layers, and made the moon therein a light and made the sun a lamp? And Allah has caused you to grow from the earth as a plant.** <note>Quran 71:15-17. Ibn Rushd is citing these verses to show that the Quran itself points to the functional design of the universe as evidence of God.</note> And like the Almighty's saying: **﴿ [It is] Allah who made for you the earth a carpet and the sky a structure ﴾** <note>Quran 2:22.</note>.

If we were to pursue the abundance of these verses and detail what they point to regarding the <term>Divine Providence</term> <note>Arabic: *al-‘Inaya*. This is the "Argument from Design," which posits that the universe's suitability for life proves the existence of a purposeful Creator.</note> which indicates the <term>Craftsman</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Sani‘*. A common philosophical term for God, emphasizing His role as an artisan or builder of the cosmos.</note> and the <term>Created</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Masnu‘*.</note>, it would fill many volumes. That is not our intention in this book. Perhaps, if God grants us more time and we find the leisure, we shall write a book specifically on the Providence that the Mighty Book <note>The Quran.</note> has highlighted.

You should know that this type of <term>inference</term> <note>Arabic: *istidlal*. The logical process of moving from observed evidence to a conclusion.</note> is in total opposition to the inference claimed by the <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>A major school of Sunni Islamic theology. Ibn Rushd, an Aristotelian philosopher, often criticized them for what he saw as their lack of logical rigor.</note> to be the path to knowing God, may He be glorified. This is because they claim that the evidence of existing things for God, Blessed and Exalted, is not because of the wisdom within them that necessitates Providence, but rather through the principle of **Possibility** <note>Arabic: *al-Jawaz*. Also translated as contingency. The Ash'arite view was that because the world *could* have been different, its current state requires a Willer to choose one possibility over another.</note>. That is, because of the possibility apparent in all existing things—that it is permissible in the mind for a thing to be in its current state or its opposite.

If this "possibility" were equal on both sides, then there would be no <term>Wisdom</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Hikma*.</note> here, and no harmony would exist at all between man and the parts of the world. This is because, if it were possible—as they claim—for existing things to be other than they are just as easily as they currently exist, then there is no harmony between man and the things whose creation God has bestowed as a favor upon him, and for which He commanded him to be grateful.

This opinion [of the Ash'arites] implies that the possibility of creating man as a part of this world is the same as the possibility of creating him in a void, for example, which they believe exists. Indeed, they believe it is possible for man to have been created in another shape and another constitution while still performing human actions. According to them, it is even possible that there could be a part of another world—different in its definition and description from this world—so there would be no "blessing" here to bestow upon man. This is because whatever is not necessary, nor contributes to the best possible state for man's existence, is something man can do without. And whatever he can do without, its existence is not a "favor" granted to him. All of this contradicts what is ingrained in human nature.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Quranic proof for God is based on "Providence"—the universe’s perfect design for human life. He critiques the Ash'arite theologians, arguing that their focus on the "possibility" of things being different undermines the concept of Divine Wisdom and the blessings of creation.</summary>
