<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the Ash’arite theological school. Having previously argued that the universe shows signs of purposeful design (Providence), he now addresses the specific Ash'arite claim that natural "causes" have no real power, but are merely "habits" established by God.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

In short, just as whoever denies the existence of <term>effects</term> <note>Arabic: *al-musabbabat*. Literally "the things caused."</note> being ordered by <term>causes</term> <note>Arabic: *al-asbab*. Literally "the means" or "reasons."</note> in the industrial arts—or whose understanding fails to grasp them—has no knowledge of the craft nor the <term>Craftsman</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Sani‘*. A term for God emphasizing His role as a purposeful artisan.</note>; likewise, whoever denies the existence of the ordering of effects upon causes in this world has denied the Wise Craftsman, may God be highly exalted above such a thing. 

Their claim that God Almighty established a **"habit"** <note>Arabic: *‘ada*. The Ash'arite doctrine of "occasionalism," which suggests that what we see as cause-and-effect is actually God creating two separate events in constant succession.</note> with these causes, and that they have no actual influence on the effects by His permission, is a view very far from the requirements of **Wisdom**; indeed, it nullifies it. For if effects could exist without these causes just as easily as they exist through them, then what wisdom would there be in their being tied to these causes? 

This is because the existence of effects from causes does not fall outside of three possibilities: 

1. Either the existence of causes is for the sake of the effects out of **necessity** <note>Arabic: *al-idtirar*. Things required for basic survival.</note>—such as a human being nourished; 
2. Or it is for the sake of the **"better"**—meaning so that the effects are more perfect and complete, such as a human having two eyes; 
3. Or it is neither for the sake of the "better" nor out of necessity. 

In this last case, the existence of effects from causes would be by **chance** <note>Arabic: *al-ittifaq*. Coincidence or randomness.</note> and without purpose; thus, there would be no wisdom at all, nor would it indicate anything but coincidence. 

For instance, if the shape of the human hand, the number of its fingers, and its size were neither necessary nor for the sake of the "better" regarding its function of **grasping**—and in its ability to encompass all objects of different shapes and its suitability for holding the tools of all crafts—then the existence of the hand's actions arising from its shape, the number of its parts, and its size would be by chance. If that were the case, there would be no difference between a human being specifically granted a hand or a hoof, or any other shape granted to various animals that corresponds to their specific actions. 

In short, whenever we remove causes and effects, there remains nothing with which to respond to those who argue for chance—meaning those who say there is **no Craftsman** here, and that everything that has occurred in this world is only due to material causes.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that denying the natural link between cause and effect (causality) undermines the proof of God's wisdom and leaves no defense against atheists who claim the world is a product of chance.</summary>
<keywords>causality, Ash'arites, Divine Wisdom, teleology, chance vs. design, Ibn Rushd, occasionalism, natural philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the Ash’arite school of theology. He is moving from discussing "habits" to the Ash’arite argument that the universe is "contingent" (possible but not necessary), arguing that their logic actually leads to the conclusion that the world was created by chance rather than by a Wise Creator.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

For [the occurrence of] one of two possibilities is more rightfully attributed to **chance** than to a **chosen agent** <note>Arabic: *fa‘il mukhtar*. A technical term for God as a being who acts by free will rather than by natural necessity.</note>. 

This is because if the Ash’arite says that the existence of one of two possibilities—or several possibilities—is proof that there is a "Specifier" <note>Arabic: *mukhassis*. In Ash'arite theology, this is God's role in choosing one specific way for the world to be among many possibilities.</note> who acts, his opponents could respond that the existence of beings in one of several possible ways happens by **chance**. This is because the Will only acts for the sake of a specific cause or reason. That which occurs without a cause or reason is what we call **chance**, as we see many things happening in this manner. For example, we observe the <term>Elements</term> <note>Arabic: *al-ustuqussat*. Derived from the Greek *stoicheia*, referring to the four basic building blocks of the physical world: earth, air, fire, and water.</note> mixing together by chance, and from that mixture, a certain being arises; then they mix again in another way, and by chance, another being arises from that mixture. On this view, all existing things would be the result of mere coincidence.

As for us, we say that it is necessary for there to be an order and a system here, such that no more precise or perfect system could possibly exist. The mixtures of elements are limited and measured, and the beings resulting from them are necessary; because this order is constant and never fails, it cannot possibly exist by chance. This is because what occurs by chance is, by definition, less than necessary <note>i.e., chance is random and inconsistent, whereas the laws of nature are stable.</note>. This is what God Almighty refers to in His saying:

> "The handiwork of God, who has perfected all things." <note>Quran 27:88. The original Arabic word *atqana* implies both perfection and solid craftsmanship.</note>

For, I wonder, what "perfection" could there be in existing things if they were merely based on **contingency**? <note>Arabic: *al-jawaz*. The idea that something could just as easily be one way or its opposite.</note> For a contingent thing has no more right to exist than its opposite. This is what God Almighty refers to in His saying:

> "You see no imperfection in the creation of the All-Merciful. Look again: can you see any rift?" <note>Quran 67:3. The word translated as "imperfection" (*tafawut*) literally means a lack of proportion or a "difference" that implies a mistake.</note>

And what imperfection could be greater than for all things to be capable of existing in another form, yet they exist in this one—and perhaps that non-existent form would have been better than the existing one? Anyone who claims, for instance, that if the movement of the heavens from East to West were instead from West to East, or vice versa, it would make no difference to the **craftsmanship of the world**, has effectively nullified **Wisdom**. It is the same as someone claiming that if the right side of an animal were the left, and the left were the right, it would make no difference to the **craftsmanship of the animal**. For if one of two possibilities...

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) philosophical critique of the Ash’arite school of theology. He is specifically attacking their "argument from contingency," where they claim that because the world *could* have been different, its specific form proves a "Specifier" (God) chose it. Ibn Rushd argues this logic is flawed because it makes the universe look like a product of chance rather than divine wisdom.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

Just as it can be said that a thing exists in one of two possible states because of a **chosen agent** <note>Arabic: *fa‘il mukhtar*. A technical term for a being who acts by free choice rather than natural necessity.</note>, it can also be said that it exists in one of those two states by **chance** <note>Arabic: *al-ittifaq*.</note>. This is because we see many "contingent" things <note>Things that are possible but not necessary.</note> occurring in one specific way rather than another through their agents by mere coincidence.

You will observe that people collectively consider "base" or "lowly" products to be those that they feel could have been made in a different way. In fact, it is the very lowliness found in many such products that leads people to think they happened by **chance**. Conversely, they consider "noble" products to be those which they believe could not possibly exist in a state more perfect or excellent than the form their maker has given them. 

Therefore, this opinion held by the **theologians** <term>theologians</term> <note>Arabic: *al-mutakallimun*. Scholars who used dialectical reasoning to defend religious creed, often criticized by philosophers for lacking rigorous logic.</note> is contrary to both the **Divine Law** and to **Wisdom**. What we mean when we say that the argument for **contingency** <note>Arabic: *al-jawaz*. The theological principle that every aspect of the world is "possible" and could have been otherwise.</note> is closer to denying the Creator than to proving His existence—while also stripping Him of Wisdom—is this: if one does not recognize that there are "intermediaries" <note>Logical steps or natural laws.</note> between the starting points and the final goals of crafted things, upon which the existence of those goals depends, then there is no order or arrangement. 

If there is no order or arrangement, then there is no evidence that these existing things have a Willing, Knowing Agent. This is because it is the **arrangement**, the **order**, and the building of **effects upon causes** that demonstrates they originated from knowledge and wisdom. 

As for the existence of a "contingent" thing in one of two possible ways, this could result from an unwise agent by **chance**. For example, a stone falls to the earth because of its weight; it lands on one specific side rather than another, in one place rather than another, or in one position rather than another. [The theologians'] argument leads necessarily to either the total denial of the existence of a Creator, or the denial of a Wise, Knowing Creator—may God be exalted and His names be sanctified far above such a thing! 

As for what led the **Ash’arite** <term>Ash’arites</term> <note>A major school of Sunni theology that emphasized God's absolute power, often denying natural cause-and-effect to ensure God is the only "True Actor."</note> theologians to this position, it was their attempt to escape from acknowledging the action of **natural forces** <note>Arabic: *al-quwa al-tabi‘iyya*.</note> which God has embedded in the existing things which...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Ash'arite focus on "contingency" (that the world could have been anything) accidentally implies the world was created by chance. He asserts that true evidence of God is found in the perfect, necessary order of cause and effect, which reflects Divine Wisdom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theologians. In the previous section, he argued that their doctrine of "contingency"—the idea that everything in the universe could have been different—implies that the world is a product of chance rather than divine wisdom. Here, he moves to a defense of natural causality, arguing that God works *through* causes rather than by bypassing them.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...here, just as He implanted souls within them and other such **effective causes** <term>effective causes</term> <note>Arabic: *al-asbab al-mu’athira*. In philosophy, these are factors that have a direct power to produce a specific effect, such as fire having the "effective cause" to burn.</note>. Thus, they fled from the claim that there are any acting causes here other than God. But how far from the truth that is! For there is no agent here except God, inasmuch as He is the Inventor of the causes themselves; their existence as effective causes only occurs by His permission and His preservation of their existence. We shall explain this meaning more fully in the chapter regarding **Divine Decree and Predestination** <term>Divine Decree and Predestination</term> <note>Arabic: *al-qada’ wa-l-qadar*. The theological concept of God’s ultimate foreknowledge and execution of all events.</note>.

Furthermore, they feared that by affirming **natural causes** <note>Arabic: *al-asbab al-tabi‘iyya*.</note>, it would follow that the world resulted from a natural process alone. Yet, had they known that **Nature** <note>Arabic: *al-tabi‘a*.</note> is itself created, and that nothing points more clearly to a **Craftsman** <note>Arabic: *al-Sani‘*. A common term for God in this context, emphasizing His role as a purposeful designer.</note> than the existence of a being possessing such orderly characteristics, they would have realized that those who deny nature have discarded a vast portion of the evidence used to prove the existence of the Wise Creator. By denying a part of God’s creation, they have effectively denied one of the Creator’s acts—and this is nearly as grave as denying one of His attributes.

In general, because the views of these people were taken from **superficial impressions** <note>Arabic: *badi’ al-ra’y*. Initial thoughts or "common sense" observations that haven't been subjected to deep logical scrutiny.</note>—which are merely the conjectures that occur to a person at first glance—and because it appears to the superficial mind that the term "**Will**" <note>Arabic: *al-irada*.</note> is only applied to someone who is capable of doing a thing or its opposite, they believed that if they did not establish that all existing things are "contingent" <note>Meaning they could just as easily not exist or be different.</note>, they would be unable to prove the existence of a Creator who possesses Will. Therefore, they claimed that all existing things are merely possible, so they could use that to prove that the First Principle is a Willing Agent. 

It is as if they did not see that the order found in **manufactured goods** <note>Arabic: *al-umur al-sina‘iyya*. Literally "industrial" or "crafted" things.</note> is necessary for their function, and yet that order still originates from a Willing Agent, namely the craftsman. These people neglected the consequence of their argument: the denial of **Wisdom** <note>Arabic: *al-hikma*.</note> in the Creator, and the introduction of **accidental cause** <note>Arabic: *al-sabab al-ittifaqi*. Essentially, coincidence or chance.</note> into the world. For things done by "will" without an intended purpose—by which I mean a specific goal or end—are considered futile and attributed to chance. As we have said, it is well known that the order found in the acts of nature necessitates that they originate from a Wise Creator; otherwise, that order would be entirely by chance.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that denying natural causes undermines the proof of God's wisdom, as the orderly laws of nature are the best evidence for a purposeful Creator. He critiques theologians for thinking that "Will" requires things to be random or contingent, using the analogy of a craftsman whose purposeful designs are both intended and necessary.</summary>

<keywords>natural causes, divine will, wisdom, Ash'arites, causality, chance, craftsman analogy, Ibn Rushd, theology vs philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s defense of natural causality against the Ash’arite theologians. Having previously argued that denying the "effective causes" in nature undermines the concept of divine wisdom, he now elaborates on how God uses nature as an instrument and how the religious Law (Shari‘a) communicates these complex truths to the general public through relatable imagery.</meta>

90
-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

They found it necessary to deny the actions of **Nature** <note>In this context, "Nature" refers to the inherent properties and laws that govern the physical world, which theologians often feared would replace God’s direct action.</note>, thereby denying a host of **God’s soldiers** <note>original: "jundan min junud Allah." Ibn Rushd uses this poetic metaphor to describe natural laws as instruments that carry out God's will.</note> that He has employed to bring many beings into existence by His permission and to preserve them. This is because God, Blessed and Exalted, brought beings into existence through causes He subjected for them from the outside—namely, the celestial bodies—and through causes He placed within the essences of those beings—namely, souls and **natural powers** <term>natural powers</term> <note>Arabic: *al-quwa al-tabi‘iyya*. These are the internal forces in plants, animals, and minerals that allow them to grow, change, or remain stable.</note>. Through these, the existence of all beings is preserved and Divine Wisdom is perfected. Who, then, is more unjust than one who nullifies this wisdom and fabricates a lie against God?

This is the extent of the distortion that has occurred within the **Religious Law** <note>Arabic: *al-Shari‘a*. Here meaning the correct understanding of Islamic doctrine.</note> regarding this concept, as well as other concepts we have explained previously and will continue to explain hereafter, God willing. It has become clear from this that the religious paths God established for His servants—so they may know the world is His creation and handiwork—are those that demonstrate the **Wisdom and Providence** <term>Providence</term> <note>Arabic: *al-‘inaya*. The philosophical concept that the universe is intentionally designed to support life and human existence.</note> evident in all existing things, especially in mankind. This method is as clear to the intellect as the sun is to the senses.

As for the path taken to help the **general public** <term>general public</term> <note>Arabic: *al-jumhur*. In Ibn Rushd's philosophy, this refers to non-philosophers who require clear, physical imagery rather than abstract logical proofs to understand spiritual truths.</note> conceive of this meaning, it is the method of **representation using the observable world** <note>Arabic: *al-tamthil bi-l-shahid*. The pedagogical technique of explaining the unknown (creation) by comparing it to things people see every day (like a craftsman making a house).</note>, even if the reality has no exact parallel in our daily experience. It is impossible for the public to grasp the true essence of something that has no likeness in the visible world. Thus, the Exalted informed them that His creation of the world occurred in **time** and that He created it **from something**, since in the observable world, no created thing is known to exist except through these two conditions.

Therefore, the Glorified said, reporting on the state of affairs before the world’s existence: **"And His Throne was upon the water"** <note>Qur'an 11:7. This verse provides a physical image—water and a throne—to represent a pre-creation state that the public can visualize.</note>. The Exalted also said: **"Indeed, your Lord is God, who created the heavens and the earth in six days"** <note>Qur'an 7:54.</note> and: **"Then He directed Himself to the heaven while it was smoke"** <note>Qur'an 41:11.</note>, along with other verses in the Mighty Book regarding this meaning. It is obligatory that none of this be reinterpreted <note>original: *la yuta’awwal*. Ibn Rushd warns against using complex metaphorical interpretation (*ta’wil*) when speaking to the public, as it confuses their simple faith.</note> for the general public, nor should anyone attempt to present it as anything other than this representation. To do otherwise would be to nullify the wisdom of the Law. If it were said to the public that the religious creed regarding the world is that it is originated, but was created from nothing and outside of time, that is something they would be unable to conceive.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that nature and its laws are "God's soldiers" through which He exercises His wisdom, and that the Qur'an uses physical imagery (like the Throne and water) because the general public cannot grasp abstract concepts like creation outside of time.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Divine Wisdom, Providence, General Public (Jumhur), Representation (Tamthil), Creation, Qur'anic Interpretation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s exploration of how the Religious Law (Shari‘a) communicates complex truths. On the previous page, he argued that denying natural causality undermines Divine Wisdom. Here, he shifts to how the Law uses specific imagery to explain the creation of the world to the masses without causing the intellectual confusion that arises from technical theological terminology.</meta>

91
-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

Scholars, not to mention the general public, should not—as we have stated—deviate in the Religious Law from the conceptualization it has established for the masses. Nor should they disclose anything else to them. For this type of representation concerning the creation of the world is what is found in the Quran, the Torah, and all other revealed Books. One of the wonders in this regard is that the representation of the world’s creation found in the Law matches the meaning of <term>temporal origination</term> <note>Arabic: *al-huduth*. In Islamic theology, this refers to something coming into existence after being non-existent.</note> as observed in the physical world. However, the Law did not explicitly use this specific term (*huduth*). This was a subtle hint to the scholars that the origination of the world is not exactly like the origination observed in everyday experience.

Instead, the Law used terms like "creation" (*khalq*) and "originating" (*futur*). These terms are suitable for conceptualizing both meanings—that is, the <term>temporal origination</term> observed in the physical world, and the origination that logical demonstration leads scholars to conclude regarding the unseen realm. Therefore, using the technical terms "temporal origination" (*huduth*) or **pre-eternity** <term>pre-eternity</term> <note>Arabic: *al-qidam*. The concept of existing without a beginning in time.</note> is a **religious innovation** <note>original: "bid'a." In this context, it refers to introducing technical, non-scriptural language that confuses the faithful.</note> within the Law. It leads to great doubt that corrupts the beliefs of the masses, especially the **dialecticians** among them.

This is why the most intense confusion and the greatest doubt occurred for the **theologians** <note>Arabic: *al-mutakallimun*. Scholars who used dialectical reason to defend religious doctrines.</note> of our faith—namely, the <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>A dominant school of Sunni theology. Ibn Rushd frequently critiques them for using logic that he finds inconsistent or confusing to the public.</note>. Specifically, when they declared—and this is an innovation as we have said—that "God wills with an eternal will," and established that the world is "temporally originated," they were asked: "How can a temporal effect arise from an eternal will?"

They replied that the eternal will became attached to bringing the world into existence at a specific time—namely, the time in which it appeared. To this, it was said: "If the relationship of the Willing Agent to the produced object at the time of its non-existence is exactly the same as His relationship to it at the time of its creation, then the object's existence was no more likely at one time than another." If there was no specific action at the moment of existence that was absent during the moment of non-existence, then the object would not have been created. But if the relationship was different, then there must necessarily be a "new" or "temporal" will. It would be more appropriate to say that a temporal effect comes from an eternal **action**; for whatever logical problem arises regarding the "action" also arises regarding the "will."

Indeed, it is said to them: "When the specific time for the world's existence arrived and it came into being, did it exist by an eternal action or a temporal action?" If they say "by an eternal action," they have permitted a temporal effect to come from an eternal action. If they say "by a temporal action"...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Religious Law uses accessible language like "creation" rather than technical terms like "origination" to avoid confusing the public. He critiques the Ash'arite theologians for creating logical paradoxes by using technical terms to describe God's eternal will in relation to the creation of the world.</summary>
<keywords>temporal origination (huduth), pre-eternity (qidam), Ash'arites, divine will, creation of the world, religious innovation (bid'a), theologians (mutakallimun)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) critique of the theologians (Mutakallimun). On the previous page, he argued that the Quran uses accessible language for the masses regarding creation, avoiding technical jargon. Here, he explains how technical debates about the "Divine Will"—whether it is eternal or temporal—are unnecessary innovations that confuse the faithful and deviate from both the simple truth of the Law and the complex certainty of philosophy.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...they would be forced to conclude that there is a **temporal will** <term>temporal will</term> <note>Arabic: *irada muhdatha*. A will that comes into existence at a specific point in time, implying change within the Divine essence.</note>. If they claim that the will is identical to the act itself, they have uttered an absurdity; for the will is the cause of the act in the one who wills. If the one who wills, upon willing something at a specific time, found that thing existed at its appointed time without any further action proceeding from that prior will, then that thing would exist without an agent. Furthermore, it might be thought that if it is necessary for a created object to proceed from a created (temporal) will, then it must be necessary for an eternal object to proceed from an **eternal will** <term>eternal will</term> <note>Arabic: *irada qadima*. A will that has existed forever without beginning.</note>. Otherwise, the object of the eternal will and the temporal will would be one and the same, which is impossible.

All these doubts were only stirred up in Islam by the **theologians** <term>theologians</term> <note>original: *Ahl al-Kalam*. Scholars who used dialectical inquiry to defend religious tenets.</note> through their explicit declarations in the Law of things for which God gave no permission. For it is not stated in the Law that He, the Exalted, wills through a temporal will nor an eternal one. Thus, they neither followed the literal meanings of the Law—thereby becoming those whose happiness and salvation lie in following the literal word—nor did they reach the rank of the **people of certainty** <term>people of certainty</term> <note>Arabic: *ahl al-yaqin*. Philosophers who use logical demonstration to reach absolute truth.</note>, who find their happiness in the demonstrative sciences.

For this reason, they are neither among the true scholars nor among the generality of the believing masses who affirm the faith. Rather, they are among "those in whose hearts is a deviation" and "in whose hearts is a disease" <note>This is a reference to the Quran (3:7 and 2:10), describing those who pursue ambiguous verses to sow confusion.</note>. They say things with their **outer speech** <note>original: *al-nutq al-kharij*.</note> which their **inner speech** <note>original: *al-nutq al-batin*. Refers to internal rational conviction.</note> contradicts. The cause of this is sectarianism and passion. Indeed, becoming accustomed to such doctrines can cause a total detachment from rational principles, as we see happening to those who have mastered the methods of the **Ash'arite school** <term>Ash'arite school</term> <note>Arabic: *al-Ash'ariyya*. The dominant school of Sunni theology, which Ibn Rushd frequently criticized for its rejection of natural causality.</note> and have been trained in them since youth. These people are undoubtedly veiled by the "veil of habit" and their upbringing. What we have mentioned regarding this issue is sufficient for our purpose. Let us move on to the second issue.

### ۞ The Second Issue: The Sending of Messengers ۞

The investigation of this issue takes place in two parts. First: the proof of the existence of Messengers. Second: the explanation of how to identify that a specific person claiming a message is truly one of them and is not lying in his claim.

As for the existence of this class of people, a group has attempted to prove it through **analogy** <note>original: *al-qiyas*.</note>. These are the theologians, and they said: it has been proven...

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...that God is a **speaker** <term>speaker</term> <note>original: *mutakallim*. A reference to the divine attribute of speech, central to debates about the nature of the Quran.</note>, a **willer** <term>willer</term> <note>original: *murid*. One who possesses the attribute of Will (*irada*).</note>, and a master over His servants. It is permissible for a speaker who wills and who is the master of his servants' affairs—in the **witnessed world** <term>witnessed world</term> <note>original: *al-shahid*. The physical, observable world used by theologians as a basis for making analogies about God.</note>—to send a messenger to the servants he owns. Thus, it must follow that this is possible in the **unseen realm** <term>unseen realm</term> <note>original: *al-gha'ib*. The divine or metaphysical realm that cannot be directly observed.</note>. They supported this position by refuting the absurdities that the **Brahmins** <term>Brahmins</term> <note>original: *al-Barahima*. In this context, it refers to rationalist groups who argued that prophecy is unnecessary because human reason is sufficient for moral guidance.</note> seek to impose regarding the existence of messengers from God. 

They said: "If this concept has been shown to be possible in the unseen realm, just as it exists in the witnessed world, it also appears in the witnessed world that if a man stands in the presence of the King and says: 'O people, I am the King's messenger to you,' and signs of the King appear upon him, it becomes necessary to acknowledge that the claim of that messenger is true." 

They said: "This sign is the appearance of a **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *mu'jiza*. Literally 'an act that renders others incapable [of replicating it].'</note> at the hands of the messenger." This method is persuasive and is somewhat suitable for the **general public** <term>general public</term> <note>original: *al-jumhur*. The masses who require accessible rhetorical proofs rather than technical philosophy.</note>; however, when scrutinized, certain flaws appear within it because of the principles they establish. 

This is because our belief in the one claiming a **message** <term>message</term> <note>original: *al-risala*. The office of prophecy or messengership.</note> from the King is only valid when we know that the sign which appeared on him is indeed the sign of the King's messengers. This is known either by the King saying to his subjects: "Whomever you see possessing such-and-such a sign of my specific signs, he is a messenger from me," or by knowing through the King’s custom that such signs never appear except on his messengers. 

If this is the case, a questioner might ask: "How does it become evident that the appearance of miracles at the hands of certain people is the specific sign of messengers?" This must be perceived either through **divine law** <term>divine law</term> <note>original: *al-shar'*. Transmitted religious tradition.</note> or through **reason** <term>reason</term> <note>original: *al-'aql*.</note>. It is impossible for this to be perceived through divine law because the law itself has not yet been established <note>The messenger is still trying to prove the law exists; you cannot use the law to prove the messenger.</note>. Reason, as well, cannot judge that this sign is specific to messengers unless it has observed its occurrence many times for people whose messengership is acknowledged, and it never appeared at the hands of anyone else. 

This is because the establishment of messengership is built upon two premises: 
1. That this claimant of the message has produced a miracle at his hands.
2. That everyone at whose hands a miracle appears is a prophet. 

From these two, it follows...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the theological argument for prophecy, which uses an analogy of a human king to explain God's messengers. He argues that miracles alone do not logically prove a messenger's claim without a prior way to verify that such signs are exclusive to God's chosen prophets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critical examination of the proofs for prophecy used by the Mutakallimun (dialectical theologians). Having previously discussed their analogy between a human king and God, he now turns to a logical deconstruction of the syllogism: "This person performed a miracle; everyone who performs a miracle is a messenger; therefore, this person is a messenger."</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...by necessity that this man is a prophet. As for the premise stating that "this claimant of messengership has performed a **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *mu'jiza*. Literally "that which renders others incapable." In Islamic theology, it is a supernatural act performed by a prophet to prove their divine mission.</note>," we may say that this premise is derived from sensory perception—provided we concede that there are indeed acts appearing at the hands of created beings which we can certain are not achieved through any strange craft of a maker, nor through a specific property of substances, and that what appears is not a mere illusion.

As for the second premise, which states that "everyone at whose hands a miracle appears is a **messenger** <term>messenger</term> <note>original: *rasul*. A prophet sent with a specific law or message for a community.</note>," this is only valid after acknowledging the existence of messengers and acknowledging that miracles never appear except to those whose messengership is authentic. 

We say that this premise is only valid for one who already acknowledges the existence of **messengership** <term>messengership</term> <note>original: *al-risala*. The office or function of being a divine messenger.</note> and the existence of miracles because this is the nature of a **predicative statement** <term>predicative statement</term> <note>original: *al-qawl al-khabari*. A logical statement that asserts a relationship between a subject and a predicate.</note>. I mean that if a person has it demonstrated to them, for example, that "the world is created," it must already be known to them that "the world" exists and that "created things" exist. Since this is the case, a critic might ask: "How do we know the truth of our statement that 'everyone at whose hands a miracle appears is a messenger' when the existence of messengership itself has not yet been established?" This remains true even if we concede the existence of the miracle in the manner required for it to be truly miraculous. It is necessary that the components of this statement—the **subject and the predicate** <term>subject and the predicate</term> <note>original: *al-mubtada' wa-l-khabar*. The two essential parts of a sentence; logically, the thing being talked about and the quality attributed to it.</note>—be recognized as existing before acknowledging the truth of judging one by the second.

It is not for anyone to say that the existence of messengers is indicated by **reason** <term>reason</term> <note>original: *al-'aql*. The faculty of the intellect.</note> because it is "permissible" in the mind. For the **possibility** <term>possibility</term> <note>original: *al-jawaz*. Often translated as contingency or permissibility.</note> they point to is merely ignorance <note>Ibn Rushd argues that the theologians call something "possible" simply because they do not know it to be impossible, rather than understanding its actual nature.</note>; it is not the possibility found in the **nature of existents** <term>nature of existents</term> <note>original: *tabi'at al-mawjudat*. The inherent ontological properties of things in the world.</note>, such as our saying "it is possible for rain to fall or not to fall." This is because the possibility belonging to the nature of an existent is that a thing is perceived to exist at one time and be absent at another, as is the case with the falling of rain. The intellect then renders a universal judgment on this nature as being "possible." The **necessary** <term>necessary</term> <note>original: *al-wajib*. That which must be and whose non-existence is inconceivable.</note> is the opposite of this; it is that which is always perceived to exist, leading the intellect to render a universal and final judgment that this nature cannot change or be overturned.

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the logical structure of the theologians' proof for prophecy, arguing that one cannot prove a prophet's validity through miracles without first establishing the existence and nature of prophecy itself. He also distinguishes between "possibility" as a lack of knowledge versus "possibility" as an inherent property of nature.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, miracles, prophecy, logic, predicative statement, possibility, nature of existents, Ash'arites, epistemology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's critique of the Ash'arite theologians' logical framework for proving prophecy. On the previous page, he began dissecting the syllogism that links miracles to the truth of a messenger's claim. Here, he focuses on the logical definition of "possibility" and the circularity of using miracles as a definitive sign without prior empirical or rational grounds.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

If the opponent had admitted to the existence of a single **messenger** <term>messenger</term> <note>original: *rasul*. A prophet bringing a specific divine law.</note> at any point in time, it would have become apparent that **messengership** <term>messengership</term> <note>original: *al-risala*</note> is among those matters whose existence is **possible** <term>possible</term> <note>original: *ja'iz*. In this context, it refers to something that is logically "permissible" or "possible" to occur, as opposed to necessary or impossible.</note>. However, since the opponent claims that such an event has never been perceived, the "possibility" they claim is merely an expression of their ignorance regarding which of the two opposites—**possibility** <term>possibility</term> <note>original: *al-imkan*</note> or **impossibility** <term>impossibility</term> <note>original: *al-imtina'*</note>—is actually the case. For those people who have correctly affirmed the possibility of the existence of messengers, this possibility was only validated for us because we have actually perceived the existence of messengers among them. 

You might argue that perceiving the existence of messengers among humanity indicates the possibility of their being sent by the Creator, just as the fact that **Amr** <note>A traditional Arabic placeholder name, similar to "John Doe."</note> sent a messenger indicates the possibility of **Zaid** <note>Another traditional placeholder name.</note> doing the same. However, this would require an equality between the two natures (the human and the divine), and therein lies a great difficulty. Even if we assume this possibility in itself—even if it were regarding the future—it would be a possibility based on the nature of the matter, not based on our lack of knowledge. 

But when one of the two opposites regarding this "possibility" has already passed into existence, then this possibility exists only in our knowledge. In reality, the matter itself is already settled as one of the two opposites: either he was sent, or he was not sent. Thus, we have nothing but ignorance, similar to doubting whether Amr sent a messenger in the past or did not. This is different from doubting whether he will send a messenger tomorrow or not. For if we are ignorant of whether Zaid, for instance, sent a messenger in the past, we cannot rightly judge that someone who displays Zaid’s sign is indeed his messenger—unless we first know that such a sign is specifically the mark of his messenger, and that can only happen after we already know that he has, in fact, sent a messenger.

Beyond all this: even if we grant that messengership exists and the **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *al-mu'jiz*. An act that "renders others incapable" of mimicking it, intended to prove a prophet's claim.</note> exists, from what basis is it valid for us to conclude that whoever performs a miracle is necessarily a messenger? This is because such a judgment cannot be taken from **scriptural tradition** <term>scriptural tradition</term> <note>original: *al-sam'*. Knowledge derived from revelation or reported tradition.</note>, since the authority of tradition is not established prior to this very principle. To do so would be a case of **circular reasoning** <term>circular reasoning</term> <note>original: *tashi' al-shay' bi-nafsihi*. Literally, "proving a thing by itself."</note>, which is logically invalid. There is also no way to claim the validity of this premise through **empirical experience** <term>experience and habit</term> <note>original: *al-tajriba wa al-'ada*. The way we learn things through repeated observation of the natural order.</note>, unless you have observed miracles appearing specifically at the hands of messengers—meaning those whose messengership you already acknowledge—and you have never seen them at the hands of others. Only then would it serve as a definitive sign of...

**Vocabulary:** 
**Messengership** <note>original: *al-risala*</note>, **Messengers** <note>original: *al-rusul*</note>, **Possibility** <note>original: *al-imkan*</note>, **Impossibility** <note>original: *al-imtina'*</note>, **The Miracle** <note>original: *al-mu'jiz*</note>, **Scriptural Tradition** <note>original: *al-sam'*</note>, **Circular Reasoning** <note>original: *tashi' al-shay' bi-nafsihi*</note>.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that "possibility" is often used to mask ignorance and that miracles cannot logically prove prophecy unless one already knows messengers exist, otherwise the argument is circular.</summary>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...distinguishing who is a **messenger** <term>messenger</term> <note>original: *rasul*. In Islamic theology, a messenger is a prophet sent with a specific divine law or scripture.</note> from God from who is not—that is, between the one whose claim is true and the one whose claim is false. Regarding these matters, it is evident that the **theologians** <term>theologians</term> <note>original: *al-mutakallimun*. Religious scholars who used rationalist dialectics to defend the faith.</note> missed this point concerning the way a **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *al-mu'jiz*. Literally "the incapacitating act," an event that breaks natural laws to prove a prophet's truthfulness.</note> functions as evidence. This is because they treated **possibility** <term>possibility</term> <note>original: *al-imkan*</note> as equivalent to **existence** <term>existence</term> <note>original: *al-wujud*</note>—specifically, that "possibility" which is actually just a lack of knowledge <note>Ibn Rushd argues that just because we don't know if something is impossible, we shouldn't assume it is a "possible" proof of God's will.</note>. Then, they validated this premise: that anyone from whom a miracle proceeds is a messenger.

Yet, this premise is only correct if the miracle indicates the nature of **messengership** <term>messengership</term> <note>original: *al-risala*</note> itself and the One who sends the message. The human **intellect** <term>intellect</term> <note>original: *al-'aql*</note> does not have the inherent power to see a wondrous act that breaks natural laws—which everyone perceives as divine—as a definitive proof of the existence of messengership. This is only possible if one believes that whoever performs such things is virtuous, and a virtuous person does not lie. However, it only proves this person is a messenger if it is already accepted that "messengership" is a real thing that exists, and that such supernatural acts only appear at the hands of a messenger and no other virtuous person.

The reason a miracle does not inherently prove messengership is that the intellect perceives no logical connection between the two—unless one admits that a miracle is an actual "work" or "function" of messengership. Consider the example of **healing** <term>healing</term> <note>original: *al-ibra'*</note>, which is a function of the art of **medicine** <term>medicine</term> <note>original: *al-tibb*</note>. If someone performs the act of healing, it proves the existence of the medical art and that the person is a physician. 

This is one of the weaknesses in this style of reasoning. Furthermore, even if we were to admit the existence of messengership—while still treating the "possibility" born of ignorance as if it were established existence—and we made the miracle a sign of the claimant's truthfulness, it follows by necessity that this proof would not be binding for someone who believes that miracles can appear at the hands of those who are *not* messengers. This is a problem for the theologians themselves, because they allow for miracles to appear at the hands of a **sorcerer** <term>sorcerer</term> <note>original: *al-sahir*</note> or a **saint** <term>saint</term> <note>original: *al-wali*. A "friend of God" believed to possess God-given wonders (karamat) that are not prophetic miracles.</note>. 

As for the condition they set to solve this—claiming that a miracle only proves messengership when it is accompanied by a formal claim of prophecy, and that God would not allow a miracle to appear for a liar—this is a claim without any proof. It is known neither by tradition nor by reason. I mean that if someone claims...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the logical link between miracles and prophecy, arguing that a miracle only proves a messenger's status if the miracle is a functional product of prophecy itself, much like healing is a product of medicine.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, messengership, logic, theologians, Ash'arites, medicine analogy, sorcerer, saint, possibility vs existence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s critique of the Ash’arite theologians' logic regarding miracles. On the previous page, he argued that the mere "possibility" of a miracle does not provide a solid rational foundation for identifying a true messenger of God. Here, he examines the arguments regarding whether miracles can appear for liars or sorcerers, and moves toward his central argument: that the Quran itself is the Prophet Muhammad's true miracle.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...anyone through whose hands a false claim appears, a **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *al-mu'jiz*. An act that renders others "incapable" of duplicating it, serving as a sign of divine authority.</note> will not be manifested. However, as we have stated, since it is not logically impossible, one might argue that such signs only appear through the **virtuous** <note>original: *al-fadhilin*. Those of high moral and spiritual standing.</note> whom God cares for. If such people were to lie, they would no longer be virtuous, and thus no miracle would appear through them. 

Yet, the persuasive power found in this argument does not exist for those who allow that wonders can appear at the hands of a **sorcerer** <term>sorcerer</term> <note>original: *sahir*</note>, for a sorcerer is not virtuous. This is the inherent weakness in this method of reasoning. Because of this, some people believe that the safest position is to hold that **wonders** <term>wonders</term> <note>original: *al-khawariq*. Literally "the breaking of habits" or natural laws.</note> only appear through **prophets** <term>prophets</term> <note>original: *al-anbiya*</note>, and that **magic** <term>magic</term> <note>original: *al-sihr*</note> is merely an illusion <note>original: *takhyil*</note> and not an actual **transformation of substance** <note>original: *qalb 'ayn*. The physical change of one object into another, like a staff into a serpent.</note>. Among these thinkers are those who, for this reason, even deny the **charismatic wonders** <term>charismatic wonders</term> <note>original: *al-karamat*. Miracles performed by saints or holy people who are not prophets.</note> of the saints.

You can clearly see from the life of the **Lawgiver** <term>Lawgiver</term> <note>original: *al-shari'*. Here referring to the Prophet Muhammad as the conveyor of the divine law.</note> (peace and blessings be upon him) that he did not call any person or nation to believe in his message by performing a physical wonder, such as the **transformation of a substance** from one thing into another, as a precursor to his claim. The wonders that did appear through him (peace and blessings be upon him) occurred during the course of his life without him using them as a specific challenge to prove his status. This is indicated by the words of the Exalted:

> "And they say: 'We will not believe you until you make a spring gush forth from the earth for us...'" until the verse, "Say: 'Glory be to my Lord! Am I anything but a human messenger?'" <note>Quran 17:90-93. This verse shows the Prophet declining to perform "miracles on demand" to prove his station.</note>

And the Exalted says:

> "Nothing prevents Us from sending signs except that the former people denied them." <note>Quran 17:59. A reference to how previous nations were given physical miracles but still rejected their prophets.</note>

Rather, that which he used to call the people and challenged them with was the **Noble Book** <note>The Quran.</note>. The Exalted said:

> "Say: 'If humans and jinn were to gather to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce the like of it, even if they backed one another.'" <note>Quran 17:88. The "Challenge of the Quran" (tahaddi) is central to Islamic theology.</note>

And He said:

> "Then bring ten surahs like it, invented." <note>Quran 11:13. A "surah" is a chapter of the Quran.</note>

Since this is the case, the miracle of the Prophet (peace and blessings be upon him) which he used to challenge people and made the evidence for his truthfulness in his claim to messengership is the **Noble Book**. 

If it is asked: "This is clear, but how does it appear that the Noble Book is a miracle, and how does it prove he is a messenger—especially when you have already pointed out the weakness of using miracles to prove the existence of messengership in general, let alone the identity of a specific person?"

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s examination of the nature of miracles. Having previously critiqued the theologians' shaky logical foundations for prophecy, he now turns specifically to the Quran. He addresses the internal debates among Muslim scholars regarding whether the Quran is a miracle because of its linguistic superiority or because God physically prevented others from imitating it (the doctrine of Sarfa). He begins to lay out his own two-part philosophical proof for prophecy based on the social and spiritual function of divine legislation.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...sent with it. Even though people have disagreed regarding the nature of the **Quran** <term>Quran</term> being a **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *mu'jiz*. An act that renders humans "incapable" of imitation.</note>, for among them are those who believe that a condition for a miracle is that it must be of a different **genus** <note>original: *jins*. A logical category or type of action.</note> than habitual, natural actions. Since the Quran was of the same genus as natural actions in their view—as it is speech, even if it surpasses all human-made discourse—they argued that it only became a miracle through **divine prevention** <term>divine prevention</term> <note>original: *al-sarfa*. The theological doctrine that the miracle lay in God "turning away" or preventing humans from being able to produce something like the Quran, rather than the text itself being inherently supernatural.</note>. By this, they mean God prevented people from producing its like, not that the Quran itself exists in a transcendent state of eloquence. They argue that such a state, if it were possible, would only differ from the habitual by degree (the "more" or "less") and not by its fundamental category; and whatever differs only by degree belongs to the same genus.

Others held the view that the Quran is a miracle in itself, not through *divine prevention*. They did not require a **miraculous act** <term>miraculous act</term> <note>original: *al-khariq*. Literally, "that which breaks the habit" of nature.</note> to be of a different genus than natural actions. They believed it sufficient for an act to be of a natural genus but performed at such a peak of perfection that it remains beyond the reach of all humanity. 

We say: All of this is as the objector has stated, but the matter is not as these groups have imagined. The fact that the Quran serves as evidence for the truth of his—peace be upon him—**prophecy** <term>prophecy</term> <note>original: *nubuwwa*</note> is built, in our view, upon two principles which the Book itself has pointed out. 

The first principle is that the existence of the class of people called **Messengers** <term>Messengers</term> <note>original: *rusul*</note> and **Prophets** <term>Prophets</term> <note>original: *anbiya*</note> is self-evident. This class of people are those who establish **divine laws** <term>divine laws</term> <note>original: *al-shara'i'*</note> for humanity through **divine revelation** <term>divine revelation</term> <note>original: *wahy*</note>, and not through human learning. Indeed, their existence is only denied by those who deny **widely attested facts** <note>original: *al-umur al-mutawatira*. Information passed down by so many sources that it cannot be false.</note>, such as the existence of species we have not personally witnessed or figures famous for their wisdom and other traits. 

Indeed, the **philosophers** <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: *al-falasifa*</note> and all people—except those whose opinions carry no weight, namely the **materialists** <term>materialists</term> <note>original: *al-dahriyya*</note> <note>Those who believe the universe is eternal and governed only by time (*dahr*), denying a creator and revelation.</note>—agree that there are certain individuals among humanity who receive revelation. Through this revelation, they convey to people matters of knowledge and beautiful actions by which human happiness is completed, and they forbid them from false beliefs and ugly actions. This is the very function of the prophets.

The second principle is that anyone who performs this act—namely, the establishment of divine laws by revelation from God Almighty—is a prophet. This principle is also beyond doubt within the **innate human nature** <term>innate human nature</term> <note>original: *al-fitra*. The natural, primordial disposition of a human being.</note>. For just as it is self-evident that the function of medicine is to heal...

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument that prophecy is proven by the specific function of the prophet—legislating divine Law—rather than merely by supernatural physical feats. He uses the analogy of a doctor: just as healing proves someone is a physician, establishing a transformative Law through revelation proves someone is a prophet.</meta>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

Just as it is understood that one who performs healing is a physician, it is likewise self-evident that the specific activity of the **Prophets** (peace be upon them) is the establishing of **Laws** <term>Laws</term> <note>original: *shara'i'*. The systematic divine codes of conduct and belief.</note> through **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *wahy*. The process by which God communicates His word to a human messenger.</note> from God. Thus, anyone who performs this act is a Prophet. 

As for the first principle <note>The principle that Prophets are defined by the act of legislating through revelation.</note>, the Mighty Book has drawn attention to it in the words of the Almighty: *“Indeed, We have revealed to you as We revealed to Noah and the prophets after him,”* up to His words, *“and to Moses God spoke directly”* <note>Quran 4:163-164</note>. And the Almighty says: *“Say: I am not a novelty among the messengers”* <note>Quran 46:9. Meaning: I am not bringing something unprecedented or alien to the history of divine messengers.</note>.

As for the second principle—which is that Muhammad (peace be upon him) performed the specific work of the Messengers, namely establishing Laws for the people through revelation from God—this is known from the Mighty Book itself. For this reason, God drew attention to this principle by saying: *“O mankind, there has come to you a proof from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a clear light”*—meaning the **Quran**—and He said: *“O mankind, the Messenger has come to you with the truth from your Lord, so believe; it is better for you.”* The Almighty also said: *“But those firm in knowledge among them and the believers believe in what has been revealed to you and what was revealed before you,”* and He said: *“But God bears witness to what He has revealed to you. He has sent it down with His knowledge, and the angels bear witness, and God is sufficient as a witness.”* 

If it is asked: "How can the first principle be known—that there is a class of people who establish Laws through revelation from God? And likewise, how can the second principle be known—that the beliefs and practices contained in the Quran are indeed from God's revelation?" 

The answer is: as for the first principle, it is known through the warnings they give regarding the existence of things that have not yet occurred, which then come into existence exactly as they warned and at the time they predicted. It is also known through the actions they command and the sciences they point toward, which do not resemble humanly acquired knowledge or practices that are typically learned. 

This is because a **breaking of the habitual** <term>breaking of the habitual</term> <note>original: *khariq lil-mu'tad*. The technical term for a miracle; something that "tears" the veil of natural habit.</note>, when it occurs within the realm of knowledge through the establishing of Laws, indicates that the establishment of those Laws was not through learning, but rather through revelation from God; this is what is called **prophecy** <term>prophecy</term>. However, a supernatural act that does not exist within the very act of establishing the Law—such as the **splitting of the sea** <note>Reference to the miracle of Moses.</note> and the like—does not necessarily or inherently prove this specific quality <note>i.e., the quality of being a divinely inspired legislator.</note>.

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that prophecy is verified by the Prophet's ability to establish divine laws and reveal unique knowledge, which he considers a more essential "miracle" than physical wonders like splitting the sea. He supports this by citing Quranic verses that emphasize Muhammad's continuity with previous law-giving prophets.</summary>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...those called prophecy; however, they only provide such indication when they are joined with the first type of proof. When they occur in isolation, they do not indicate prophecy. For this reason, these acts do not prove this status even if they are found among others, because the other type of **miraculous act** <term>miraculous act</term> <note>original: *khariq*. Literally "that which breaks the habit" or the natural order; a supernatural event.</note>—the one that provides **definitive proof** <term>definitive proof</term> <note>original: *dalala qat'iyya*. A proof that yields absolute certainty.</note>—is not present in them. 

Accordingly, one should understand the nature of the **miracle** <term>miracle</term> <note>original: *mu'jiz*. An act that renders others "incapable" of duplicating it, serving as a sign of divine mandate.</note> as it relates to the Prophets. I mean that the miracle found within **knowledge and action** is the definitive proof of the quality of prophecy, while miracles in other types of deeds serve as a witness to it and a source of strength for it.

It has thus become clear to you that this class of people [the Prophets] exists, and from where knowledge of their existence reaches people—to the point that their existence has been transmitted to us through **mass transmission** <term>mass transmission</term> <note>original: *tawatur*. A report passed down by so many independent chains of narrators that it is impossible for it to be a fabrication.</note>, just as the existence of the **Sages** <note>original: *hukama'*. Often referring to the great philosophers and scientists of antiquity, like Aristotle or Plato.</note>, wisdom itself, and other classes of people have been transmitted to us.

If it is asked: "In what way does the **Quran** show that it is an extraordinary miracle of the type that provides definitive proof of the quality of prophecy?" By this, I mean the miracle within the specific function of prophecy, which indicates it just as healing indicates the quality of **medicine** <note>This continues the analogy from the previous page: just as a doctor is proven by his ability to heal, a prophet is proven by his ability to legislate.</note>, which is the very act of medicine.

We say: This is established through several facets:
1.  One must know that the **Divine Laws** <note>original: *shara'i'*. The systematic codes of conduct and belief intended to guide a community.</note> contained within it, regarding both knowledge and action, are not of the sort that can be acquired through human learning, but rather through **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *wahy*.</note>.
2.  What it contains regarding the **foretelling of the unseen** <note>original: *al-i'lam bi-l-ghuyub*. Knowledge of future events or hidden realities.</note>.
3.  Its **linguistic composition** <note>original: *nazm*. The unique stylistic and structural arrangement of the Quranic text.</note>, which is outside the style produced by human thought and reflection. By this, I mean it is recognized as being of a different genus than that of the eloquent orators who speak the Arabic tongue—whether those who speak through learning and craft (those who are not native Bedouins) or those who speak it by natural disposition (the early Arabs).

However, the primary reliance is placed upon the first facet. 

If it is then asked: "How is it known that the Laws containing this knowledge and practice are by revelation from God Almighty, such that they deserve to be called the 'Speech of God'?"

We say: This is established through several paths. One of them is that the knowledge of how to establish Laws is only attained after knowledge of God, of **human happiness** and **human misery**, and of the volitional matters through which one reaches...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Quran's primary proof of divinity is its internal wisdom and legal framework, which surpass human learning, rather than external physical miracles. He likens the Quran's legislative power to a doctor's ability to heal—both are the "definitive proof" of their respective crafts.</summary>

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...by which one attains **happiness** <term>happiness</term> <note>original: *al-sa‘ada*. In Islamic philosophy, this refers to both worldly well-being and ultimate spiritual salvation.</note>, which consists of good deeds and virtues, and what matters hinder happiness and result in **wretchedness in the hereafter** <term>wretchedness</term> <note>original: *al-shaqa’ al-ukhrawi*. The state of spiritual suffering or deprivation in the afterlife.</note>, which consists of evils and bad deeds. Knowledge of human happiness and human wretchedness requires knowing what the **soul** <term>soul</term> <note>original: *al-nafs*.</note> is, what its essence is, and whether it has a state of happiness or wretchedness in the afterlife or not. If it does, one must know the extent of this happiness and wretchedness, and also to what extent good deeds serve as a cause for happiness. 

For just as food is not a cause for health regardless of the amount used or the time it is consumed—but rather in a specific measure and at a specific time—the same applies to good and bad deeds. For this reason, we find all of these things defined in the **Divine Laws** <term>Divine Laws</term> <note>original: *al-shara’i‘*. The plural of Sharia; the systems of religious law revealed to various prophets.</note>. All of this, or most of it, cannot be clarified except through **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *wahy*.</note>, or at the very least, its clarification through revelation is superior. 

Furthermore, the complete **knowledge of God** <note>original: *ma‘rifat Allah*.</note> is only attained after gaining knowledge of all existing things. The Lawgiver <note>The one who establishes the religious law, referring here to the Prophet as the messenger of the Divine Law.</note> needs all of this in order to know the extent of knowledge by which the **masses** <term>masses</term> <note>original: *al-jumhur*. The general public, as opposed to the specialized elite or philosophers.</note> can become happy, and to determine which paths should be taken to lead them to this knowledge. All of this—or rather, most of it—is not perceived through learning, nor through any craft, nor through human **wisdom** <term>wisdom</term> <note>original: *hikma*. In this context, it often refers to philosophy or the sciences acquired through human reasoning.</note>. This fact can be known with certainty by observing the disappearance of the sciences, particularly the establishment of laws, the setting of regulations, and the informing of the states of the **Afterlife** <term>Afterlife</term> <note>original: *al-ma‘ad*. Literally "the Return" or the place/state of the soul after death.</note>. 

When all of these elements were found in the **Mighty Book** <note>The Quran.</note> in the most complete form possible, it was known that this was through revelation from God and that it is His word which He placed upon the tongue of His Prophet. For this reason, the Exalted said, calling attention to this:

> Say: "If mankind and the jinn were to gather to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce the like of it..." <note>original Arabic: *qul la’in ijtama‘ati al-insu wa-l-jinnu ‘ala an ya’tu bi-mithli hadha al-qur’ani la ya’tuna bi-mithlih*. Quran 17:88.</note>

This verse and its meaning are confirmed—reaching the level of absolute certainty—when one considers that he [the Prophet], peace and blessings be upon him, was **unlettered** <term>unlettered</term> <note>original: *ummi*. Traditionally meaning one who does not read or write, emphasizing that his knowledge was divinely inspired rather than studied.</note>. He was raised among an unlettered, common, nomadic nation that never practiced the sciences, nor was any formal science ever attributed to them. They did not engage in the investigation of existing things in the manner practiced by the **Greeks** <note>original: *al-yunaniyyin*.</note> and other nations in whom wisdom reached perfection over long ages. To this is the indication...

...by the words of God the Almighty:

This verse is central to the traditional argument that the Prophet Muhammad’s inability to read or write proves the Quran’s divine origin.</note>

> ﴿ It is He who has sent among the unlettered <term>unlettered</term> <note>original: *al-ummiyin*. This term refers to those who do not read or write, or specifically to the Arabs who did not have a previous scripture.</note> a Messenger from themselves ﴾

In this context, Ibn Rushd uses it to mean the entire revealed system of religion, including its legal and theological components.</note> with all other laws. That is because, if the function of **Prophets** <term>Prophets</term> <note>original: *al-anbiya’*.</note>—by virtue of which they are prophets—is to establish laws through **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *wahy*.</note> from God the Almighty (as is agreed upon by everyone who acknowledges the existence of laws and prophets, peace be upon them), then if one considers what the **Noble Book** <note>original: *al-kitab al-aziz*, a common honorific for the Quran.</note> contains of laws that benefit both knowledge and action—the two paths to happiness—and compares this with other books and laws, one finds that it excels over all other laws by an infinite degree.

In short, if there are books containing laws that deserve to be called the "Word of God" due to their uniqueness and how they transcend the usual scope of human speech—differing from it in the knowledge and action they contain—then it is evident that the Noble Book, which is the **Quran**, is far more worthy and deserving of that description many times over. This will be very clear to you if you have studied the [previous] books—meaning the **Torah** <term>Torah</term> <note>original: *al-tawrat*.</note> and the **Gospel** <term>Gospel</term> <note>original: *al-injil*.</note>—for it is not possible that they have been entirely altered. 

If we were to set out to demonstrate the superiority of one law over another—the superiority of the law established for the Jews and Christians versus the teaching laid out for us regarding the knowledge of God, the knowledge of the **Return** <term>Return</term> <note>original: *al-ma'ad*. This refers to the resurrection and the life hereafter.</note>, and the knowledge of what lies between them—it would require many volumes. Yet, we admit our inability to fully encompass it. For this reason, it is said of this Divine Law that it is the **Seal of Divine Laws** <term>Seal of Divine Laws</term> <note>original: *khatimat al-shara'i'*. The final and most perfect revelation that completes those before it.</note>. And the Prophet, peace be upon him, said: 
> If Moses were alive today, he would have had no choice but to follow me. 

He spoke the truth, peace be upon him. Because of the universality of the teaching found in the Noble Book and the universality of the laws within it—meaning their suitability for everyone—this [law] was...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Prophet's illiteracy and the Quran's objective superiority over the Torah and Gospel in matters of "knowledge and action" prove its divine origin. He asserts that the Islamic Sharia is the "Seal of Laws" because its teachings are universally applicable to all humanity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s comparative analysis of the Prophet Muhammad’s mission. Having previously discussed his illiteracy as a proof of divine origin, he now transitions to the universality of the Islamic message and the philosophical distinction between "external" miracles (like those of Moses or Jesus) and the "intrinsic" miracle of the Quran.</meta>

١٠٣

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

The **Divine Law** <term>Divine Law</term> <note>original: *al-shari'a*.</note> is universal for all mankind. For this reason, He (the Almighty) said:
> ﴿ Say: "O mankind, indeed I am the Messenger of Allah to you all." ﴾ <note>original: Quran 7:158.</note>
And the Prophet (peace be upon him) said:
> I was sent to the red and the black. <note>original: *bu'ithtu ila al-ahmar wa-al-aswad*. This is a traditional idiom expressing the universality of his message to all races and ethnicities of humanity.</note>

It appears that the matter of Divine Laws is like the matter of nutrition: just as there are foods that suit all people, or most of them, so it is with Divine Laws. It is for this reason that the laws before our law were specific to certain peoples to the exclusion of others, whereas our law is universal for all mankind. Because of all this, the Prophet (peace be upon him) excelled all other **Prophets** <term>Prophets</term> <note>original: *al-anbiya’*.</note>, for he surpassed them in the **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *al-wahy*.</note> by which a prophet deserves the title of "prophecy." The Prophet (peace be upon him) said, pointing to this meaning which God specifically granted him:
> There is no prophet among the prophets but that he was given **signs** <term>signs</term> <note>original: *al-ayat*. In a religious context, these are the miracles or verses that serve as proof of a prophet's truthfulness.</note> the likes of which caused humanity to believe. However, what I was given was a revelation, and I hope to be the one with the greatest number of followers on the Day of Resurrection.

Since all of this is as we have described, it has become clear to you that the Quran’s evidence for his prophecy (peace be upon him) is not like the evidence of a staff turning into a serpent for the prophecy of Moses (peace be upon him), nor the raising of the dead for the prophecy of Jesus, or the healing of the blind and the leper. While those are acts that only appear at the hands of prophets and are persuasive to the general public, they do not provide **definitive proof** <note>Ibn Rushd is making a logical distinction here: a miracle like walking on water proves a person has supernatural power, but it doesn't logically prove they are a messenger of a *law*. Only the law itself proves the lawgiver.</note> when considered in isolation, because they are not acts belonging to the specific quality by which a prophet is called a prophet.

As for the Quran, its evidence for this quality is like the evidence of healing for the craft of **medicine** <term>medicine</term> <note>original: *al-tibb*.</note>. For example, if two people claimed to be physicians, and one said, "The proof that I am a physician is that I can walk on water," and the other said, "The proof that I am a physician is that I heal the sick," and the former walked on water while the latter healed the sick—our belief that the second person possesses the skill of medicine would be based on a logical demonstration. Our belief in the medical skill of the one who walked on water, however, would only be persuasive, or a matter of secondary association. The reason for the assumption that occurs among the general public in such a case is that someone capable of walking on water—which is beyond human capacity—must also be capable of...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the Quran is a superior, "intrinsic" miracle because it directly relates to the Prophet's function as a lawgiver, unlike "external" miracles such as walking on water or staff-turning.</summary>
<keywords>universality of Islam, revelation, miracles, Moses, Jesus, medicine analogy, definitive proof, logic of prophecy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's argument regarding the nature of miracles as proof of prophecy. He is specifically concluding a section on why the Quran is a superior, intrinsic proof compared to the physical wonders (external miracles) of previous prophets, before transitioning to the theological problem of free will.</meta>

١٠٤

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...then he is even more worthy of being able to perform the act of healing, which is a human endeavor. Likewise is the nature of the connection between an **external miracle** <term>external miracle</term> <note>original: *al-mu‘jiz al-barrani*. These are supernatural acts performed by prophets that are external to their actual message, such as Moses parting the sea or Jesus healing the sick.</note>—which is not part of the prophet’s essential function—and the specific quality by which a prophet deserves to be a prophet, which is **revelation** <term>revelation</term> <note>original: *al-wahy*.</note>. From this quality comes that which occurs in the soul—an experience only possible for one whom God has empowered with this extraordinary act and distinguished from all other people of his time. Thus, it is not far-fetched for him to claim that God has favored him with His revelation. 

In short, once it is accepted that messengers exist and that supernatural acts are only performed by them, the miracle serves as proof of the prophet's truthfulness. By this, I mean the "external miracle" which does not necessarily match the specific attribute that defines a prophet. It appears that belief resulting from an external miracle is the path for the **general public** <term>general public</term> <note>original: *al-jumhur*. In Ibn Rushd's philosophy, this refers to those who understand religion through rhetoric and imagination rather than strict logical demonstration.</note> only. However, belief through a "corresponding" [intrinsic] miracle is a path shared by both the public and **scholars** <term>scholars</term> <note>original: *al-‘ulama’*.</note>. This is because the general public does not perceive the doubts and objections we have raised regarding the external miracle. Yet, when the **Divine Law** <term>Divine Law</term> <note>original: *al-shar‘*.</note> is contemplated, one finds that it relies upon the intrinsic and corresponding miracle, not the external one. What we have said on this matter is sufficient for our purpose and sufficient for the truth itself.
