## The Third Issue: Regarding Divine Decree and Predestination ##

This issue is among the most difficult of religious matters. When the **textual evidence** <note>original: *dala’il al-sam‘*. Literally "evidence of hearing," referring to truths known through the Quran and the traditions of the Prophet rather than through independent reason.</note> is examined, it is found to be contradictory, as are the arguments of reason. 

As for the contradiction in the textual evidence, it is found in the **Book** <note>The Quran.</note> and the **Sunnah** <note>The recorded practices and sayings of the Prophet Muhammad.</note>. In the Book, one finds many verses which, in their generality, indicate that everything is determined by **predestination** <term>predestination</term> <note>original: *qadar*.</note> and that man is compelled in his actions. Yet, one also finds many verses indicating that man has **acquisition** <term>acquisition</term> <note>original: *iktisab*. A theological term used to explain how human beings "acquire" responsibility for their actions even if God is the ultimate creator of those actions.</note> through his deeds and that he is not compelled.

As for the verses indicating that all matters are necessary and that decree has preceded them, they include the saying of the Almighty:
> "Indeed, all things We created with predestination." <note>original: Quran 54:49.</note>

And the saying of the Almighty:
> "And everything with Him is by due measure." <note>original: Quran 13:8.</note>

And the saying of the Almighty:
> "No disaster strikes upon the earth nor..." <note>original: Quran 57:22. The verse continues: "...nor in yourselves but it is in a register before We bring it into being."</note>

<summary>Ibn Rushd distinguishes between "external" miracles (public wonders) and "intrinsic" miracles (like the Quran), arguing the latter is the true basis of faith. He then introduces the complex theological debate over free will versus predestination in Islamic scripture.</summary>
<keywords>miracles, revelation, scholars vs. the public, Divine Decree, Predestination, free will, Ibn Rushd, Quranic evidence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s exploration of theological dilemmas. Having finished the discussion on the nature of miracles as proof of prophecy on the previous page, he now transitions to one of the most contentious issues in Islamic theology: the conflict between divine decree (predestination) and human free will. He begins by citing the seemingly contradictory scriptural evidence that fueled these debates.</meta>

١٠٥

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...in yourselves, but it is [written] in a book before We bring it into being—indeed, that is easy for Allah." <note>original: Quran 57:22. This verse is frequently cited to support the idea that all events are pre-recorded by God.</note> This is in addition to other verses that carry this same meaning. 

On the other hand, there are verses that indicate that a human being possesses **acquisition** <term>acquisition</term> <note>original: *iktisāb*. This is a technical theological term referring to the way humans "acquire" responsibility for their actions, even if those actions are ultimately created by God.</note>, suggesting that matters in themselves are "possible" rather than "necessary." For example, the Almighty says: "Or He may destroy them for what they have **earned** <note>original: *kasabū*.</note> and yet He pardons much," and His saying: "That is for what your hands have earned," and: "And those who earned evil deeds," and: "For [the soul] is what it has earned, and against it is what it has acquired," and: "As for Thamud, We guided them, but they preferred blindness over guidance."

Sometimes, a single passage may even appear to show a contradiction in this meaning. For instance, the Almighty says: "When a disaster struck you, although you had struck [the enemy] with one twice as great, you said, 'From where is this?' Say, 'It is from yourselves.'" <note>original: Quran 3:165. This refers to the defeat of the Muslims at the Battle of Uhud.</note> Yet, in this very same context, He says: "And what struck you on the day the two armies met was by permission of Allah." Similarly, the Almighty says: "Whatever good reaches you is from Allah, but whatever evil befalls you is from yourself," and yet says: "Say, 'All is from Allah.'" 

In the same way, the **Prophetic Traditions** <term>Hadith</term> are also found to be contradictory on this subject. For example, his saying (peace and blessings be upon him): "Every child is born according to the **innate nature** <term>fitra</term> <note>original: *al-fitra*. The primordial, natural state of a human being, often interpreted as an innate inclination toward monotheism.</note>, then his parents make him a Jew or a Christian." This is contrasted by his saying (peace be upon him): "I created these for Paradise, and with the deeds of the people of Paradise they shall work; and I created these for the Fire, and with the deeds of the people of the Fire they shall work." 

The first Hadith indicates that the cause of **unbelief** <term>kufr</term> is merely the environment in which one is raised, while **faith** <term>iman</term> is caused by the innate nature of the human being. However, the second Hadith indicates that disobedience and unbelief are created by God and that the servant is **compelled** <note>original: *majbur*.</note> toward them. 

Because of this, the Muslims split into two main factions regarding this issue:
1. One faction believed that human "acquisition" is the cause of both sin and good deeds, and because of this, punishment and reward are assigned to them. These are the **Mu'tazilites** <term>Mu'tazilites</term> <note>A rationalist school of Islamic theology that emphasized human free will and divine justice.</note>.
2. Another faction believed the exact opposite: that man is compelled and coerced in his actions. These are the **Jabarites** <term>Jabarites</term> <note>original: *al-Jabriyya*. From the root meaning "compulsion," this group argued that humans have no free will and all acts are authored by God.</note>.

As for the **Ash'arites** <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology, which sought a synthesis between pure rationalism and traditionalism.</note>, they sought to provide a middle path between these two views. They proposed that humans possess "acquisition" (*kasb*), and that the action acquired through it...

<summary>Ibn Rushd outlines the scriptural and traditional evidence for both predestination and free will, noting the apparent contradictions in the Quran and Hadith. He introduces the three major theological responses to this problem: the Mu'tazilites (free will), the Jabarites (predestination), and the Ash'arites (a middle ground).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s exploration of the theological dilemma regarding free will versus predestination. While the previous page focused on seemingly contradictory Quranic verses, this page shifts to the rational and logical arguments (kalam) used by different schools of thought to resolve the tension between divine omnipotence and human responsibility.</meta>

١٠٦

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...and "acquisition" are both created by God the Almighty, but this is a meaningless statement. For if both the act of acquiring and the result itself are created by God, Glory be to Him, then the servant is necessarily **compelled** <term>compelled</term> <note>original: *majbūr*. This term refers to the theological doctrine of "compulsion" (jabr), which suggests humans have no choice in their actions.</note> in their "acquisition." This is one of the reasons for the disagreement on this issue.

As we have stated, the disagreement also stems from a cause other than scripture <note>original: *al-sam‘*, literally "the hearing," referring to transmitted religious texts.</note>, which is the conflict of rational evidence on this matter. Specifically, if we assume that a human being is the author of their own actions and their creator, it would follow that there are actions occurring without the will or choice of God the Almighty. This would imply the existence of a creator other than God. The theologians argue that Muslims have reached a consensus that there is no creator but God, Glory be to Him. However, if we also assume the human does not "acquire" their actions, they must be compelled to do them, for there is no middle ground between compulsion and **acquisition** <term>acquisition</term> <note>original: *iktisāb*. The Ash'arite attempt to find a middle path where God creates the act and the human "acquires" it.</note>.

If a human is compelled to perform their actions, then **religious obligation** <term>religious obligation</term> <note>original: *taklīf*. The divine command to follow religious laws.</note> would be a matter of demanding the impossible. If a person were tasked with what is impossible for them, there would be no difference between their obligation and the "obligation" of an inanimate object. An inanimate object has no **capacity** <term>capacity</term> <note>original: *istiṭā‘a*. The actual power or ability to perform a specific action.</note>, and likewise, a human would have no capacity for that which they cannot do. For this reason, the majority of scholars have concluded that capacity is a prerequisite for religious obligation, just like the possession of intellect.

Because of this, we find that **Abu al-Ma’ali** <note>original: Abu al-Ma’ali al-Juwayni (1028–1085), a master of the Ash'arite school of theology.</note> stated in his work *The Nizamiyya* <note>original: *Al-’Aqida al-Nizamiyya*, a famous theological treatise.</note> that humans do possess an "acquisition" of their actions and a capacity for the deed. He based this on the impossibility of being obligated to do what one cannot, though he arrived at this conclusion from a different direction than the **Mu’tazilites** <note>A rationalist school of theology that insisted humans must have free will so that God’s judgment remains just.</note>. As for the early **Ash’arites** <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology that prioritized God's absolute power.</note>, they permitted the idea of "obligation beyond one's power" to avoid the premise used by the Mu’tazilites—namely, the idea that such a thing is "rationally repugnant"—whereas the later Ash’arites disagreed with them.

Furthermore, if a human possessed no "acquisition," then the command to be prepared for expected evils would be meaningless. Likewise, the command to seek out good things would be pointless. All **crafts** <note>original: *ṣanā’i‘*. This refers to practical skills, professions, or industries.</note> whose purpose is to obtain benefits—such as the craft of farming and others—would be rendered void. Similarly, all crafts intended for protection and warding off harm—such as the art of war, navigation, medicine, and others—would be nullified. This whole...

<summary>Ibn Rushd analyzes the rational deadlock between the "compulsion" of predestination and the "acquisition" of free will, noting how each side tries to avoid compromising God's power or the fairness of religious law. He highlights the practical absurdity of total predestination, which would make all human effort, from farming to medicine, useless.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument for a middle ground between total free will and total predestination. On the previous page, he discussed the contradictions between scriptural evidence for both sides and the theological concept of "acquisition" (iktisab). He now moves to reconcile human agency with divine decree through the role of external causes.</meta>

١٠٧

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...external to what a human being does. If it is asked: if the matter is so, then how can one reconcile the conflict that exists within both scripture itself and within reason itself? We say: it appears that the objective of the Divine Law is not to separate these two beliefs <note>The two beliefs being: 1) humans create their own acts, and 2) God creates all acts.</note>, but rather its intention is to combine them through a **middle way** <term>middle way</term> <note>original: *al-tawassut*. This is Ibn Rushd's hallmark approach: finding a synthesis between extreme theological positions using Aristotelian logic.</note>, which is the truth in this matter. 

This is because it appears that God, Blessed and Exalted is He, has created for us faculties by which we are able to "acquire" things that are opposites. However, since the acquisition of those things is only completed for us through the assistance of **external causes** <term>external causes</term> <note>original: *al-asbab al-kharija*. These are environmental or situational factors that God has pre-ordained.</note> which God has subjected to us, and the removal of obstacles from them, the actions attributed to us are completed by both matters together. 

If this is the case, then the actions attributed to us are completed by our own wills in conjunction with the harmony of external actions with those wills—and these external factors are what is expressed as the **decree of God** <term>decree of God</term> <note>original: *qadar Allah*. In this context, Ibn Rushd redefines "decree" not as a mysterious whim, but as the consistent laws of nature and circumstance established by God.</note>. These causes that God has subjected from the outside are not merely the completion of the actions we intend to perform, or the obstacles against them; they are also the reason we choose one of two opposites in the first place. For the **will** <term>will</term> <note>original: *irada*.</note> is simply a desire that arises within us due to a certain imagination or an assent to something. This assent is not by our own choice, but rather it is something that happens to us due to external matters. 

For example: if a desirable thing appears to us from the outside, we desire it by necessity without choice, and thus we move toward it. Similarly, if an avoidable thing occurs from the outside, we dislike it by necessity and flee from it. If this is so, then our will is preserved by external matters and is bound to them. This is the indication in the Almighty’s saying: 

> *“For him [each person] are successive [angels] before him and behind him who guard him by the command of God.”* <note>Quran, Surah Ar-Ra'd 13:11. Ibn Rushd interprets the "guardians" or "successive ones" as the system of causes that govern human life.</note>

Since these external causes run according to a limited system and an arranged order that does not fail, according to how their Creator has decreed them, and since our will and actions do not reach completion or exist at all except through agreement with these external causes, it follows necessarily that our actions run according to a limited system—meaning they exist at fixed times.

<summary>Ibn Rushd proposes a "middle way" to resolve the free will debate, arguing that while humans have the internal faculty to choose, their actions and even their desires are dependent on a pre-ordained system of external causes established by God.</summary>
<keywords>free will, predestination, Ibn Rushd, external causes, divine decree, irada, iktisab, Surah Al-Ra'd</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) reconciliation of human agency and divine predestination. On the previous page, he established that human actions result from a combination of internal will and external circumstances. He now explains that this causal chain is what the Quran refers to as "Decree," "Predestination," and the "Preserved Tablet."</meta>

١٠٨

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...and a limited measure. This was necessary because our actions are caused by those external causes, and every effect resulting from limited, determined causes is itself necessarily limited and determined. This connection exists not only between our actions and **external causes**, but also between our actions and the causes God has created within our own bodies. 

The determined system found within both internal and external causes—meaning those that are unchanging—is the **Decree and Predestination** <term>decree and predestination</term> <note>original: *al-qada' wa-l-qadar*. Ibn Rushd defines these not as arbitrary divine interventions, but as the consistent laws of nature and causality established by God.</note> which God the Exalted has written for His servants. This system is the **Preserved Tablet** <term>preserved tablet</term> <note>original: *al-lawh al-mahfuz*. Traditionally viewed as a physical book in heaven, Ibn Rushd interprets it here philosophically as the fixed causal order of the universe.</note>. God’s knowledge of these causes and what results from them is the very reason these causes exist. 

Because of this, no one can fully encompass the knowledge of these causes except God alone, and thus He alone is the Knower of the Unseen in reality, as the Exalted says: "Say: None in the heavens and the earth knows the unseen except Allah." <note>Quran 27:65</note>. 

Indeed, knowledge of the causes constitutes **knowledge of the unseen** <term>knowledge of the unseen</term> <note>original: *al-'ilm bi-l-ghayb*.</note>, because the "unseen" is knowing whether a thing will exist in the future or not. Since the arrangement and system of causes is what necessitates the existence or non-existence of a thing at a certain time, it follows that knowledge of a thing's causes is necessarily knowledge of that thing’s existence or non-existence at that time. Knowledge of causes in an absolute sense is knowledge of what exists or what does not exist at any point in the entirety of time. 

Glory be to Him who has encompassed every cause of every existent through both creation and knowledge! These are the **keys of the unseen** <term>keys of the unseen</term> <note>original: *mafatih al-ghayb*.</note> intended in the Exalted’s saying: "And with Him are the keys of the unseen; none knows them but He..." <note>Quran 6:59</note>. 

If all this is as we have described, it has become clear to you how we possess **acquisition** <term>acquisition</term> <note>original: *iktisab*. The theological concept that humans "acquire" responsibility for actions that are ultimately created by God.</note> and yet how all our acquisitions are governed by a prior decree and predestination. This synthesis is exactly what the Divine Law intended by those general verses and traditions that are often thought to contradict one another. When their general meanings are specified by this understanding, the contradiction disappears. By this, all the doubts that have been raised regarding this issue are also resolved—specifically the conflicting rational arguments regarding the fact that existing things originate from...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that Divine Decree and the "Preserved Tablet" are synonymous with the universal laws of causality. He explains that God’s unique knowledge of the "unseen" is actually His perfect knowledge of the entire causal chain of the universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) argument that human actions depend on both internal will and external causes. He is now addressing a potential theological objection: if God is the only "Agent" or "Creator" (as Islamic orthodoxy maintains), how can we say that natural causes actually *do* anything?</meta>

١٠٩

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...our will is brought into existence by both matters together: namely, our own will and external causes. 

If actions are attributed to only one of these two exclusively, the previously mentioned doubts arise. If it is then asked: "This is a fine answer in which reason agrees with the religious Law, but this statement is built on the premise that there are **active causes** <term>active causes</term> <note>original: *asbab fa'ila*. In philosophy, these are the "efficient causes" that trigger an effect.</note> for **passive effects** <term>passive effects</term> <note>original: *musabbabat maf'ula*. These are the results produced by a cause.</note>, yet the Muslims have agreed that there is no Agent <term>agent</term> <note>original: *fa'il*. In Islamic theology, this refers to the ultimate 'Doer' or Creator who brings things into being.</note> but God." 

We respond: What they have agreed upon is true, but there are two ways to answer this. 

The first is that what can be understood from this statement is one of two things. Either it means there is no Agent but God—blessed and exalted be He—and that everything else among the causes He employs is not called an "agent" except **metaphorically** <note>original: *majazan*. Ibn Rushd is distinguishing between God as the true, primary cause and nature as a secondary, symbolic cause.</note>. This is because their very existence is only through Him; He is the one who brought them into existence as causes. Rather, He is the one who preserves their existence in their capacity as agents, preserves their effects after they occur, and **originates** <term>originates</term> <note>original: *yakhtari'u*. This refers to God creating the very essence of a thing out of nothing.</note> their **essences** <term>essences</term> <note>original: *jawahir*. Literally 'substances' or 'atoms'; the fundamental building blocks of reality.</note> when causes are joined with them. 

Likewise, He preserves them in themselves. If it were not for Divine preservation, they would not exist for any perceivable time—meaning, they would not exist for even the shortest moment that could be perceived as time. 

**Abu Hamid** <note>Referring to the famous theologian Al-Ghazali (1058–1111 AD), who often argued against the necessary power of natural causes.</note> says that the example of one who associates a natural cause with God in the name of "Agent" or "Action" is like someone who associates the **pen** with the **writer** in the act of writing. By this, he means that if one says "the pen is a writer" and "the human is a writer," the term "writing" is applied to both through **homonymy** <term>homonymy</term> <note>original: *ishtirak al-ism*. This is a logical term for when one word is used for two very different concepts, like a 'bank' of a river and a 'bank' for money.</note>. He means they are two meanings that share nothing but the word itself, while in their true nature, they are utterly distinct. The same applies to the term "Agent" when it is used for God, the Blessed and Exalted, and when it is used for all other causes. 

We say that there is a certain **imprecision** <note>original: *tasamuhan*. Ibn Rushd thinks Al-Ghazali’s metaphor is a bit too loose or simplified.</note> in this analogy. The analogy would only be truly clear if the writer were the one who originated the very essence of the pen and preserved it as long as it remained a pen. Then, [the writer] would be the one who preserves the writing after it is written and who creates it when the pen is applied—as we will explain later: that God the Exalted is the Creator of the essences of all things which are joined by their causes according to **habit** <note>original: *al-'ada*. In this context, 'habit' refers to the regular, observable laws of nature that God consistently maintains.</note>.

<summary>Ibn Rushd reconciles the theological concept of God as the sole Creator with the existence of natural causes, critiquing Al-Ghazali's metaphor of the pen and the writer as being too simplified.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd (Averroes), Al-Ghazali, causality, Agency, essence (jawhar), homonymy, Divine preservation, natural law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) argument from page 109. He is refining the definition of God as the "Only Agent" by explaining that while God is the ultimate creator, He has established a system of secondary causes—both internal (natures) and external (celestial bodies)—that are necessary for the existence of life and the order of the universe.</meta>

١١٠

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...to be said that they are causes for them. This understanding—that there is no Agent <note>original: *fa'il*. Here referring to the ultimate "Doer" or Producer of effects.</note> but God—is a concept attested to by the senses, reason, and religious law. 

As for the senses and reason, they perceive that there are things here from which other things are generated, and that the order prevailing in existing things results from two matters. The first is what God has embedded within them of **natures** and **souls** <note>In the Aristotelian tradition used by Ibn Rushd, "natures" refer to the inherent properties of inanimate objects, while "souls" refer to the animating principles of plants, animals, and humans.</note>. The second is from the external existing things that encompass them. 

The most famous of these are the movements of the celestial bodies. It is evident that the night, the day, the sun, the moon, and the rest of the stars are **subjected** <note>original: *musakhkharat*. A Quranic term implying that natural phenomena are pressed into service for humanity by God's command.</note> to us. It is because of the system and order that the Creator established in their movements that our existence, and the existence of everything here, is preserved by them. So much so, that if one were to imagine the removal of one of them, or imagine it in a different position, or of a different size, or moving at a speed other than that which God has set for it, the existing things on the surface of the earth would be destroyed. This is according to what God has placed in their natures and what He has placed in the natures of the things here to be affected by them. 

This is very apparent in the sun and the moon—I mean their influence on things here. It is clear in the waters, the winds, the rains, and the seas; in short, in all sensible bodies. The necessity of their existence appears most clearly in the life of plants and in many animals—indeed, in all animals entirely. 

Furthermore, it is clear that were it not for the powers God placed in our bodies for **nutrition** and **sensation**, our bodies would perish. You find <term>Galen</term> <note>Galen of Pergamum (c. 129–216 AD) was a Greek physician and philosopher whose work formed the basis of medieval medicine.</note> and the rest of the **wise men** <note>original: *al-hukama'*. This term refers to the philosophers and scientists of antiquity.</note> acknowledging this; they say that were it not for the powers God placed in the bodies of animals to govern them, it would not be possible for animal bodies to remain for a single hour after their creation. We say that were it not for the powers in the bodies of animals and plants, and the powers flowing through this world from the movements of the celestial bodies, it would not be possible for them to remain at all, not even for the blink of an eye. Glory be to the **Subtle**, the **Aware**! <note>original: *al-Latif al-Khabir*. Two of the "Beautiful Names" of God in Islam, emphasizing His precise and all-knowing nature.</note>

God the Exalted has drawn attention to this in more than one verse of His Book, where He—the Exalted—says: ﴾And He subjected to you the night and the day, and the sun and the moon ﴿ <note>Quran, Surah Ibrahim 14:33 or An-Nahl 16:12.</note> and His saying: ﴾ Say...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that the universe operates through a system of secondary causes, including celestial movements and biological "powers," all established by God. He reconciles the theological idea of God as the sole Agent with the scientific observation of natural causality, citing Galen and the Quran as evidence.</summary>

<keywords>Causality, Celestial Bodies, Galen, Divine Order, Nature, Souls, Ibn Rushd, Aristotelianism, Islamic Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument regarding the relationship between God as the Ultimate Cause and the secondary causes found in nature. On the previous page, he argued that the removal of celestial movements would lead to the destruction of the world. Here, he provides Quranic evidence for the utility of these natural systems and introduces the distinction between "substances" and "accidents" to clarify how God remains the only true Creator while still allowing for the influence of natural causes.</meta>

١١١

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

... "Say: Have you considered: if God should make the night continuous for you until the Day of Resurrection..." <note>Quran 28:71. Ibn Rushd uses these verses to show that the predictable cycles of nature are deliberate gifts from God.</note>—to the end of the verse. And His saying, Exalted is He:
> "And out of His mercy He made for you the night and the day that you may rest therein and that you may seek of His bounty." ❁ And His saying, Exalted is He: "And He has subjected to you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, all from Him." ❁ And His saying: "And He subjected for you the sun and the moon, both constant in their courses, and He subjected for you the night and the day." ❁

And many other verses that carry this same meaning. If these celestial and natural things had no effect on the world here below, there would be no **wisdom** in their existence for which He could favor us, nor would they be considered **blessings** <note>original: *ni'am*. Favors or graces bestowed by God.</note> for which we are specifically required to give thanks.

As for the second answer, we say: Existing things that are created <note>original: * حادثة (haditha)*. Refers to things that come into being in time, as opposed to the eternal.</note> consist of some that are **substances** and **essences** <term>Substances (jawahir) and Essences (a'yan): In medieval philosophy, these are the fundamental "things" themselves (like a human or a tree), as opposed to their temporary properties.</term> and others that are **accidents**, such as movements, heat, and cold—in short, all accidental qualities. 

As for the **substances** and **essences**, their creation <note>original: *ikhtira'aha*. A term Ibn Rushd uses for the "invention" or bringing into being of a thing's fundamental nature.</note> belongs to none but the Creator, Glorified be He. The secondary causes associated with them only influence the **accidents** of those essences, not the essences themselves. 

For example: the semen only provides the woman (or the menstrual blood) with heat. However, the formation of the embryo and its **soul** <note>original: *nafs*. The animating principle of life.</note>—which is life itself—is granted only by God, Blessed and Exalted is He. Similarly, the farmer only performs the tilling or the preparation of the soil and sows the seeds within it. But as for the one who grants the form and growth of the **ear of corn**, that is God, Blessed and Exalted is He. 

Therefore, according to this view, there is no Creator but God, since the things truly "created" are the **substances**. It is to this meaning that He, the Exalted, refers in His saying:
> "O mankind, a comparison is cited, so listen to it. Indeed, those you invoke besides God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for that purpose. And if the fly should steal away from them a [tiny] thing, they could not recover it from him. Weak are the pursuer and the pursued." ❁

This is the very point upon which the disbeliever tried to deceive **Abraham**, peace be upon him, when he said, "I give life and cause death." When Abraham saw that the man did not understand this distinction <note>The distinction that the king could only kill the body (an accident of state), but not create the essence of life itself.</note>, he moved to a proof that silenced him, saying:
> "Indeed, God brings the sun from the east, so bring it from the west."

In summary, when the matter of the **Agent** <note>original: *al-Fa'il*. The "Doer" or cause of an action.</note> and the **Creator** is understood in this way, no contradiction arises between revelation <note>original: *al-sam'*. Specifically the "heard" or transmitted religious texts.</note> and reason. Because of this, we believe that the name "The Creator"...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that while natural "secondary" causes exist, they only affect the "accidents" (like heat or movement) of things, whereas God alone creates "substances" (the essential nature and life of an embryo or plant). He uses the debate between Abraham and Nimrod to illustrate how God is the ultimate cause of both cosmic movements and the essence of life.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Rushd, substances and accidents, secondary causes, creation, Abraham, Quranic proofs, nature, philosophy and religion</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument from page 111, where he established that God is the sole "Creator" in the sense of being the originator of substances. Here, he defends the reality of secondary causes in the physical world, arguing that to deny them is to deny the very possibility of human knowledge and the wisdom of God.</meta>

١١٢

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

No creature shares in this name with Him, neither by a direct nor a remote metaphor, because the meaning of the word **Creator** is the one who invents **substances** <term>Substances (jawahir): The fundamental building blocks of reality that exist by themselves, as opposed to qualities or "accidents" like color or shape.</term>. It is for this reason that God, the Exalted, said:
> "While God created you and that which you do." <note>Quran 37:96. Ibn Rushd cites this to show that while humans act, the underlying substance of the human and the capacity for action are created by God.</note>

You should know that whoever denies that **causes** have an effect on their results—by God's permission—has essentially nullified **wisdom** and nullified **knowledge**. This is because knowledge is the understanding of things through their causes, and wisdom is the knowledge of the "final causes" <note>original: *al-asbab al-gha'iyah*. The intended purposes or goals for which something is created.</note>. To deny causes entirely is an idea very alien to human nature. Furthermore, one who denies causes in the **visible world** <note>original: *al-shāhid*. The physical, observable realm of human experience.</note> has no way to prove an **Acting Cause** in the **unseen world** <note>original: *al-ghā'ib*. The divine or metaphysical realm.</note>. This is because judging the unseen can only be done by drawing an analogy from what is witnessed in the visible world. 

Those who deny causes have no way to know God, the Exalted, as they would have to admit that not every act requires an actor. Since this is the case, the consensus of Muslims—that "there is no actor but God, the Purified"—cannot be understood as a total denial of any acting cause in the visible world. It is precisely from the existence of actors in the visible world that we infer the existence of the Actor in the unseen world. However, once we have established the existence of the Unseen Actor, it becomes clear to us through the knowledge of His essence that everything other than Him acts only by His permission and through His will.

Through this, it becomes clear in what sense we possess **acquisition** <note>original: *iktisab*. A theological term for how humans "acquire" responsibility for their actions even if God is the ultimate creator of all things.</note>. Anyone who takes one of the two extreme sides of this issue is mistaken, such as the **Mu'tazilites** <note>An early theological school that emphasized human free will, often arguing humans create their own actions independently.</note> and the **Jabrites** <note>From *jabr* (compulsion); a school that argued humans have no free will and are forced in their actions like puppets.</note>. 

As for the middle path which the **Ash'arites** <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology during Ibn Rushd's time.</note> claim to hold as the truth, it has no real existence at all. This is because they do not grant the human any meaning for the term "acquisition" except the distinction a person feels between the involuntary trembling of a hand <note>original: *al-ra'sha*. A tremor or shiver.</note> and moving the hand by choice. There is no meaning to their recognition of this difference if they then say that *both* movements are not actually caused by us. For if they are not caused by us, then we have no power to refrain from them, and thus we are compelled. Therefore, the involuntary tremor and the movement they call "acquired" are identical in meaning; the difference is merely in the word, and a verbal difference does not change the nature of the thing itself. All of this is self-evident.

<vocab>Ibn Rushd, The Creator, Substances, Causes, Wisdom, The Visible, The Unseen, Mu'tazilites, Jabrites, Ash'arites, Acquisition, Kasb</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that denying natural cause-and-effect makes both science and religious knowledge impossible, while criticizing the Ash'arite theory of "acquisition" as being functionally no different from determinism.</summary>
<keywords>causality, free will, Ash'arite theology, Mu'tazilites, Jabrites, Islamic philosophy, epistemology, Ibn Rushd</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s systematic critique of the Ash'arite school of theology. Having previously defended the reality of secondary causes in nature, he now turns to the ethical implications of their theology, specifically how we define justice and injustice in relation to God.</meta>

۱۱۳

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-
( 15 )

Let us proceed to what remains of the issues we promised to address.

**﴿ The Fourth Issue ﴾** Regarding Injustice and Justice:

The **Ash'arites** <term>Ash'arites (al-Ash‘ariyya): A major school of Sunni Islamic theology that emphasized God's absolute will and power, often arguing that human reason cannot independently determine right and wrong without revelation.</term> have adopted a very strange view regarding justice and injustice in relation to God, the Exalted—a view strange to both reason and religious law. By this, I mean they have expressed a meaning that the Law itself did not express; in fact, the Law expressed its opposite. 

This is because they claim that the **Unseen** <term>The Unseen (al-gha'ib): In theological discourse, this refers to the divine realm or God, as opposed to the "Witnessed" or physical world.</term> in this matter is contrary to the **Witnessed** <term>The Witnessed (al-shahid): The observable world of human experience and social interaction.</term>. They allege that a human being is only described as just or unjust because of the **legal restriction** <term>Restriction (al-hajr): A legal status where an individual is constrained by laws or the authority of another; the Ash'arites argued that since no one is above God, He cannot be "restricted" by moral laws.</term> placed upon their actions by the Religious Law. Thus, whenever a person does something that the Law defines as "justice," they are just; and whoever does what the Law defines as "injustice" is a wrongdoer. 

They argue that for One who is not subject to religious obligation, nor falls under the restriction of the Law, there is no such thing as an act that is "injustice" or "justice" in His regard. Rather, they claim all His actions are justice. They have committed themselves to the idea that there is nothing that is, in itself, justice, and nothing that is, in itself, injustice. 

This is the height of absurdity <note>original: *al-shana'a*. Can also be translated as "monstrosity" or "repulsiveness," referring to a logically or morally offensive argument.</note>, for it would mean there is nothing that is inherently good and nothing that is inherently evil. Since justice is known by itself to be good, and injustice is known to be evil, [under their view] associating partners with God <note>original: *al-shirk*. The act of assigning equals to God, considered the gravest sin in Islam.</note> would not be inherently unjust or a wrongdoing except by way of the Law's decree. It would mean that if the Law had commanded the belief in a partner for God, that would have been "justice." Likewise, if the Law had commanded disobedience to Him, that too would have been "justice."

This contradicts both revelation and reason. As for revelation, God has described Himself in His Book as maintaining equity and has denied injustice of Himself. The Exalted said:
> "God bears witness that there is no god but He, and the angels and those of knowledge, maintaining **equity**." <note>Quran 3:18. Ibn Rushd uses the word *qist* (equity/justice) to show God defines Himself by an objective standard.</note>

And the Exalted said:
> "And your Lord is not ever unjust to His servants." <note>Quran 41:46.</note>

And He said:
> "Indeed, God does not wrong the people at all, but it is the people who wrong themselves." <note>Quran 10:44.</note>

Now, if it is asked: "What do you say regarding the **leading astray** <term>Leading astray (al-idlal): The theological problem of why a just God would permit or cause people to lose their way or sin.</term> of His servants? Is it injustice or justice? God has explicitly stated in more than one verse of His Book that He leads astray and guides, such as the Exalted’s saying:
> 'God leads astray whom He wills and guides whom He wills.'" <note>Quran 35:8.</note>

And like His saying:
> "And if We had willed, We could have given every soul its guidance." <note>Quran 32:13.</note>

We reply: These verses cannot be interpreted as...

<summary>Ibn Rushd critiques the Ash'arite view that justice and injustice are merely arbitrary labels defined by divine decree, arguing instead that they are inherent qualities of actions. He cites various Quranic verses to prove that God acts according to an objective standard of equity and begins to address the problem of God "leading people astray."</summary>
<keywords>divine justice, Ash'arites, ethics, inherent good and evil, leading astray, Ibn Rushd, theology, equity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s systematic critique of the Ash'arite school of theology. Having previously defined the difference between the "Witnessed" (human) and "Unseen" (divine) realms of justice, he now addresses the Quranic paradox of how a just God can be said to "misguide" people.</meta>

١١٤

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...the apparent meaning of these verses. This is because there are many other verses that contradict them in their apparent sense, such as the verses in which the Almighty denies that He commits any injustice, and His saying: ﴿And He is not pleased with disbelief for His servants.﴾ <note>Quran 39:7; original: "ولا يرضى لعباده الكفر"</note> It is clear that if He is not pleased with disbelief for them, then He does not misguide them. As for what the **Ash’arites** <term>Ash’arites (al-Ash‘ariyya): A major school of Islamic theology that Ibn Rushd critiques for their view that God's will is absolute and not bound by human logic or "causes."</term> say—that it is permissible for God to do what He does not "will" or command what He does not "desire"—we seek refuge in God from such a belief about Him! Indeed, this is a form of **disbelief** <term>Kufr: Here meaning a fundamental error in faith or ingratitude toward the truth of God’s nature.</term>. 

Evidence that people were not created for error can be found in His saying: ﴿So set your face toward the religion as a pure monotheist—the natural disposition of God upon which He has created mankind.﴾ <note>Quran 30:30; the term used is "Fitra," referring to an innate, original human inclination toward truth and goodness.</note> and His saying: ﴿And when your Lord took from the Children of Adam from their loins...﴾ <note>Quran 7:172, referring to the "Primordial Covenant" where all human souls acknowledged God's Lordship before the world began.</note>—the verse; and the saying of the Prophet (peace be upon him): "Every child is born according to their **innate nature** (Fitra)." 

Since this contradiction exists, it is necessary to reconcile these verses in a manner required by reason. We say: regarding His saying ﴿He misleads whom He wills and guides whom He wills,﴾ <note>Quran 16:93</note> this refers to the **Pre-ordained Will** <term>Al-mashi’a al-sabiqa: The primordial divine plan that established the laws of nature and the variety of human temperaments.</term> which necessitated that among the various species of existing things, there would be creatures who are "lost"—meaning they are predisposed to error by their very temperaments and are driven toward it by the surrounding causes, both internal and external. 

As for His saying: ﴿And if We had willed, We could have given every soul its guidance,﴾ <note>Quran 32:13</note> its meaning is that if He had willed not to create beings who are predisposed to error—either because of their temperaments, or because of external causes, or both—He could have done so. However, because the creation of temperaments in this world is diverse, it happened that some verses "misguide" some people and "guide" others. This is because these verses are *intended* to be a trial leading to error for some, as in His saying: ﴿He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby; but He misleads none except the defiantly disobedient,﴾ <note>Quran 2:26</note> and His saying: ﴿And We did not make the vision which We showed you except as a trial for the people, and [likewise] the cursed tree in the Quran,﴾ <note>Quran 17:60</note> and His saying after mentioning the number of angels over Hellfire: ﴿Thus does God mislead whom He wills and guide whom He wills.﴾ <note>Quran 74:31</note> That is to say, it occurs to evil natures that these [revelations] become...

<summary>Ibn Rushd reconciles the theological paradox of God "misguiding" people with the concept of divine justice. He argues that "misguidance" is not an arbitrary act of God, but a result of the diverse natural temperaments and external causes that exist within the created order.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Justice, Misguidance (Dalal), Guidance (Hidaya), Fitra (Innate Nature), Ash’arites, Causality, Predestination, Quranic Exegesis, Pre-ordained Will</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s argument regarding divine justice. He has just stated that the Prophet's teaching—that every child is born with a natural inclination toward truth—suggests that misguidance is not the primary purpose of creation. He now uses a medical analogy to explain why some people are nonetheless led astray.</meta>

١١٥

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...the verses are misguiding for them, just as it happens to diseased bodies that even nutritious foods become harmful to them. If it is asked: "What is the need for creating a category of creatures who are, by their very natures, predisposed to misguidance? Is this not the height of injustice <term>Jawr: Injustice or tyranny; here referring to the idea of God acting unfairly toward His creation.</term>?"

It is answered: **Divine Wisdom** <term>Divine Wisdom (al-Hikma al-Ilahiyya): The philosophical concept that God’s actions follow a purposeful and optimal order.</term> necessitated this, and injustice would actually have resided in the alternative. This is because the nature from which man was created, and the composition upon which he was fashioned, required that some people—though they are the minority—would be wicked by their very natures. Similarly, it was necessary that the external causes arranged to guide people would also result in misguidance for some, even if they provide guidance for the majority.

According to what wisdom dictates, there was no choice but to follow one of two paths:
1. Either to refrain from creating species in which evil is found in the minority and good in the majority—but this would mean the greater good would be lost because of a minor evil.
2. Or to create these species so that the greater good exists alongside the minor evil.

It is self-evident that the existence of a greater good despite a minor evil is better than the total absence of that greater good for the sake of avoiding the minor evil. This aspect of wisdom—the necessity of the minor evil for the sake of the greater good—is precisely what was hidden from the **angels** when God (Glory be to Him) informed them that He was placing a **Successor** <term>Khalifa: A deputy or representative; in the Quranic context, this refers to the appointment of Adam (humanity) as God's steward on Earth.</term> on the earth, meaning the children of Adam. As He recounts, they said:

> "Will You place therein those who will make mischief and shed blood, while we celebrate Your praise?" <note>Quran 2:30; original: "أتجعل فيها من يفسد فيها ويسفك الدماء ونحن نسبح بحمدك"</note>

Until His saying:

> "Indeed, I know that which you do not know." <note>Quran 2:30; original: "اني أعلم مالا تعلمون"</note>

He means that the knowledge hidden from them was that when the existence of something contains both good and evil, but the good is predominant, wisdom dictates its creation rather than its absence. 

Thus, it has become clear through this argument how "misguidance" is attributed to God alongside **justice** and the denial of wrongdoing. He only created the "causes of misguidance" because they result in guidance far more often than they do in misguidance. Indeed, among existing beings are those who were given causes of guidance in which no misguidance occurs at all—this is the state of the **angels**. But there are others who were given causes of guidance in which misguidance occurs in the minority of cases, as it was not possible for them to exist in any more perfect way.

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that "misguidance" is a secondary byproduct of a system designed for the "greater good." Using the story of the angels questioning Adam's creation, he argues that a world with some evil is better than no world at all.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Wisdom, The Greater Good, problem of evil, angels, Khalifa, justice, Ibn Rushd, Quranic commentary</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's explanation of why evil exists in a world created by a just God. He transitions from the medical analogy of the previous page to a discussion of why the Qur'an contains seemingly contradictory verses and provides the famous philosophical example of fire to illustrate "necessary evil."</meta>

١١٦

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...from that, because of the composition <term>al-Tarkib: The way a being is physically or metaphysicaly put together; here referring to the complex nature of humans which allows for the possibility of failure.</term>, and this is the state of man.

If it is asked: "What is the wisdom behind the inclusion of these contradictory verses regarding this subject, to the point that one is forced to resort to **metaphorical interpretation** <term>Ta’wil: The practice of interpreting scriptural text beyond its literal meaning to resolve apparent contradictions.</term>, while you generally reject such interpretation in most cases?"

We say: Making the common people <term>al-Jumhur: The general public or non-specialist believers, whom Ibn Rushd believes should generally follow the literal text rather than complex philosophy.</term> understand the matter as it truly is in this issue necessitated this. This is because they needed to know that God Almighty is characterized by **Justice** and that He is the Creator of all things—both good and evil. This was necessary because many misguided nations used to believe that there were two gods: a god who creates good and a god who creates evil. Thus, they were informed that He is the Creator of both things together.

Since misguidance is an evil, and there is no creator other than Him, it had to be attributed to Him just as the creation of evil is attributed to Him. However, this should not be understood in an absolute sense, but rather that He is the Creator of good for the sake of the good itself, and the Creator of evil for the sake of the good—meaning, for the sake of the good that is associated with it. In this sense, His creation of evil is an act of His **Justice**.

An example of this is that **fire** was created because of its role in the preservation of existing things, which could not exist without the presence of fire. However, it follows from its nature that it destroys some existing things. When the destruction that arises from it (which is the evil) is weighed against the existence that arises from it (which is the good), its existence is better than its non-existence; therefore, it is a good.

As for His word, Most High: *"He is not questioned about what He does, but they are questioned,"* <note>original: "لا يسئل عما يفعل وهم يسألون" - Qur'an 21:23.</note> its meaning is that He does not perform an action because it is an obligation <term>Wajib: A duty or necessity.</term> upon Him. This is because whoever is in such a position has a need for that action; and whatever is like this needs that action for its existence, either as a matter of necessity or to achieve completion. The **Creator** <term>al-Bari': The Maker or Originator.</term>, glorified be He, is far above such a meaning.

A human being acts justly to gain a benefit for their own soul through that justice; if they did not act justly, they would not attain that good. But He, glorified and exalted, acts justly not because His essence is perfected by that justice, but because the **perfection** <term>al-Kamal: The state of being complete and without flaw.</term> that is already in His essence necessitated that He act justly. If this meaning is understood in this way, it becomes clear that He is not described [with injustice]...

<vocab>التركيب (Composition), التأويل (Interpretation), الجمهور (The Masses), العدل (Justice), الخير والشر (Good and Evil), الاضلال (Misguidance), النار (Fire), البارىء سبحانه (The Creator), الكمال (Perfection)</vocab>

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains that God creates "evil" only as a necessary byproduct of a much greater good, using fire as a primary example. He argues that divine justice flows from God's inherent perfection rather than from an external obligation or a need to improve Himself.</summary>
<keywords>Theodicy, Problem of Evil, Divine Justice, Fire analogy, Qur'anic Interpretation, Ibn Rushd, Metaphorical Interpretation, Perfection of God</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's exploration of the problem of evil. Having established that evil is a necessary byproduct of certain physical realities (like fire), he now addresses the theological challenge of God's "Justice" and explains why scripture uses general language that can be interpreted differently by philosophers and the general public.</meta>

١١٧

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...with justice in the manner that a human is described. However, this does not require us to say that He is not described as possessing justice at all, or that all actions regarding Him are neither justice nor injustice, as the **theologians** <term>al-Mutakallimun: Early Islamic scholars who used dialectical reasoning (Kalam) to defend the faith, whom Ibn Rushd often criticized for their lack of logical rigor.</term> believed. For this would be an invalidation of what human reason understands and an invalidation of the literal sense of the **Divine Law** <term>ash-Shari'a: The revealed religious law; here referring to the text of the Qur'an and its clear moral instructions.</term>.

But these people sensed a meaning yet fell short of grasping it: This is because if we assume He is not characterized by justice at all, it would invalidate the understanding that there are things which are, in themselves, just and good, and things which are, in themselves, unjust and evil. Conversely, if we assume He is characterized by justice in the same way a human is, it would imply a deficiency in His Essence—Glory be to Him. This is because a human who acts with **justice** <term>al-'Adl: The principle of fairness or balance. In Islamic theology, there is a massive debate over whether "good" and "evil" are intrinsic properties or merely whatever God decides.</term> only exists for the sake of the one for whom he is being just; thus, in his capacity as a "just" person, he is a servant to someone else.

You should know that this level of **metaphorical interpretation** <term>at-Ta’wil: The process of moving beyond the literal meaning of a text to find a deeper philosophical truth that resolves apparent contradictions.</term> is not a duty for all people. Rather, those for whom this knowledge is mandatory are those who have encountered doubt regarding this concept. Not everyone among the **common people** <term>al-Jumhur: The general public or non-specialist believers. Ibn Rushd argues they should follow the clear, literal meaning of scripture to avoid confusion.</term> is aware of the contradictions found in those general scriptural statements. Whoever does not notice these contradictions is required to believe in those generalities according to their literal appearance.

This is because there is another reason for the inclusion of those generalities in scripture: the common people cannot distinguish between the **impossible** <term>al-Mustahil: Something that is logically a contradiction, like a square circle.</term> and the **possible** <term>al-Mumkin: Something that could either exist or not exist without violating logic.</term>. God Almighty is not described as having power over the impossible. If it were said to them—regarding something that is impossible in its nature but which they imagine to be possible—that God is not described as having power over it, they would imagine this to indicate a deficiency or weakness in the **Creator** <term>al-Bari: An Arabic name for God meaning 'The Maker' or 'The Evolver'.</term>, Glory be to Him. This is because one who lacks power over what is "possible" is considered weak. Since the existence of all beings entirely free from any evil is "possible" in the imagination of the common people, the Almighty said:

> "If We had willed, We could have given every soul its guidance; but the word from Me will come true, 'I will surely fill Hell with jinn and men all together.'" <note>Qur'an 32:13. Ibn Rushd uses this to show that while God could have made everyone perfect, the current system of the world includes "evil" as a necessary part of its complex design.</note>

The common people understand one meaning from this, while the elite understand another. The elite's understanding is that it is not mandatory for Him, Glory be to Him, to create a creation whose existence is not accompanied by any evil. Thus, the meaning of His saying "If We had willed, We could have given every soul its guidance" is: If We had willed, We would have created a creation whose existence is not accompanied by evil, but rather...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that God's justice is not identical to human justice (which implies servitude) and explains that scripture uses general language because the masses cannot distinguish between logical possibilities and impossibilities.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, Mutakallimun, Ta'wil (Interpretation), The Masses vs The Elite, Possible vs Impossible, Problem of Evil, Qur'anic Interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of divine justice and the problem of evil to the fifth major "question" of the text: the nature of the afterlife. Ibn Rushd (Averroes) is setting the stage to explain how religious descriptions of heaven and hell are symbolic representations of a truth that can also be proven through reason.</meta>

۱۱۸

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...creatures who are pure good, such that every soul would be granted its guidance. This extent [of explanation] regarding this issue is sufficient. Let us now proceed to the fifth question.

### ۞ The Fifth Question: Regarding the Resurrection and its States ۞

The **Resurrection** <term>al-Ma‘ad: Literally "the Return"; the Islamic concept of life after death and the soul's return to its Creator.</term> is among those matters whose existence all **religious laws** <term>al-Shara’i‘: The plural of Sharia, here referring to all divinely revealed religions (Judaism, Christianity, and Islam).</term> have agreed upon, and for which **logical demonstrations** <term>al-Barahin: Rigorous philosophical proofs that produce certain knowledge, as opposed to mere rhetorical or dialectical arguments.</term> have been established among the scholars. Religions only differ in the *description* of its existence, and not in the reality of its existence; they only differ in the observable things used as symbols to represent that hidden state to the general public.

This is because some religious laws describe it as purely spiritual—meaning for the **souls** <term>al-Nufus: The plural of nafs (soul); in this context, the debate is whether only the soul is immortal or if the body is resurrected as well.</term>—while others describe it as being for both the bodies and the souls together. The consensus on this matter is based on the agreement of revelation on the one hand, and the agreement of necessary logical demonstrations among all people on the other. By this, I mean that everyone agrees that for a human being, there are two **felicities** <term>sa‘adatayn: Two forms of "happiness" or "success"—one achieved in this world through virtue and the other in the hereafter.</term>: one in the hereafter and one in this world. This is built, for everyone, upon principles that are universally acknowledged. Among these is that the human being is nobler than many other existing things. Another is that if the condition of every existing thing shows that it was not created **in vain** <gloss>abathan: without purpose or meaning</gloss>, and that it was only created for a required action which is the "fruit" of its existence, then the human being is even more worthy of this purpose. God—Exalted be He—pointed out the existence of this meaning in all beings in the Mighty Book, where the Exalted said:

> ۞ And We did not create the heaven and the earth and that between them in vain. That is the opinion of those who disbelieve; then woe to those who disbelieve from the Fire. ۞ <note>original: "وما خلقنا السموات والارض وما بينهما باطلا ذلك ظن الذين كفروا من النار" (Qur'an 38:27).</note>

And He said, praising the scholars who recognize the **ultimate purpose** <term>al-Ghaya: The "telos" or final cause; the reason for which a thing exists.</term> required of this existence:

> ۞ Those who remember Allah standing and sitting and on their sides and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth: "Our Lord, You did not create this in vain; Glory be to You! So protect us from the punishment of the Fire." ۞ <note>original: "الذين يذكرون الله قياما وقعودا وعلى جنوبهم ويتفكرون في خلق السموات والارض ربنا ما خلقت هذا باطلا سبحانك فقنا عذاب النار" (Qur'an 3:191).</note>

The existence of a purpose in the human being is more apparent than in all other existing things. God—Glory be to Him—has pointed this out in more than one verse of His Book, saying:

> ۞ Did you then think that We created you in vain and that you would not be returned to Us? ۞ <note>original: "أفحسبتم أنما خلقناكم عبثا وأنكم الينا لا ترجعون" (Qur'an 23:115).</note>

And He said:

> ۞ Does man think that he will be left neglected? ۞ <note>original: "أيحسب الانسان أن يترك سدى" (Qur'an 75:36). The word 'suda' implies being left without purpose or accountability.</note>

And He said:

<summary>Ibn Rushd introduces the concept of the Resurrection, arguing that while religions use different symbols to describe the afterlife, all agree on its reality based on the philosophical principle that humans have a noble purpose and were not created in vain.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Afterlife, Telos, Divine Purpose, Human Felicity, Quranic Proofs, Ibn Rushd, Islamic Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the afterlife (the Fifth Question). Ibn Rushd is transitioning from the general necessity of the Resurrection to the purpose of human existence and how religion facilitates the soul's perfection through knowledge and action.</meta>

۱۱۹

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

-> ۞ **"And I did not create the jinn and mankind except to worship Me."** ۞ <note>Quran 51:56. Ibn Rushd cites this to show that the purpose of sentient life is centered on the Creator.</note> <-

This refers to the genus of beings that He knows. He said this to draw attention to the fact that the obligation of worship arises from the knowledge of the Creator:

-> ۞ **"And why should I not worship Him who created me and to whom you will be returned?"** ۞ <note>Quran 36:22. This verse links the act of creation to the necessity of worship and the eventual return to God.</note> <-

When it becomes clear that man was created for the sake of certain intended actions, it also becomes clear that these actions must be specific to him. We see that every single one of the existing things was created for the sake of an action that is found in it and not in others—meaning the action that is unique to it. 

If this is the case, then the ultimate goal of man must lie in the actions that distinguish him from all other animals; these are the actions of the **rational soul** <term>al-nafs al-natiqa: A classical philosophical term for the human soul, which possesses the unique faculty of reason and speech, unlike the vegetative or animal souls.</term>. 

Since the rational soul consists of two parts—a practical part and a theoretical part—it follows that the primary requirement is for the soul to reach its perfection in these two powers. By this, I mean the **theoretical virtues** and the **scientific virtues** <term>al-fada’il al-‘ilmiyya wa al-fada’il al-nazariyya: In Aristotelian ethics, these represent the highest forms of human excellence—one involving moral action and the other involving the contemplation of universal truths.</term>. 

The actions that allow the soul to acquire these two virtues are the "goods" and "beauties," while those that hinder it are the "evils" and "sins." Since the determination of these actions is mostly achieved through **revelation** <term>al-wahy: Divine communication to prophets.</term>, the **religious laws** <term>al-shara’i‘</term> were sent to establish them, to define them, and to encourage them. 

Thus, the laws commanded virtues and forbade vices. They defined the measure of knowledge and action necessary for the happiness of all people—meaning the **common happiness** <term>al-sa‘ada al-mushtaraka: The baseline level of spiritual and intellectual well-being accessible to the general public, as opposed to the specialized "private happiness" of the philosopher.</term>. Regarding theoretical matters, the laws defined what every person must know: the knowledge of God (blessed and exalted is He), the knowledge of the angels, the knowledge of the noble beings, and the knowledge of ultimate happiness. 

Likewise, the laws defined the extent of those actions by which souls become virtuous through the practical virtues. This is especially true of **this law of ours** <note>Meaning Islam.</note>, for when it is compared to other religious laws, it is found to be the absolutely complete law. For this reason, it was the "Seal of all Laws." 

Since revelation in all religions has warned that the soul is immortal, and logical demonstrations have been established among the scholars regarding this, [it is understood that] souls are subject to a state after death where they are stripped of bodily desires. If the soul was pure, its purity is multiplied by this shedding of desires...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues that humans were created specifically to exercise their "rational soul" through theoretical and practical virtues. He asserts that religious law (Sharia) exists to provide the masses with the necessary knowledge and moral guidelines to achieve soul-perfection and eternal happiness.</summary>

<keywords>rational soul, theoretical virtues, common happiness, religious law, purpose of creation, immortality of the soul, perfection, Islam as Seal of Laws</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rational soul's perfection. Ibn Rushd is explaining how the soul's state at the moment of death—whether it has attained virtue or succumbed to vice—determines its experience in the afterlife. He is moving from the philosophical necessity of the soul's refinement to how different religions represent the state of the soul after it departs from the body.</meta>

۱۲۰

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...those bodily dispositions, even if they are base, find their wretchedness increased by the soul's separation from the body. This is because the soul suffers from the vices it acquired, and its regret intensifies over the purification it missed once it departs the body, for it is unable to acquire such purification except through this physical frame. This station is alluded to by the words of the Almighty:
> ۞ **"Lest a soul should say, 'Oh, how great is my regret over what I neglected in regard to God, and I was indeed among those who mocked.'"** ۞ <note>Quran 39:56. This verse illustrates the psychological pain and regret of a soul that realizes its missed opportunities for spiritual growth after it is too late.</note>

All religious laws <term>al-sharāʾiʿ: The plural of Sharia, used here by Ibn Rushd to refer to the various revealed dispensations or "religions" throughout history.</term> have agreed on teaching this state to the people, calling it **Ultimate Happiness** <term>al-saʿāda al-akhīra: Also translated as Eternal Felicity; the highest state of human well-being in the hereafter.</term> and **Ultimate Misery**. However, because this state has no equivalent in our witnessed, everyday world, the extent of what is perceived through revelation varies from one prophet to another, due to their differing levels in this regard—meaning in the capacity of revelation. Thus, religions have differed in how they represent the conditions of the souls of the blessed and the souls of the wretched after death.

Some religions did not represent the pleasure experienced by pure souls, or the pain felt by wretched souls, through witnessed, physical things. Instead, they explicitly stated that all such states are **spiritual conditions** and **angelic pleasures** <note>original: "lazzāt malakiyya"</note>. 

Other religions relied on representing these states through witnessed things; that is, they represented the pleasures perceived in the hereafter by the pleasures perceived here in this world, while stripping away the pain that usually accompanies them. Likewise, they represented the pain that exists there by the pain that exists here, while stripping away the relief that usually accompanies it. 

This may be because the authors of these religious laws perceived through revelation certain aspects of these states that were not perceived by those who represented them purely through spiritual existence. Alternatively, it may be because they saw that representation through **sensory things** <term>al-maḥsūsāt: Things perceived by the five senses (sight, touch, etc.) as opposed to the intellect.</term> is more effective in making the general public understand, as the masses are more powerfully moved toward or away from such things. Thus, they informed us that God Almighty returns the happy souls to bodies in which they enjoy eternal delight through the most intense sensory pleasures—such as **Paradise** <note>original: "al-Janna", literally "The Garden"</note>—and that He returns the wretched souls to bodies in which they suffer eternal pain through the most intense sensory torments—such as **The Fire** <note>original: "al-Nār"</note>. 

This is the case with our own law—Islam—in its representation of this state. In our Mighty Book <note>The Quran</note>, evidence has been provided that offers a shared path of belief for everyone...

<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd's (Averroes) exploration of how religious laws communicate the concept of the afterlife. He has just stated that while all religions agree on the existence of the soul’s ultimate happiness or misery, they differ in how they represent these states to the people based on the prophet’s capacity for revelation and the audience's needs.</meta>

۱۲۱

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...the possibility of these states, as the intellect does not perceive in these matters anything more than the possibility inherent in the shared perception of all people. These [arguments] all fall under the category of the **analogy of possibility**—comparing the existence of one thing to the existence of its equal (meaning, its emergence into existence) and comparing the possibility of the existence of the lesser and the greater to the emergence of the largest and greatest into existence. An example of this is His saying:
> ۞ **"And he produces for us a similitude and forgets his own creation."** ۞ <note>Quran 36:78. This verse refers to a skeptic who questioned how dry bones could be brought back to life, forgetting that God created him from nothing in the first place.</note>

The argument in these verses is based on the **analogy of the Return to the Beginning** <term>qiyās al-ʿawda ʿalā al-badāʾa: The logical argument that if God was capable of the initial creation (beginning), He is certainly capable of the resurrection (return), as both require the same power.</term>, for they are equal. In this verse, along with this analogy that proves the possibility of the Return, there is a refutation of the skeptic’s doubt regarding this view—a doubt based on the difference between the Beginning and the Return. This is found in His, the Almighty's, saying:
> ۞ **"He who made for you fire from the green tree."** ۞ <note>Quran 36:80. This addresses the materialist objection that life (warmth/moisture) cannot come from death (cold/dryness) by showing that God produces fire (hot/dry) from a living green tree (cool/moist).</note>

The doubt was that the Beginning arose from heat and moisture, while the Return [from death] starts from coldness and dryness. This doubt is countered by the fact that we perceive that God Almighty brings forth a thing from its opposite and creates it from it, just as He creates a thing from its like.

As for the analogy of the possibility of the existence of the lesser compared to the existence of the greater, it is like the saying of the Almighty in the verse:
> ۞ **"Is not He who created the heavens and the earth able to create the like of them? Yes, indeed, and He is the All-Knowing Creator."** ۞ <note>Quran 36:81. A "greater-to-lesser" argument: if God can create the vast universe, creating a human being again is a smaller task by comparison.</note>

Thus, these verses contain two proofs for the Resurrection and a refutation of the denier’s argument. If we were to exhaustively track the verses in the Precious Book <note>The Quran.</note> regarding these proofs, the discussion would be long, but they are all of the genus we have described.

All religious laws, as we have said, agree that souls have states of **happiness** or **misery** after death, but they differ in the **representation** <term>al-tamthīl: The use of symbols, metaphors, or imagery to make abstract or metaphysical truths understandable to a general audience.</term> of these states and in making their existence understood by the people. It appears that the representation found in this religious law of ours [Islam] is the most complete in making the majority of people understand, and the most effective in moving their souls toward what is there [the hereafter]. 

The majority of people are the primary target of religious laws. As for **spiritual representation** <term>al-tamthīl al-rūḥānī: Describing the afterlife in terms of intellectual or purely spiritual pleasures/pains.</term>, it seems to be less effective in moving the souls of the masses toward the afterlife; the masses have less desire for it and less fear of it than they do for **corporeal representation** <term>al-tamthīl al-jismānī: Describing the afterlife through physical imagery, such as gardens, fruits, and fire.</term>. Therefore, it appears that corporeal representation is more effective in moving [the soul]...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains the Quranic use of analogy to prove the possibility of resurrection, arguing that physical descriptions of the afterlife are more effective than spiritual ones for motivating the general public toward virtue.</summary>
