--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s analysis of how different groups within Islam interpret the descriptions of the afterlife (the Return). Having established the Quranic basis for the possibility of resurrection on the previous page, he now categorizes the various theological and philosophical schools of thought regarding whether the rewards and punishments of the hereafter are physical, spiritual, or a transformation of both.</meta>

١٢٢

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...what is there is spiritual, and the spiritual is more readily accepted by the **Mutakallimūn** <term>al-Mutakallimūn: Speculative theologians who used dialectical reasoning to defend religious doctrines.</term> and the disputants among men, though they are the minority. For this reason, we find that the people of Islam fall into three groups regarding the understanding of the representations found in our religion concerning the states of the **Return** <term>al-Maʿād: The "Return" or the Resurrection; the state of existence after death.</term>:

One group holds that that existence is identical in essence to this existence—the bliss and pleasure found here. By this, I mean they believe the two are one in genus, and that the only difference between the two existences is duration and cessation: the former is eternal while the latter is fleeting.

Another group holds that the two existences are distinct. This group is itself divided into two: 

One faction believes that the existence represented by these sensory things is **spiritual**, and that it was only represented in this physical way for the purpose of clarification <note>i.e., using physical metaphors to help the common person grasp abstract spiritual joy or suffering.</note>. This group possesses many well-known arguments from the Divine Law, so there is no need to enumerate them here. 

The other faction believes that it is **corporeal** <term>al-jismānī: Physical or bodily in nature.</term>, but they believe that the corporeality existing there differs from the corporeality here; for this world is perishable while that one is eternal. This view also has arguments from the Law. It appears that **Ibn Abbas** <note>ʿAbdallāh ibn ʿAbbās (d. 687 AD), a cousin of the Prophet Muhammad and a foundational figure in Quranic interpretation.</note> was among those who held this view, as it is narrated that he said: "There is nothing in this world from the Hereafter except the names." <note>original: "ليس في الدنيا من الآخرة إلا الأسماء" — This implies that while we use the same words (like "honey" or "milk"), the actual substance in the afterlife is fundamentally different from what we know on Earth.</note>

It seems that this latter view is the most appropriate for the **elite** <note>al-khawāṣṣ: The philosophers and deep thinkers, as opposed to the general public.</note>. This is because the possibility of this view is built upon matters that are not disputed by anyone. First, that the **soul** <term>al-nafs: The vital spirit or intellect that survives the body.</term> is immortal. Second, that the return of the soul to *other* bodies does not result in the same logical impossibilities that arise from the return of those *exact same* bodies. 

This is because it is evident that the **matter** <term>al-mādda: The physical substance that makes up the world.</term> of the bodies here is in a state of constant succession and transformation from one body to another. By this, I mean that the exact same matter may belong to many individuals at different times. Such bodies cannot all exist at once in actuality because their matter is one and the same. 

For example: a man dies and his body turns to dust; that dust then turns into a plant; another man feeds on that plant, and it becomes the seed <note>i.e., sperm.</note> from which another human is born. <note>This is a classic philosophical problem regarding the "identity of matter" in the resurrection: if one person's body becomes part of another's, how can both be resurrected physically at the same time?</note> However, if one posits *other* bodies <note>i.e., new, eternal bodies for the same soul.</note>, this difficulty does not occur. The truth in this matter is that every person’s position is simply what their own investigation has led them to conclude.

<summary>Ibn Rushd outlines three Islamic perspectives on the afterlife: literal physicalism, purely spiritual metaphor, and a "transformed" corporeality. He favors the third view—that the soul inhabits new, eternal bodies—as it avoids the logical paradoxes of recycled physical matter.</summary>

<keywords>Resurrection (al-Maʿād), Mutakallimūn, Ibn Abbas, Soul (al-Nafs), Matter (al-Mādda), Spiritual vs. Corporeal, Islamic Philosophy, Averroes</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s analysis of the afterlife, transitioning from the different schools of thought regarding the nature of the "Return" to a philosophical and scriptural defense of the soul's immortality. It concludes the section on the afterlife and introduces the final topic of the book: the rules of scriptural interpretation (Ta’wīl).</meta>

١٢٣

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...in it, provided it is not an inquiry that leads to the total negation of the fundamental principle, which is the complete denial of existence [after death]. For this type of belief necessitates declaring its adherent an unbeliever <term>Takfīr: The act of declaring a person a non-Muslim or infidel due to their specific beliefs.</term> because knowledge of this state for man is known to people through both the Divine Laws and reason. All of this is built upon the **survival of the soul**. 

If it is asked: Is there evidence in the Divine Law for the survival of the soul, or a pointer toward that? 

We say: That is found in the Mighty Book, which is the word of the Exalted: 

> **God takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that have not died during their sleep.** <note>Quran 39:42</note>

The point of evidence in this verse is that He equated sleep and death regarding the suspension of the soul's activity. If the suspension of the soul's activity in death were due to the destruction of the soul itself, and not due to a change in the soul's instrument <note>The "instrument" refers to the physical body or its organs.</note>, then it would necessarily follow that the suspension of its activity during sleep would also be due to the destruction of its essence. If that were the case, the soul would not return to its original state upon waking. Since it does return, we know that this suspension does not occur because of something that happened to its core substance, but rather it is something that happened to it because of the suspension of its instrument. Therefore, it does not follow that if the instrument is suspended, the soul itself is suspended. Death is a suspension [of the body], so it must be for the instrument, just as is the case in sleep. 

As **the Philosopher** <term>al-Ḥakīm: "The Wise One." In this context, Ibn Rushd is referring to Aristotle, whom he often cites to harmonize philosophy with revelation.</term> says: if an old man were to find an eye like the eye of a youth, he would see just as the youth sees. 

This is what we have seen fit to establish regarding the discovery of the beliefs of this religion, which is our religion, the religion of Islam. There remains for us, from what we promised, to look into what is permissible regarding **interpretation** <term>Ta’wīl: The process of allegorical or figurative interpretation of scripture, moving from the literal (zāhir) to a deeper meaning (bāṭin).</term> in the Divine Law and what is not permissible; and for whom it is permitted among those for whom it is allowed. We shall conclude the discourse of this book with this.

We say: The meanings found in the Divine Law exist in five types. This is because they are first divided into two categories: one category is not further subdivided, while the other is divided into four types. 

The first category (the undivided one) is where the meaning that is explicitly stated is itself the very meaning that exists in reality. 

The second category (the divided one) is where the meaning explicitly stated in the Divine Law is not the actual meaning intended, but rather was taken as a substitute by way of representation. This category is divided into four parts, the first of which...

<summary>Ibn Rushd argues for the soul's immortality using a Quranic analogy between sleep and death, then transitions into a systematic classification of how scriptural texts should be interpreted.</summary>
<keywords>survival of the soul, sleep and death, Quran 39:42, Aristotle (The Philosopher), Ta’wīl (Interpretation), Islamic Jurisprudence, Religious Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s discussion on scriptural interpretation (Ta’wīl), specifically categorizing the types of symbolic language used in sacred texts and how scholars should handle them based on the intellectual capacity of the audience.</meta>

١٢٤

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

...[The first type is] that the meaning which has been expressed through a symbolic representation is not known to exist except through long, complex, and compounded logical deductions that require a great deal of time and many arts to learn. These cannot be accepted except by those with superior natural dispositions. Furthermore, it is not known that the symbol expressed is actually different from the thing it represents except through the same kind of complex reasoning we have described. 

The second type is the opposite of this: it is when both matters are known through immediate and accessible knowledge—meaning, both the fact that the expression is a symbol and what it is a symbol for. 

The third type is when it is known through immediate knowledge that it is a symbol for something, but what it symbolizes is only known through complex, distant knowledge. 

The fourth type is the inverse of this: it is when it is known through immediate knowledge what it symbolizes, but it is only known through complex, distant knowledge that it is actually a symbol.

As for the first category of these types, interpreting it allegorically is undoubtedly a mistake. As for the first category of the second type—which is complex in both aspects—its interpretation is reserved specifically for **those firmly rooted in knowledge** <term>Al-Rasikhun fi al-'ilm: A Quranic term (3:7) referring to scholars with deep, stable understanding who are capable of interpreting ambiguous verses.</term>, and it is not permissible to disclose its interpretation to anyone else. 

As for the opposite of this—which is immediate in both aspects—its interpretation is the very thing intended by it, and disclosing it is obligatory. 

As for the third category, the matter is different, because in this category the symbolic representation was not used because the meaning was too difficult for the masses to grasp; rather, the symbol was used to stir the souls toward it. An example of this is the saying of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him:

> The Black Stone <note>original: Al-Hajar al-Aswad. The sacred stone set into the eastern corner of the Kaaba in Mecca.</note> is the right hand of God on earth.

And other similar sayings which are known by themselves, or through immediate knowledge, to be symbols, while what they symbolize is known only through complex knowledge. In such cases, the duty is that none should interpret them except the elite <note>Al-Khawass: In Islamic philosophy, this refers to the intellectually gifted or trained scholars, as opposed to the general public.</note> and the scholars. As for those who sense it is a symbol but are not among the people of knowledge, they should be told regarding what it symbolizes either that it is among the **ambiguous matters** <term>Al-Mutashabih: Verses or traditions whose meanings are not clear or have multiple interpretations.</term> known only to the scholars firmly rooted in knowledge, or the symbol should be translated for them into something closer to their own understanding. This latter approach is perhaps better for removing the doubt that arises in the soul from such matters.

The rule for this investigation is the path followed by Abu Hamid <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), the famous theologian and philosopher whom Ibn Rushd frequently critiques, though here he cites him as a source of methodological agreement.</note> in his book *The Decisive Criterion for Distinguishing Between Islam and Godlessness* <note>original: Kitab al-Tafriqa [bayna al-Islam wa al-Zandaqa].</note>. This involves informing this class of people that a single thing may have five levels of existence. These are what Abu Hamid calls: **Essential** existence, **Sensory** existence, **Imaginative** existence, **Intellectual** existence, and **Analogical** existence. So, when...

<summary>Ibn Rushd categorizes religious symbols into four types based on how obvious their metaphorical nature and underlying meanings are, arguing that complex interpretations must be kept from the masses to avoid confusion. He references Al-Ghazali’s framework of the "five levels of existence" as a tool for explaining difficult metaphors to non-specialists.</summary>

<keywords>Ta'wil, interpretation, Al-Ghazali, Black Stone, Al-Rasikhun fi al-'ilm, symbolism, religious logic, levels of existence, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues Ibn Rushd’s systematic breakdown of how religious texts should be interpreted. He is currently discussing the third and fourth categories of symbolic language in scripture, focusing on when it is appropriate to move from a literal to an allegorical meaning (Ta’wīl) and how this relates to different levels of human intellect.</meta>

١٢٥

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

The question is a matter of consideration: which of these four types of existence <note>Ibn Rushd refers here to a framework used by Al-Ghazali: essential, sensory, imaginative, and intellectual existence. If a literal (essential) meaning is impossible, scholars look to these other "levels" to find a valid interpretation.</note> is most convincing to the group who find it impossible that the intended meaning is **objective existence** <term>Al-Wujud al-Dhati: External reality; the way things exist in the world outside of our thoughts or perceptions.</term>? For such people, this symbolic representation should be applied to whichever type of existence they believe is possible. Within this category falls the saying of the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him:

> "There is no prophet whom I have not seen, for I have seen them all in this very place, even Paradise and Hell."

And his saying:

> "Between my basin <note>Original: "Hawdi." Refers to the Prophet's Pool or Fountain (Al-Kawthar) in the afterlife.</note> and my pulpit is a garden from the gardens of Paradise, and my pulpit is over my basin."

And his saying:

> "Every son of Adam is consumed by the dust, except for the tailbone." <note>This Hadith refers to the "Ajb al-Dhanab," a small bone at the base of the spine which, in Islamic tradition, is the only part of the body that does not decay and from which the body is recreated on the Day of Resurrection.</note>

All of these can be understood through immediate, accessible knowledge to be symbols. However, understanding *why* they are symbols—their deeper purpose—requires "distant" or specialized knowledge. In these cases, the interpretation should be explained to those who perceive the symbolism by using the closest of those four types of existence. This kind of **allegorical interpretation** <term>Ta’wīl: The process of interpreting a text beyond its literal meaning to uncover a deeper, symbolic truth.</term> is permissible in the Divine Law when used in these specific places and in this manner.

However, if used in other contexts, it is an error. **Abu Hamid** <note>Abu Hamid al-Ghazali (1058–1111), the famous theologian. Ibn Rushd often argues that Ghazali was not rigorous enough in deciding who should be allowed to see allegorical interpretations.</note> did not provide a detailed distinction for this. For instance, there are cases where both aspects—that it is a symbol and why it is a symbol—are only known through "distant" or complex knowledge. In such cases, there may be a suspicion that leads one to think at first glance that a passage is a symbol, but that suspicion is actually false. The duty in this situation is to invalidate that suspicion and not resort to allegorical interpretation at all. We have already shown you many places in this book where the theologians—namely the **Ash’arites** and the **Mu’tazilites** <note>The Ash'arites and Mu'tazilites were the two major schools of Islamic speculative theology (Kalam). Ibn Rushd, a philosopher, often critiques them for using dialectical arguments that confuse the common people.</note>—have made this mistake.

As for the fourth category—which is the opposite of the one just mentioned—it is when the fact that a passage is a symbol is only known through "distant" knowledge, but if it is accepted as a symbol, it becomes immediately clear why it is a symbol. The interpretation of this type is also subject to consideration: specifically, regarding those people who would understand the "why" but only suspect it is a symbol based on a persuasive but non-demonstrative argument (since they are not among **those firmly rooted in knowledge**). It could be argued that the safest path for the Divine Law is not to interpret these allegorically for such people. Instead, one should invalidate the reasons why they thought the statement was a symbol in the first place. This is the preferred approach. It is also possible that...

<summary>Ibn Rushd explains how to interpret Prophetic sayings that are clearly symbolic but difficult to explain, while critiquing Al-Ghazali and theological schools for over-interpreting texts where literalism should be maintained.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Rushd, Ta’wil, Al-Ghazali, Ash'arites, Mu'tazilites, symbolic existence, Prophetic Hadith, methodology of interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page concludes Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) treatise on the methodologies of interpretation. He has been explaining why certain symbolic texts exist in the Quran and Hadith and how improper interpretation by unqualified individuals leads to sectarianism and the declaration of heresy.</meta>

١٢٦

-> ### The Discovery of the Methods of Proof Regarding the Beliefs of the Religion ### <-

Allegorical interpretation <term>Ta’wīl: The process of moving from the literal, apparent meaning of a text to a symbolic or metaphorical one.</term> is permitted for them due to the strong resemblance between the literal thing mentioned and the concept it represents. However, whenever interpretation is allowed regarding these two categories, it gives rise to beliefs that are strange and far removed from the literal meaning of the Law <term>Sharia: Here used to refer to the body of religious texts and their apparent meanings.</term>. These interpretations often spread, leading the general public <term>Al-Jumhur: The masses or the majority of believers who rely on the literal sense of scripture.</term> to reject them. This is exactly what happened to the Sufis and to those scholars who followed their path. 

When the power of interpretation in this Law was seized by those who could not distinguish these specific contexts—nor distinguish the class of people who are actually permitted to engage in interpretation—the matter fell into disorder. Consequently, differing sects emerged, each declaring the other to be unbelievers <term>Takfīr: The practice of excommunicating other Muslims by labeling them as "Kafir" or infidels.</term>. All of this is born from an ignorance of the Law’s purpose and is an assault upon it.

By now, you have grasped from our words the extent of the error that occurs because of interpretation. It would be our wish to achieve this objective for all the statements of the Law—by which I mean discussing what ought or ought not to be interpreted, and if it is interpreted, by whom it should be done—concerning all the difficult passages found in the Quran and the Prophetic Traditions <term>Hadith: The recorded sayings, actions, and approvals of the Prophet Muhammad.</term>. 

We would show how all these passages return to these four categories <note>Referencing the four types of symbolic representation Ibn Rushd established earlier in the text.</note>. The purpose we intended for this book has now been fulfilled; we have presented it first because we saw it as the most important of all objectives related to the Law.

->**And God is the Granter of success in reaching what is right,**<-
->**and the Guarantor of reward, by His grace and mercy.**<-

->**"It was completed in the year five hundred and seventy-five."**<-

In this context, "Wisdom" refers specifically to Philosophy.</note>. In this work, he clarified the "Methods of Proof regarding the Beliefs of the Faith" and refuted every deviant innovation and misleading doubt. May God grant him the best of rewards on our behalf and shower him with the clouds of His mercy and pleasure.

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

->۞ Said <term>Shaykh al-Islam</term> <note>A title of honor for a scholar of supreme authority.</note> Ahmad bin Abd al-Halim bin Taymiyyah ۞<-

<margin>Page 31, Line 1</margin> He [Averroes] has categorized the classes of the Islamic community into four: the <term>Batinis</term> <note>Those who believe in a hidden, esoteric meaning of scripture beyond the literal text.</note>, the <term>Hashwiyyah</term> <note>A pejorative term used for literalists who were accused of collecting "worthless" or "unrefined" traditions.</note>, the <term>Mu'tazilites</term> <note>A school of Islamic theology that prioritized human reason and rationalism.</note>, and the <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology that attempted to reconcile reason and revelation.</note>. He was narrow in his view, for he did not mention the <term>Salaf</term> <note>The "Righteous Predecessors"—the first three generations of Muslims, whose path Ibn Taymiyyah argues is the true theological middle ground.</note>, which is the path of the best of this community until the Day of Resurrection.

<margin>Page 33, Line 7</margin> I say: among their principles—which they inherited from the <term>Mu'tazilites</term>—is the idea that "that which does not precede <term>temporal events</term> <note>original: "al-hawadith." In theology, this refers to things that begin to exist in time, as opposed to the "eternal" (qadim).</note> is itself a temporal event." This is agreed upon by rational people if what is meant is a *specific* temporal event; for that which does not precede a specific event must itself be a temporal creation. However, that which does not precede the *genus* (or type) of temporal events is the subject of dispute among people, and upon this principle, this entire proof—and many others—is built. Many people in this field do not distinguish between something that is itself a single temporal event and something where the individual instances are temporal but the *genus* has always existed. Indeed, many of the theologians <note>original: "Ahl al-Kalam." Scholars who use dialectical legalism and logic to defend the faith.</note>, when they saw that motions or other accidents are temporal events, believed that whatever does not precede them must also be temporal. They failed to distinguish between what does not precede a *specific* event and what does not precede a *perpetual genus* whose individual parts are temporal. The latter does not "precede" the genus even if it precedes every individual instance within it. When many theologians realized this distinction, they sought to prove the impossibility of an infinite sequence of temporal events through the "method of correspondence" <note>A logical argument used to prove that a series must have a beginning by comparing two sets of sequences.</note> and similar methods mentioned elsewhere. They do not accept the existence of temporal events with no beginning originating from an Eternal Actor, yet they accept the existence of a specific temporal act originating from an Eternal Actor. He [Averroes] says that a temporal event must have its existence linked to a temporal act, and that act to another temporal act, and so on. Thus, the individual temporal acts are perpetual in their *genus* as they originate from an Eternal Actor. He argues that it is impossible for a temporal event to exist from an Eternal Actor except through a sequence of individual temporal acts, but they [the other theologians] do not accept this. (End of quote from the book *The Integration of Reason and Revelation* <note>original: "al-Jam' bayna al-'Aql wa al-Naql."</note>). He wrote this regarding the statement: "As for the Ash'arites..." down to the statement: "among their principles is that which does not precede the temporal is temporal."

<margin>Page 33, Line 8</margin> He wrote this regarding the statement: "If the Actor was acting at one time..." down to the statement: "...finite," quoting it in its entirety. Then he said: I say, this is the very point that necessitated the view of <term>al-Nazzam</term> <note>A famous Mu'tazilite theologian known for his original and often controversial views on physics and theology.</note> and others regarding <term>the Leap</term> <note>original: "al-tafrah." The theory that an object can move from point A to point C without passing through the intermediate point B, proposed to solve paradoxes of infinite divisibility.</note>, and the view of a group of philosophers and theologians regarding the acceptance of the infinite divisibility of matter in potentiality, though not in actuality. A group of those who deny the existence of the "indivisible part" (the atom) responded that everything that exists is capable of division in the sense that one part of it is distinct from another—this is a conceptual, hypothetical division. However, this does not necessitate the existence of infinite parts. For even if it is said that an existing thing cannot be divided in actuality, it does not contain infinite parts. And if it is said that it *can* be divided in actuality, then once its parts become sufficiently small, they undergo a transformation and vanish <note>original: "tastahil wa tafsad." A term from Aristotelian physics meaning to change from one substance to another, like water turning to vapor.</note>, just as small particles of water transform into air. Once they transform upon reaching the limit of smallness, it does not follow that they remain as they were, capable of infinite further divisions, nor does it necessitate the existence of infinite parts. (End of quote).

***

<summary>This page contains Ibn Taymiyyah's critiques of Averroes regarding the classification of Islamic sects and the philosophical nature of time and matter. Ibn Taymiyyah argues for the "perpetual genus" of God's creative acts and addresses paradoxes of infinite divisibility in physics.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, Averroes (Ibn Rushd), Salaf, Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, temporal events, infinite sequence, al-Nazzam, the Leap, divisibility of matter, Islamic theology, Kalam</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription of marginal notes by Shaykh Tahir al-Jaza'iri, reflecting the critiques of Ibn Taymiyyah on Averroes's theological summaries. The previous page discussed the classification of Islamic sects and the nature of temporal events.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

<margin>Page 36, Line 3</margin> I say: those who claim that the **act of originating** <note>original: "al-ihdath." The process by which something is brought into existence.</note> is identical to the **thing originated** <note>original: "al-muhdath." The object or event that has been created.</note>, and that the **created** is the same as the **Creator**, and the **effect** is the same as the **act**—as is the position of the <term>Ash'arites</term> <note>A dominant school of Sunni theology that seeks a middle ground between rationalism and literalism.</note>—do not concede that "origination" is an **accident** <note>original: "arad." In philosophy, a property that exists only within a substance, like color or motion.</note>, nor that it has a locus, let alone it being a distinct existential entity. However, he [Ibn Taymiyyah] has already presented a refutation of this, arguing that for every effect there must be an act. In that case, it is said: the act of originating subsists in the **Originator** <note>The Creator.</note>, while the beginning of the originated thing is not an existing "accident" subsisting in anything other than the Originator's act. It is also said that this whole argument is built on the idea that the **nonexistent is a "thing"** <note>A controversial theological debate: whether a thing "exists" in some form of potentiality before it is actually created.</note> and that **essences** <note>original: "al-mahiyat." The "what-ness" or defining nature of a thing.</note> in external reality are additional to their existence. Both these ideas are false. Even assuming they were true, the answer would be: that which is capable of beginning to exist is those very entities and essences. However, the point he mentioned is established through the well-known method of his companions: that every temporal event is preceded by **possibility** <note>original: "al-imkan." The potential for something to exist.</note>, and possibility must have a locus; therefore, the originated thing must have a locus. <note>End of note.</note> He wrote this regarding his [Averroes's] statement: "And among the customary doubts..."

---

<margin>Page 36, Line 13</margin> I say: This is the famous paradox: if it is said that the act of the agent and the origination of the originator relate to a thing at the **time of its nonexistence**, it would necessitate the thing being both existent and nonexistent at once. If it is said they relate to it at the **time of its existence**, it would necessitate "attaining the attained" <note>An Arabic idiom (tahsil al- حاصل) meaning a redundant or impossible action, like trying to create something that already exists.</note> and the thing having existence twice. The answer is: the act relates to the thing *at the moment of its existence*, meaning the act is what makes it exist—not that the thing existed without the act and then the act made it exist again. <note>End of note.</note> He wrote this regarding his statement on the two groups being forced <gloss>on them</gloss> to affirm the existence of a **void** <note>original: "al-khala'." Empty space containing no matter; a major point of contention in medieval physics.</note>.

---

<margin>Page 36, Line 19</margin> His statement: "So these doubts": I say: The claim by this author and his likes that <term>Abraham</term> <note>The Prophet Ibrahim, regarded as the father of monotheism.</note> used the argument of **motion** <note>The "argument from motion" suggests that because things move, there must be a First Mover.</note> is the same kind of claim made by the <term>theologians</term> <note>original: "ahl al-kalam." Scholars who used rational and speculative discourse to defend the faith.</note> whom his [Ibn Taymiyyah's] companions and the <term>Salaf</term> <note>The righteous early generations of Muslims.</note> condemn. They claim Abraham argued via motion, but he [Averroes] asserts that the "way of the elite" is the way of <term>Aristotle</term> <note>The Greek philosopher whose works heavily influenced Islamic philosophy.</note> and his followers, who argued from motion to show that the movement of the celestial spheres is voluntary and moves out of a desire to resemble an unmoving substance. Those theologians, on the other hand, say that Abraham’s use of the argument from motion was because a moving thing must be originated, as it is impossible for an infinite chain of motions to exist. Each of these two groups [the philosophers and the theologians] invalidates the other’s method and demonstrates its contradictions through rational evidence. 

The truth of the matter is that Abraham followed neither of these two paths, nor did he use motion as his proof. Rather, he argued from **vanishing** <note>original: "al-uful." Referring to the setting of the stars, moon, and sun as described in the Quran (Chapter 6).</note>, which means disappearing and being veiled, as has been explained elsewhere. That which vanishes is not worthy of worship; this is why he said: 
> "I am free of what you associate [with God] except for He who created me" 
and he said: 
> "I have turned my face toward He who created the heavens and the earth, as a monotheist, and I am not of the polytheists." 

His people acknowledged the existence of the Lord, but they were **polytheists** <note>original: "mushrikin." Those who associate partners with God.</note>. Thus, he argued for the condemnation of polytheism, not for the existence of a Creator. If the goal had been to prove a Creator, the story of Abraham would have been an argument *against* his position, not *for* it; for from the moment the star, the sun, and the moon rose until they set, they were in motion, yet he did not renounce his "love" for them or declare himself free of them—as he did with the things they associated with God—until they actually **vanished**.

<summary>This page contains Ibn Taymiyyah's critique of the Ash'arite and Aristotelian views on creation and motion, specifically refuting the idea that the Prophet Abraham used the "argument from motion" to prove God's existence.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, Ash'arites, Averroes, Aristotle, Abraham, creation, motion, uful (vanishing), polytheism, Kalam, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical critique of Ibn Taymiyyah regarding the nature of causality, time, and the eternity of the world, specifically addressing the views of philosophers like Averroes and theologians like the Ash'arites.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah <-

...This indicated that their motion was not contrary to Abraham’s purpose; rather, it was their **setting** <note>original: "aful." Referring to the celestial bodies disappearing or setting below the horizon.</note> that was contrary to it. End of quote.

<margin>Page 37, Line 8</margin> Regarding his statement: "And also, time is among the **accidents**." <note>original: "arad." In philosophy, a property that exists only within a substance, like color or motion.</note> I say: the substance of this argument is that an **infinite regress** <note>original: "tasalsul." A chain of causes or events going back forever without a beginning.</note> in causes is impossible, because a cause must exist for the effect to exist. However, in the case of conditions and traces—such as a father being a condition for the existence of a son, or a cloud being a condition for the existence of rain—this is not impossible. This is a well-known dispute and has been mentioned elsewhere. 

There is nothing in this that benefits the philosophers in their claim regarding the **eternity of the celestial spheres** <note>original: "qidam al-aflak." The Aristotelian belief that the heavens have existed forever without a beginning.</note>; rather, its goal is only to invalidate the claim of those who say that temporal events must necessarily come to an end. It has been stated more than once that the philosophers' argument is void under both of two contradictory premises: 

1. If the existence of the infinite is impossible, then their claim [of an eternal world] is void. 
2. If the existence of the infinite is permissible, then it is not impossible for the existence of the celestial spheres to be dependent on events preceding them, where every event is conditioned by what came before it. This is exactly what they say regarding well-known events like the birth of humans or the falling of rain. 

Indeed, this actually necessitates the impossibility of events arising from a **complete cause** <note>original: "illa tamma." A cause that contains all necessary conditions, meaning the effect must happen immediately.</note> which necessitates its effects. Their position would imply that events have no **Actor** <note>original: "fa'il." A conscious agent or creator.</note>, since every event would be conditioned by a prior event. It is impossible—according to them and others—for anything to arise, either through an intermediary or without one, from a "complete cause" that necessitates its effect. This is because that would imply the delay of some of its effects, meaning the cause would not be "complete," but would rather contain some "potentiality" that has not yet become "actual"—which is the opposite of what they claim. End of quote.

<margin>Page 40, Line 7</margin> Written regarding his statement: "As for the second method, it is that which was derived by **Abu al-Ma'ali**." <note>Referring to Imam al-Haramayn al-Juwayni (d. 1085), a famous scholar of the Ash'arite school.</note> I say: the substance of this talk is to affirm the **wisdom** <note>original: "hikma." Purposeful design.</note> and the appropriate **purpose** <note>original: "ghaya." Teleology or end-goal.</note> found in all existing things, which explains why each thing is specified with its unique characteristics. The connection between certain matters may be a condition for existence, or a condition for perfection. 

By affirming this, he begins to critique the argument of Abu al-Ma'ali and those like him who affirm nothing but **pure Divine Will** <note>original: "al-mashi'a al-mahda." The idea that God chooses one design over another for no reason other than His absolute will.</note> as the sole factor that specifies each creature's attributes and measures. This is the view of a group of theologians such as the <term>Ash'arites</term> and the <term>Zahirites</term> <note>A school of law and theology that sticks strictly to the literal meaning of texts.</note>, as well as a group of jurists from the followers of the four Imams. 

However, the majority of Muslims and others, while they affirm the Will and Volition of God, also affirm His **Wisdom** and **Mercy**. These "philosophers" <note>referring to the followers of the Hellenistic tradition like Averroes.</note> criticized the Ash'arites for denying **teleological wisdom** <note>original: "al-hikma al-gha'iya." The belief that things are created for a specific purpose.</note>, as is the view of the majority of Muslims. For it follows that they [the philosophers] should affirm the Divine Will by way of priority and more fittingly; for whoever acts for a purpose they desire is, by way of priority, a "willer" of that action. 

If they [the philosophers] then deny the **Free Actor** <note>original: "al-fa'il al-mukhtar." A creator who acts by choice rather than by natural necessity.</note> and claim that God is a "necessitating cause" for the effect without will, this is the height of contradiction. Whoever follows the method of Abu al-Ma'ali in this evidence does not need to deny Wisdom; rather, when he affirms the intended Wisdom, he can affirm the Will **a fortiori** <note>original: "bi-tariq al-awla." By a stronger reason.</note>. Thus, the world and all the specific ways it is designed...

<summary>This page contains Ibn Taymiyyah's philosophical critique of the eternity of the world and his defense of Divine Wisdom. He argues against both the "complete cause" theory of the philosophers and the "pure will" theory of the Ash'arites, suggesting instead that God's actions are driven by both Will and purposeful Wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, causality, infinite regress, Divine Will, teleology, Ash'arites, philosophers, celestial spheres, Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni, necessity versus choice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Taymiyyah’s critique of philosophical and theological definitions of existence. It specifically addresses the debate between Ibn Rushd (Averroes) and Ibn Sina (Avicenna) regarding whether a "possible" being can be eternal, and refutes the idea that the "scholars" praised in the Quran are the Greek philosophers or their followers.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah <-

...some of which point to the **Will** <note>original: "mashi'ah." The divine intent to choose one possibility over another.</note> of its Agent, as well as His **Wisdom** and His **Mercy**, which encompasses benefit and benevolence toward His creation. 

If this is the case, our statement that "any way other than this way is permissible" means that it is permissible and possible in itself, and that the Lord is capable of a way other than this just as He is capable of this one. This does not contradict the fact that Divine Will and Wisdom have **specified** <note>original: "khassasat." The act of choosing a specific form or time for a created thing out of all possibilities.</note> some possible things over others. Therefore, this premise mentioned by Abu al-Ma'ali <note>referring to the Imam al-Haramayn al-Juwayni, a famous Ash'arite theologian.</note> is a correct premise about which there is no doubt. The issue lies in establishing the second premise—which he discussed elsewhere—concerning whether the specification of **possibilities** <note>original: "al-mumkinat." Things that do not have to exist by necessity but can exist if a cause brings them about.</note> in certain ways rather than others necessitates their being **temporal** <note>original: "huduth." Having a beginning in time, as opposed to being eternal.</note> or not. End of quote.

<margin>Page 41, Line 19</margin> Written regarding his statement: "And we find Ibn Sina..." I say: Ibn Rushd’s <note>Averroes, the 12th-century Andalusian philosopher known for his commentaries on Aristotle.</note> intention is that a **produced effect** cannot be pre-eternal and beginningless. According to him, and according to all rational people—including Aristotle and his followers, and even Ibn Sina and his followers, despite their contradictions—the **Pre-eternal** <note>original: "al-qadim al-azali." That which has always existed and whose non-existence is impossible in the past or future.</note> is that which cannot possibly be non-existent. Something that can either exist or not exist is necessarily **temporal** (created in time), and the temporal cannot turn into the eternal. 

For this reason, he [Ibn Rushd] said: "The possible cannot be Necessary." As for the "possible" whose existence and non-existence are both possible—which is the temporal—becoming **Necessary of Existence by another** <note>original: "wajib al-wujud bi-ghayrihi." A philosophical term for something that exists because something else necessitates it, a core concept in Avicenna's thought.</note>, there is no doubt about this. I do not think Ibn Rushd disputes this, though some of the **theologians** <note>original: "mutakallimun." Scholars who use rational arguments to defend religious doctrines.</note> do. This is the truth, even if Ibn Sina said it; not everything Ibn Sina said is false. Rather, the doctrine of the **Sunni people** <note>original: "ahl al-sunna."</note> is: "Whatever God wills happens," and thus its existence becomes necessary; "and whatever He does not will does not happen," and thus its existence is impossible. The majority of humanity agrees on this. End of quote.

<margin>Page 42, Line 8</margin> Written regarding his statement: "As for the second proposition..." I say: As for his claim that the "scholars" mentioned in the Quran are the brothers of the philosophers, the logicians, and the followers of the Greeks, this is a false claim. We know by **necessity** <note>original: "bi-l-idtirar." Knowledge that is self-evident or undeniable within a religious framework.</note> from the religion of Islam that those whom God praised for their **Monotheism** <note>original: "Tawhid."</note> are not the polytheists who worship stars and idols and practice magic. Nor are they those who believe in the **eternity of the celestial spheres** <note>original: "qidam al-aflak." The belief that the heavens had no beginning.</note>, nor those whose words imply that events happen by themselves without a Creator. 

We know by necessity that the knowledge of Monotheism is not dependent on the unique contributions of the philosophers in **Logic** <note>original: "mantiq."</note> regarding definitions and syllogisms—matters on which most people disagree with them. This includes their distinction between **essentials** <note>original: "dhatiyat." Internal attributes that make a thing what it is.</note> and **accidents** <note>original: "aradiyat." External attributes that are not part of a thing's core essence.</note> that are inseparable from an essence, and their distinction between the "reality" of existing things and "existence" itself. 

Whoever disputes these two [theologians/philosophers] supports the view of Aristotle, saying that what is "possible" to exist or not exist can only be temporal. Such a person denounces Ibn Sina’s claim that the "possible" can be pre-eternal. As for Ibn Sina attributing this [eternal possible] to Plato, it may be said that this is not accurate regarding what he [Plato] affirms of "rational substances"—such as Time, Matter, and the Void—for he considers them to be substances...

<summary>Ibn Taymiyyah critiques the attempt to equate Quranic scholars with Greek-influenced philosophers, while also navigating the technical debate between Averroes and Avicenna over whether a "possible" being can be eternal or must be created in time.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, Ibn Sina (Avicenna), Ibn Rushd (Averroes), Aristotle, Logic, Necessary Existence, Pre-eternity, Monotheism (Tawhid), Islamic Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ibn Taymiyyah’s critique of philosophical cosmology and the nature of the Divine Will. The previous page addressed the views of Ibn Rushd (Averroes) and Ibn Sina (Avicenna) regarding whether a "possible" being can also be eternal, specifically looking at how theologians like Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni define the "specification" of created things.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah <-

...an ancient, eternal intellect, yet they simultaneously claim it is "permissible" and "possible" <note>The author is highlighting a logical tension in philosophy: how can something be both "eternal" (having no beginning) and "possible" (dependent on a cause)?</note>. The transmission of this view from him requires closer scrutiny. As for the **celestial spheres** <note>original: "al-Aflak." In medieval science, these were the nested transparent shells that carried the planets and stars around the Earth.</note>, what is reported from **Plato** <note>The famous Greek philosopher and student of Socrates.</note> and others is that they are **temporal** <note>original: "muhdatha." Meaning they were brought into existence at a specific point in time.</note>. However, **Aristotle** <note>Plato’s student, who departed from him by arguing the universe had no beginning.</note> argued for the eternity of the spheres, the intellects, and the souls. They report that the first among them to claim the eternity of the world was Aristotle, who is known as the "Master of Instruction" <note>original: "Sahib al-Ta'alim." A traditional honorific for Aristotle in the Arabic tradition, recognizing his role in codifying logic and science.</note>. As for the ancients like Plato and his predecessors, they—or many of them—did not argue for the eternity of the world itself; rather, they believed in the eternity of other matters from which something else is created, and from that, another thing is created, until the process of creation reaches this world. This view held by the ancients, or many of them, is better than the view of Aristotle and his followers. 

<margin>Page 42, Line 13</margin> Written regarding his statement: "As for Abu al-Ma'ali..." <note>Referring to Imam al-Haramayn al-Juwayni (d. 1085), a towering figure in Ash'arite theology.</note> I say: As for his concession that the **Will** <note>original: "al-Iradah." The divine attribute that chooses between different possibilities.</note> specifies one of two identical options, this contradicts what he mentioned earlier—that an effect must have a **Wisdom** <note>original: "hikmah." A purposeful reason or benefit for an action.</note> that necessitates its existence over its opposite. The Will relates to the effect because the One who wills knows the sought-after wisdom within that effect. Whoever holds this view finds it impossible for the Will to specify one of two identical things arbitrarily. Rather, one must be specified by a factor that necessitates the Will’s connection to it. Otherwise, if they were truly equal in every way, it would be impossible for one to be willed over the other according to this view. Given this concession, it could be said that the mere choice of the Agent—which is His Will—specified existence for one era over another despite their similarity, and in one measure over another, and with one description over another. As for his dispute regarding the world having a boundary that encompasses it: they do not need to prove an "existing thing" in which the world resides. Rather, they say we know the possibility of it being moved to the right or left by necessity, even if what lies beyond it is "pure nothingness" <note>original: "‘adam mahd." Absolute non-existence or void.</note>. Calling that a "place" is like someone saying the world is in a "location"; the terms "location," "place," and "space" <note>original: "mawdi‘," "makan," "hayyiz." Three technical terms for where an object exists.</note> can refer to something that exists or to a conceptual void.

<margin>Page 43, Line 5</margin> Written regarding the premise stating: "The Will..." I say: The discussion regarding the Will—whether it is multiple or a single entity, its genus or its specific type, its generality or its particularity, its eternity or its temporal occurrence, or the occurrence of its types versus its individual instances—and the disputes people have over this is not for this place. It is among the greatest debates of the theologians, and the discussion on it resembles the discussion on **Speech** <note>Referring to the famous "Createdness of the Quran" debate regarding whether God’s Speech is eternal or created.</note> and similar attributes. However, the mere concession that something is an "effect" of a Will necessitates its **temporality** <note>original: "huduth." Having a beginning in time.</note>. Indeed, the mere concession that a thing is an "effect" <note>original: "maf‘ul." Literally "something acted upon."</note> necessitates its temporality. The idea of an eternal, beginningless "intended effect" that has always co-existed with its "Willing Agent"—having no agent other than an eternal Will and an eternal Action—is something the majority of rational people know to be false by natural reason. Thus, assuming that the Creator has eternally willed to act upon one thing after another, everything besides Him would be temporal, existing after it was not. In this case, the Will would be "eternal" in the sense that its **kind** is eternal <note>This is Ibn Taymiyyah's famous concept of *qadim al-naw‘, hadith al-ahad*: God has always been an acting Creator (eternal kind), but each specific act of creation has a beginning (temporal instances).</note>, even if each created thing is willed by a specific temporal act of Will.

<margin>Page 44, Line 5</margin> Written regarding his statement: "It has thus become clear..." I say: The action whose root is the **Love of God** <note>original: "hubb Allah."</note> is something the Sacred Law has commanded because it is intended for its own sake. It is an aid toward righteous deeds and toward other beneficial knowledge.

<summary>This page explores the conflict between Aristotelian "eternity of the world" and the theological view of "creation in time," focusing on how the Divine Will chooses between possibilities. Ibn Taymiyyah argues that while God is eternally a Creator, every individual created thing must have a beginning, fueled by Divine Wisdom and Love.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, eternity of the world, Divine Will, Abu al-Ma'ali al-Juwayni, celestial spheres, temporality, wisdom, Ash'arite theology, love of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Taymiyyah’s critique of theological and philosophical methods, specifically responding to Ibn Rushd’s (Averroes) analysis of the Mu'tazilite and Ash'arite schools. It shifts from discussing the eternity of the world to the specific proofs for God's existence, the nature of divine providence, and the logical necessity of God's oneness.</meta>

# Discussion of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> **Discussion of Ibn Taymiyyah** <-

**Page 45, Line 5** — He wrote regarding his [Ibn Rushd's] statement: *“As for the Mu'tazilites...”*
I say: The path of the **Mu'tazilites** <note>A rationalist school of Islamic theology that emphasized human reason and the absolute unity of God, often leading them to interpret divine attributes metaphorically.</note> is the same path he mentioned regarding the **Ash’arites** <note>The dominant school of Sunni theology, founded by Abu al-Hasan al-Ash’ari, which sought a middle path between extreme rationalism and literalism.</note>. Indeed, those among the Ash'arites who adopted this path only took it from them [the Mu'tazilites]. The Mu'tazilites are the original source of this method; it spread from them and is attributed to them. For this reason, because **Al-Ash'ari** <note>The founder of the Ash'arite school, who was himself a Mu'tazilite before publicly renouncing their views to defend the traditions of the early scholars.</note> would sometimes agree with them and sometimes agree with the **Salaf** <note>The "Predecessors," referring to the early generations of Muslim scholars whose approach was based strictly on the Quran and Hadith.</note>, the leading Imams, and the People of Hadith and Sunnah, he condemned this [Mu'tazilite] method, as his words on that matter were previously mentioned. He criticized and faulted it in agreement with the Salaf and the Imams.

If **Ibn Rushd** <note>Also known as Averroes, the famous Andalusian philosopher and jurist who tried to harmonize philosophy with Islamic law.</note> saw what he saw in the books of the Ash'arites and observed their reliance upon it, that is why he spoke against it. The best of the later Mu'tazilites is **Abu al-Husayn al-Basri** <note>A 11th-century Mu'tazilite scholar known for his expertise in legal theory and theology.</note>; he relies on this method in all of his books, even in his work titled *The Brilliance of the Proofs* <note>original: "Ghurar al-Adilla."</note>. He says at the beginning of it: "We are mentioning the objective of this book and its benefit, so that when a person recognizes the nobility of that benefit and the nobility of the goal, his soul will endure the hardships of seeking it and the effort of attaining it. We say: the objective is that which leads through proofs to the knowledge of God the Exalted, and the knowledge of what is permissible and impermissible for Him regarding **Attributes** <note>The characteristics of God, such as Knowledge, Power, and Will.</note> and **Actions**, and the truthfulness of His messengers and the validity of what they brought." He stated that the benefit of this is clearly great and noble from several perspectives, one of which is that whoever knows these things through proofs is safe from being led astray by others.

**Page 45, Line 7** — He wrote regarding his [Ibn Rushd's] statement: *“If it is said: it has become clear that all these paths are not...”* etc.
I say: This demonstrates that the movements of the **celestial spheres** <note>The orbiting bodies of the heavens.</note> do not originate from themselves, but rather from a mover separate from them, such that this mover is the "Subjugating Command." This is clarified through extensive arguments in other places.

**Page 46, Line 13** — He wrote regarding his [Ibn Rushd's] statement: *“As for the Second Principle...”*
I say: Regarding this verse and the verse of the **Taking of the Covenant** <note>Refers to Quran 7:172, where God is said to have extracted the descendants of Adam from his loins and made them bear witness to His Lordship.</note>, there is much to say that does not belong here. The same applies to his [Ibn Rushd's] claim that the path is restricted to these two types, and his statement that in the verses there is that which indicates **Providence** <note>original: "al-‘Inaya." The philosophical argument that the order and utility of the universe prove a caring Creator.</note> but not **Invention** <note>original: "al-Ikhtira‘." The argument that the sheer existence of things from nothing proves a Creator.</note>, and so on—this is talk that does not belong here. Rather, everything that indicates Providence also indicates Invention. However, the purpose here is simply to recount what he mentioned.

**Page 49, Line 12** — He wrote regarding his [Ibn Rushd's] statement: *“A Discourse on Oneness...”*
I say: It is self-evident that a single, specific effect cannot be the action of two independent actors, neither through independence nor through cooperation. A single effect cannot be the result of two independent or shared causes. This is something no rational person disputes once they have conceptualized it. For if one of them were independent in producing it, it would necessitate that the entire effect occurs by him alone. If we supposed the other was also like that, it would necessitate that the action of each of them is the whole [effect] alone. One acting alone negates having a partner in it, let alone another independent actor. This would necessitate the joining of two opposites: affirming the independence of one and denying it, and affirming his exclusivity and denying it. This is a contradiction. It is self-evident that the specific effect produced by a creator cannot be shared by another, just as it cannot be independent of him; for if another shared in it, it would not be his effect, but rather...

<summary>Ibn Taymiyyah critiques the theological methods of the Mu'tazilites and Ash'arites, arguing they share a flawed rationalist origin. He also challenges Ibn Rushd's distinction between the "Providence" and "Invention" proofs for God, asserting they are logically intertwined.</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, Ibn Rushd, Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, Abu al-Husayn al-Basri, Ghurar al-Adilla, Providence, Invention, Divine Oneness, Celestial Spheres, Logical Contradiction</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ibn Taymiyyah’s critical engagement with the arguments of Ibn Rushd (Averroes) and the scholastic theologians (the Mutakallimun). The focus shifts to the logical and scriptural proofs for the oneness of God, specifically analyzing the "Argument from Mutual Hindrance" and the interpretation of Quranic verses regarding the impossibility of multiple deities.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> **Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah** <-

...part of it is his effect, and it would be an effect for him and for another simultaneously. It is impossible for a single effect to result from two independent causes. For this reason, the only rational understanding of "partnership" is cooperation, where each party performs a different action than the other. Examples include people cooperating in construction—where one carries the bricks and another places them—or carrying a wooden beam—where one carries one end and another carries the other. All created beings assist one another in their actions; there is no created being that is entirely independent in producing an effect alone. <note>End of quote.</note>

**Page 50, Line 11** — He wrote regarding the verse, *"Say: If there were [with Him other gods]..."* <note>Referring to Quran 17:42: "Say, [O Muhammad], 'If there were with Him [other] gods, as they say, then they [those gods] would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne.'"</note>:
I say: Having first established the impossibility of two agents producing a single identical effect, he then established the impossibility of multiple lords whose actions differ. This is because a difference in actions would prevent the effect from being a single, unified whole—yet the world is one. His interpretation of this verse in this manner is consistent with his earlier method of interpreting other verses. <note>End of quote.</note>

**Page 50, Line 21** — Regarding his statement: *"And for that reason, God Almighty said..."* etc.
I say: He has followed the same path he previously mentioned regarding this verse. There are two well-known interpretations among the commentators for the phrase *"they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne."* 

The first is that it means seeking a way to Him through **closeness, worship, and petition.** 

The second is through **resistance and overcoming Him.** 

The first interpretation is the correct one. This is because the verse says, *"If there were with Him gods as they [the polytheists] say,"* and the polytheists did not claim that their gods resisted or sought to overcome Him. This contrasts with another verse: *"No god was with Him; otherwise, each god would have taken what he created, and some of them would have sought to overcome others"* <note>Quran 23:91</note>. This latter verse refers to the hypothetical "negated gods" and does not imply they would overcome God Himself, nor that the polytheists claimed such a thing. 

Furthermore, the phrase *"sought a way to the Lord of the Throne"* indicates this [seeking closeness]. As the Almighty said: *"Indeed, this is a reminder; so whoever wills may take a way to his Lord"* <note>Quran 73:19</note>, meaning taking a path of worship and obedience. Conversely, when the opposite is intended, the preposition changes; for example: *"If they obey you, seek no way against them"* <note>Quran 4:34</note>, where the text says "against them" (*'alayhinna*) rather than "to them" (*ilaihinna*). 

Additionally, "seeking a way to Him" is something commanded, as in: *"and seek the means of approach to Him"* <note>Quran 5:35</note> and: *"Say, 'Invoke those you have claimed besides Him... Those whom they invoke seek the means of access to their Lord, [striving as to] which of them is nearest, and they hope for His mercy and fear His punishment'"* <note>Quran 17:56-57</note>. Thus, it is explained that those who are called upon besides God actually seek a means to reach Him. This aligns with the verse: *"If there were with Him gods... they would have sought a way to the Lord of the Throne."* My purpose here is to clarify what he mentioned regarding the methods of the **Mu'tazilites** and those **Ash'arites** who followed their path, not to provide an exhaustive explanation of the verse's meaning. <note>End of quote.</note>

**Page 50, Line 21** — He wrote regarding his statement: *"This is the proof of both nature and law in knowing the Oneness [of God]..."* etc.
I say: Rather, what the **Nuzzar** <note>Speculative theologians or dialecticians who use formal logic to prove religious dogmas.</note> among the **Mutakallimun** <note>Theologians of the Kalām tradition.</note> mentioned—which they called the **Argument from Mutual Hindrance** <term>Dalil al-Tamanu': A logical proof stating that if there were two omnipotent gods, they could disagree; if one succeeded, the other would be weak, and if neither succeeded, the world wouldn't exist.</term>—is a complete proof for their objective. That objective is the impossibility of the world originating from two [creators]. However, this is specifically the **Unity of Lordship** <term>Tawhid al-Rububiyyah: The belief that God is the sole Creator, Owner, and Disposer of all affairs.</term>, whereas the Quran clarifies both the **Unity of Divinity** <term>Tawhid al-Uluhiyyah: The belief that God alone is worthy of worship and that no partners should be associated with Him in devotion.</term> and the Unity of Lordship together. <note>End of quote.</note>

**Page 52, Line 11** — He wrote regarding his statement: *"And it may indicate to you that the proof understood by the theologians..."* etc.
I say:

<summary>Ibn Taymiyyah critiques Ibn Rushd's interpretation of Quranic verses on divine unity, arguing that the "Argument from Mutual Hindrance" correctly proves God is the sole Creator (Lordship), but that the Quran's primary focus is the necessity of worshipping Him alone (Divinity).</summary>
<keywords>Ibn Taymiyyah, Ibn Rushd, Argument from Mutual Hindrance, Tawhid al-Rububiyyah, Tawhid al-Uluhiyyah, Mu'tazilites, Ash'arites, Quranic exegesis, Divine Unity</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Quranic proofs for the oneness of God, specifically focusing on the term "corruption" (fasad) in the context of the "Argument from Mutual Hindrance." It then transitions into a complex critique of the nature of Divine Knowledge, contrasting "Eternal Knowledge" with "Originated Knowledge," likely responding to the views of the philosopher Ibn Rushd (Averroes) or the scholastic theologians (the Mutakallimun).</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> **Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah** <-

The corruption mentioned in the verse <note>Likely referring to Quran 21:22: "Had there been within the heavens and earth gods besides Allah, they both would have been ruined (corrupted)."</note> was not restricted to a specific time. This "corruption" is not the "impossibility of existence" that is imagined in the argument of "mutual hindrance" <term>Tamānu': a logical argument stating that if two all-powerful gods disagreed, neither could act, leading to a state where nothing exists</term>—which occurs when one agent intends something and another intends its opposite. Nor is it the impossibility of an action occurring when one object is supposedly produced by two independent agents. All of these scenarios would result in a total lack of existence. As for **corruption**, it is the opposite of **rectification** <note>original: *al-salah*, meaning "goodness," "proper order," or "well-being"</note>, just as the Almighty said: *(And when it is said to them, "Do not cause corruption on the earth," they say, "We are but reformers [rectifiers]")* [Quran 2:11]. And the Almighty said: *(And Moses said to his brother Aaron, "Take my place among my people, and rectify, and do not follow the way of the corrupters")* [Quran 7:142]. And He said: *(And do not cause corruption upon the earth after its reformation)* [Quran 7:56]. And He said: *(Had the Truth followed their desires, the heavens and the earth and whoever is in them would have been corrupted. Rather, We have brought them their reminder, but they are turning away from their reminder)* [Quran 23:71].

The "rectification" of a thing is the attainment of its perfection, through which its happiness is realized; its "corruption" is the opposite. For created beings, their rectification and happiness lie in God being their sole object of worship—the One to whom their love and will ultimately lead, making Him the "Goal of all goals" and the "End of all ends." The Almighty said: *(And I did not create the jinn and mankind except to worship Me)* [Quran 51:56]. Thus, worshiping Him is the ultimate purpose in which their well-being resides. <note>End of quote.</note>

**Page 54, Line 19** — There is a missing portion here, which is: ("What is said to the elite is that <term>Eternal Knowledge</term> <note>Divine knowledge which has no beginning</note> does not resemble the <term>Originated Knowledge</term> <note>Human knowledge which is acquired and changes over time</note> of humans. What a human perceives regarding the difference in originated knowledge between the past, the future, and the present is something specific to originated knowledge. As for Eternal Knowledge, these types of knowledge must be united within it. This is because it is impossible for God to lack knowledge of what occurs among these three states of existence. Certainty has been established regarding His—Glory be to Him—knowledge of them, while negating 'the how' <term>Takyīf: the attempt to define the specific modality or "how" of God's attributes</term>, because assigning a 'how' would necessitate likening Eternal Knowledge to originated knowledge.") This is correct. <note>End of quote.</note>

I say: This discourse is of the same type he narrated from the <term>Scholastic Theologians</term> <note>The *Mutakallimun*, who used rationalistic arguments to defend faith</note>. For if Eternal Knowledge united the knowledge of the past, present, and future such that one was not distinct from the other, then the knowledge of a thing while it exists and while it does not exist would be one and the same. This contradicts what was stated previously: that knowledge of two distinct existing things must be different. The most this man <note>Referring to the author being critiqued, likely Ibn Rushd</note> says on this matter is that the "lack of distinction" is fixed in Eternal Knowledge but not in originated knowledge. 

There is no doubt that those theologians say this, but they also say that if we were to assume the persistence of originated knowledge, its ruling would be like that of the Eternal. They claim this falls under the category of "originated relations and additions" <note>original: *al-nisab wa-l-idafat*</note>—attributes that change depending on perspective but do not imply a change in the essence itself, much like how a person can be "to the right" or "to the left" without their nature changing. This person says that only the relations and additions are renewed; he mentioned this in an essay of his regarding knowledge. However, the theologians are better than him in this regard, because they affirm God's knowledge of things *after* they come into existence—either through an "additional knowledge" according to some, or through that "initial knowledge" according to others. As for this man, he only affirms the knowledge that is the *cause* of their existence, as his words will show later. To the theologians, this is a rule that applies to both the Necessary <note>The Necessary Being, i.e., God</note> and the Eternal, whereas this man says it is a rule specific to the originated. He has provided no proof for this distinction other than a mere claim. He explained this in a separate treatise dedicated to the issue of knowledge, where he sought to defend...

<summary>Ibn Taymiyyah defines "corruption" as a disruption of divine order rather than mere non-existence, and critiques the philosophical view that God's eternal knowledge does not differentiate between past, present, and future.</summary>
<keywords>corruption (fasad), rectification (salah), Eternal Knowledge, Originated Knowledge, Mutakallimun, Ibn Rushd, divine attributes, relations and additions</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of philosophical views on Divine Knowledge, specifically transitioning from the "Argument from Mutual Hindrance" to the problem of whether God knows specific, individual events (particulars) or only general laws (universals). It introduces a lengthy correction or missing passage regarding the nature of the Divine Will and the theological concept of "originated events" (hawādith) occurring within the Divine Essence.</meta>

# Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah

-> **Discussions of Ibn Taymiyyah** <-

...by that [to respond] to the philosophers of whom it was said that they claim He [God] knows the universals <term>al-kulliyāt: general concepts or laws of nature</term> but does not know the particulars <term>al-juz’iyāt: specific, individual events or entities</term> except in a universal manner. [End of quote].

**Page 55, Line 8: There is a missing passage here** (And it has become clear from our statement that the occurrences <term>al-hawādith: temporal events or accidents</term> which necessitate the "origination" of the locus in which they subsist are those occurrences that change the essence of a thing. As for the realization of God’s Will, this belongs to the knowledge of the "specialized elite" <note>likely referring to the philosophers or specialized theologians</note>. These people intended to make the public understand the "Will" in a sense other than the known, understood meaning of "Will" as explicitly stated by the Law <note>original: *al-Shar‘*, referring to Islamic revelation</note>. It is a meaning that the general public does not understand and which human minds cannot conceptualize. They made this one of the foundations of the Religion and declared those who did not subscribe to it as disbelievers. The furthest scholars have gone in this matter is to establish a proof that there exists a "Will" that is "beyond modality" <term>ghayr mukayyafah: something that cannot be assigned a "how" or a physical description</term>. It cannot be called an "Eternal Will" from which a temporal event necessarily follows, nor is it a "Temporal Will" like that of the human witness. Rather, it is a Will that human intellects are too limited to conceptualize, just as they are limited in conceptualizing the rest of the Attributes with which He [God] described Himself. For whenever they are conceptualized, they resemble temporal, conceptualized attributes. Thus, one must believe in all of them through demonstrative proofs, "without asking how" <term>bi-lā kayf: a theological principle of accepting revelation without anthropomorphic explanation</term>). Correct.

I say: As for it being a Will that is not like the will of the [created being] <note>The text says "Creator" here, but the context of the comparison suggests the author is distinguishing the Divine Will from the human will</note>, this is a necessity for it and for all other Attributes. This is not exclusive to the "Will," just as the Lord Himself has "nothing like unto Him" [Quran 42:11], so too are His Attributes. However, the mere negation of similarity is something no one disputes. The substance of his [the philosopher's] words is to refrain from speaking on its eternity or its temporal origination, rather than providing a solution to the theological problem—just as he did in the issue of Knowledge. The Materialist Philosophers <term>al-Dahriyyah: those who believe in the eternity of the world and deny a creator</term> are confused on this point. Anyone who speaks on this matter finds their words contradictory because of the corruption of the principle they build upon: the idea that temporal events proceed from a "Complete Cause" <term>‘illa tamma: a cause that necessitates its effect immediately</term> that produces its effect either with or without a medium. This is impossible; in fact, it is a combination of two opposites. This is because a "Complete Cause" cannot be separated from any of its necessary effects, nor the effects of its effects. Since temporal events are, by definition, delayed, they cannot be among its necessary effects or the effects of its effects. [End of quote].

<summary>This page analyzes the contradictions in the philosophical view of the Divine Will and Knowledge, specifically critiquing the idea that an Eternal "Complete Cause" can produce temporal effects. It emphasizes the "bi-la kayf" (without how) approach to Divine Attributes while pointing out the logical failings of the Materialist (Dahriyyah) position.</summary>

# Advertisement

-> ❊ From the Jamaliyya Press ❊ <-

Located at Haret al-Rum <note>original: *Haret al-Rum*, literally "The Quarter of the Greeks/Europeans," a historic district in Cairo known for its diverse population and artisan shops</note>, Atfat al-Tatari, Number 9: By the success and help of God Almighty, we have completed the establishment of the aforementioned press with the most complete preparations. 

We have acquired a new-model machine for it and prepared fonts of all types—both Istanbul <note>original: *Istanbuliyya*, referring to the refined calligraphic printing styles favored in the Ottoman capital</note> and Egyptian—along with all necessary tools. We are ready to accept contracts for printing Arabic scientific books, whether large or small, with or without vowelization <term>shakl: the addition of short vowel marks to the text to ensure correct pronunciation</term>, at moderate rates while strictly adhering to contract deadlines. 

Furthermore, we have prepared many varieties of paper required for book printing. Anyone wishing to contract for both the printing and the paper of a book may do so, with the assurance that our paper prices are the most affordable on the market. This is because we import the paper directly from its factories in Europe, and experience is the fairest witness of our quality.

-> The Press Owners <-

-> (Muhammad Amin al-Khanji and Partners — and Ahmad Arif) <-

<note>The names listed here are significant in the history of Arabic publishing. Muhammad Amin al-Khanji (1865–1939) was a famous Syrian-born bookseller and publisher in Cairo who played a vital role in preserving classical Arabic heritage through his high-quality editions.</note>
